## The Americans and Philosophy - Reds in the Bed

- ch. 1 "Cover" *(not yet read)*
- ch. 2 "Half Title" *(front)*
- ch. 3 "Series" *(front)*
- ch. 4 "Title" *(front)*
- ch. 5 "Copyright" *(front)*
- ch. 6 "Dedication" *(front)*
- ch. 7 "Contents" *(front)*
- ch. 8 "Acknowledgements" *(notes)*
- ch. 9 "1 Introduction" **·** Thomas Hobbes, John Locke, Jean-Jacques Rousseau, Karl Marx, Charles W. Mills, Aristotle, Michael Walzer, Catharine MacKinnon, W.E.B. Du Bois — The Social Contract; The Racial Contract; Class Struggle; Feminism — Smith introduces his project of explaining the subordination of women and blacks philosophically and empirically by comparing the social contract liberalism of Hobbes, Locke and Rousseau with Marxism, building on Carole Pateman's Sexual Contract and Charles Mills's Racial Contract/Contract and Domination, which show the classic contracts were male supremacist and white supremacist. He argues sexual and racial egalitarianism is about politics and power, cataloguing nine power bases women and blacks should acquire, and stresses that because family and state are historically connected (Aristotle's Politics, Hobbes's 'little kingdoms', Locke's conjugal society, Rousseau's family as the oldest society, Engels's 'first class oppression' of the female by the male), equality in marriage may be a precondition for equality in society. He defends Marxism against Mills's dismissal of it as moribund, endorses MacKinnon's linking of sexism and racism while arguing (with Linda LaRue and Du Bois) that the racial contract has been more tyrannical than the sexual contract, and closes with Walzer's definition of egalitarianism as a society free of domination.
- ch. 10 "2 Power and Politics" **·** Karl Marx, Aristotle, Niccolò Machiavelli, Thomas Hobbes, John Locke, Jean-Jacques Rousseau, Bertrand Russell, Karl Popper, Catharine MacKinnon — Class Struggle; Ideology and False Consciousness; Historical Materialism; Virtù and Fortuna — The chapter traces politics from the classical ethics-fused view (Aristotle's Politics, where politics exists for the sake of the good life yet also supplies one of the first justifications of patriarchy and the public/private split) to the Machiavelli-Hobbes break where politics becomes simply the possession and exercise of power, adopting Bertrand Russell's claim that power is the fundamental concept of social science. It then builds the book's central instrument, a Weber-derived analysis of nine power bases (money, knowledge/technology/information, religion, morality, authority, charisma, size/solidarity, violence, status), contrasting the Marxian reading - money as ultimately determinative, the ruling ideas of each age as the ideas of the ruling class, religion as the opium of the people, the state as a committee for managing bourgeois affairs - with the liberal-Weberian reading in which no single base is decisive. It closes by showing that Hobbes, Locke, Rousseau and Marx divide over the morality of egalitarianism (Popper's Marx moved by moral impulse versus Locke's possessive individualism), and that power is domain-specific, inert until used, skill-dependent and always relative.
- ch. 11 "3 On the Origin of the Family" **·** Thomas Hobbes, John Locke, Jean-Jacques Rousseau, Karl Marx, John Stuart Mill, Shulamith Firestone — The Social Contract; Historical Materialism; The Subjection of Women; The Dialectic of Sex — The chapter compares the liberal contract theorists with Engels (building on Morgan's Ancient Society) on the origin of the patriarchal monogamous family: Hobbes derives sovereign-husbanded 'little monarchies' from a state of nature in which women are men's equals, a derivation Susan Okin shows is contradictory; Locke grounds conjugal society in a voluntary compact with the husband as 'abler and stronger' last determiner, yet leaves women excluded from the social contract; Rousseau pairs passionate economic equality with some of the most sexist writing in Western thought, including fear of wives' sexual power in Emile. Against these static philosophies Engels gives a historical-materialist account in which the overthrow of mother right - the 'world historical defeat of the female sex' - followed from private property and inheritance, turning the bourgeois wife into a proletarian or prostitute while opening 'sex love' to the propertyless, and prescribing women's return to the labor force, socialized child-care, industrialized housework and easy divorce. John Stuart Mill's Subjection of Women appears as liberalism's one exception to its sexism, and a footnote sets Firestone's biological account of patriarchy against Engels's property account.
- ch. 12 "4 On the Origin of the State" **·** John Locke, Thomas Hobbes, Jean-Jacques Rousseau, Karl Marx, John Stuart Mill, Immanuel Kant, G.W.F. Hegel, David Hume — The Social Contract; The State of Nature; Labor Theory of Property; The General Will — Opening with Newt Gingrich's complaint that students 'don't read enough Locke' and the Chinese premier's appeal to Rousseau, the chapter compares the three contract theorists' states of nature and purposes of government: Hobbes's war of every man against every man yielding an absolute sovereign with no separation of powers, Locke's peaceful nature in which mixing labor with land creates property and money then creates inequality, so that government's 'great and chief end' is protecting property through limited, separated powers and no food stamps, and Rousseau's compassionate natural humans corrupted when the first man enclosed land, so the state was founded on fraud and a general will that should 'force him to be free' and give people food stamps. Smith then reads the actual US founding as a chaste Lockean contract 'bastardized' on race and sex - the Declaration plagiarized from Locke, Abigail Adams's 'remember the ladies' laughed off, Dred Scott, with the 14th Amendment as the de-bastardizing 'Charter of Universal Freedom' - while Engels derives the state historically from the Iroquois and Greek gens, private property and class conflict. A final section documents white supremacy inside the tradition (Kant inventing the scientific concept of race, Hegel and Hume on Black inferiority), John Stuart Mill's 'Negro Question' as the tradition's decisive anti-racist break, Locke's own entanglements with the slave trade, and Marx's condemnation of slavery as super-exploitation.
- ch. 13 "5 The Changing Bases of Sexual Politics, 1960–2020" **·** Betty Friedan, John Locke, Karl Marx — The Feminine Mystique (the problem with no name); Feminism — Measuring women's progress since 1960 by access to the nine power bases, the chapter shows women using the size/solidarity base to launch the modern feminist movement - Betty Friedan's The Feminine Mystique as its manifesto, NOW as its vehicle, Ms. as its outlet and Gloria Steinem as its charismatic leader - and how law followed: the accidental Smith amendment adding sex to the 1964 Civil Rights Act, Reed and Frontiero subjecting sex classifications to scrutiny under the 14th Amendment, Griswold and Roe/Casey making reproductive control (which Justice Blackmun called 'necessary for the complete emancipation of women') a condition of women's power, and the 1970s no-fault divorce revolution that Engels's liberation program had demanded. It then charts each base statistically: the narrowing labor-force and wage gaps, women's still minor share of the bourgeoisie, the reversal of the college gender gap and Women's Studies programs built on the Black Studies model, women's entry into cabinet, Court, Congress, clergy and the military, secularization that weakens the religion base behind Elizabeth Cady Stanton's old challenge, and a new moral consensus that unequal treatment of women is wrong.
- ch. 14 "6 Wives and Husbands The Marital Power Struggle" **·** John Locke, Karl Marx, Thomas Hobbes, Jean-Jacques Rousseau, Simone de Beauvoir, Catharine MacKinnon, Shulamith Firestone — Feminism; Objectification; The Dialectic of Sex — Taking Susan Okin's 'the personal is political' and Kate Millett's Weberian herrschaft against Locke's public/private dichotomy, the chapter reviews the empirical studies of marital power (Blood and Wolfe, Blumstein and Schwarz, Steil) and finds money is the master base in marriage: the husband's income and occupational prestige set his say, working wives gain power but couples conspire to understate wives' earnings ('manning up and womaning down'), while knowledge and status are the next strongest resources. Authority from religion, tradition and morality still favors husbands (Ephesians' 'wives submit yourselves unto your husbands' alongside the philosophers' 'abler and stronger'), violence persists from the rule of thumb to marital rape and murder despite legal change, housework remains women's burden - Beauvoir's never-won 'battle against dust', Engels's 'leaden boredom', Davis's industrialization proposal and Okin's demand that it be shared - and sex itself works as a power base in the bedroom, with MacKinnon diagnosing heterosexuality as the linchpin of gender inequality. The chapter ends with black couples' comparatively egalitarian patterns traced by Angela Davis to slave-quarters equality, Schwarz's rare 'peer marriages', and the Marxist (Okin, the Swedish model) versus liberal (Wilson, the Japanese model) readings of family change.
- ch. 15 "7 The Bourgeoisie and the Blacks" **·** Karl Marx, W.E.B. Du Bois, John Locke — Class Struggle; Historical Materialism; The Racial Contract; Totalitarianism — The chapter sets business - the US bourgeoisie whose privileged position Charles Beard read into the Constitution and which survived the Jacksonian and New Deal challenges by breaking labor through Taft-Hartley, right-to-work laws and the PATCO lockout, its solidarity secured while worker solidarity was undercut by what Marx called the Achilles heel of racism - against blacks as the paradigmatic powerless, shut out of every power base since slavery, which the chapter treats with Baptist and Marx's 1846 letter as a totalitarian system of super-exploited, tortured labor that built American capitalism, its neo-slavery progeny (Blackmon) pauperizing the black community under Dred Scott's 'subordinate and inferior class of beings'. It then narrates black counter-power base by base: the abolitionist interracial coalition wielding charisma (Douglass, Nat Turner, John Brown), information, morality and religion; the NAACP era and King's fusion of charisma, church religion, Myrdal's An American Dilemma and televised nonviolent provocation against Bull Connor's violence, contested by Malcolm X, SNCC and the Black Panthers; and a post-civil-rights scorecard showing a wealth base 94.2 percent white, persistent education and media gaps, constrained black officials from mayors to the symbolic Obama presidency, and a captive but potentially leveraged black vote in a 'Sisyphean' struggle.
- ch. 16 "8 Conclusion" **·** Karl Marx, John Locke, Jean-Jacques Rousseau — Class Struggle; The State of Nature — The conclusion restates the book's method - centering political philosophy in a consciously normative, empirical study of power - around its two contrasts: the family as a natural, subordination-requiring institution in liberalism versus a property-rooted, historically determined one in Marxism, and the state as a freely agreed contract of all (Locke, hence no food stamps) versus what actually happened, a charter 'ordained and established by a small group of wealthy white men, meeting in secret' to protect their wealth, so that the state which created gender and racial inequality must now play an equally decisive role in undoing it. Reviewing the nine bases since the 1960s, it finds women have closed the knowledge gap and nearly completed Engels's liberation program except universal child-care and non-exploitative housework, against conservative marriage-movement scholars like the Lockean Yenor, while blacks still face a tripling wealth gap that only social democratic capitalism on the Danish model plus comprehensive reparations can close - the latter argued on the moral base of power, Congress's 2009 apology, and Bernard Boxill's Lockean inheritance argument for a decades-long 'Reparations Superfund' as the civil rights issue of the 21st century.
- ch. 17 "Index" *(notes)*

## The Atkins Diet and Philosophy - Chewing the Fat with Kant and Nietzsche

- ch. 1 "The Atkins Diet and Philosophy" *(not yet read)*
- ch. 2 "The Atkins Diet and Philosophy" *(not yet read)*
- ch. 3 "The Atkins Diet and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 4 "The Atkins Diet and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 5 "The Atkins Diet and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 6 "The Atkins Diet and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 7 "The Atkins Diet and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 8 "The Atkins Diet and Philosophy" *(notes)*
- ch. 9 "The Atkins Diet and Philosophy" **·** Plato, Friedrich Nietzsche — The editors' introduction argues that dieting — not just food — deserves philosophical attention, and that the Atkins Diet specifically raises 'chewy' questions about how to decide what to eat, what to believe, and whom to believe, precisely because it defies balanced-diet convention and much expert opinion. It opens with Plato's Republic prescription of roasted meats for campaigning guardians (no pots to carry), cites Nietzsche's Ecce Homo and its 'lengthy disquisition' on his own dietary choices as the rare philosophical treatment of diet, and credits Feuerbach with 'Der Mensch ist was er isst' — you are what you eat. It closes by mapping the volume's sixteen essays across Kant, Hume, Nietzsche, Marx, Dewey, environmental and feminist philosophy, critical race theory, and the philosophy of science.
- ch. 10 "The Atkins Diet and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 11 "The Atkins Diet and Philosophy" **·** John Dewey, Charles Sanders Peirce, René Descartes — Pragmatism; The Pragmatic Method; Moral Psychology (character, vice, weakness of will) — Auxier, an Atkins dieter, argues the diet works for the reason Dewey gives in Human Nature and Conduct: habits built in calm moments are the bulwark against desire in crisis, and Atkins succeeds by reshaping which gratifications are habitually available — 'a steak for the black eye of desire.' He then transfers Atkins's physiology to thinking: television and advertising serve 'conceptual carbs,' packaged bundles of doubt, emotion, and readiness to act that bypass inquiry and leave the public 'intellectually diabetic' and ideological. Peirce's pragmatic maxim is translated into an eight-step mental exercise (worked through a parent's tattoo-or-piercing doubt about a teenage daughter), while Descartes's hyperbolic doubt is discarded in favor of pragmatism's 'genuine doubt.'
- ch. 12 "The Atkins Diet and Philosophy" **·** Friedrich Nietzsche — Framed by The Onion's 'New Nietzschian Diet Lets You Eat Whatever You Fear Most' and its 'Fat is dead' riff on the death of God, Irwin argues via Nietzsche's Ecce Homo that 'small things — nutrition, place, climate' matter more than philosophy usually admits, and that dieting belongs to giving style to one's character: we are both artists and works of art, so a 'sound mind in an Atkins body' is self-creation. Drawing on his own two years on the diet (187 to 165 pounds, steadier mood, lower cholesterol), he contends Atkins fits some constitutions — Schwarzenegger's carnivorous persona — and not others: Tony Soprano, devouring gabagool amid agita and fainting spells, could not do Atkins without ceasing to be who he is. Nietzsche's 'One has to know the size of one's stomach' becomes the rule of dietary self-knowledge.
- ch. 13 "The Atkins Diet and Philosophy" **·** Immanuel Kant — Autonomy of the Will; The Categorical Imperative; Humanity as an End in Itself — Dennis uses Kant to sort eaters into the fundamentally unfree (Herbert, raised on Atkins and thieving, who merely ploughs on), the fundamentally random (Rita, whose synapses and muscles would fire in chaos), and the autonomous, arguing that only an autonomous person can genuinely decide whether to follow Atkins — by weighing friends' testimonials against the medical establishment rather than habit, whim, or advertising. The categorical imperative grounds the autonomous life: 'lie when you can gain' fails universalization, and treating humanity as an end means offering a vegetarian guest food she is comfortable with instead of pressing Atkins meat on her. His own 'Dan Dennis Diet,' built by experiment and reasoning, illustrates autonomy in practice, and the case of Kitty shows that observing someone's diet never reveals whether its holder is unfree or autonomous.
- ch. 14 "The Atkins Diet and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 15 "The Atkins Diet and Philosophy" **·** Jean-Jacques Rousseau, Frantz Fanon — Natural Goodness and Corruption by Society; The State of Nature; Black Skin, White Masks — Knight places Atkins's caveman utopia — Paleolithic abundance, hunter-gatherer vitality, downfall through agriculture — inside the primitivist tradition running from Ovid's Golden Age to Rousseau's Noble Savage, whose state of nature Rousseau himself admitted was 'mere conditional and hypothetical reasoning' aimed at European corruption. Using postcolonial critics (Spurr, Torgovnick, and Fanon's line that whites who feel 'too mechanized' turn to 'the men of color' for 'human sustenance'), she argues low-carb primitivism is self-reflexive nostalgia — South Beach cannot even date its own paradise — that also freezes living Inuit and Indigenous Australians as Stone Age remnants, hiding the colonial and economic causes of their diabetes and poverty.
- ch. 16 "The Atkins Diet and Philosophy" **·** David Hume — Problem of Induction — O'Connell extracts Brillat-Savarin's 1825 anti-flour-and-starch regimen — clear soup without croutons, shun beer, rye over white bread — as a 'proto-Atkins' and reconstructs its real argument: farmers fatten livestock on starches, so by Humean 'species of Analogy' starches fatten humans, from which abstinence from floury foods is 'deduced.' Hume's Enquiry Section IX, 'Of the Reason of Animals,' supplies the philosophical audit, distinguishing relations of ideas from matters of fact and grading the animal-to-human analogical inference as probable at best. The chapter ends with Hume's fork turned on the diet-book shelf: any volume without experiential reasoning concerning matter of fact deserves the flames — 'nothing but sophistry and illusion.'
- ch. 17 "The Atkins Diet and Philosophy" **·** Jean-Jacques Rousseau, Confucius, Democritus — Li (ritual propriety); Natural Goodness and Corruption by Society; Atomism; The General Will — In an imaginary salon hosted by the baker 'la Brioche,' the goddess Eris drops a golden apple inscribed 'Atkins,' and three philosophers fight over the credit. Rousseau claims Atkins's war on refined flour and 'invented fake food' as his own back-to-nature, state-of-nature legacy; Confucius counters that Americans are 'grazers' without li, that culture is nature's flower, and that only ritual propriety can counteract the advertising that fills the void; Democritus claims the scientific core — 'hyperinsulism,' 'benign dietary ketosis' — as atomism's heir, deriding Rousseau's general will as democratic truth-concocting and Plato's otherworldly philosopher-king. The debate ranges over free will versus scientific prediction-and-control and ends unresolved over a refusal of brioche.
- ch. 18 "The Atkins Diet and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 19 "The Atkins Diet and Philosophy" **·** Karl Popper, Thomas Kuhn, David Hume — Falsificationism; Paradigm Shifts; Problem of Induction — Steele starts from Orwell's worry that laypeople accept scientific consensus as unreflectively as tribesmen accept witch-doctors — sharpened by Lysenko and Nazi 'racial science' — and answers it with Popper's critical rationalism: since Hume's problem of induction bars proof from instances, we can only provisionally prefer theories that survive severe attempts at falsification, so trusting experts is reasonable exactly when their community tests rather than defends its theories. Kuhn's paradigms explain why dissident alternatives like Atkins talk past the dominant school (Atkins and Agatston are cardiologists, not dieticians), and the 2002–2004 studies showing improved lipids on more fat figure as the kind of surprise that precedes paradigm change. His verdict: neither trusting nor resisting experts is foolish; 'human knowledge is too important to be left to experts.'
- ch. 20 "The Atkins Diet and Philosophy" **·** Friedrich Nietzsche — Perspectivism; The Death of God — Bamford argues that Atkins's insistence that dieters evaluate the plan for their individual needs parallels Nietzsche's cultural therapy: as the death of God left modern culture nihilistic, so a processed-food culture suffers 'nutritional nihilism,' and the 'mega-orthodox' doctors who worship medical consensus with 'religious fervor' treat science as a replacement religion. Reading Ecce Homo's 'question of nutriment' alongside Twilight of the Idols, she shows that Nietzsche's perspectivism — no uninterpreted facts, only interpretations from physiological perspectives — makes each dieter fully responsible for interpretation, which is why Atkins must ask us to think for ourselves. The limit: where Atkins keeps an 'absolute faith in empirical science' (and Atkins Nutritionals mass-markets processed low-carb products), a properly Nietzschean therapy would question that faith too.
- ch. 21 "The Atkins Diet and Philosophy" **·** Thomas Kuhn — Paradigm Shifts — Womack tests whether Atkins counts as a Kuhnian paradigm shift, using the Ptolemaic-to-Copernican transition as the model: paradigms define which problems matter, normal science accumulates anomalies, and crisis ends in a new paradigm. Measured against the dieting paradigm's own goals, Atkins flunks — its short-term wins fade by twelve months in the JAMA comparison with the Ornish, Zone, and Weight Watchers diets, with 30–50 percent dropout — so the dieting paradigm itself, which treats obesity as an individual plate problem, is the thing in crisis. Her proposed replacement shifts from the individual to the 'toxic food environment': sidewalks, zoning, food advertising rules, and the French Paradox as the existence proof.
- ch. 22 "The Atkins Diet and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 23 "The Atkins Diet and Philosophy" **·** Karl Marx — Commodity Fetishism; Alienation — Bar On examines the Atkins website's country-by-country packaging and asks what kind of commodity the diet is, answering with Marx's Capital: use-value versus exchange-value, money's erasure of qualitative difference (an Atkins cookbook priced like a tub of supplements), and capitalists who produce use-values only as 'material depositories of exchange-value.' Advertising succeeds not by manipulation but because alienated people — labor sold, selves split to fit the job market — already live in exchange relations, and Atkins mediates even the dieter's relation to herself through purchased bars, supplements, and books. Her conclusion: a weight-loss diet only the relatively privileged can afford, in a world where the poor eat carbohydrates because they are cheap, is itself a symptom of the alienation Marx diagnosed.
- ch. 24 "The Atkins Diet and Philosophy" **·** Charles W. Mills, Thomas Hobbes — The Social Contract; The Racial Contract; Feminism — Wilkerson borrows the social contract tradition — Hobbes's mutual-restraint founding, radicalized by Pateman's sexual contract and Mills's racial contract — to recast antifat bias as a systematic breach of the social contract enforced by medicine (hallway weigh-ins, weight lectures instead of care), employment, transit seating, and TV fat jokes. Against Atkins's medical discourse of the 'obesity epidemic' she assembles Health at Every Size evidence, trans-fat and high-fructose corn syrup data, and the documented health damage of stigma and dieting itself, concluding that Atkins profits from recasting social problems as individual failings. Atkins for Life's success stories (Kerry Feather's volleyball and new husband, Luann Lockhart's proud daughter) are read as testimony that dieting works as ritual self-abasement purchasing group membership — hence Fat Liberation's 'another world is possible.'
- ch. 25 "The Atkins Diet and Philosophy" **·** Peter Singer, James Rachels, Jeremy Bentham — Animal Liberation; Speciesism; Food Ethics — Detmer argues moral vegetarianism follows from principles nearly everyone accepts — cruelty, the infliction of unnecessary or disproportionate suffering, is wrong — and that Atkins, which escalates meat eating without being necessary for weight loss, fails both his necessity and proportion tests. Rebutting human superiority, he deploys Rachels's point that no single feature like language is relevant to every difference in treatment and Bentham's 'Can they suffer?', holds factory-farm castration without anesthetic up against the civet-cat musk trade his students already condemn, and answers Narveson's appeal to intuition and the appeal to tradition. Even granting Atkins maximal health benefits, the margin over a well-planned vegetarian diet (Denmark's WWI blockade, the Framingham data) cannot justify the animal suffering, world-hunger, and environmental costs — most of that case drawn from Singer's Animal Liberation.
- ch. 26 "The Atkins Diet and Philosophy" **·** Immanuel Kant, Karl Marx, René Descartes — The Categorical Imperative; Food Ethics — Cox and Bender subject Atkins to Kant's universal-law formula — could every one of the billion overweight people on earth adopt it? — in a 'factive' thought experiment: a 25 percent rise in animal-protein output, nearly 250 million additional feed-grain acres, overgrazed ranges, and possibly a tenth of the world's remaining forests cleared for pasture. Calling Atkins reductionism's child (a metabolic switch judged on bathroom scales), they use Marx's 'metabolic rift' — soil nutrients shipped to cities, feedlots severing crops from livestock — and the Cartesian body-as-machine (via Federici) to place low-carb's atomized foods (bungalow subs, soy 'mashed potatoes,' pork rinds) as capitalism's latest rupture in the food system. Their verdict: only plant-leaning regimens could survive universalization, and the deeper fix is Wendell Berry's — eating understood as an agricultural act.
- ch. 27 "The Atkins Diet and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 28 "The Atkins Diet and Philosophy" **·** Feminism — Bentley argues Atkins 'masculinized' dieting: since meat is coded male (the hunter's large game, yang, 'strong as an ox') and dieting female, a diet whose emblematic meal is steak, bacon, and creamed spinach let men come out of the dieting closet — Terry Free's competitive wager, business-school professors trading weight charts, and half-male Atkins success stories against Jenny Craig's sixteen men. The symbolism runs both ways: women now order steak openly, while 'white trash' foods like pork rinds ($840 million in 2003 sales) gained middle-class respectability, a class crossing she explains through Bourdieu's Distinction — taste, habitus, and cultural capital.
- ch. 29 "The Atkins Diet and Philosophy" *(no index entry yet)* — Parasecoli reads the Atkins program through Lacanian psychoanalysis: the mirror stage explains why dieters invest in the 'you in the picture' Atkins asks them to visualize — an ideal-ego built from others' gazes and shifting cultural body standards (Botticelli's Venus to Jennifer Lopez); hunger, which Atkins treats as an uncontrollable instinct, occupies the place of the Real that resists symbolization; and 'carbs are bad' functions as the upholstery nail that stabilizes the whole symbolic food system. Science plays the external authority whose impenetrable rationale licenses the dieter's leap of faith, while the body that will not slim on schedule — 'your body is not a machine' — is the Real breaking back in.
- ch. 30 "The Atkins Diet and Philosophy" **·** Michel Foucault, René Descartes, Iris Marion Young, Marilyn Frye — Mind–Body Dualism; The Panopticon (surveillance); Objectification; Embodiment (the lived body) — A café dialogue pits second-waver Marie against third-waver Dorothy over whether Atkins can be a feminist tool: Marie wields the feminist critique of Cartesian dualism (Descartes made the mind essential and the body animal, and women were stigmatized as body), Foucault's internalized surveillance — 'every mirror becomes a guard, every scale an alarm,' with Lifetime Maintenance as 'maniacal' hyper-vigilance — Lorde's 'master's tools,' and Young's 'Throwing Like a Girl.' Dorothy answers with Our Bodies, Ourselves self-knowledge, Title IX athletes like Venus and Serena Williams, Frye's Willful Virgin, and existentialist facticity and bad faith (we are in bad faith when we abdicate control), defending dieting as a chosen women's project. The exchange ends drawish, over Brazilian waxes, French women, and whether ugliness is a feminist virtue.
- ch. 31 "The Atkins Diet and Philosophy" *(notes)*

## The Beatles and Philosophy - Nothing You Can Think that Can't Be Thunk

- ch. 1 "Cover" *(not yet read)*
- ch. 2 "Unknown" *(not yet read)*
- ch. 3 "Unknown" *(not yet read)*
- ch. 4 "Unknown" *(not yet read)*
- ch. 5 "Unknown" *(not yet read)*
- ch. 6 "PROLOGUE “WHAT’S THAT SUPPOSED TO MEAN ”" **·** Friedrich Nietzsche — Will to Power — The prologue closely re-watches Bob Dylan's 1965 Newport 'going electric' set and argues that the famous booing began for mundane musical reasons — the bassist missing the change to the five chord in 'Maggie's Farm' and an under-rehearsed band — not ideological betrayal by folk purists. It contends that the audience collectively transmuted a flawed performance into an epochal myth of folk-vs-rock rupture, and that this shared fiction, not how the music actually sounded, is what mattered culturally. Framed by the Nietzsche epigraph on self-overcoming, it reads Dylan as a catalytic, quasi-messianic figure who performed the 'necessary murder of the old order' (culminating in the 1966 'Judas!' shout), enjoyed even the booing as part of the cultural drama, and refused every label by calling his new style 'vision music.'
- ch. 7 "I. “STANDING IN THE GATEWAY”" *(front)*
- ch. 8 "CHAPTER 1 “LET’S GET REAL, REAL GONE FOR A CHANGE”" **·** G.W.F. Hegel, William James — The Dialectic; Authenticity; Geist (World Spirit); Zen Buddhism — The chapter argues that Elvis's Sun recordings — the staged rupture in 'Milkcow Blues Boogie' ('That don't move me. Let's get real, real gone for a change') and the accidental 'acting the fool' emergence of 'That's All Right' — enacted an epistemological revolution: a leap out of modernity's rationalized, disembodied mode into repressed bodily affect, defining American cool as affective authenticity against Bill Haley's affected performativity. Elvis's immersion in a Black Memphis neighborhood and his emulation of Black performers made him a catalyst for racial-musical integration and the 'visceral empathy' that prepared desegregation, while the panic of critics (Sinatra, the Times, Congressman Celler) is reread via Hegel's 'tarrying with the negative' as the resistance Geist needs for its self-overcoming. Creative novelty is described with Whitehead's and William James's metaphors — the 'lure of feeling,' sudden phase transition, radical empiricism in what Elvis kept or dropped onstage — with his 'did it make me feel good' studio criterion and a Zen-like mushin statesmanship of loosing egoic control in the band.
- ch. 9 "CHAPTER 2 “A CREATIVE TENSION”" **·** William James, René Descartes — Authenticity; The Dialectic; The Cogito — The chapter traces the Beatles' rise as a case study in how novelty enters culture: Lennon and McCartney polarize into transgressive truth and melodic beauty, their 'creative tension' producing an emergent whole greater than either (the 'holding a mirror' songwriting method, their fused harmonies, the Decca audition versus Hamburg swings between commerce and authenticity). Hamburg is the underworld initiation where 'Mach Schau!' humiliation, absurdist humor read as Freudian catharsis, and Preludin-fueled marathon sets broke them out of English propriety into ecstatic physicality, before Epstein's suits and George Martin's harsh Abbey Road critique dragged the pendulum back toward rational professionalism. Against the Cartesian thesis that humanity is defined by the thinking mind ('I think, therefore I am'), the chapter reads 'Please Please Me,' Beatlemania's screaming crowds (citing Ehrenreich on female assertiveness and solidarity), and Rubber Soul as successive dialectical phase transitions integrating affect with intellect — a rock-and-roll answer to rationalized modernity, in James's wave-crest image of novelty flung ahead of history.
- ch. 10 "CHAPTER 3 “LOOKING FOR THE INNER HEAT”" **·** Thomas Kuhn, G.W.F. Hegel, Jean-Paul Sartre, William James — Paradigm Shifts; Authenticity; Existentialism — The chapter reads Dylan's self-becoming as a chain of identity-emulations and overcomings — Hank Williams, Woody Guthrie, Ramblin' Jack Elliott — with Jon Pankake's folk-police critique and the Mike Seeger epiphany acting as Kuhnian anomalies that force a paradigm shift from folksinger to songwriter, each gatekeeper playing the 'Hegelian negative' Dylan needed. It argues Dylan fused folk, beat poetry, and rock and roll into an 'archaic revival' of mythic, enchanted consciousness against modern rationalization, rejecting the Sartrean/beat existentialism of a meaningless world in favor of co-creating meaning, and treating authenticity as the refusal of fixed identity (the Zimmerman-to-Dylan renaming, 'I'm gonna get me a new Bob Dylan'). The Newsweek exposé, Irwin Silber's open letter, and Johnny Cash's guitar-gift blessing stage his passage from 'King of Folk' to electric rock, with the little-known 1962 cut of Presley's 'That's All Right' showing that going electric was a return home — folk having been, in Dylan's words, 'just an interruption.'
- ch. 11 "II. “YOU REALLY DON’T KNOW UNTIL YOU STEP INSIDE”" *(not yet read)*
- ch. 12 "CHAPTER 4 “A REVELATION TO BEHOLD”" **·** William James, Aristotle, G.W.F. Hegel — The Will to Believe; The Four Causes; Truth as What Works; The Leap of Faith — Argues that the Beatles' devotion to rock and roll was a literal religious phenomenon rather than loose metaphor: Lennon's first hearing of Elvis is analyzed as a William James-style conversion, the Radio Luxembourg broadcasts were treated 'like scripture,' Hamburg was a 'baptism by fire,' and Epstein's new suits at the Cavern were announced as 'a revelation.' Drawing on James's Varieties of Religious Experience and Pragmatism, the chapter reads Beatlemania's screaming crowds as collective ecstatic ritual and the 'toppermost of the poppermost' chant as the will-to-believe making its own truth, in the way James says truth 'happens' to an idea. Aristotle's four causes supply the metaphysical frame — the 'magic' of Liverpool and the sacred quality fans reported are formal-final causation against the material-efficient causation of disenchanted modernity — while the band's double irony ('tarrying with the negative,' after Hegel) let them hold skepticism inside belief, as in Lennon's 'flaming pie' origin parody and the 'more popular than Jesus' episode.
- ch. 13 "CHAPTER 5 “AN EXTREME SENSE OF DESTINY”" **·** Aristotle, William James, Heraclitus, Thomas Kuhn — The Four Causes; The Will to Believe; Fate and Foreknowledge — Reads Bob Dylan's 'extreme sense of destiny' as lived Aristotelian final causation: Dylan's passivity about fame ('It happened like anything else happens'), his 'Jack Fate' persona, and the odyssey of 'goin' home' all treat his future self as the telos drawing him forward rather than his past pushing him. William James's Will to Believe ('faith creates its own verification') explains the self-fulfilling portents Dylan found in Gorgeous George's wink, his felt succession to Woody Guthrie, and his premonition of Joan Baez, while the chapter insists this destiny is not fatalism since Dylan could have ignored the signs and 'gone off another way.' It closes by taking Dylan at his word that 'Bobby Zimmerman' died when his namesake was killed in a 1961 motorcycle crash — a Jungian synchronicity that seals the shamanic death-and-rebirth 'transfiguration' Dylan claims for himself, where Heraclitus's river captures that the old self cannot be recovered.
- ch. 14 "EPILOGUE “THROUGH THE MIRROR”" **·** William James — The epilogue reads the three pairwise meetings — Elvis and the Beatles at his Bel Air home in 1965 ('Long live the King!'), Dylan turning the Beatles on to cannabis on August 28, 1964, and Dylan's refusal to ever meet Elvis, memorialized in 'Went to See the Gypsy' — as mirrors through which each figure's transformation passed to the next. Its central argument is that the 'marijuana summit' was an epistemological watershed: following Terence McKenna's claim that a culture's sanctioned drugs select its ways of knowing, cannabis (against caffeinated, speed-fueled rationalism) opened the affective, intuitive mode that became the counterculture, audible in Lennon's turn from 'Help!' to 'Come Together.' The song's gypsy and 'pretty dancing girl' are finally read as the Jungian anima, the archetypal feminine drawing the West back to embodied, enchanted experience — a book-length thesis the epilogue attributes to the 'radical empiricist' lineage of James, Jung, Bergson, and Whitehead.
- ch. 15 "BIBLIOGRAPHY" *(notes)*
- ch. 16 "ACKNOWLEDGEMENTS" *(notes)*
- ch. 17 "NOTES" *(notes)*

## The Chronicles of Narnia and Philosophy - The Lion, the Witch, and the Worldview

- ch. 1 "The Chronicles of Narnia and Philosophy" *(not yet read)*
- ch. 2 "The Chronicles of Narnia and Philosophy" *(not yet read)*
- ch. 3 "The Chronicles of Narnia and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 4 "The Chronicles of Narnia and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 5 "The Chronicles of Narnia and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 6 "The Chronicles of Narnia and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 7 "The Chronicles of Narnia and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 8 "The Chronicles of Narnia and Philosophy" *(notes)*
- ch. 9 "The Chronicles of Narnia and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 10 "The Chronicles of Narnia and Philosophy" *(no index entry yet)* — Jerry Walls's introduction opens with the film Shadowlands (the Oxford dons teasing Lewis about the fur coats in the wardrobe) to present Lewis as a part-time philosopher who taught philosophy at Oxford and presided over the Socratic Club. It previews the questions the Chronicles raise: truth versus illusion, whether might makes right, why be moral, what may be accepted on faith, whether more than one religion could be true, whether time flows differently in different worlds, and whether Eustace could become a dragon and still be the same person. It reassures Narnia fans that the essays enrich rather than ponderously distort ('gas') stories that should first be enjoyed on their own terms.
- ch. 11 "The Chronicles of Narnia and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 12 "The Chronicles of Narnia and Philosophy" *(no index entry yet)* — Stephen Webb argues that Lewis gives hearing, not sight, the fundamental role in knowledge: the Dufflepuds choose invisibility over ugliness, the crew in the Dark Island hear nightmares they cannot see, and in The Magician's Nephew Aslan is heard singing before he is seen. Against A.J. Ayer's logical positivism, Webb develops a 'vocative philosophy of sound' in which meaning lives in voices: Jadis's destructive Deplorable Word versus Aslan's world-creating song, Uncle Andrew's tone-deafness to warranted authority, and Shasta in The Horse and His Boy learning who he is from the voice in the dark that answers 'Myself.'
- ch. 13 "The Chronicles of Narnia and Philosophy" **·** Socrates — Socratic Ignorance (knowing that you don't know); The Gettier Problem — Kevin Kinghorn uses virtue epistemology (Sosa, Code) to solve the puzzle of why Uncle Andrew alone hears only roaring when Aslan sings Narnia into being: he fails all four intellectual virtues. He does not value truth for its own sake (unlike Frank the Cabby), indulges wishful thinking (like Edmund defending the Witch for Turkish Delight, or imagining Jadis fancies him), lets fear dictate belief (terror of the animals he has vivisected), and lacks Socratic self-knowledge, which the dragonized Eustace painfully acquires. A closing reading of Prince Caspian has each character's ability to see Aslan track their earlier trust in Lucy's testimony: sin blinds, and faith produces its own authenticating evidence.
- ch. 14 "The Chronicles of Narnia and Philosophy" **·** Philosophical Skepticism — Thomas Senor turns the Professor's trilemma about Lucy (lying, mad, or telling the truth) into a general account of when to believe incredible testimony: weigh the testifier's reliability against how well the claim fits your background beliefs. He shows why Lucy's siblings should have believed her second report of Aslan in Prince Caspian, since by then talking animals and a resurrected Lion no longer clashed with what they knew, and then applies the framework to 'Julia's' report of hearing God, arguing that believing, disbelieving, or withholding rationally depends on the hearer's own background commitments. A footnote explains why Hume on miracles does not fit Lucy's case, since she claims no violation of natural law.
- ch. 15 "The Chronicles of Narnia and Philosophy" **·** William James, Blaise Pascal, Augustine of Hippo — Pascal's Wager; The Will to Believe; Faith and Reason — Steven Lovell reads the Green Witch's enchantment in The Silver Chair (the sun is only a copy of the lamp, Aslan only a bigger cat, Narnia a children's tale) as Lewis's staging of the Freudian critique of religion, and answers that similarity cannot tell copy from original, that the genetic fallacy vitiates arguing from a belief's origin, and that Aslan is 'not safe,' so Christianity is hardly wish-fulfillment (Augustine's restless heart and Pascal's God-shaped hole point the other way). Puddleglum's stamping out the fire and vow to live 'as like a Narnian as I can even if there isn't any Narnia' is defended through Pascal's Wager and James's 'The Will to Believe': the reality of Narnia is a live, forced, and momentous option that may legitimately be decided on passional grounds.
- ch. 16 "The Chronicles of Narnia and Philosophy" **·** Thomas Kuhn, René Descartes, John Locke, Immanuel Kant, Alvin Plantinga, John Dewey — Paradigm Shifts — Bruce Reichenbach recounts the failure of the Enlightenment quest for certainty from Descartes and Locke through Kant to the positivists, and argues that Lewis's Narnia instead models knowing as perspectival (the Stable door whose inside is bigger than its outside), value-laden (the Dwarfs' self-chosen dark paradigm that turns Aslan's feast into refuse and violets into stable litter), and personal (Lucy's unarguable knowledge that she has seen Aslan in Prince Caspian, read through Michael Polanyi's tacit knowledge). Kuhn's competing paradigms frame theism versus atheism, James's will to believe opens the chapter, and Plantinga's defeaters keep personal knowledge from collapsing into mere subjectivity.
- ch. 17 "The Chronicles of Narnia and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 18 "The Chronicles of Narnia and Philosophy" **·** Thomas Hobbes, Alasdair MacIntyre, David Hume, Aristotle, William Paley — Ethical Egoism; Philosophy of Love; Eudaimonia (flourishing) — Laura Garcia assembles Narnian heroes: Jill, Eustace, and Puddleglum untying Rilian at likely lethal cost, Eustace hacking at the Sea Serpent, unarmed Shasta charging the lion to save Aravis, and Digory refusing the Witch's offer of the apple that could heal his dying mother. She asks whether egoist theories can explain such deeds and argues they cannot: Hobbes's disguised self-interest, Butler's and Paley's reward schemes, Hume's sympathy, and MacIntyre's identity-based interests all fall short. Only love of others for their own sake, and (for Jill and Digory) trust in Aslan's proven goodness, explains both the heroic choices and our admiration of them as supererogatory acts beyond the call of duty.
- ch. 19 "The Chronicles of Narnia and Philosophy" **·** John Stuart Mill, Epicurus, Aristotle, Karl Marx, Thomas Aquinas, Jeremy Bentham — Higher and Lower Pleasures; Eudaimonia (flourishing); The Golden Mean; Epicurean Pleasure (ataraxia) — Devin Brown uses Eustace, who dodges work on Dragon Island until he wakes as a dragon and discovers he would rather be hungry among human friends than comfortable alone, to contrast Epicurean hedonism and Tom Sawyer's work-avoidance with Mill's higher pleasures and the 'Sherlock Holmes philosophy of work.' Weighing Aristotle's contemplative life against Lewis's love of quests, he reads Father Christmas's tailored gifts and 'everyone must do what he can do best' as an Acorn Philosophy of Vocation with a Marxian 'from each according to his ability' echo, condemns wrong livelihoods like Governor Gumpas's slave trade, and closes on leisure and Aristotle's golden mean, which Lewis turns into 'golden extremes.'
- ch. 20 "The Chronicles of Narnia and Philosophy" **·** Thomas Aquinas, Friedrich Nietzsche, Plato — Natural Law; Moral Relativism; Will to Power — Tim Mosteller identifies Lewis's Tao from The Abolition of Man with Aquinas's Natural Law, an objective moral order knowable by all rational beings, and maps three responses onto Narnian characters: wholehearted acceptance (Peter, Lucy, Tirian, Reepicheep), selective 'Innovators' who pick and choose (Shift and Miraz, with Nietzsche's invented master-values as the philosophical extreme), and total rejection by 'Conditioners' (Ginger the Cat, who reverts to dumb beast, and Uncle Andrew, who loses the animals' speech). Individual laws are illustrated by Mr. Tumnus freeing Lucy, Aslan scratching Aravis for her gladness at the slave girl's beating, Nikabrik's treacherous Realpolitik, Lucy's eavesdropping spell in the Magician's book, and Jill's showing-off at the cliff.
- ch. 21 "The Chronicles of Narnia and Philosophy" **·** Aristotle, Thomas Aquinas, Plato — Virtue Ethics (arete); Cardinal and Theological Virtues; Moral Psychology (character, vice, weakness of will) — Bill Davis argues that Narnia works as a classical moral-education environment: against Experiment House's science-worship, Narnia supplies the three ingredients Aristotle and Aquinas require, namely instruction, virtuous exemplars to imitate (Reepicheep's courage, Puddleglum's temperance), and habituation through repeated right action, which together transform Peter's indecision, Edmund's treachery, Lucy's envy, and Jill's timidity. What finally lasts beyond Narnia, he contends, is a transforming encounter with Aslan: Eustace's dragon skin torn off by the Lion, Jill's drink at the stream where 'there is no other stream,' and Digory's tearful confession. He closes by arguing today's self-esteem-based character education cannot replicate this, since it pursues virtue from love of self rather than love of the good.
- ch. 22 "The Chronicles of Narnia and Philosophy" **·** Plato, Friedrich Nietzsche, Socrates, Thomas Hobbes — Master and Slave Morality; Justice in the Republic; Moral Relativism — Janice Daurio sets Plato's Thrasymachus and Callicles and Nietzsche's immoralism against three Narnian immoralists: Uncle Andrew, who exempts 'profound students and great thinkers' from rules meant for little boys, servants, and women; Queen Jadis, whose Deplorable Word and 'What else were they there for but to do my will?' embody might-makes-right; and the Green Witch, who extends going beyond good and evil to going beyond truth itself. Following Lewis's moral realism (the Golden Rule and Natural Law as facts like gravity, with Hobbes's lesson that someone always turns up with more power), she shows the immoralists cannot live by their own creed, since Uncle Andrew resents being treated as Jadis's slave, and since Lewis ties real authority to service, as when humble Frank the Cabby is made king.
- ch. 23 "The Chronicles of Narnia and Philosophy" **·** Alasdair MacIntyre, Plato — Virtue Ethics; Moral Relativism — Gayne Anacker defines moral imagination, the capacity to see one's values and life from fresh moral perspectives, and argues the Chronicles exercise it through three character types readers live through: flawed repenters (Edmund, Eustace, Bree, Aravis), the essentially good under trial (Lucy believing her Aslan sighting in Prince Caspian, Caspian's greed on Goldwater Island), and the fatally self-absorbed (Uncle Andrew, Shift), together with portrayals of worship and of true versus usurped nobility (Peter versus Miraz). Philosophically he contends the stories' effectiveness supports virtue ethics over action-centered modern theories, and that disagreement among ethical theorists is no ground for relativism: no one needs theoretical consensus to know Miraz is evil.
- ch. 24 "The Chronicles of Narnia and Philosophy" **·** Plato, Martin Heidegger, René Descartes, Alasdair MacIntyre — Allegory of the Cave; Just War Theory; The Ethics of Political Violence — Wendy Hamblet asks whether Narnia feeds children a dangerous militaristic moral imaginary: Homeric warrior heroes, swarthy Calormene villains, voiceless animals excluded from moral concern, and battles that decide truth, in a lineage running from Plato's censorship of nursery stories through Descartes's modernity to Heidegger's seduction by Hitler's myths. On closer reading she finds Lewis undermining militarism: morally mixed characters, condemnation of aggression, despotism, and 'reasons of state,' and room only for just war and righteous self-defense. The finale is read through Plato's cave, as the Last Battle's worlds-within-worlds expose our political 'truths' as shadow-images that unscrupulous leaders manipulate when the god is absent.
- ch. 25 "The Chronicles of Narnia and Philosophy" **·** Simone de Beauvoir — Woman as the Other; One Is Not Born a Woman; Feminism — Argues that Narnia is 'unfriendly to the feminine' by reading Susan's exclusion from Aslan's country at the end of The Last Battle ('no longer a friend of Narnia', too interested in nylons and invitations) as the fate of the one heroine who stays stereotypically feminine. It shows that the admired girls (Lucy, Aravis, Jill) succeed only by approximating a masculine standard of adventure, applying Beauvoir's point that measuring women against a male norm implies something wrong with femaleness itself. It further traces how evil in Narnia wears a feminine face: the Green Witch seduces Prince Rilian into Underland, the White Witch tempts Edmund and Digory where the girls resist, and the Witch's Lilith/Jinn lineage ties women to the Fall via 'Daughters of Eve'.
- ch. 26 "The Chronicles of Narnia and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 27 "The Chronicles of Narnia and Philosophy" **·** Plato, Socrates, Aristotle — Allegory of the Cave; Theory of Forms; Socratic Method (elenchus) — Takes Digory's dying remark 'It's all in Plato' as the key to the Chronicles. It compares the Professor's guided questioning of Peter and Susan about Lucy's wardrobe testimony to the Socratic method, though noting he supplies evidential maxims rather than eliciting a virtue-definition as Socrates does with Cephalus on justice. It maps Underland in The Silver Chair onto the Allegory of the Cave (Rilian bound in his silver chair like the chained prisoners, the Green Witch's enchantment that denies any world beyond), observing that the Witch 'plays Aristotle' by ridiculing Aslan as an invented perfect Form, while Puddleglum's 'stand by the play-world' speech echoes Socrates's reply to Glaucon that the ideal pattern is none the worse for being unrealizable. Finally it reads the 'real Narnia' of The Last Battle ('world within world, Narnia within Narnia') as Christian Platonism, with the nested heavens even raising Plato's Third Man problem.
- ch. 28 "The Chronicles of Narnia and Philosophy" **·** John Locke, René Descartes, Aristotle — Personal Identity; Personal Identity (memory theory); Ship of Theseus — Uses Narnia's magical transformations to probe personal identity, starting with the qualitative/numerical identity distinction illustrated by the look-alike twins Shasta and Corin in The Horse and His Boy. Aristotle's essential/accidental properties frame the question of what Eustace can lose and remain Eustace, including turning into a dragon, and Rabadash into a donkey, showing persons are not identical to their bodies. Descartes's soul argument and Locke's memory theory are tested and rejected, the latter because it cannot handle Rilian's enchanted amnesia, Shasta's forgotten infancy, or Susan's disowned memories without circularity. The chapter defends instead a first-person-perspective theory on which a person is constituted by, not identical to, a body, illustrated by the repeatedly repaired Dawn Treader and by the heroes surviving death in The Last Battle as restored points of view.
- ch. 29 "The Chronicles of Narnia and Philosophy" **·** Friedrich Nietzsche, Plato — Master and Slave Morality; Tripartite Soul — Reads Eustace, Uncle Andrew, Experiment House, and Miraz's censored Narnia as Lewis's portraits of modern secularism, which treats the world as mere resource and dismisses the transcendent regardless of evidence. It argues that denying intrinsic value collapses into a Nietzschean master morality of power, visible in Uncle Andrew's use of the children as guinea pigs and the Lone Islands' slave trade, and exposes the secular 'conditioner's' self-exemption as a violation of the Principle of Relevant Difference. Against the Green Witch's debunking of Puddleglum's sun and Aslan as copies of a lamp and a cat, it deploys the Platonic-Cartesian copy/original model of ideas to argue transcendent ideas (eternity, perfection, necessary being) cannot be derived from lower material causes, suggesting instead that the material world is the 'shadowland' copy of a divine original.
- ch. 30 "The Chronicles of Narnia and Philosophy" **·** Boethius — Fate and Foreknowledge; The Metaphysics of Time Travel — Starts from Narnia's time discrepancies (hours in the wardrobe, reigns of years that cost seconds in England, 1303 Narnian years in one) and shows Einstein's relativity makes different time frames plausible before admitting science cannot explain Aslan's 'I call all times soon'. It interprets that claim through Boethius's doctrine of God's timelessness, the eternal present embracing all moments as a simultaneous whole, and defends its compatibility with creaturely free choice: not even Aslan can override the Dwarfs' self-imprisoned perception or force Uncle Andrew to hear him. The chapter closes on the end of Narnian time in The Last Battle, Father Time making an end, and Aslan's timeless country as the fuller reality the temporal world shadowed.
- ch. 31 "The Chronicles of Narnia and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 32 "The Chronicles of Narnia and Philosophy" **·** David Hume, Augustine of Hippo — The Problem of Evil; Punishment: Retribution vs Rehabilitation — Frames the problem of pain with Hume's Dialogues (Demea and Philo) and reconstructs Lewis's answer as played out on Aslan: pain is God's megaphone that rouses Edmund on the Witch's frozen sledge and strips Eustace's dragon skin in the transforming embrace. Suffering also reveals hidden strengths, as when Aslan's pursuit goads Shasta into unexpected courage, modeled on Augustine's reading of the binding of Isaac as teaching Abraham, not God, what Abraham could endure. Aravis's claw wounds are treated as retributive punishment, tear for tear, exactly proportioned to the drugged slave's whipping, which the chapter defends via Lewis's retributivist theory against purely deterrent punishment. Because divine goodness aims at making creatures good rather than merely happy, Aslan can be both good and terrible, 'not a tame lion', since free will cannot be coerced into perfection.
- ch. 33 "The Chronicles of Narnia and Philosophy" *(no index entry yet)* — Argues that Lewis is an inclusivist about salvation, not a universalist or pluralist: Shift and the self-enclosed Dwarfs are lost, and Aslan explicitly denies being one with Tash, yet Emeth's service to Tash is counted by Aslan as service to him ('all find what they truly seek'). Shasta's providential guidance by a lion he has never heard of, and Jill being saved by drinking from the one stream before knowing whose stream it is, illustrate that the water saves, not knowledge about the water. The chapter supports inclusivism over exclusivism with Lewis's Mere Christianity passages, the 'ought implies can' justice argument against condemning those who never heard the gospel, and Rabadash's healing in Tash's own temple as Aslan working through pagan means.
- ch. 34 "The Chronicles of Narnia and Philosophy" **·** Forgiveness — Reconstructs Narnian atonement as the early Christian Ransom Theory: Digory's confession, remorse, and 'undoing' of his wrong in The Magician's Nephew, and Aslan giving his life as ransom for Edmund against the Witch's Deep Magic claim in The Lion, the Witch, and the Wardrobe, ending with the Deeper Magic that cracks the Stone Table. It answers John Lucas's four objections (literal Satan, Satan's rights, God negotiating with evil, divine deception) by treating evil as self-chosen bondage whose doors are 'locked on the inside', arguing Aslan's sacrificial identification with wrongdoers displays a love that mere omnipotent force could not, and that wrongdoers like the Witch are not owed full disclosure. It also defends mercy's tension with strict justice, via the example of a deserving prisoner mercifully freed, and finds humor rather than crudeness in Peter Lombard's cross-as-mousetrap image.
- ch. 35 "The Chronicles of Narnia and Philosophy" **·** Elizabeth Anscombe — Faith and Reason — Debunks the 'Anscombe legend' that Lewis abandoned rational apologetics for children's stories after his 1948 Socratic Club defeat by Elizabeth Anscombe over the argument that naturalism is self-refuting. It shows Lewis revised and expanded the argument in the 1960 Miracles, incorporating Anscombe's reasons/causes distinction, and kept writing apologetic essays, while the Professor's trilemma about Lucy (liar, lunatic, or truthful) mirrors the Trilemma of Mere Christianity and shows Narnia using logic, not fleeing it. It rejects A.N. Wilson's identification of the Green Witch with Anscombe and argues the Chronicles teach that prudence and faith are compatible virtues: Edmund's rationalizing, Trumpkin's honest skepticism, and Puzzle's blind trust in Shift all show that fideistic faith is a vice, not what Lewis asks of children.
- ch. 36 "The Chronicles of Narnia and Philosophy" **·** René Descartes, Thomas Aquinas — Immortality and the Soul; The Problem of Evil; Karma and Rebirth — Observes that only Talking Animals enter Aslan's country when Narnia ends in The Last Battle, then critically examines Lewis's case against wider animal salvation: derivative immortality through human love (Sarah Smith's train of beasts in The Great Divorce), compensation of animal pain in an afterlife, Lewis's follow-Descartes claim that most animals are sentient but not conscious enough to feel pain or have a surviving 'self'. The chapter argues each point fails: the physiological evidence shows animals suffer, resurrection or God-given souls remain possible (Strawberry's transformation into a winged Talking horse), and a compensatory hereafter is still the most promising answer to why a loving God permits the fawn's miserable death. Aquinas's rational-creature hierarchy frames the Talking/Dumb divide, and the Hindu-Buddhist rebirth theodicy is considered and rejected as unbiblical.
- ch. 37 "The Chronicles of Narnia and Philosophy" *(notes)*
- ch. 38 "The Chronicles of Narnia and Philosophy" *(notes)*
- ch. 39 "The Chronicles of Narnia and Philosophy" *(front)*
