## The Devil and Philosophy - The Nature of His Game

- ch. 1 "The Devil and Philosophy" *(not yet read)*
- ch. 2 "The Devil and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 3 "The Devil and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 4 "The Devil and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 5 "The Devil and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 6 "The Devil and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 7 "The Devil and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 8 "The Devil and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 9 "The Devil and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 10 "The Devil and Philosophy" **·** Sigmund Freud — The Problem of Evil; Civilization and Its Discontents; Immortality and the Soul — Robert Arp's introduction opens with Halloween culture and his own seminarian past to ask whether belief in the Devil can survive scrutiny. He lays out a 'just deserts' argument - Hitler, Stalin, and Mao died unpunished while Job, Socrates, and Thomas More died unrewarded, so God, the Devil, Heaven, and Hell must exist to balance the scales - and then answers it with Freud's projection critique (religion as wishful thinking for comfort and moral control) and with the neuroscience tying the mind to a dying brain, which undermines the soul that Hell would punish. The chapter frames the whole book's project: testing devil-belief with arguments and evidence rather than taking it on authority.
- ch. 11 "The Devil and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 12 "The Devil and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 13 "The Devil and Philosophy" **·** Rectification of Names — Nicolas Michaud catalogues the demonic hierarchy - de Spina's goblins, incubi, and familiars, Dante's Malebranche, Binsfeld's seven princes of Hell - through Dungeon Keeper's Dark Mistress, World of Warcraft succubi, Se7en, Hellraiser's Pinhead, Hellboy, and Spawn. His real argument is ontological: names are power, and what we call the Devil fixes how we treat him. Recovering the Hebrew sense of 'satan' as an appointed adversary and 'Lucifer' as light-bringer (compared to Prometheus), he argues the same being could be reframed from tempter to giver of knowledge, much as naming Columbus's invasion a 'discovery' launders atrocity.
- ch. 14 "The Devil and Philosophy" **·** William of Ockham — Fate and Foreknowledge; Ockham's Razor; Possible Worlds; Free Will vs Determinism — Sharon Kaye stages a fourteenth-century debate over a contradiction: Jesus truthfully prophesied that the Antichrist will come, yet the Antichrist remains free not to. Peter Aureoli denies bivalence for statements about the future, Thomas Bradwardine has God swap among infinite possible-world 'movies,' and Ockham's follower John of Mirecourt concludes that Jesus simply lied - each solution self-defeating or heretical. Using Ockham's Razor and her comic fantasy of seducing the Antichrist, Kaye argues the unsolvable prophecy problem helped discredit prophetic religion, ushered in theological determinism via Luther and Calvin, and opened a secular age.
- ch. 15 "The Devil and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 16 "The Devil and Philosophy" **·** Jean-Paul Sartre — The Look (hell is other people); Bad Faith — James McLachlan splits the Satans of history into 'the Rebel' of Romanticism (Blake, Shelley's turn to Prometheus, the sexy vampires of film) and 'the Asshole' who wants to be God. Using Sartre's 'hell is other people' and the desire to be a complete being, he reads Dracula, Scarface's Tony Montana, and Bond villains as figures who cannot endure the otherness of real persons. With Bohme and especially Schelling's two wills - self-assertion and love of others - he argues evil is a positive, perverted order of pure egoism, and that not even Satan can achieve the total asshole-ness he wants, because selfhood exists only in relation to others.
- ch. 17 "The Devil and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 18 "The Devil and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 19 "The Devil and Philosophy" **·** Jean-Paul Sartre — Bad Faith; Radical Freedom and Responsibility; Existence Precedes Essence — Elizabeth Butterfield reads The Devil's Advocate through Sartre: Kevin Lomax's 'You set me up!' excuse - after defending the guilty Gettys and Cullen and abandoning MaryAnn - is textbook bad faith, a lie to himself about his own free choices. Milton the Devil plays existentialist, insisting 'I'm no puppeteer... I only set the stage. You pull your own strings,' until Kevin admits his vanity, his drive to win, motivated every choice. Radical freedom entails radical responsibility, and Kevin's final suicide is his last free act, chosen to keep coming out on top, while free will remains the one limit the Devil cannot breach.
- ch. 20 "The Devil and Philosophy" **·** Moral Psychology (character, vice, weakness of will) — Jim Berti opens with a dream-fable in which the Devil tempts him with a bank-robbing 'quick fix' for his house-buying cash crunch, then reads the Rolling Stones' 'Sympathy for the Devil' as the Devil's resume of history's atrocities - Anastasia, the Blitzkrieg, the Kennedys - where he supplies the lighter fluid but never strikes the match. The chapter argues we give God too much credit and the Devil too much blame while omitting ourselves: the Devil is the 'drug dealer of sin,' peddling the seven deadly sins as shortcuts like Rocky IV's steroids, and only effortful virtue refuses them. As the song says, 'it was you and me.'
- ch. 21 "The Devil and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 22 "The Devil and Philosophy" **·** Aristotle, Plato, Epicurus — Virtue Ethics; The Golden Mean; Tripartite Soul — Jennifer Baker asks whether the Devil does ethics any good and answers no. Against the tempter hypothesis she sets the traditional virtue ethics of Plato, Aristotle, the Stoics, and the Epicureans, where bad behavior traces to habituated vice rather than demons, and the cure is consciously cultivating virtues by hitting the mean between excess and deficiency - Johnny's practiced honesty, the Evangelical cousin's marital vows. She faults the Devil as a one-size-fits-all explanation of evil, a glamour machine (via Postrel and the Romantic Satan of Blake, Byron, and Milton), and a scare tactic the Epicureans warned corrupts character, closing with Plato's divided-soul counsel to aim at long-term goods and a dismissal of Luther's spite-drinking stratagem.
- ch. 23 "The Devil and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 24 "The Devil and Philosophy" **·** Plato, René Descartes — Methodological Doubt (evil demon); Philosophical Skepticism; Reliabilism — John Karavitis (with tongue-in-cheek co-author 'Lucifer') treats The Ninth Gate as an epistemology case study, sorting the characters' beliefs about the Devil - true-believer Balkan, skeptic book-dealer Corso, the occult-tourist Order of the Silver Serpent - and testing them against Plato's Theaetetus definitions of knowledge as perception, true belief, and justified true belief. Balkan's justification is only an internally coherent web of book-learned beliefs, while Corso's external evidence (engravings signed 'LCF,' his journey's experiences) is weighed by the general reliability of his sources. Descartes' dreaming and the deceiving demon then leave the viewer skeptical that the Devil ever appeared at all.
- ch. 25 "The Devil and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 26 "The Devil and Philosophy" **·** David Hume, René Descartes, Thomas Kuhn — On Miracles; Paradigm Shifts; Mind–Body Dualism; Philosophical Skepticism — The Bocks use the courtroom frame of The Exorcism of Emily Rose to ask whether anyone could be justified in believing in demon possession, since the same seizures, polyglot voices, and levitations read differently under naturalism and supernaturalism. Kuhn's paradigms explain why neither worldview abandons ship at anomalies, and they weigh Wiebe's 'finite spirits as theoretical entities,' Hume's miracles argument (with Levine's Red Sea thought experiment as rebuttal), and the Cartesian ghost-in-the-machine premise that possession would require. Testing even a Men in Black alien hypothesis against the religiously specific phenomena, they conclude that a witnessed case like Emily's could rationally justify belief in possession.
- ch. 27 "The Devil and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 28 "The Devil and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 29 "The Devil and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 30 "The Devil and Philosophy" **·** Immanuel Kant — The Categorical Imperative; Autonomy of the Will — Danny Smith applies Kant's three grades of evil from Religion within the Limits of Reason Alone to The Dark Knight. Batman's saving Harvey over Rachel out of love is normal evil (pathological inclination trumping duty, his 'never kill' rule hardened into moral self-satisfaction), Two-Face's coin is radical evil (outsourcing his freedom and responsibility to chance), and the Joker - burning his own money, making his scarred face the true mask, wanting only chaos - is diabolical evil done for its own sake. Smith then presses Kant's embarrassing problem that evil pursued purely on principle, with no pathological motive, is indistinguishable from the highest good, and half-sympathetically re-reads the Joker as the honest rebel in a corrupt Gotham.
- ch. 31 "The Devil and Philosophy" **·** Immanuel Kant, Aristotle, Socrates — The Categorical Imperative; Virtue Ethics (arete); The Banality of Evil; Respect for Persons — George Dunn asks whether the Joker's 'some men just want to watch the world burn' motivelessness even makes sense. Against Baumeister's 'Myth of Pure Evil' and the Socratic-Aristotelian thesis that all wrongdoing aims at some apparent good, he tests Kant's claim that no human can be a diabolical being, Claudia Card's definition of diabolical evil as engineering others' moral corruption, and the Satan of Job as prosecuting accuser - which fits the Joker's ferry 'social experiment' and his breaking of Harvey Dent. The upshot is that the Joker's evil, fed by pride and the wish for credit for the conflagration, is instrumental, pathological, and banal after all, its best answer being the convict who throws the detonator out the window.
- ch. 32 "The Devil and Philosophy" **·** Immanuel Kant — The Categorical Imperative; Humanity as an End in Itself; Deontology — Nicole Pramik asks whether the demons of Brandon Mull's Fablehaven can be taught morality, using Kant's deontology as the measuring stick. Seth's kindness to Graulas looks dutiful and universalizable, and Graulas at first seems a rational agent respecting the categorical imperative - until healed, he betrays Seth, demolishes the preserve's treaty, and declares himself 'pure, deliberate evil' who used Seth as a mere means. Nagi Luna never even tries, and the Sphinx who models her is himself a serial user of persons, so the chapter concludes that rational demons remain impervious to the moral law: you can lead a demon out of Zzyzx, but you cannot make it good.
- ch. 33 "The Devil and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 34 "The Devil and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 35 "The Devil and Philosophy" **·** Augustine of Hippo — The Problem of Evil — A.G. Holdier pits Augustine against Star Wars: South Park's pay-per-view Satan-Jesus boxing match and the Force's balanced light and dark sides picture God and the Devil as equals, but Augustine - drawing on his Manichaean past and Zoroastrianism's Ahura Mazda versus Angra Mainyu - shows this is really two-gods worship, not monotheism. For Augustine the Devil is a derivative creature, created good and freely fallen, and evil is not a positive force but a privation, an absence of God like cold or darkness, so Vader's corruption and redemption both hang on free choice. Mordred's rebellion against Arthur gives the right picture: the Dark Side can never win because it is only diminished goodness.
- ch. 36 "The Devil and Philosophy" **·** Simulacra and Simulation (the hyperreal); Power/Knowledge — Jonathon O'Donnell reads DmC: Devil May Cry's Limbo City - demon-run banks, the Fox-News parody Raptor News, Virility soda - as the newest stage in the Devil's career. Tracing the shift from Dante's frozen, defeated Satan to Milton's active deceiver and the Black Mass's sacramental counterfeits, he argues the game's demons are both the hiders and the hidden: reality itself is the demonic lie, a counterfeit truth with nothing truer beneath it. When Dante and Vergil's WikiLeaks-style reveal frees humanity, what is unveiled is not God's order but just another contingent world, a secular tale of media institutions manufacturing 'truth' for a conforming public.
- ch. 37 "The Devil and Philosophy" **·** Boethius — The Consolation of Philosophy; The Problem of Evil; Fate and Foreknowledge; The Wheel of Fortune — Ray Bossert reads Tolkien's Silmarillion through Boethius's Consolation of Philosophy: elves, being artists, understand Melkor not as tempter but as bad artist, from his rebellious solo in the Music of the Ainur to his botched sub-creations - orcs bred from kidnapped elves - and his covetous collecting of the silmarils. Boethius supplies the machinery: evil seeks a good by unnatural means and only approximates it, wickedness debases its practitioner into a beast, God's foreknowledge embraces free choices without causing them, and Providence is a spinning disc at whose center alone one is steady - so Melkor's rebellion leaves him less free, dragged around the rim. Tolkien's contrast between art (for beauty) and magic (for control) explains why the Devil can only mimic creation, never make anything real.
- ch. 38 "The Devil and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 39 "The Devil and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 40 "The Devil and Philosophy" **·** Faith and Reason — Michael Versteeg and Adam Barkman explain C.S. Lewis's Screwtape doctrine that the Devil conquers minds not by argument but by unreason, since human reason fights on God's side. They unpack Screwtape's tactics as textbook fallacies: jargon like 'religious phase' working as a red herring, dismissing Christianity out of boredom as a non sequitur, the 'Historical Point of View' as the chronological snobbery that cuts generations off from the past, and the endless loop of pride about one's humility as an infinite regress that paralyzes thought. Because reason is, for Lewis, the road toward Heaven, the Devil's highway to Hell is built entirely of bad logic.
- ch. 41 "The Devil and Philosophy" **·** Augustine of Hippo — The Problem of Evil — Thomas Ward reconstructs C.S. Lewis's argument from The Problem of Pain that either demons exist or God doesn't. The free will defense, credited to Augustine, covers the evil humans freely do - freedom being required for love, as the rubber-ducky world shows - but John Perry's baby bat eaten by cockroaches and William Rowe's burned fawn are natural evils no human is responsible for. Given the not-crazy assumptions that God exists and is never morally responsible for evil, something non-human, personal, and wicked must bear that responsibility: hence demons, who like us misused their freedom, stretching the free will defense to cover every evil whatsoever.
- ch. 42 "The Devil and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 43 "The Devil and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 44 "The Devil and Philosophy" *(no index entry yet)* — Eric Swan tours the Devil-song canon - Elvis's 'Devil in Disguise' and 'Devil with a Blue Dress,' the Stones' 'Sympathy for the Devil' with its Altamont aftermath, the Grateful Dead's 'Friend of the Devil,' 'The Devil Went Down to Georgia,' Van Halen's 'Runnin' with the Devil,' and Beck's 'Devil's Haircut.' His argument is that these songs chart shifting conceptions of the Devil: from a misogynist shorthand for the betraying woman, to the transpersonal 'you and me' of human atrocity, to a beatable tempter, a party companion in self-destructive excess, and finally a mood of dystopian survival at century's end. The Devil of pop music, Swan suggests, becomes whatever humans need him to be.
- ch. 45 "The Devil and Philosophy" **·** Friedrich Nietzsche — Amor Fati (love of fate); Eternal Recurrence — Steven Neumann reads the sold-my-soul legends around Led Zeppelin ('here's to my sweet Satan' backwards on 'Stairway to Heaven'), Black Sabbath, and bluesman Robert Johnson at the Clarksdale crossroads as a parable of Nietzsche's amor fati: every joy comes bundled with suffering, so greatness means affirming the whole package. He deploys the eternal recurrence thought experiment - would you will your life, Plant's dead son and Bonham's fatal bender included, to repeat eternally? - and contrasts the ascetic denial of pleasure with Nietzsche's claim in Ecce Homo that his own sickness and obscurity made him love fate all the more.
- ch. 46 "The Devil and Philosophy" **·** Authenticity — Nick Jones asks whether worshipping the Devil and doing evil can ever be good, and answers: artistically yes. Using the transgression that defines extreme metal - blasphemy, church burnings, and Varg Vikernes's murder of Euronymous - he argues Satanic metal's immoral features can enhance its artistic value when they achieve three aims: rebellion, blasphemy, and authenticity. Genuine theistic Satanists who really believe in the Devil they invoke do all three best, though the chapter ends by conceding the cupboard of actual theistic Satanist metalheads is empty.
- ch. 47 "The Devil and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 48 "The Devil and Philosophy" **·** Carl Jung, Aristotle — The Shadow; The Collective Unconscious — Liz Stillwaggon Swan explains why crime dramas like Criminal Minds, CSI, and Bones are so watchable: Jung's shadow archetype and collective unconscious mean the Devil in human form on screen taps humanity's shared dark layer, while Aristotle's catharsis lets viewers experience pity and fear safely from the couch, identifying with characters like repulsed Garcia or intellectualizing Dr. Brennan. She adds the CSI effect - jurors with warped expectations of DNA and fingerprint work - and argues the shows satisfy a deep need to see devils like Bundy and Dahmer caught within a tidy forty-three minutes.
- ch. 49 "The Devil and Philosophy" **·** Karl Marx — Jeff Ewing reads Faustian bargains - Marlowe's and Goethe's Faust, Dorian Gray's wager with Lord Henry, Tommy Johnson's guitar in O Brother Where Art Thou?, Rosemary's Baby, and Twilight Zone's 'Escape Clause' - through Marx's Capital. The soul becomes a universal commodity with both use value and exchange value, and the Devil a perfect buyer's monopoly who can demand everything, a trade Ewing contrasts with capitalism's effective-demand rule that those who cannot pay go unaided even unto death.
- ch. 50 "The Devil and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 51 "The Devil and Philosophy" **·** Socrates — Socratic Method (elenchus); The Unexamined Life — Roger Hunt reads Bill Hicks's stand-up as a Socratic comedy exposing Satan's three-point plan: suppress knowledge, breed ignorance, and profit from hypocrisy - from the Waffle House 'readah' scene to Bush-era Gulf War spin ('we looked at the receipts'), JFK-assassination distraction via American Gladiators, and pop stars and marketers who 'suck Satan's cock' (Rick Astley, corporate rock, advertising). Where the Genesis serpent offered knowledge, Hicks's Devil is the force that suppresses it, and Hicks paid a Socratic price - an early death for 'corrupting' his audience - for pointing this out.
- ch. 52 "The Devil and Philosophy" **·** Simone de Beauvoir — Woman as the Other; Objectification — Jones, Hansmann, and Stachura trace the trope of woman as the Devil's handmaiden - Eve and Lilith, succubae and Sirens, the sixteenth-century witch hunts, down to 'maneater' and 'Poison' rock songs and Blurred Lines - arguing it perpetuates patriarchal power. They apply Beauvoir's The Second Sex: woman constructed as the perpetual Other, defined as secondary to man with sexuality at the heart of her Othering, and extend the analysis with Gloria Anzaldua's Borderlands and mestiza consciousness, which rejects the gender binary the woman-as-Devil trope depends on.
- ch. 53 "The Devil and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 54 "The Devil and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 55 "The Devil and Philosophy" **·** Free Will vs Determinism — Aaron Rabinowitz argues that modern Devil stories - Milton's 'Better to reign in Hell than serve in Heaven', Neil Gaiman's Sandman where Lucifer quits Hell like a two-weeks notice, and Mike Carey's Lucifer building a second creation whose only commandment forbids worship - reveal a Devil whose real motive is free will and autonomy in a predetermined cosmos. Rereading the serpent in Eden, the wager over Job, and the temptation of Jesus through that lens makes Lucifer look reasonable, even commendable, and since we all feel pushed around by obligations, survival needs, and neuroscience, our sympathy for the Devil is inevitable.
- ch. 56 "The Devil and Philosophy" *(no index entry yet)* — Tim Jones tests Kundera's thesis that the Devil stands for meaninglessness against the angels' excess of imposed meaning. The Quran's Iblis refuses to bow to Adam but really just wants his own hierarchy with himself on top; the Devil's temptations of Jesus in the desert (stones into bread, the temple leap, dominion in exchange for worship) pick apart the meaning of Jesus's fast only to install his own significance; and Lord of the Flies shows how the conch's collapse into 'just a glorified seashell' lets Jack's hunters turn the island into Hell - so the grinning, meaning-destroying Devil of the pig's head is as terrifying as a plainly evil one.
- ch. 57 "The Devil and Philosophy" **·** Immanuel Kant, Arthur Schopenhauer, Niccolò Machiavelli — Humanity as an End in Itself; The Ends Justify the Means — James Edwin Mahon dismantles the 'sympathy for the Devil' puzzle by testing three accounts of diabolical evil: Machiavelli's prince ('learn how not to be virtuous') shows self-interest is mere amoral Realpolitik; Schopenhauer's malice and Schadenfreude ('injure all people as far as you can') are still self-interested pleasure; and only Kant's 'diabolical will' - doing evil because it is evil, Paradise Lost's 'Evil, be thou my good' - truly captures the Devil, though Kant deemed it impossible for humans. Hence nobody actually likes the Devil; people like the intelligence, charm, and underdog status of the characters who play him in The Devil's Advocate and Angel Heart.
- ch. 58 "The Devil and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 59 "The Devil and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 60 "The Devil and Philosophy" **·** Personal Identity; Mind–Body Dualism — Greg Littmann asks whether the Devil could actually get you by testing theories of the afterlife against Hamlet's ghost, Freddy Krueger, the possessions of The Exorcist and Evil Dead, Paranormal Activity, Dante's Inferno, Marlowe's Doctor Faustus, and Doom. Substance dualism cannot explain how a non-physical soul moves so much as a finger; the view that you are your body founders on recycled grave matter and the primacy of the brain; and if you are a program running on your brain, the Devil could torture infinite copies of you in Hell - even re-running you there from Heaven - unless duplication means neither copy is really you.
- ch. 61 "The Devil and Philosophy" **·** The Buddha, Carl Jung — The Shadow; Non-Self (anatman) — John Thompson argues Mara, who unleashes armies and seductive daughters on the Buddha-to-be under the bodhi tree, is not 'the Buddhist Satan': he has not fallen, wages no cosmic war against a supreme God, and merely personifies trsna (grasping) as overlord of samsara. He finds textbook glosses like Huston Smith's misleading, and praises Bertolucci's Little Buddha, where Mara rises from a pool as Siddhartha's mirror twin - 'Oh Lord of my own Ego, you are pure illusion. You do not exist' - a portrayal he reads through Karen Armstrong's Jungian shadow self and Campbell's Freudian eros-and-thanatos frame, concluding that we are Mara/Satan.
- ch. 62 "The Devil and Philosophy" **·** Immanuel Kant, John Stuart Mill — The Categorical Imperative; Utilitarianism — Robert Arp treats Voltaire's 'If God did not exist, then it would be necessary to invent Him' as a conditional claim and argues it is false: Kant's categorical imperative (universalize the action, as with lying and the collapsing practice of borrowing) and Mill's utilitarian cost-benefit calculus secure social order with no god and no Devil required. He then concedes that since most people are driven by guilt and fear rather than reason, inventing the Devil as an archetype of evil to be avoided at all costs may after all be a useful fiction.
- ch. 63 "The Devil and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 64 "The Devil and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 65 "The Devil and Philosophy" **·** Friedrich Nietzsche — The Übermensch; The Problem of Evil; Moral Relativism — Olli Pitkänen maps the Satanist spectrum beyond the Devil-worship of films like The Mother of Tears: LaVey's atheistic, morally subjectivist Church of Satan; the Nietzschean Übermensch cult of the Order of Nine Angles with its initiatory violence; the chaos-gnostic Temple of the Black Light (whose ideal, he says, is the Joker in The Dark Knight); and Finland's Star of Azazel, theistic Satanists whose theosophical moral realism makes Satan a presenter of choices rather than a willer of evil. On that last reading Vader destroying Palpatine and Sauron perishing with the One Ring show evil as human projection, and even Jesus counts as 'satanic' in the rebel-against-dogma sense.
- ch. 66 "The Devil and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 67 "The Devil and Philosophy" **·** Epicurus — Epicurean Pleasure (ataraxia); Death Is Nothing to Us — Clint Jones argues LaVey's Satanic Bible rests on a misreading of Epicurus: genuine Epicurean hedonism is not limitless indulgence but the ordered life of the Tetrapharmakon - don't fear gods or death, the good is easy to get, pain is endurable - together with the threefold division of desires, which reserves constant gratification for natural and necessary things like water, bread, and friendship. By licensing gluttony and every whim so long as no one else is hurt, LaVeyan Satanism fails as the pleasure-as-virtue philosophy it claims to revive, leaving its followers with base, unfulfilling pleasures that are, well, Hellish.
- ch. 68 "The Devil and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 69 "The Devil and Philosophy" **·** Epicurus — Ethical Egoism; Epicurean Pleasure (ataraxia) — Magus Peter Gilmore, High Priest of the Church of Satan, reads pop culture's turn to villains - Loki outshining Thor, Ledger's Joker, Hellboy, Man of Steel's killing Superman - as the mainstream finally arriving at the Satanist party, since LaVeyans always used Satan as a symbol of self-deification ('I-theist') and carnal pride. He answers Pitkänen that Satanism needs no 'higher knowledge' and cannot include theists, and he answers Jones by citing the Satanic Bible chapter 'INDULGENCE... NOT COMPULSION': LaVey taught self-controlled indulgence and rational self-interest, not reckless abandon, and Epicurean moderation is no basis for dismissing him.
- ch. 70 "The Devil and Philosophy" *(no index entry yet)* — Olli Pitkanen replies to Magus Peter Gilmore's critique: against the Church of Satan's claim to own 'Satanism', he argues definitions are never stable (materialism means Democritus', Newton's, quantum or Marx's) and that had LaVey's church never formed in the 1960s, some other - probably more religious - Satanism would have defined the term differently, so no group has final authority over the label.
- ch. 71 "The Devil and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 72 "The Devil and Philosophy" **·** Epicurus — Epicurean Pleasure (ataraxia) — Clint Jones answers Magus Gilmore by conceding he judged only The Satanic Bible but arguing that is the right target: even if LaVey later refined his views, he never retracted the book's Epicurean pretensions, and naming his system Epicurean is itself an appeal to an intellectual forebear. Since LaVey 'plundered' his sources - cherry-picking 'pretty gemstones rather than weightier keystones' - the foundation of Satanism remains shaky, and a Garden of Eden stripped of its Judeo-Christian trappings, Jones adds, was already a remarkably Epicurean life.
- ch. 73 "The Devil and Philosophy" **·** Epicurus — Epicurean Pleasure (ataraxia); Death Is Nothing to Us — Magus Peter Gilmore closes the exchange: LaVeyan Satanism honestly embraces the selfishness most people already live by (sharing Ayn Rand's enshrined self-interest), and Epicurus's own letters to Menoeceus - on not fearing death, choosing the most pleasant over the longest life, pragmatic justice, and the need for natural science - show deep congruence with LaVey. Against Pitkänen on Eastern religions, whose reincarnation and karmic debt he reads as denigration of carnal existence, and against Jones's shaky-foundation charge (Christianity itself was a Hebraic/Hellenic mash-up), he concludes Satanists are proud 'Hell-raisers' celebrating the only life there is.
- ch. 74 "The Devil and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 75 "The Devil and Philosophy" **·** Free Will vs Determinism — Robert Arp compiles the 'Devil made me do it' record: Flip Wilson's catchphrase, actual possession pleas from Christian v. State to the Demon Murder Trial of Arne Cheyenne Johnson, the M'Naghten Rule born of Daniel M'Naghten's botched assassination, the Son of Sam's possessed dog Harvey, exorcism lore from Roland Doe (The Exorcist) to Anneliese Michel (The Exorcism of Emily Rose), and the bizarre Mayo v. Satan lawsuit. The law's answer throughout is that only a defect of reason - never the Devil - robs defendants of free will, and the possession cases read better as epilepsy, schizophrenia, and negligence than as demons.
- ch. 76 "The Devil and Philosophy" *(notes)*
- ch. 77 "The Devil and Philosophy" *(notes)*
- ch. 78 "The Devil and Philosophy" *(notes)*

## The Golden Compass and Philosophy - God Bites the Dust

- ch. 1 "The Golden Compass and Philosophy" *(not yet read)*
- ch. 2 "The Golden Compass and Philosophy" *(not yet read)*
- ch. 3 "The Golden Compass and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 4 "The Golden Compass and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 5 "The Golden Compass and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 6 "The Golden Compass and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 7 "The Golden Compass and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 8 "The Golden Compass and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 9 "The Golden Compass and Philosophy" **·** Personal Identity; Immortality and the Soul — The editors' introduction argues that His Dark Materials is philosophy-rich rather than a mere children's fantasy: its metaphysics of parallel worlds, dæmons, angels, armored bears, and mulefa poses genuine epistemic questions and ethical dilemmas. It frames the volume around the controversy over whether exposing children to Pullman's anti-religious vision is spiritual violence or an education in thinking for oneself like Lyra. It also sets up the trilogy's alternative afterlife, where Lyra frees the dead from the drab land of the dead to dissolve into Dust, and flags dæmons as puzzles of personal identity and soul-body interaction.
- ch. 10 "The Golden Compass and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 11 "The Golden Compass and Philosophy" **·** Friedrich Nietzsche — The Death of God; The Übermensch; Will to Power; Amor Fati (love of fate) — Auxier argues the trilogy is narrated on a Nietzschean lattice rather than being a mere inverted Milton: after tracing the Paradise Lost, Genesis 6, and Pseudepigrapha sources (Azazel as the model for Asriel, Metatron from Third Enoch, Dust from Milton's warring particles), he reads Mrs. Coulter's speech to Father MacPhail about seeking God out and giving him 'the gift of death' as Nietzsche's death of God fused with a description of Nietzsche's own final dementia. Will and Lyra become overman and overwoman beyond good and evil: Will rises by his will and his willingness to kill (the alethiometer's verdict 'He is a murderer' makes him a worthy companion), while Lyra commands both truth and lies, the alethiometer embodying truth as 'a mobile army of metaphors.' But the chapter's payoff is critical: Pullman does not endorse Nietzsche, since in a world where the will to power dominates, the overwoman Mrs. Coulter has no place and falls with Asriel and Metatron into the Abyss, while Xaphania's teaching offers a disciplined, workful imagination as an alternative.
- ch. 12 "The Golden Compass and Philosophy" **·** The Problem of Evil; Fate and Foreknowledge — Robison measures Pullman's Authority against the three attributes that make the Christian God worthy of worship and finds it fails all three: the suffering of intercision victims like Tony Makarios and the Authority's delegation to the malevolent Metatron defeat omnibenevolence, because unlike with the real God we know no free-will theodicy applies, since the Authority simply does not care; his ignorance of Lyra's destiny (which witches and the Consistorial Court learn long before he does) defeats omniscience; and his demented weeping in the crystal litter before dissolving in open air defeats omnipotence. Since the Authority never created the universe and lied about being its creator, he is not God at all, so Pullman's plot never refutes the God of the philosophers. What the books do establish, she argues, is a powerful attack on organized religion: the Magisterium's suppression of Dust research and harming of children mirror real religious institutions' punishment of Galileo, resistance to evolution, and persecution of dissenters.
- ch. 13 "The Golden Compass and Philosophy" **·** Friedrich Nietzsche, John Stuart Mill, Socrates, Jean-Paul Sartre — On Liberty; The Death of God; Bad Faith — Fairweather casts the campaign against Pullman's books as Socrates's trial rerun (impiety plus corrupting the young) and asks whether legally protected speech can still be morally wrong, weighing Mill's On Liberty point that children lack the 'maturity of their faculties' against the charge that Pullman's 'killing God' rhetoric inflicts spiritual violence on young believers, by his own image of intercision. The answer comes through Nietzsche's Three Metamorphoses: Asriel, Will, and Lyra play the lion that slays the great 'thou shalt' of the Authority, but the lion's destruction is only the possibility of freedom, since full freedom is Nietzsche's child creating its own values rather than lapsing into nihilism or Sartrean bad faith. Pullman's Republic of Heaven, with its virtues of courage, compassion, love, kindness, and curiosity held in community, is a social ideal rather than a radically Nietzschean one, so the charge of corrupting young readers is deflated.
- ch. 14 "The Golden Compass and Philosophy" **·** David Hume — Faith and Reason; Applied Ethics (bioethics, technology, AI) — Yuen reads the silver guillotine of intercision against infant circumcision: both cut unconsenting children as acts of faith, Mrs. Coulter's belief that severing dæmons prevents original sin mirroring the Abrahamic covenant of Genesis 17. Deploying W.K. Clifford's shipowner ('It is wrong always, everywhere, and for anyone to believe anything upon insufficient evidence') and Hume's claim that belief is an involuntary feeling no will can command, he argues that the real moral fault lies in acting on uninvestigated beliefs in ways that harm others, while Mrs. Coulter's non-consensual experiments on kidnapped children are wrong independently of what she believes. He concludes that if cutting Pantalaimon from Lyra is wrong, circumcision is wrong for the same reasons though less severely, at worst as irresponsible as exposing a child to chicken pox.
- ch. 15 "The Golden Compass and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 16 "The Golden Compass and Philosophy" **·** The Noble Lie — Vaclavik follows Lyra's career as liar and trickster, from the Silvertongue performance that goads Iofur Raknison into fighting Iorek Byrnison, up to the reversal in the Land of the Dead where the harpy screams 'Liar!' and only true stories can feed the ghosts. She frames Lyra's boast that she is the best liar there ever was yet is not lying as a version of the Liar Paradox, then dissolves the puzzle by showing that lies continue after the katabasis (Will's comforting fib, Mrs. Coulter's noble treachery with Metatron) just as truth-telling appeared before it. Truth triumphs in the underworld not for its own sake but because it suits the audience: the dead need the world they have lost, so storytelling, truthful or invented, is judged by what it does for its listeners.
- ch. 17 "The Golden Compass and Philosophy" *(no index entry yet)* — Mackey tracks the ontological migrations of Lyra, Pantalaimon, and the alethiometer from black marks on a page into the Listening Library audiobook with fixed voices, the National Theatre's puppet dæmons whose black-clad puppeteer stunningly turns out to speak as Lyra's death, the film's computer-graphic Pan with its explanatory opening voice-over, the dæmon-assignment website, a videogame that reduces Pan's shape-shifting to four player-chosen forms, and a Tonner collectible doll with a resin Pantalaimon. She asks whether 'reinforced fiction' supported by extratextual evidence (publisher websites, playbill portraits supposedly showing historical dæmons) makes stories feel more real, and what essential qualities make each embodied version still count as Lyra or Pan. Her conclusion is a research program: understanding how readers conjure fictional beings 'out of all air and thought' across media would be a giant step toward understanding the intricacies of the human mind.
- ch. 18 "The Golden Compass and Philosophy" **·** Everything Flows / Logos — Russell argues that in Pullman's universe stories generate Dust and truth is a product of the telling: Lord Asriel defends the Adam and Eve story as an 'imaginary number' that lets you calculate what you could not otherwise imagine, and Mary Malone must first tell a lie to a guard and later narrate her own story of leaving her convent to bring Lyra and Will to the moment of free will. Reading the companion pieces Lyra's Oxford and Once Upon a Time in the North, she shows Lyra and Lee Scoresby (guided by Hester and Iorek) learning to read clues, birds, maps, and attached ephemera without closing too fast on meaning, Lyra's detective-story certainty proving mistaken about the alchemist Sebastian Makepeace. The self that emerges, citing Santiago Colas, is relational and becoming rather than being: we become the stories we tell and read, and Dust is renewed by each narrative interaction rather than settled into final answers.
- ch. 19 "The Golden Compass and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 20 "The Golden Compass and Philosophy" **·** David Lewis, Charles Sanders Peirce, Plato — Possible Worlds; Free Will vs Determinism — Auxier tests Pullman's many-worlds premise, setting Asriel's coin-toss explanation and the Barnard-Stokes hypothesis against Book III's claim that unchosen possibilities are 'snuffed out like candles,' then measures the trilogy against David Lewis's causally independent possible worlds, which forbid the subtle knife's transworld travel and leave the chill Lyra feels at the thought of 'another Lyra' in Will's Oxford as an unresolved transworld-identity puzzle. Compiling fourteen kinds of conscious beings from humans and dæmons to specters and the Abyss itself, he argues Pullman's cosmology is panpsychist and aesthetic rather than moral or epistemic, ordered by what the alethiometer, the knife, and the spyglass can and cannot do, with the alethiometer submitting to the knife and love alone able to break it, as Will's love for his mother and for Lyra does twice. Peirce's agapicism, evolution by creative love alongside chance and mechanical necessity, supplies the closest philosophical analogue to Pullman's 'Matter loved Dust.'
- ch. 21 "The Golden Compass and Philosophy" **·** Harry Frankfurt — Free Will vs Determinism; Compatibilism; Moral Luck — Michaud asks whether a prophesied savior can be a hero: since Lyra can succeed only in ignorance of the witches' prophecy (Dr. Lanselius's condition), either others keep her ignorant and she saves the world or someone tells her and she fails, so the outcome never depends on her any more than Iorek's eating of Iofur's heart depends on bear choice. Frankfurt's counterexample, recast with the Magisterium's Fra Pavel standing by to force your choice, shows one can be blameworthy despite being unable to do otherwise, and Fischer's guidance control, a reasons-responsive decision-making mechanism that is your own, checked across possible worlds, explains why responsibility survives absent alternatives. But Lyra's case exposes a flaw in that account: her success hinges on things she cannot control, so responsibility is shared among all who kept the prophecy secret, and heroism lies in character and self-sacrifice rather than sole control of the outcome.
- ch. 22 "The Golden Compass and Philosophy" **·** René Descartes, David Lewis, G.E. Moore — Methodological Doubt (evil demon); Philosophical Skepticism — Michaud wonders whether the Magisterium's censorship of Dust research could be epistemically justified, building the answer from Descartes's dreaming and deceiving-demon doubts (imagine waking to find your dæmon was only a dream), Moore's common-sense 'I know I have two hands' reply, and Dretske's relevant alternatives, arriving at Lewis's 'Elusive Knowledge': inquiry itself ratchets up the standards for knowledge until certainty dissolves, which is exactly what happens to Lyra, and to readers, once they start asking questions about Dust and other worlds. On this view the Magisterium had a Lewisian motive to suppress inquiry, since unquestioning belief preserves the certainty on which its power and promise of salvation rest. But because philosophical questions like 'Is God good?' can never meet the raised standards anyway, the chapter concludes that a little doubt is preferable to the dogmatism and bigotry certainty breeds.
- ch. 23 "The Golden Compass and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 24 "The Golden Compass and Philosophy" **·** David Lewis — Possible Worlds — Finley puts Pullman's parallel worlds beside philosophers' possible worlds: the twin Oxfords sharing Simon Parslow's scratched initials invite the inference that Lyra's world has its own Madagascar, while modal realists like Lewis ('out there going on') take possible worlds to really exist and more skeptical theorists treat them as convenient fictions like 'in the story.' She contrasts their origins, quantum splitting at the level of elementary particles versus eternal logical possibility, explains why the indexical 'actual world' is one we can never leave, and argues that Lewis's underdetermination of fiction means the books describe not one possible world but vast numbers differing in unmentioned details like the population of alternate Madagascar. Lewis's counterpart theory then resolves Lyra's chill at 'perhaps there was a Lyra': the counterpart in Will's Oxford could never be her, only someone remarkably like her.
- ch. 25 "The Golden Compass and Philosophy" **·** John Locke, Derek Parfit, René Descartes, David Chalmers — Personal Identity; Personal Identity (memory theory); Mind–Body Dualism; Immortality and the Soul — Harris and Baer use the Boatman scene ('Pan is me... we'd die') to test criteria of personal identity against the trilogy: Lyra and Will's inference to a thinking third part, the ghost, reenacts Descartes's dualist reasoning about mind-body distinctness, Iorek's lack of a ghost exemplifies the same-body criterion, and Pantalaimon's instant shape-shifting favors the psychological criterion, spelled out through Locke's prince-and-cobbler memory thought experiment and Parfit's psychological connectedness and continuity. They ask whether Lee Scoresby's dissolution into conscious atoms that reunite with Hester's counts as genuine survival, best accommodated by Locke's consciousness criterion or a panpsychist matter criterion, with Chalmers cited as the contemporary defender of conscious matter. The chapter closes with individuation, whether Lyra and Pan are one person or two, applied to a Primal Fear-style multiple-personality murder trial, the hypothetical of Pan killing someone while Lyra is innocent, and living wills in persistent vegetative states.
- ch. 26 "The Golden Compass and Philosophy" **·** Epicurus — Death Is Nothing to Us; Immortality and the Soul — Greene applies the philosophy of death to Pullman's worlds, starting from the Epicurean puzzle that death is bad only if experienced, so it cannot be bad, plus the asymmetry puzzle about prenatal non-existence, then testing the Deprivation View (Father Gomez's bullet would rob Lyra of Oxford, the alethiometer, and love) and the Desire-Frustration View (Iorek's frustrated desires) against a universe whose dead do persist as ghosts. The world of the dead, with its harpies, eternal boredom, and the pain of leaving Pan with the Boatman, seems to refute Epicurus by making death a miserable experience after all. But the twist reframes everything: the dead who stream through Will's knife-hole to dissolve into Dust are choosing death over the afterlife, and since for them the good things of life are already off the table, death is not bad at all, which is exactly why they choose it.
- ch. 27 "The Golden Compass and Philosophy" *(notes)*
- ch. 28 "The Golden Compass and Philosophy" *(notes)*
