## The Good Wife and Philosophy - Temptations of Saint Alicia

- ch. 1 "The Good Wife and Philosophy" *(not yet read)*
- ch. 2 "The Good Wife and Philosophy" *(not yet read)*
- ch. 3 "The Good Wife and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 4 "The Good Wife and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 5 "The Good Wife and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 6 "The Good Wife and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 7 "The Good Wife and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 8 "The Good Wife and Philosophy" **·** Authenticity; Feminism — The editors introduce the book by contrasting The Good Wife with earlier law shows like Law and Order: for the first time we see lawyers at home, and the central figure is Alicia Florrick, juggling teenagers, a firm in bankruptcy, and a marriage wrecked by Peter's prostitution scandal while keeping up appearances as the 'good wife' and taking lover Will Gardner. They argue the show refuses a single right answer - her choices are selfish, cowardly, vengeful, or simply her being true to herself - and that her growth as a woman, mother, and lawyer challenges preconceived social norms about women and about lawyers. Philosophy enters as the natural sequel: ethics, justice, feminism, privacy rights, epistemology, and social-political philosophy are all sewn into the episodes.
- ch. 9 "The Good Wife and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 10 "The Good Wife and Philosophy" **·** Applied Ethics (bioethics, technology, AI); On Liberty — Judith Andre uses the show's cases - Alicia's overnight prison visit to Peter for case information in 'Conjugal', snatching evidence before the police in 'Hi', Kalinda's inadmissible but coercive evidence in 'Heart', and Will's advice to use perjured testimony in 'Long Way Home' - to ask whether the profession's zealous advocacy actually serves justice. She explains the adversary system's two-level defense (each side's pursuit of victory supposedly forces truth out, mirroring sport) and its weaknesses: money decides outcomes, and no one in the courtroom is responsible for the truth, as the buried Childs tapes in 'Hybristophilia' show. Individual liberty against state power justifies criminal defense at most, and she endorses David Luban's sober conclusion that the system is justified yet 'cannot mandate lawyers to cut throats', with Alicia remaining the show's uneasy, unreconciled conscience.
- ch. 11 "The Good Wife and Philosophy" **·** Thomas Aquinas, Augustine of Hippo, Cicero — Just War Theory; Applied Ethics (bioethics, technology, AI) — Jai Galliott takes the Season Three episode 'Whiskey Tango Foxtrot', in which Nevada-based drone operator Sergeant Elkins kills twelve Afghan civilians and is convicted of murder despite Alicia's scapegoating defense, as a case study in the ethics of drone warfare. Tracing just war theory from the Mahabharata through Augustine to Aquinas's jus ad bellum and jus in bello framework, he applies proportionality and discrimination to the strike and argues that sensor limits and the physical and emotional distance of push-button killing (Grossman's spectrum from knife combat to drones) erode operators' capacity for discriminate targeting. He concludes that drones are a moral hazard but a legitimate asset, and that blame must be apportioned among operator, manufacturer, and commanders rather than dumped on Elkins alone.
- ch. 12 "The Good Wife and Philosophy" **·** Jeremy Bentham, John Stuart Mill — Utilitarianism; Consequentialism — Rod Carveth maps Bentham's act-utilitarianism onto Lockhart Gardner's defense-side reasoning and Mill's rule-utilitarianism onto the prosecution's, then tests the pairing on the firm's recurring client Colin Sweeney. Sweeney's cases - the wrongful-death suit in 'Bad' where Alicia says 'Of course' he killed his wife, the manslaughter plea and merger opportunism in 'Hybristophilia', and the perjured testimony Alicia may still use in 'Long Way Home' - show the firm letting the end of client and firm gain justify the means. Sweeney walking free, restored to his company, and a father despite admitting the murder is the counter-intuitive consequence that makes Carveth question whether act-utilitarianism should ground our decisions at all.
- ch. 13 "The Good Wife and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 14 "The Good Wife and Philosophy" **·** Plato, Socrates — Platonic Love (the ladder of love); Philosophy of Love; Feminism — Celine Morin reads Alicia's love life through the Greek triad agape, eros, and philia as laid out in Plato's Symposium: her unconditional agape for Peter is eroded by his demands for forgiveness ('Stripped', 'Boom'), while the Socratic myth of Eros born from Penia's lack explains why she tells Will their affair is 'romantic because it didn't happen'. She argues Alicia abandons the romantic soul-mate model of her pearl-necklace marriage for Giddens's egalitarian 'pure relationship' with Will, but stays moored to the old family structure, hesitating on the doorstep in the Season Three cliffhanger 'The Dream Time'. Her discovery of feminism through Diane Lockhart is what lets Alicia weigh leaving that structure without destroying her family.
- ch. 15 "The Good Wife and Philosophy" **·** Carol Gilligan, Immanuel Kant, Nel Noddings — The Ethics of Care; The Categorical Imperative; Caring as the Ground of Ethics — Kathleen Poorman Dougherty sets Kant's universal, emotion-free morality (echoed in Kohlberg's justice stages and Judge Abernathy's 'blind court' claim in 'Infamy') against Carol Gilligan's care ethic, which she says fits Saint Alicia far better: Alicia's practical advice to Jennifer Lewis in the pilot, driving Kenny home in 'Home', and telling Kenny's father 'I care a lot more about your son than he does' show connection producing both better morality and better lawyering. Feminist theorists like Nel Noddings and Sara Ruddick treat this care orientation as morally superior, not merely different, but the chapter's later sections weigh the cost: Kalinda's burnout warning, Alicia's resentment at always hand-holding clients, and the double bind by which women absorb society's unpaid caring labor. The verdict is that care is genuinely moral, but it must become a shared burden.
- ch. 16 "The Good Wife and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 17 "The Good Wife and Philosophy" *(no index entry yet)* — Mark D. White uses the show's bench - umpire-like Judge Park, the 'cleanse'-addled judge of 'Double Jeopardy', Judge Lessner's 'in my opinion' ritual, liberal Judge Abernathy, and Judge Baxter's sentencing kickbacks in 'Lifeguard' - to ask how far judicial bias infects rulings. He walks through legal formalism's chess-like model, legal realism (Holmes's law as 'prophecies of what the courts will do in fact', Will's lesson to Ms. Cabrini in 'Mock'), Hart's open texture of law, and Dworkin's principles-and-integrity theory, on which Abernathy and Lessner facing the same case would each reach their own 'right answer'. The upshot: judges cannot be neutral umpires, so winning lawyers like Will predict the judge, not just the law.
- ch. 18 "The Good Wife and Philosophy" **·** Immanuel Kant, Blaise Pascal — The Categorical Imperative; Pascal's Wager — Lisa McNulty asks what could oblige us to believe someone: Peter's lying fails Kant's Categorical Imperative (a world of universal liars makes lying impossible), while friendship explains Alicia's readiness to believe client Kenny Chatham even before the evidence does. The 'Death Zone' libel trial over an Everest death shows that one's position of power fixes whom one owes presumptive belief - a reader should side with Cardiff, a judge must presume Lambrose innocent - and Pascal's Wager supplies the method of 'behaving like a believer': withholding rumors, withholding conviction, and Alicia publicly playing the wife and sleeping with Peter in a bid to believe in her marriage again. She concludes Peter forfeited any right to be believed, so Alicia has no moral obligation to behave like a good wife.
- ch. 19 "The Good Wife and Philosophy" **·** John Stuart Mill, Immanuel Kant — Moral Psychology (character, vice, weakness of will); Deontology — James Edwin Mahon sorts the show's plots by the gap between deed and motive: Patti Nyholm helps save Marjorie Garnett's liver in 'In Sickness' purely to leverage her firm, Lockhart Gardner sues Chumhum in 'Great Firewall' for the mercenary reason Alicia laments, and the Demorys bribe a juror in 'Fixed' out of altruism. Working through Mill's exchange with Llewellyn Davies and Kant's shopkeeper, he argues motives can be good or bad but never right or wrong, and that no good motive makes a wrong action right - Kant denied even a philanthropic right to lie. Hence Alicia's persuading Shen Yuan to lie about his torture, however compassionate, was wrong.
- ch. 20 "The Good Wife and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 21 "The Good Wife and Philosophy" **·** Plato — Ring of Gyges (why be moral?); Moral Psychology (character, vice, weakness of will) — Skyler King and Robert Arp catalogue the excuses cheating men offer - 'my wife let herself go', 'so much eye candy', 'a moment of weakness' - and add evolutionary psychology's parental investment theory to explain why Peter Florrick-types stray while women like Alicia cheat less. The centerpiece is Glaucon's Ring of Gyges story from Book II of Plato's Republic: politicians such as Peter, Bill Clinton, and Eliot Spitzer wear the invisibility ring of power and come to think moral rules no longer bind them. The chapter closes with prudential and moral reasons against infidelity - scorned spouses, broken promises, wounded children - capped by Eli Gold fielding questions about a Brazil-shaped birthmark in 'Anatomy of a Joke'.
- ch. 22 "The Good Wife and Philosophy" **·** Moral Psychology (character, vice, weakness of will) — Roger Hunt treats Louis Canning, Peter Florrick, and Colin Sweeney as psychiatric cases read through Freud's later drive theory, in which a life drive (Eros) and a death drive (Thanatos) fuse in varying proportions. Sweeney is the purest death-driven type, deriving low-grade sadistic pleasure while feigning personhood to escape punishment; Peter's paranoia assembles a protective barrier of handlers (Eli, Jackie, Alicia, the DNC); Canning may convert self-attack into the very tardive dyskinesia symptoms he weaponizes before juries. The chapter's claim is that the show's evil is not absolute lawlessness but pathology seen from the other characters' perspective, and that the death drive accounts for much of the wickedness on screen.
- ch. 23 "The Good Wife and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 24 "The Good Wife and Philosophy" **·** Aristotle — Virtue Ethics (arete); Eudaimonia (flourishing) — Jennifer Swanson asks whether Alicia can 'have it all', starting from Aristotle: Peter fails the moral virtues (faithfulness, honesty, courage), Jackie's intrusive moments undermine her kindness since Aristotle's unity of the virtues ties them together, and Eli Gold's perfect frankness leaves no room for perfect tact. Following Michael Slote's partial values and impossibility of perfection, she argues career and family are paired values in which gaining one loses the other, as Alicia discovers being pulled between Molly the nanny, Cary's 'she'll have something to fall back on', Diane's partner-track warning, and dumping Will after Grace's twelve butt-dials in 'Parenting Made Easy'. Since perfection is impossible in principle, the answer is balance across all the values of a good life, not Caitlin's retreat into 'I just want to be a mom'.
- ch. 25 "The Good Wife and Philosophy" **·** Betty Friedan — The Feminine Mystique (the problem with no name); Feminism — Karen Adkins argues the show exposes the 'choice' to stay home as neither free nor innocent: Betty Friedan's Feminine Mystique and Linda Hirshman's Get to Work frame how Alicia's more-promising-than-Will-or-Peter career was sacrificed to Peter's ambitions and now constrains her, while Peter's 'thanks for playing the breadwinner', Cary's condescension in 'Fleas', and Jackie's 'we stay in the shadows' speech treat her work as a hobby. The show's feminism goes bland, she contends, when Alicia declines to fight Caitlin's resignation in 'Long Way Home' and endorses choice feminism against her own history. The chapter's evidence runs both ways, though: the 'We're not the cops, honey' double standard, and Alicia's mom skills and lawyer skills flowing productively between nursery and deposition room.
- ch. 26 "The Good Wife and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 27 "The Good Wife and Philosophy" **·** Aristotle — Virtue Ethics (arete); Eudaimonia (flourishing); Aristotle on Friendship (philia) — John Fitzpatrick asks whether voters should care that Alicia stands beside Peter, from the Season One resignation press conference to the finale's withheld hand. After a rapid tour of right-action theories grounded in the show - Eli Gold's egoism, Peter's 'pitted' evidence tip as consequentialism, Alicia keeping Jonas Stern's dementia secret as deontology, the Florrick-Childs no-children pact as social contract - he turns to Aristotle's Nicomachean Ethics: eudaimonia as a life weaving pleasure, friendship, and practical activity, in which mentors like Will and Diane let us contemplate virtue in others and improve our own character. His conclusion: Alicia's endorsement matters not because she is a good wife but because she is a good person whose judgment reveals Peter's character.
- ch. 28 "The Good Wife and Philosophy" **·** Plato, Aristotle — Justice in the Republic; Virtue Ethics (arete); Ethical Egoism — The Azevedos read Plato's Republic Book I as a manual for professionalism: Socrates argues the practitioner - doctor, ruler, lawyer - serves the patient, citizen, and client rather than himself, while Thrasymachus's cynicism that injustice pays and people only seem just is embodied by Eli Gold ('I lie for a living') and by stunt lawyers Nancy Crozier, Patti Nyholm, Louis Canning, and the secretive Jonas Stern. Alicia answers with Aristotle's virtue ethics: professionalism as a cluster virtue cultivated through habituated action, hitting the mean and doing the right thing at the right time for the right reasons. Saint Alicia shows how costly being good all the time is, yet the chapter sides with her striving for virtue over Thrasymachus's goldbrick glory.
- ch. 29 "The Good Wife and Philosophy" **·** Feminism — Offered as a salute to The Good Wife's female lawyers (Alicia, Diane, Caitlin, Geneva Pine, Dana Lodge, Elsbeth Tascioni), this piece collects good, bad, and memorable real women attorneys: pioneers Bettisia Gozzadini, Arabella Mansfield, Myra Bradwell - barred by Justice Bradley's 'wife and mother' dictum in Bradwell v. Illinois - and Charlotte E. Ray; notorious figures like accused murderer Mary Leonard; and trailblazers Gloria Allred, Sandra Day O'Connor, Constance Baker Motley, and Ruth Bader Ginsburg. The parade of firsts grounds the show's fictional women in the real feminist struggle to enter and practice the legal profession.
- ch. 30 "The Good Wife and Philosophy" **·** Protagoras, Jeremy Bentham, Cicero, Plutarch, Averroes (Ibn Rushd), Francis Bacon, René Descartes, Gottfried Leibniz — This companion piece profiles philosophers who actually practiced law or rhetoric: the sophists Thrasymachus and Protagoras (whose 'man is the measure' relativism was rhetoric for hire and whom Plato and Aristotle despised), Demosthenes, Cicero - called 'the closest thing to a Philosopher King any society has ever seen' - Plutarch with his virtue-and-vice maxims, Cordoba's jurist Averroes, legal-team veteran Francis Bacon, natural-law theorist of war Hugo Grotius, law-degree holders Descartes and Leibniz, Montesquieu of the separation of powers, Bentham (panopticon architect whose utilitarianism lets ends justify means like waterboarding), and legal positivists Hart and Dworkin. Its running joke is that the lawyer-sophist connection is as old as philosophy itself, which is precisely the stereotype The Good Wife plays against.
- ch. 31 "The Good Wife and Philosophy" *(no index entry yet)* — The book's comic interlude: eleven numbered lawyer jokes, from the vacuum-cleaner dirt bag and the drowning lawyer thrown his partners to 'God doesn't think he's a lawyer' and the closer that 'only three lawyer jokes exist; the rest are true stories'. The list spikes the same lawyers-are-scoundrels stereotype that Judith Andre's opening chapter analyzed seriously and that the editors' introduction answered with Dick the Butcher's 'first thing we do, let's kill all the lawyers'.
- ch. 32 "The Good Wife and Philosophy" *(notes)*
- ch. 33 "The Good Wife and Philosophy" *(notes)*

## The Grateful Dead and Philosophy - Getting High Minded about Love and Haight

- ch. 1 "section-1" *(not yet read)*
- ch. 2 "Title Page" *(not yet read)*
- ch. 3 "The Grateful Dead and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 4 "Popular Culture and Philosophy Series Editor George A. Reisch" *(front)*
- ch. 5 "Foreword" **·** Zen Buddhism — Steve Silberman's foreword frames Dead shows as 'half baseball game and half church' — a space of collective permission where fans could dance, weep, or trip while taking stock of their lives. He argues the band's philosophical import lies less in doctrine than in the total experience: Robert Hunter's allusive lyrics (shaped by Joyce and Eliot), Garcia's improvising that made each show a story within stories, and the band's very name, which lands you in the middle of what Zen teachers call the Great Matter, life and death, with a grinning skeleton as your guide. He closes by likening thirty years of Dead tours to Kerouac's 'floating zendo' from The Dharma Bums — a movable refuge where any Bodhisattva could find a spot among friends.
- ch. 6 "Saying Thank You for a Real Good Time" *(notes)*
- ch. 7 "Some Folks Trust to Reason" *(no index entry yet)* — Editor Steven Gimbel's introduction argues that whenever norms are challenged there is philosophy to be done, and the Dead challenged norms everywhere: as house band for the Acid Tests they probed the nature of mind, musically they broke the three-and-a-half-minute song template and genre walls, and as an organization they let fans tape shows and sold their own tickets. He maps how the book's essays apply different traditions to the band — Buddhist and Taoist lyrics, analytic logic applied to the storyteller's choice in 'Lady with a Fan', the justice of the narrator's murder in 'Me and My Uncle', continental thought on Deadhead community and gratitude for death, and American philosophy reading 'Eyes of the World' and the band's Beat roots. Philosophy, he insists, does not end discussion but advances insight — 'some of the time you do see the light in the strangest of places if you look at it right.'
- ch. 8 "In the Parking Lot—Fireworks, Calliopes, and Clowns - The Culture of the Dead" *(front)*
- ch. 9 "Need that Cash to Feed that Jones" **·** Adam Smith, Karl Marx — The Invisible Hand; Alienation; Social Darwinism — Steven Gimbel and Brendan Cushing-Daniels argue that the Dead's parking-lot economy — tie-dye shirts priced at ten, twelve, or fifteen dollars, veggie burritos, dueling bootlegs — realized Adam Smith's classical picture of capitalism, not the corporate capitalism Marx attacked. They trace Smith's Newtonian invisible hand and market equilibrium, his ethics of sympathy tempering self-interest, then the Social Darwinist twist given Smith under industrialization and Marx's four types of alienation under postwar white-collar work that pushed hippies to drop out. The punchline: deadhead sellers were not alienated — making shirts was creative self-expression — and buyer-seller sympathy ('it was simply uncool to screw over a fellow deadhead') made the lot a sustainable, kinder 'caring capitalism' that kept the scene alive.
- ch. 10 "The Gathering of the Tribe Ironic Sacramentalism" **·** John Stuart Mill, Thomas Hobbes, John Locke, John Rawls — Anarchism; On Liberty; The Harm Principle; The Social Contract — Horace Fairlamb argues the Grateful Dead were the Sixties' real revolutionary success: where the New Left failed by keeping confrontational politics, the Dead changed consciousness first, extending Mill's liberal 'experiments in living' into a whole alternative culture. He reads the total improvisation learned at the Acid Tests ('the form that follows chaos'), the band's leaderless 'functional anarchy' — Lesh: if anyone tried to take charge 'it would just dissolve in their hands' — and its flat business hierarchy with roadies as corporate presidents as a spontaneous order lived at the edge of chaos. The Deadhead community's ritual communitas, analyzed through Victor Turner's separation-liminality-reincorporation model, amounts to an 'ironic sacramentalism' that achieves what social contract from Hobbes (force), Locke (property), and Rawls (self-interest) cannot: unity that transcends difference while leaving it in place.
- ch. 11 "Forgetting the Love We Bring" **·** Herbert Marcuse, Martin Heidegger, Ludwig Wittgenstein — One-Dimensional Man; Language Games — John Drabinski argues that the Deadhead parking lot is an 'occasional community' offering real hope against modernity's managed, one-dimensional life. Starting from his own Amherst-to-Worcester commuter routine read through Wittgenstein's forms of life — a community of nodding strangers at the same mini-marts — he stages Marcuse's One-Dimensional Man and Bauman's managed death as the deadening background against which such moments crack open. Where the commuter's community stays merely occasional, the parking lot's saturated friendship and shared undergoing (com-passion, good trip and bad, frat guy comforting and comforted) converts occasion into a genuine alternative ethos, drawing on Heidegger's sense of ethos as dwelling place — an everyday miracle of conversion he says we need now more than ever.
- ch. 12 "A Solution Dissolving Special Status" **·** Labor Theory of Property — Peter Bradley uses the Dead's taping ethos and the 2005 Archive.org shutdown to argue copyright wrongly grants authors special status. Distinguishing natural from conventional morality (the four-year-old who knows hitting Jenny stays wrong even if the teacher allows it), he contends that respect for irreducibly unique creative expression — not authorial intent, and not Bob Weir's appeal to the covered songwriters' rights — grounds the tapers' moral code, since Olivier could not copyright his double-cast Hamlet ghost and Woody Guthrie welcomed copyists. Ownership means control, from one's own labor outward; but a performance, like another person, is metaphysically impossible to control, and in the digital world (per Barlow) recordings escape physical control entirely — so authorship should be dissolved into improvisational performance, with published works treated like live recordings rather than rigid canonical objects.
- ch. 13 "Soundcheck - Describing a Band Beyond Description" *(front)*
- ch. 14 "The Music Never Stopped" **·** Friedrich Nietzsche — The Apollonian and the Dionysian — David MacGregor Johnston reads the Grateful Dead through Nietzsche's Birth of Tragedy: the Acid Tests and Dead shows revived the Dionysia, with LSD-fueled crowd ecstasy playing the Dionysian chorus and the concert's structure, set lists, and amphitheater-like outdoor venues supplying the Apollonian form Nietzsche required for the highest art. Leary's Psychedelic Experience — built on the Tibetan Book of the Dead as an Apollonian guide to the trip — parallels the band's dictionary-derived name, 'a song meant to show a lost soul to the other side'. Following Nietzsche's story of Euripides and Socratic rationalism destroying tragic music, he argues the Dead restored music's power to carry listeners (even sober Wharf Rats) beyond language into dispossession and the primordial unity of existence.
- ch. 15 "Tough and Tender, The Dead Keep Truckin’" **·** Ralph Waldo Emerson, William James, Aristotle, Cornel West — Self-Reliance; The Will to Believe; Eudaimonia (flourishing); Pragmatism — Gary Ciocco traces a line from Emerson and James through Kerouac, Ginsberg, and Neal Cassady to the Grateful Dead: Emerson's nonconformist 'infinitude of man' and James's 'live options' and religious appetite for being 'more fully alive' become, in the Beats, the project of living life itself as art ('the only people for me are the mad ones'). He argues the Beats splintered because each chased an atomistic, individual epiphany — Kerouac swinging wholesale from tender- to tough-minded temperament — while the Dead turned the same Jamesian hunger into a shared communal religious experience, a 'kindler, gentler' scene that better achieves Aristotle's eudaimonia. Cassady, muse of On the Road and driver of the Pranksters' bus, literally connects the two movements, but the Dead's unity-in-diversity made them the evolution, not the echo, of the Beats.
- ch. 16 "High Art and Definition" *(no index entry yet)* — Michael Gettings argues that on Tolstoy's definition of art in 'What Is Art?' — the communication of sincerely lived feeling that 'infects' audiences and unites them in joy and spiritual union — the Grateful Dead rank among the greatest art of the last century. Collective, improvisatory Dead performances (the band found songs by experimenting until 'the music played the band') meet the sincerity condition that mass-produced TV, film, and pop fail, and the grief of American Beauty, traceable to Garcia's mother's death, shows real feeling handed on to listeners. Tolstoy's prescriptive (not merely descriptive) definition even condemns Beethoven's Ninth as bad artificial art while 'Dark Star' succeeds — so a theory ranking the Dead above the high-art canon is a feature, not a reductio.
- ch. 17 "Was Jerry Garcia the Soul of the Grateful Dead" **·** Aristotle, John Locke — Personal Identity (memory theory); Ship of Theseus; Personal Identity — John Uglietta asks what made the Grateful Dead the Grateful Dead when they swapped five keyboardists yet stopped being themselves the moment Jerry Garcia died, prompting the rename to The Dead. Testing Aristotle's essential versus accidental properties, Peter French's structural account of group identity (the Yankees, the Glenn Miller Orchestra), same-stuff theories via a VW bus rebuilt piece by piece from its old parts — a Ship of Theseus puzzle — and Locke's memory theory applied to Phil Lesh's liver (but feared brain) transplant, he proposes that the band's essence was a group spirit born of members 'surrendering their own little trip'. The soul of the Dead was bigger than Jerry but smaller than the whole band, and its loss at his death was something the survivors could recognize but not replace.
- ch. 18 "Set One—Who’s to Guide You - Ethical Questions in the Lyrics of the Grateful Dead" *(front)*
- ch. 19 "Encore" **·** The Buddha, Immanuel Kant, Socrates, David Hume — Immortality and the Soul; The Four Noble Truths; The Problem of Evil; The Categorical Imperative — Randall Auxier adopts 'I trust Phil' — Phil Lesh the bass player who hears the whole sound — as his epistemic principle for asking whether death is to be dreaded, staging the essay around interview riffs with Deadheads Dale, Robbie, and Cory. Socrates in the Phaedo and the Buddha's Four Noble Truths converge on welcoming death — samsara is the 'Samsonite Scenario', bad luggage your own wishing keeps repacking — while Hume's Dialogues make God's power, goodness, and evil an inconsistent triad, so immortality is 'not that cool'. Kant's postulates (God, freedom, immortality) and categorical imperative prove too rule-bound for creative life; Whitehead's self-creativity and 'objective immortality of the past' supply the real bass line: even God cannot unmake what you have played, and the properly Grateful response is gratitude for the chance to add your riff to the Acid Test.
- ch. 20 "Once I Was the Student, Now I Am the Master" **·** Thomas Hobbes, Plato, Socrates — The State of Nature; Justice in the Republic; Ethical Egoism — Johannes Bulhof reads 'Me and My Uncle' — the Dead's most-performed song, though hardly a fan favorite — as a parable against rational self-interest. The nephew learned everything from his uncle: how to cheat at cards, steal, and shoot first, so he rationally guns down the cowboys, takes the gold, and leaves his uncle's 'dead ass there by the side of the road' — the student out-mastering the teacher. Framing the song with Thrasymachus's Republic I claim that the unjust always profit (and his own students' admission they would cheat if they could get away with it), then with Hobbes's state of nature where equality, contested goods, and anticipatory striking make everyone enemies — even family — Bulhof argues the song's drunken lesson is that rational self-interest is communally irrational.
- ch. 21 "Analyzing Emptiness" **·** The Buddha, Nagarjuna — Emptiness (sunyata); Non-Self (anatman); The Four Noble Truths; Impermanence (anicca) — Paul Gass reads the Dead's hippie milieu (the Beats, the Acid Tests, Buddhist-monk lyricist Peter Monk) as a conduit for Buddhist doctrine, taking the 'Eyes of the World' chorus as an invitation to awaken to Buddha-nature. He explains the Four Noble Truths, karma, and the five aggregates to argue the self is an illusion, using the image of a flame passed between lighters at a Dead show to picture rebirth without a persisting soul. The chapter then presents Nagarjuna's Madhyamaka middle way — emptiness itself must not be clutched as an absolute truth — and reads the 'redeemer... wagon loaded with clay' verse of 'Eyes of the World' as the Mahayana bodhisattva liberating all beings, with emptiness finally discarded like the boat after crossing the river.
- ch. 22 "I’ve Got Time to Hear His Story" **·** Emmanuel Levinas — The Face of the Other — Stephen Stern reads the 'Wharf Rat' narrator's decision to give August West not a dime but time to hear his story as an enactment of Levinas's ethics: hearing the other's call ipso facto obligates us, and listening restores the face that the label 'wharf rat' stripped away, just as Levinas was stripped of his 'human skin' as vermin in a Nazi POW camp. He parallels West's hope that the absent Pearly Baker 'been true to me' with Levinas's hope in his absent wife and daughter, locating traces of God not in a friendly maker but in human commitment and responsibility for the other. The chapter closes on the narrator walking downtown to his own Bonny Lee, suggesting that responding to August West liberates both parties from isolation.
- ch. 23 "Santayana, the Grateful Dead, and Self-Knowledge" *(no index entry yet)* — Jessica Wahman argues that 'Eyes of the World' restates George Santayana's ontology: the chorus's 'you are the eyes of the world, but the heart has its beaches' captures Santayana's distinction between consciousness (spirit) and the organism's blind, material psyche. She reads the second verse's redeemer fading away with his clay and the seeds bursting into bloom and decay as Santayana's acceptance of natural cycles and mortality — life as a carnival to be danced rather than escaped. The chapter treats the Dead show itself, from Drums and Space to the parking lot, as Santayana's 'spiritual life': a holiday of the mind in which spirit contemplates the play of essences, ending with the self-knowledge the author gained by comparing the two.
- ch. 24 "Set Two—What Shall We Say Shall We Call It by a Name - The Nature of Nature" *(front)*
- ch. 25 "Dizzy with the Possibilities" **·** Henry David Thoreau — Deep Ecology; Climate Ethics; Wilderness and Nature Aesthetics — Allen Thompson treats 'Throwing Stones' — 'the future's here, we are it, we are on our own' — as voicing the existential dread of anthropogenic global warming and the felt 'end of nature' Bill McKibben named, defending the song as the band's a-political polemic against heartless powers. He argues via Steven Vogel that humans cannot literally end nature unless we are supernatural, and recasts the crisis with Thoreau and Gary Snyder as civilization's assault on the wild, the self-willed order we too belong to. Deep ecology then supplies the response: re-identify the self as wild — as Deadheads do by dancing wildly to the music — and build a civilization wildness can endure, with the band's 1988 rainforest-benefit press conference as evidence of the Dead's own ecological consciousness.
- ch. 26 "“Just Like Mary Shelley, Just Like Frankenstein”" **·** Aristotle, Plato — Free Will vs Determinism; Compatibilism; Virtue Ethics (arete) — Chuck Ward uses 'Mama Tried' (the meek family's rebel son doing life without parole) and the mysterious birthmark in 'Mississippi Half-Step Uptown Toodleloo' to ask whether character is fixed from birth, setting the songs' fatalism against Aristotle's account of character as habituated choice and Plato's claim that traits tend to be inherited. He surveys behavioral genetics, twin studies, The Bell Curve, and the supposed 'criminality gene' before arguing that genetic determinism is a mistaken reductionism: like the groovy atmosphere of a Dead show, which shapes the very individuals who produce it, complex systems admit top-down causation, so genes never act alone. The chapter's payoff is that the songs' studied ambiguity — mama tried, yet 'that leaves no one but me to blame' — shows art expressing, rather than resolving, the tension between free will and determinism.
- ch. 27 "So There’s No Accounting for Taste" **·** J.L. Mackie, David Hume — Moral Relativism — Mary MacLeod stages her quarrel with her husband over the musical value of the Dead's Space jams as a problem in metaethics: she claims Space objectively lacks merit, Eric claims it has value, and the chapter asks whether either 'should' claim can be true. She lays out Value Realism (value as intrinsic, detectable properties), Subjectivism ('no accounting for taste'), Mackie's Humean error theory (we project feelings onto the world as we do with color), and the 'oughts by agreement' view that expert or social consensus makes things right the way traffic conventions make right-side driving correct. The Dahmer and torture cases show why subjectivism fails for ethics, and the chapter concludes that 'beauty by agreement' quietly collapses back into Realism — so Deadheads' fierce debates about Space are legitimate disagreements after all.
- ch. 28 "The Storyteller Makes No Choice" **·** Blaise Pascal — Stephen Dillingham takes at face value the storyteller's challenge in the Terrapin Station parable 'Lady with a Fan,' where a lady throws her fan into the lion's den and demands a sailor and a soldier risk 'uncertain pains of hell' to win her. Using decision theory rooted in Pascal and Fermat's probability and the subjectivist (Bayesian) reading of probability as degree of belief, he runs five expected-value cases in 'roses,' varying the soldier's and sailor's estimates of success and the values of winning the lady, being eaten, or refusing. The upshot is that 'you decide if he was wise' is a genuine question: the soldier's refusal and the sailor's dare can each be rational or foolhardy depending on probabilities and utilities no listener can observe.
- ch. 29 "Encore—Mysteries Dark and Vast - Metaphysical Quandaries" *(front)*
- ch. 30 "Sometimes We Live No Particular Way but Our Own" **·** Epictetus, Baruch Spinoza, Zhuangzi — The Dichotomy of Control; Wu Wei (effortless action); Substance Monism (Deus sive Natura); Compatibilism — Matthew Turner argues that a 'Playin' in the Band' jam — the band drifting from the tune into long collective improvisation and back — models a Stoic solution to free will shared by Epictetus, Spinoza, and Chuang Tzu: freedom is not uncaused choosing but responding well within constraints, so by the dichotomy of control there is no point resenting the band when it flubs a climax. Spinoza's definition of the free thing as one determined by its own nature, and Chuang Tzu's butcher who cuts oxen by spirit beyond skill, show the band letting the music 'play them' — Jerry's 'Tennessee Jed' riff never played the same way twice — as learned skill becomes second nature. Because the Dead jam within banks (keys, mutual listening, ability) yet generate something genuinely new and transcended every genre of 1960s music where the Monkees merely copied the Beatles, the Stoic attitude proves creative rather than quietist, an analogue for living freely within our own constraints.
- ch. 31 "I’ll Get Up and Fly Away" **·** Martin Heidegger, Epicurus, Socrates — Death Is Nothing to Us; Being-Toward-Death; Authenticity; Immortality and the Soul — Ian Duckles and Eric Rubenstein use the community's losses of Pigpen and Jerry Garcia to ask whether death is bad, running Epicurus's argument that being dead is no experience and hence no harm, the tedium-of-immortality argument that eternal life would eventually exhaust every fundamental desire, and Socrates's Phaedo claim that death frees the soul to philosophize — which disturbingly implies Pigpen's early 'burn out' was the better fate. Their resolution is Heidegger: authentically confronting death as the end of all possibilities strips socially imposed values (consumerist 'keeping up with the Joneses') and frees us to create our own projects, so mortality itself is something to be grateful for. On this reading the Deadhead counterculture — rejecting 1950s suburban scripts for free love, psychedelics, and life on tour — lived Heideggerian authenticity, and it is better to fade away (like Garcia) than to burn out (like Pigpen).
- ch. 32 "Goin’ Down the Road The Grateful Dead Discography" *(notes)*
- ch. 33 "Playin’ in the Band" *(notes)*
- ch. 34 "The ABCs We All Must Face" *(notes)*
- ch. 35 "The Grateful Dead and Philosophy" *(front)*

## The Legend of Zelda and Philosophy - I Link Therefore I Am

- ch. 1 "section-1" *(not yet read)*
- ch. 2 "The Legend of Zelda and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 3 "Popular Culture and Philosophy Series Editor George A. Reisch" *(front)*
- ch. 4 "Title Page" *(front)*
- ch. 5 "The Legend of Zelda and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 6 "Acknowledgements" *(notes)*
- ch. 7 "Setting Up the Game" **·** Plato, Socrates — Socratic Method (elenchus) — Editor Luke Cuddy's introduction: he argues videogames, and the Zelda franchise in particular, deserve philosophical attention, then frames the book's spirit with Plato's Meno. Just as Socrates' questioning leads the slave boy from thinking he knows to actually knowing that a double-sized square has area sixteen, the gamer's struggle through dungeons is rewarded with the item-room fanfare (the ladder, the silver arrow) as an 'AHA!' feeling of insight. The chapter lists the franchise's games and the book's guiding questions, stressing that the gamer guides Link rather than passively watching.
- ch. 8 "Level 1 - Emotion, Experience, and Thought" *(front)*
- ch. 9 "Evaluating Zelda" **·** Plato — Tripartite Soul — Frome tackles the paradox of fiction through Wind Waker: why do we fear Gohma, feel sad when Link leaves his grandmother on Outset Island, or care when Tetra's identity is revealed, though we know none of it is real? He rejects the illusion theory (we never suspend disbelief enough to call the police), Carroll's thought theory (fear is too fast and too vivid for mere thoughts), and Walton's pretend theory (involuntary pretending and the uniform way all players see the sail break it). Instead he argues for a multi-level mind, modeled on Plato's divided soul in the Republic and Fodor's mental modules: local unconscious appraisals react to the fire as if real, while a global appraisal of reality-status keeps us in our chairs.
- ch. 10 "Game versus Place" **·** Embodiment (the lived body) — Wilson argues that Wind Waker's Ballad of Gales warp sacrifices environmental presence for goal-oriented play: the long, uneventful sails where waves, cloud patterns, and quiet soundscapes dominate are precisely when Hyrule feels like a place, and teleporting turns it into a disposable grid of destinations. Drawing on Castells' spaces of flows versus places, Schivelbusch's 'annihilation of space and time' by the railway (the player becomes a 'human parcel'), and de Certeau's opposition between the panoptic map view and the embodied everyday view, he contends the very existence of the warp option colors all distance traveled. Even panoramic pleasures like hang-gliding over Outset Island, he notes, must be balanced against pure map-grid travel.
- ch. 11 "End Game" *(no index entry yet)* — Arp and Milarker use Zelda examples to teach informal logic: Rob's 'all gamers are geeks' is a hasty generalization, the argument that the original Zelda is chronologically first commits equivocation (Earth release order is not Hyrule chronology, since Miyamoto named Ocarina of Time earliest), and debates over banning violent games trade in false dilemmas, ad hominem, and slippery slopes. The chapter sets out premises, conclusions, deductive versus inductive arguments, validity and soundness, strength and cogency, with worked examples from Clawshot reasoning to Best Buy Wii lines. Its point is that fallacious reasoning harms real lives, so readers should audit every argument in the book and in the gaming world.
- ch. 12 "Level 2 - Dodongo and Death" *(front)*
- ch. 13 "Why I Can Play Zelda for Four Hours Straight" **·** Jean-Paul Sartre, Albert Camus, Viktor Frankl, Friedrich Nietzsche — Existence Precedes Essence; The Myth of Sisyphus; Man's Search for Meaning (logotherapy); The Death of God — Fellela reads Link's endless drudgery (slashing grass for rupees, collecting bottles, leaving the final Ganon fight at a 'permanent pause') through existentialism: Sartre's 'existence precedes essence' is dramatized in Ocarina of Time's opening, where Link must acquire sword, fairy, and skills to make himself the hero, and Nietzsche's 'God is dead' call to become gods is answered by Link restoring meaning to a hostile Hyrule. Camus' Sisyphus figures the repetitive world-saving quest as an absurd hero's owned task, and Frankl's logotherapy (meaning as the tension between what one is and what one should become) explains why humiliating menial tasks, in camp or in laundromats or in Zelda, become bearable and even meaningful. Link's fixed options even illustrate Frankl's point that freedom is the stance we take toward conditions, not freedom from them.
- ch. 14 "Game Over" **·** Friedrich Nietzsche, Plato, G.W.F. Hegel, Bernard Williams — Immortality and the Soul; The Death of God; The Meaning of Life — Janssen argues Zelda is quietly obsessed with death: turning the console off models death as pure absence of experience, which we cannot otherwise comprehend, and gives the gamer a comforting structured formula in place of a chaotic universe. Link, resurrected endlessly to serve the player, is condemned to Hegelian purgatory and what Bernard Williams called the tedium of immortality, while dungeon bosses, who die once and stay dead, are the only genuinely mortal, hence most human, beings in Hyrule. The Wind Waker prologue's eternally returning Hero does suggest Plato-style dualism with an immortal soul, but the chapter notes that Nietzsche's assault on faith destroyed dualism's credibility, leaving the series as a vessel for both materialist nothingness and romantic reincarnation.
- ch. 15 "Level 3 - Don’t Get Manhandled by Manhandla—or . . . Mind-handled" *(front)*
- ch. 16 "Can We Play Beyond Good and Evil" **·** Friedrich Nietzsche — Master and Slave Morality — Drzaic and Rauch argue Link is 'good by default' because ludus play (rule-bound, goal-directed, winning the designed story) locks the player into the designers' morality, while paidia play, player-invented goals like the Ocarina of Time flying glitch or the Fierce Deity rampage in Majora's Mask, escapes the story's moral frame entirely. They then map this onto Nietzsche's Beyond Good and Evil: protagonist Link embodies slave morality (reactive, fear-avoiding, pity, patience, restoring the old Hyrule Ganon disturbed), while the elite paidia player is value-creating like the master, honoring power and secrecy (Huizinga) rather than the protagonist's virtue. The chapter concludes that in videogames the good/evil opposition is welded to ludus, and Zelda fans' naughtiness shows play can go beyond good and evil.
- ch. 17 "Solutions to the Problem The youth whose destiny it is to lead Hyrule to the" **·** John Locke — Personal Identity (memory theory); Ship of Theseus; Personal Identity — Robison asks how Link stays the same person through Ocarina of Time's child/adult switches, Twilight Princess's wolf form, and Wind Waker's consciousness-projection into seagulls and mages. Physical-continuity views fail because Link's body changes or is left behind; Locke's memory-based psychological continuity fails because adult Link's past hasn't happened yet when he travels back. After a Ship of Theseus variant (Ganon reassembling Tetra's sunken planks) she proposes that in Zelda's stipulative metaphysics, destiny, the goddess-given roles of courage, wisdom, and power, is the necessary and sufficient condition of identity, fitting videogame universes whose metaphysics are fixed by designers.
- ch. 18 "Level 4 - The CDi Games Don’t Count! Timelines and Miyamoto" *(front)*
- ch. 19 "Putting Together the Triforce" **·** David Lewis — Possible Worlds — Duncan and Gee study the Nintendo NSider forum threads where fans build Zelda timelines, showing that their marshaling of evidence (Ganon's trident, whether the Master Sword is 'legendary' because of an earlier hero or its sage creators, Hyrule's geography as a marker of time) mirrors scientific argumentation. Using Latour and Woolgar's Laboratory Life, they argue that what 'counts' as evidence, even down to excluding the BS Zelda and CD-i games as non-canon, is socially constructed within a community of practice, not value-free data. David Lewis's plurality of worlds supplies the metaphysics for the split-timeline debate after Ocarina of Time, and the chapter concludes that knowledge in general, in labs and in fandoms alike, is constructed through tools, discourse, and interaction.
- ch. 20 "Zelda Is a Communication Game!" **·** Martin Heidegger, William of Ockham, Ludwig Wittgenstein, Gottfried Leibniz — Ockham's Razor — Johnson argues that because Zelda has no single authorial vision (teams, outside developers, cryptic Aonuma interviews), the timeline is a 'communication game' fans play together. Interpretation follows the hermeneutic circle of Heidegger and Gadamer (parts inform the whole, as when Wind Waker's references to Ganon's earlier attacks make sense of the series), faces Leibnizian underdetermination since infinitely many theories fit the games, and uses Ockham's razor, whose failures (the y=2x formula) show that simplicity is relative to a community's training, as in Wittgenstein's pupil who suddenly counts '1,004, 1,008'. Hence Miyamoto's own claim that Zelda is a communication game: designers supply puzzle pieces, and the fan community creates the meaning.
- ch. 21 "Level 5 - Time and Space in Hyrule" *(front)*
- ch. 22 "The Triforce Holds a Prosperous Future" **·** Friedrich Nietzsche — Eternal Recurrence — Sherlock reads Majora's Mask's repeating three days as Nietzsche's eternal recurrence from The Gay Science, the demon's demand that we affirm the same life 'innumerable times more', but then shows the Ocarina disrupts the mapping: the Inverted Song of Time and Song of Double Time make each cycle a non-repetition, dissolving the recurrence test's existential weight into a new anxiety about fragmented time. Items that survive the reset and rupees banked in Clock Town give time a material value, while the Deku Scrub transformation scene ('You'll stay looking that way forever') is analyzed as uncanny temporality via Freud's Das Unheimliche, with Derrida's claim that there is no non-metaphysical concept of time framing the conclusion that even game time cannot be separated from its representation.
- ch. 23 "Hyrule Regained" **·** Ludwig Wittgenstein — Language Games — Through an opening dialogue where a Tax Inspector absurdly appears in Level 9, Siabra-Fraile distinguishes physical from logical space in the NES Zelda: obstacles are chains of conditions (locked door needs key, key needs rupees, rupees need octoroks, octoroks need a sword), and this logical distance, not geometry, is what makes the adventure. Sprites mean what you can do with them (a tree burns, a rock explodes; water is 'the thing that blocks movement and holds zolas'), characters and objects combine like words in semantic fields and like words, phrases, and discourse build screens, levels, and the map. The rules that make Hyrule coherent and playable are, in Wittgenstein's sense, a language game woven into an activity or form of life, so Link can finally prove the inspector out of place.
- ch. 24 "Level 6 - Treasures of the Hyrulopolis" *(front)*
- ch. 25 "A Win for Zelda" *(no index entry yet)* — Cuddy argues that Zelda games can genuinely be art by adapting three aesthetic theories: Walton's make-believe account (art styles are rules for how to 'look', and a novice friend who plays A Link to the Past like a shooter is applying the wrong rules), Rosenstein's transparent/translucent/opaque artwork-worlds (Zelda's avatar, music, terrain, and story make it translucent, while Adventure is mere message and Second Life too close to life), and Panofsky's claim that objective properties demand aesthetic experience. The decisive third property is freeplay, gameplay not tied to beating the game, like exploring with the flippers to find the bottle under the bridge. Some games (Tetris, Super Mario Brothers) fail the test, so some videogames are art and some are not.
- ch. 26 "Paradise Found" **·** Plato, Aristotle, Immanuel Kant, Søren Kierkegaard — Utopia and Dystopia; Justice in the Republic; Judgment of Taste and the Sublime; Man as Political Animal — Brown reads The Minish Cap's Hyrule as a working utopia: its Royal Family, guards, and market traders loosely realize Plato's Republic classes from Book V onward, but with fluid social boundaries (Zelda wanders the festival unharassed), no slavery, and relaxed state control that Plato's static order lacked. The town matches Aristotle's polis design (walled, defensible, right-sized, river-powered yet self-sufficient), while the surrounding pastoral landscape embodies Kantian disinterested beauty in nature and the Schillerian idea that through beauty we arrive at freedom. Its vague, prophet-free Picori religion of benevolent Christs-in-miniature needs no Kierkegaardian leap of faith, so unlike Hitler's Germany or Stalin's Russia, Hyrule is utopia actually achieved, and explorable, through Link.
- ch. 27 "Level 7 - You are Nearing the End of Your Quest . . . Grumble, Grumble . ." *(front)*
- ch. 28 "A Link to the Present" **·** Arthur Schopenhauer, Immanuel Kant, Michel Foucault, Daniel Dennett — The World as Will; Transcendental Idealism; Free Will vs Determinism; The Cartesian Theater (critique) — Compagno argues that the word 'I' in Zelda simultaneously names Link, the virtual body, and the player, the invisible will, and builds this into a 'Schopenhauerian Console': as Schopenhauer held, we have freedom of action but not freedom of will, since the decisions of the unknowable will never appear in consciousness (against Descartes' free mind and Dennett's Cartesian theater critique). Kant's transcendental subject, standing at the border of experience, becomes the player's perspective from which Hyrule exists, what Foucault would call an empirical-transcendental doublet. The payoff: Link's quest is fated and scripted, yet the player wills it in a perpetual present, enacting Schopenhauer's lesson that a determined life is still lived as one's own free act.
- ch. 29 "Will You Say Yes" **·** Friedrich Nietzsche, Plato — Eternal Recurrence; Will to Power; Amor Fati (love of fate); Theory of Forms — Bruce finds Nietzsche's eternal return in the NES Zelda's clockless, endlessly restartable structure: the same game recurs every time you press start, yet since the player changes, each playthrough manifests the forces anew, and the demon's question 'do you desire this once more?' becomes 'do you will to play once more?'. Link's growth through eight dungeons to face Ganon is read as will-to-power, personal strength and self-overcoming rather than mere domination, and saying yes to the struggle is amor fati. Against Plato's two-worlds doctrine of Forms and the Christian after-worldly (both Nietzsche's targets), the original Zelda offers no guide, no Bible, and no linear time to a final judgment: this looped world is the only one, so it must be affirmed.
- ch. 30 "Level 8 - The Triforce—Need I Say More" *(front)*
- ch. 31 "The Golden Power" **·** Simulacra and Simulation (the hyperreal) — Dugan reads the Triforce through Lacan's psychology of the dyad and the social symbolic: Link is the player's dyadic anchor ('Miyamoto's Framework' avatar, named because he links us to the system), while the gameworld we progressively master and explore is the social symbolic. Each Triforce piece maps onto the triangle: Courage completes the two-way player-Link bond, Power is the sword/bomb/arrow system consummated in the A Link to the Past final battle, and Wisdom is the realization that these tidy symbolic trinities (Father/Son/Holy Ghost, Brahma/Vishnu/Shiva, the three branches of US government) are simplifications. The 'golden power' is seeing that all such systems are representations posing as totality, offering pre-designed, illusory choice, and inferring who designed the games we are in.
- ch. 32 "The Lesson and Legacy of Din, Nayru, and Farore" **·** Aristotle, Plato, Immanuel Kant — The Golden Mean; Ring of Gyges (why be moral?); Justice in the Republic; Deontology — Horn argues the three Triforce pieces are morally neutral: by Kant's Groundwork, only the good will that acts from duty (Link never rescues Zelda for a reward) is good without qualification, so courage, wisdom, and power can each serve evil, and the Triforce would have corrupted Zelda or Link too if fortune had dealt it otherwise. Plato's Ring of Gyges and the Glaucon-Socrates debate explain Ganondorf: he chose to lust for power, and his wealth, status, and fear-based honor are hollow because justice is good for its own sake and his soul is out of balance (which is why the Triforce split at his touch in Ocarina of Time). Aristotle's doctrine of the mean then defines proper use: Link's courage between cowardice and rashness (getting the map, compass, and weapon before the boss), Zelda's internalized prudence, and Ganondorf's power in insatiable excess.
- ch. 33 "Level 9 - Don’t Patra-nize Me!" *(front)*
- ch. 34 "Rescuing Princess Zelda" **·** Alvin Plantinga, J.L. Mackie — The Problem of Evil; The Best of All Possible Worlds; Free Will vs Determinism — Collins runs the problem of evil in Hyrule: Mackie's logical problem (an all-good, all-powerful God inconsistent with Ganondorf's evil) partially dissolves because nothing in the games says the three goddesses are omnipotent or omniscient, and Plantinga's free will defense, plus the best-of-all-possible-worlds idea, shows the Triforce is itself a free-will device, since a wish-granter that could grant any desire cannot limit evil without undoing itself. A 'game-making theodicy' modeled on Hick's soul-making theodicy explains why evil must be excessive and randomly distributed (else players would never strive to grow), and why the goddesses therefore provided a chosen hero: Link, unlike anyone on Earth, is the divine answer to excessive evil, divinely marked to reclaim the Triforce and rescue Zelda.
- ch. 35 "Zelda and Reality" **·** Simone de Beauvoir, Plato — Objectification; One Is Not Born a Woman; Woman as the Other; Theory of Forms — Havstad and Jahng inventory every Zelda across the series (royal, wise, resolved, foreseeing, yet passive, imprisoned, even turned to stone in The Minish Cap or asleep through all of Adventure of Link) and read the maiden typology through Beauvoir's The Second Sex: woman as Idea, modeled on Plato's Forms, gives men a feminine ideal that real women are then judged against, and 'women are not born, but made' explains how femininity is a male-defined myth serving male interests. Zelda is the glorified yet objectified prize whose passivity lets Link's transcendence happen, and Aonuma's 'reality over realism' design philosophy explains why such stereotypes feel true to players. Sheik and Tetra, who act while disguised as (or turned into) something other than the princess, are the promising cracks in the ideal.
- ch. 36 "20 - Getting to Know the World Next Door" *(no index entry yet)* — This afterword reflects on the vanishing instruction manual and the blurring boundary between game worlds and the real one: yesterday's games needed booklets for Hurricane Kicks, while Wii-era worlds teach themselves through naturalistic movement, so 'learning the game is the same as playing the game'. Ngim argues games were never utopias but gated communities whose very limitations (impassable rivers, knowable boards) define them, and that game structures now leak into everyday life through loyalty points, serious games, and phones as portals. He closes by urging that game studies become its own discipline rather than being fragmented across sociology, philosophy, and computer science.
- ch. 37 "Heroes of Hyrule" *(notes)*
- ch. 38 "Index" *(notes)*
- ch. 39 "The Legend of Zelda and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 40 "The Legend of Zelda and Philosophy" *(notes)*

## The Lord of the Rings and Philosophy - One Book to Rule Them All

- ch. 1 "The Lord of the Rings and Philosophy" *(not yet read)*
- ch. 2 "The Lord of the Rings and Philosophy" *(not yet read)*
- ch. 3 "The Lord of the Rings and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 4 "The Lord of the Rings and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 5 "The Lord of the Rings and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 6 "The Lord of the Rings and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 7 "The Lord of the Rings and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 8 "The Lord of the Rings and Philosophy" *(notes)*
- ch. 9 "The Lord of the Rings and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 10 "The Lord of the Rings and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 11 "The Lord of the Rings and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 12 "The Lord of the Rings and Philosophy" **·** Plato — Ring of Gyges (why be moral?); Justice in the Republic — Eric Katz reads Tolkien's One Ring as a modern retelling of Plato's Ring of Gyges: Glaucon's challenge in Republic II that no one, given impunity, would remain just. He traces each character's moment of choice - Gollum already corrupted and split into 'Slinker and Stinker', Boromir trying to seize the Ring at Amon Hen and dying redeemed, Galadriel passing the test ('I will diminish, and go into the West'), Tom Bombadil untouched, Frodo finally claiming the Ring at the Cracks of Doom while Sam rejects the vision of 'Samwise the Strong'. Katz argues Tolkien improves on Plato by showing, through living characters, that unlimited power corrupts the soul, and that the answer to 'Why be moral?' is 'to be yourself': those who refuse the Ring are exactly those who know their own limits.
- ch. 13 "The Lord of the Rings and Philosophy" **·** Applied Ethics (bioethics, technology, AI); Existential Risk — Theodore Schick takes Tolkien's remark that the Ring represents 'potency' externalized beyond its maker's control and applies it to emerging nanotechnology, genetics, and robotics. He sets Bill Joy's call for relinquishment against Eric Drexler's promise of universal assemblers, cell-repair machines, and soldier nanosuits, weighing the 'grey goo' risk against the Rings' own power to heal and preserve (Lothlórien, Bilbo 'well-preserved'). Against the Council of Elrond's solution of destroying the Ring, he argues our situation is disanalogous - Ring-making knowledge is now spread worldwide and there is no single device to destroy - so grass-roots social response, not bans, must tame the new technologies.
- ch. 14 "The Lord of the Rings and Philosophy" **·** Karl Marx, Martin Heidegger — Commodity Fetishism; Alienation — Alison Milbank argues we are all in thrall to the Ring because it mirrors our own fetishism: she reads the film's lingering close-ups, Smaug-like hoarding, and Gollum's baby-talking self-division through Freud's theory of the fetish and Marx's commodity fetishism, with Sauron as the emptied idolater who poured his power into the thing. Against this she sets Middle-earth's gift economy - Bilbo's party, Galadriel's gifts, Sam's elven rope - glossed by Heidegger's 'Thinging gathers': Sam praises the rope by restoring it to its elven makers and its use. Tom Bombadil, who treats the Ring as 'a very pretty ring' and nothing more, and Frodo's final sacrifice model the unfetishized life; the chapter closes with Sam's homecoming, where chairs are only chairs and the family circle replaces the Ring.
- ch. 15 "The Lord of the Rings and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 16 "The Lord of the Rings and Philosophy" **·** Aristotle, Epicurus, Henry David Thoreau, Marcus Aurelius, Augustine of Hippo — Aristotle on Friendship (philia); Epicurean Pleasure (ataraxia); Walden (deliberate living); Eudaimonia (flourishing) — Gregory Bassham distills six keys to happiness from Tolkien's hobbits and elves: delight in simple pleasures (Epicurus on natural pleasures, Thoreau's Walden simplicity), making light of troubles (Sam's star above Mordor, with Marcus Aurelius and Augustine on courage and hope), friendship (Aristotle's Nicomachean Ethics read against Sam, Merry, and Pippin refusing to let Frodo go alone), good character, beauty and creativity (Lothlórien versus Mordor, 'flow'), and wonder (Tom Bombadil, Frodo's 'recovery' of fresh vision at Cerin Amroth). He argues Tolkien thereby becomes a philosophical guide, pointing from the Shire and Rivendell to a richer, more joy-filled life.
- ch. 17 "The Lord of the Rings and Philosophy" **·** Aristotle, Ludwig Wittgenstein — Aristotle on Friendship (philia); Eudaimonia (flourishing); Philosophy of Love — Jorge Gracia contrasts Sam and Gollum as two hobbits of the same nature and culture seeking the same happy life, so their success and failure are instructive for ordinary people like us. Gollum's happiness is solitary possession of the Ring - he murders Déagol for it and ends 'into the dust' - while Sam's is fellowship in the Shire with Rosie and Frodo, pursued through loyalty and giving (spending Galadriel's seed-box to restore the Shire). The pivotal episode is Gollum's trembling caress of Frodo's knee, 'the most tragic moment in the Tale', squandered when Sam's jealous rebuke drives him back to treachery. With Aristotle on friendship and self-love, the chapter concludes that happiness is achievable only in a social context and its key is love.
- ch. 18 "The Lord of the Rings and Philosophy" **·** Friedrich Nietzsche, Hannah Arendt — Existentialism; Will to Power; Amor Fati (love of fate) — Eric Bronson places Tolkien among the 'sometimes merry' existentialists: Nietzsche's artist who says Yes amid catastrophe and needs 'the power to forget', Jaspers on machines estranging us from our past, and Arendt on Eichmann's obedient inhumanity and the few who create joy in dark times. The elves' songs in Rivendell and Lórien are joy born of sorrow and dispossession - Galadriel suffers because she remembers too much, trying to 'embalm time', while Arwen forgoes immortality for Aragorn. Her refusal of the Ring ('I will diminish, and go into the West, and remain Galadriel') embodies the existentialist affirmation: trust your own light, know what to remember and what to forget.
- ch. 19 "The Lord of the Rings and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 20 "The Lord of the Rings and Philosophy" **·** Friedrich Nietzsche — Will to Power; The Übermensch; The Death of God; Eternal Recurrence — Douglas Blount tests Nietzsche against Sauron, whose forging and grasp of the One Ring - even striving to supplant Ilúvatar - enacts the will to power, exploitation, the death of God, and the ideal of the Übermensch. He argues that in Middle-earth the naked will to power appears not as a brave new humanity but as plain evil, and that Tolkien answers Nietzsche not with argument but with a rival portrait: Gandalf's self-sacrifice on the Bridge of Khazad-dûm, Frodo's mercy to Gollum that becomes Middle-earth's salvation, and Sam's humble refusal of 'Samwise the Strong'. Judged by Nietzsche's own aesthetic criterion of taste, Tolkien's vision of community, humility, and abnegation is the more beautiful - and therefore the more compelling - portrait of reality.
- ch. 21 "The Lord of the Rings and Philosophy" **·** Augustine of Hippo, Aristotle — The Problem of Evil — Scott Davison asks whether Tolkien's world is Manichean - two equal forces of Good and Evil - and answers no: the Ring is not 'altogether evil' in itself but animated by Sauron's infused will, and nothing, not even Sauron, is totally evil, since 'nothing is evil in the beginning'. Developing Augustine's account, he shows evil as parasitic privation - Ringwraiths wear robes 'to give shape to their nothingness', the Eye is 'a window into nothing' - and traces evil to inordinate desire: Sam stays content with his garden while Boromir, Saruman, Denethor, and Sauron reach for more than their place allows. Evil unmakes itself (Saruman dies by Wormtongue's knife), and because goodness is the world's default, there is always hope of overcoming it.
- ch. 22 "The Lord of the Rings and Philosophy" **·** Aristotle, Immanuel Kant — Virtue Ethics (arete); Practical Wisdom (phronesis); The Golden Mean — Aeon Skoble uses Tolkien's characters to defend Aristotelian virtue ethics against Kantian and utilitarian rivals. Sam's loyalty is a virtue because critically directed at a worthy friend while Wormtongue's loyalty to Saruman is not; Sméagol's instant murder of Déagol reveals prior vice that let the Ring corrupt him quickly, whereas Bilbo resists it for sixty years; Aragorn's temperance and teachableness contrast Boromir's resentment and pride. Working through Aristotle's doctrine of the mean (courage between cowardice and rashness), practical wisdom, and the phronemos as role model, he argues that rule-following cannot capture moral perception (do we owe truth to Black Riders?) and that utilitarianism absurdly requires Galadriel's foresight.
- ch. 23 "The Lord of the Rings and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 24 "The Lord of the Rings and Philosophy" **·** Socrates, Lucretius, Albert Camus, Jean-Paul Sartre, Boethius, Aristotle, Bernard Williams — Death Is Nothing to Us; Immortality and the Soul; The Myth of Sisyphus — Bill Davis asks why Arwen would 'choose a mortal life' with Aragorn and why wise elves and men call death a gift. The Ringwraiths show that existence can be worse than death - undead kings whose frustrated nature is perpetual torment - while Socrates in the Apology, Lucretius, Sartre, and Camus supply reasons not to fear annihilation, and the elves' endless life raises Boethius's and Bernard Williams's problem of boring immortality (Camus's Sisyphus, Wowbagger the Infinitely Prolonged). He concludes that Arwen and Aragorn, uncommonly wise and loving, accept death as Ilúvatar's gift: she prefers a finite life of deep love, and they trust the old stories of a world 'remade' beyond the circles of Arda.
- ch. 25 "The Lord of the Rings and Philosophy" **·** Friedrich Nietzsche, Karl Marx — The Death of God — Joe Kraus argues Tolkien wrote The Lord of the Rings in part as a protest against modernism - the post-WWI mood (Berman, Marx's 'all that is solid melts into air', Nietzsche's 'God is dead') that tradition is obsolete and whatever can be done must be done. The heroes win by remembering: Gandalf alone thinks to watch the Shire, Sam's book-learning about elves and the password 'Elbereth' saves the day at Cirith Ungol, while Saruman and Denethor fall into modern despair through the palantír and Pippin's stone dropped in Moria brings Gandalf down. The Ring itself is modernism's false promise of remaking the world, and Tolkien answers it with a fantasy where ancient lore has tangible power - sealed by Pippin's maturing into one of Middle-earth's great librarians.
- ch. 26 "The Lord of the Rings and Philosophy" **·** Ludwig Wittgenstein — Deep Ecology; Anthropocentrism — Andrew Light identifies a distinctive environmental theme he calls 'green time': Tom Bombadil and the ents embody nature's long rhythms and are largely indifferent to the War of the Ring - Tom would simply mislay the Ring, and the ents march to war only after Saruman strips Fangorn. Against readings that make elves or dwarves stand-ins for nature, he argues the text lets nature speak for itself, from a collective, ecosystemic perspective: the ents, 'made of the bones of the earth', decline with their forests and lost the entwives to the tilled land. Deploying Wittgenstein's point that we could not understand a talking lion, he concludes that Tolkien's personified nature trains us in the long view and humility needed for problems like global warming.
- ch. 27 "The Lord of the Rings and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 28 "The Lord of the Rings and Philosophy" **·** Immanuel Kant, Augustine of Hippo — Fate and Foreknowledge; Autonomy of the Will; The Problem of Evil — Thomas Hibbs reads the Quest's ending as providential: the Ring is destroyed against Frodo's own will at the Cracks of Doom, through Gollum, exactly as Gandalf hinted when he said Bilbo was 'meant' to find the Ring, 'and not by its maker'. Providence first appears as chance - Bilbo's arrival, Aragorn's landing at the Pelennor, Gandalf 'sent back' until his task is done - and is discerned only in hindsight as the epic's dramatic unity. Against the worry that providence cancels freedom, he uses Kant on duty and dignity (Frodo's 'I will take the Ring', Sam's 'I must see it through', set against Denethor ruling his 'own end'), while noting Tolkien rejects Kant's autonomy/heteronomy split, and with Augustine he treats evil as parasitic, able only to mar itself ('Oft evil will shall evil mar').
- ch. 29 "The Lord of the Rings and Philosophy" **·** Dogen, The Buddha, Laozi — Buddhism; Yin and Yang; Zen Buddhism; The Tao (the Way) — Jennifer McMahon and Steve Csaki compare Tolkien with Zen Buddhism and Taoism on four themes while insisting on the real differences. Tolkien's talking trees (Old Man Willow, Treebeard's 'half awake' forest) invite comparison with Buddhist universal sentience and Dogen's walking mountains, but Tolkien anthropomorphizes where Buddhism grants non-human modes of sentience; the hobbits' ties to the Shire echo dependent origination, though Tolkien's stewardship and preference for the domesticated Shire over sinister wild nature remain Western. Frodo's succession of mentors (Bilbo, Gandalf, Aragorn, and the unlikely 'Master Samwise') parallels the Zen master-student relation, while Tolkien's war of light against darkness - nine Walkers against nine Riders - is decisively not the Taoist balance of yin and yang, for Taoists destroying either cosmic force would itself produce evil.
- ch. 30 "The Lord of the Rings and Philosophy" **·** Plato, Augustine of Hippo, René Descartes, Friedrich Nietzsche, Martin Heidegger, Martha Nussbaum — Allegory of the Cave; Authenticity; Methodological Doubt (evil demon); Amor Fati (love of fate) — Lenore Wright reads Sam and Frodo's Quest through the two journey motifs of Western philosophy: the journey out (Plato's cave, structuring Augustine's Confessions and Descartes' escape from the senses' deceptions) and the journey inward (the cogito, self-transformation, with Nussbaum's claim that literature gives philosophy a body). Frodo's steps out of the Shire-cave - selling Bag End, 'I will take the Ring, though I do not know the way' - create the Fellowship and its freedoms, while Boromir attains self-knowledge only at death's door and Gandalf, Aragorn, and Sam are remade by the road. Against classical heroes like Theseus and Odysseus, Sam and Frodo are modern pilgrims - Nietzschean Yea-sayers becoming who they are, with Heideggerian 'potentiality-for-Being' - whereas Sméagol and Saruman live inauthentically, defined by their pasts.
- ch. 31 "The Lord of the Rings and Philosophy" **·** Søren Kierkegaard, Boethius — The Leap of Faith; The Consolation of Philosophy — John Davenport argues that The Lord of the Rings is neither mere adventure nor Christian allegory but an epic fairy tale whose 'eucatastrophe' - Tolkien's term for the sudden joyous turn, a miraculous reprieve - carries religious hope even though the book contains no churches or prayers. Drawing on Tolkien's 'On Fairy-stories', he makes Sir Gawain and the Green Knight the model for Frodo: Gawain accepts the girdle and is scarred yet reprieved, just as Frodo claims the Ring, loses a finger as Beren lost a hand, and is saved only because the mercy shown Gollum lets the Ring fall into the fire. Gandalf's return, Éowyn's healing, and the sapling of Nimloth extend the pattern; with Kierkegaard the deliverance is grace 'by virtue of the absurd', an echo of the eschaton answering Boethius's question whether our struggles have final significance.
- ch. 32 "The Lord of the Rings and Philosophy" *(notes)*
- ch. 33 "The Lord of the Rings and Philosophy" *(notes)*
