## The Man in the High Castle and Philosophy - Subversive Reports from Another Reality

- ch. 1 "Cover" *(not yet read)*
- ch. 2 "i" *(not yet read)*
- ch. 3 "ii" *(front)*
- ch. 4 "Title Page" *(front)*
- ch. 5 "Copyright" *(front)*
- ch. 6 "Contents" *(front)*
- ch. 7 "Thanks" *(notes)*
- ch. 8 "I. Now Wait for Last Season" *(front)*
- ch. 9 "1. Juliana in Plato’s Cave" **·** Plato — Allegory of the Cave — Dennis Weiss argues the Amazon series is really about television and screened images: Juliana screening the Grasshopper newsreel in her basement replays Plato's Cave, except that the projected images, not the 'real' world of Nazi America, are what offer her 'a way out.' Following her from transfixed belief to doubt (calling the final film 'a nightmare' and choosing to trust Joe over the reel), and reading the show's media surfaces (Nazi commercials, 'Edelweiss,' the Riefenstahl comparison, Tagomi's cross-world vision), the chapter uses media thinkers like McLuhan, Meyrowitz, and Don Ihde to describe learning to see with 'compound vision' in a placeless, image-saturated culture.
- ch. 10 "2. Say Heil! to Architecture" **·** Walter Benjamin — The Aura of the Artwork — Reading the show's Nazi-remade monuments (the swastika on Mount Rushmore and the Statue of Liberty, the SS tower, VA Day spectacle) through Walter Benjamin's account of fascism as aestheticized politics that narcotizes the masses, the chapter argues architecture is what makes the alternate timeline legible to viewers. It then contrasts these fixed 'words in stone' with 'counter-monuments' that keep alternative memory alive: the clandestinely circulating newsreels, Frank and Mark's underground Hebrew prayer, and the fragile chrysanthemums Tagomi and Juliana exchange, with Tagomi's final vision of a Lolita-billboard San Francisco recognized through buildings alone.
- ch. 11 "3. Saving Hitler’s Life" **·** René Descartes — Mind–Body Dualism — The chapter claims the show takes dualism — the same physical world with different 'histories' as its mind — and multiplies it into many overlapping versions of each person: Juliana and Frank swap awe and fear between the first and ninth episodes' screenings, and the newsreel in which Joe executes Frank shifts how they see the real Joe. Its centerpiece is the finale, where viewers find themselves rooting for the Nazis Smith and Wegener to keep Hitler alive and prevent a worse war, forcing the audience to confront its own shifting allegiances and how easily fascism could take root in America.
- ch. 12 "II. The World Dick Made" *(front)*
- ch. 13 "4. Cruel Optimism and the Good Nazi Life" **·** Bertrand Russell — Applying Lauren Berlant's 'cruel optimism,' the chapter argues John Smith and Frank Frink are ruined by the very 'good life' they pursue under the Reich: Smith sustains the system of suburban family, VA Day rituals, and housewife-training daughters until Thomas's Dejerine-Roussy diagnosis makes the eugenic order threaten his own son, while Frank clings to settling down with Juliana and keeps choosing escape over resistance even after his sister's murder. Bertrand Russell's definition of the good life as 'inspired by love and guided by knowledge,' together with Henri Lefebvre's need/desire contrast and Jonathan Crary on television's anti-nomadic passivity, supplies the measuring stick this world fails to meet.
- ch. 14 "5. In the Neutral Zone, a Libertarian’s Home Is Their (High) Castle" **·** John Locke, Thomas Hobbes, Robert Nozick — Natural Rights; The State of Nature; The Minimal State — The chapter reads Dick as a left-leaning libertarian for whom the deepest evil of the High Castle world is the loss of privacy: Arnold's surveillance unit and the Kempeitai's wiretaps illustrate Locke's self-ownership and natural rights, while the Neutral Zone poses the Hobbesian question of whether even an oppressive state beats the state of nature, and the 'libertarian minimal state' names what the Reich destroys. On this reading the American Nazis like John Smith were internal collaborators all along — 'it already happened here' — and ethics lies in refusal, as when Tagomi declines to sign the order sending Frank to his death.
- ch. 15 "6. The Self-Willed and Ignorant Law" **·** Plato — The Philosopher King — Marc Cole argues the Reich's and Japan's governments have the structure of Plato's Republic: supreme power in a political expert who is above the law and manages citizens' work, breeding, education, and music from cradle to grave — in some ways more controlling than the Nazis, since Plato abolishes the family outright. John Smith's son Thomas, condemned to death by a law blind to particular circumstances, embodies the Statesman's 'self-willed and ignorant' law, and after weighing the doctor/patient defense of rule by expertise, the chapter sides with democratic republics and warns that multiplying laws and state management inches us toward Plato's republic or the Reich.
- ch. 16 "7. What if Your Hero Is a Fascist" **·** Karl Marx — Ideology and False Consciousness — Bruce Krajewski provocatively argues that Dick and his works promote fascism rather than warn against it, citing Dick's Exegesis confession that 'my real idol is Hitler' and his fascist epistemology on which what we believe becomes objective truth. Reading the opening credits' overlays (Nazi eagle on the American eagle, 'Edelweiss,' swastikas in Times Square ads) as capitalism colonizing the swastika, and noting the 'structuring absence' of the communists who actually fought fascism, he treats the show's success amid the alt-Right moment as evidence that Dick's projected fascist America is already arriving.
- ch. 17 "III. Captives of Unchance" *(front)*
- ch. 18 "8. Is It Free Will If You Pay for It" **·** Free Will vs Determinism; Fate and Foreknowledge — John Karavitis contrasts the novel, where Frank, Juliana, and Tagomi treat the I Ching oracle as alive, infallible, and inescapable even though its hexagrams are cast at random, with the TV series, where Tagomi's 'Fate is fluid, destiny is in the hands of men' asserts that choice shapes reality. After reviewing Laplace's demon, quantum uncertainty, and Libet's experiments, it concludes that the show's world, unlike the novel's fatalism, teaches us to act as the authors of our own futures, with John Smith's self-made rise the starkest exercise of free will.
- ch. 19 "9. Could the Axis Have Won the War" **·** Free Will vs Determinism; Compatibilism — Miguel Paley maps the three classic positions on metaphysical freedom onto the show: Frank the determinist (Laplace's demon; d'Holbach's claim, illustrated by the LSD interrogation, that the mind is as bound to causality as Connolly's falling body), Juliana the libertarian (Corliss Lamont's burden-of-proof argument, with Wegener's hesitation over the microfilm as deliberation a determined world would render pointless), and Tagomi the compatibilist (W.T. Stace's point that Juliana boards the bus freely though caused, while Frank is stripped by the Kempeitai unfreely though caused). Tagomi's 'Fate is fluid, destiny is in the hands of men' sums up compatibilism, and the chapter ends on what the answer means for holding Nazis responsible.
- ch. 20 "10. Defying Fate" **·** Theodor Adorno, Epictetus, Seneca, Marcus Aurelius — Stoicism; The Dichotomy of Control — Benjamin Evans reads Frank as fate's plaything, Tagomi's hydrogen-bomb gambit as an attempt to steer an uncontrollable web of events, and Hitler's claim that destiny belongs only to 'some men' as political exploitation of fate-talk. Stoicism (Zeno through Marcus Aurelius) explains Ed's serenity-prayer acceptance and the sinister Nazi version in which the 'natural order' means wolves eating sheep, but the chapter's verdict follows Adorno's studies of astrology and the occult: believing in fate seeds authoritarian submission, so Juliana letting Joe sail to Mexico — trusting her own judgment over the prophetic film — is the show's Adornian heroism.
- ch. 21 "IV. Flow My Tears, the Ethicist Said" *(front)*
- ch. 22 "11. Is Resistance to Fascism Terrorism" **·** Jürgen Habermas — The Ethics of Political Violence — Corey Horn tests whether the American Resistance counts as terrorists, first against the US Justice Department definition and then Jeremy Waldron's four conditions of coercive terror: Lem's gunpoint demand of Joe and Juliana meets only two conditions, while the Reich's torture of the factory courier and the atomic destruction of Washington meet all four. Jürgen Habermas's distinction between political terrorism with an end goal and purely fear-driven global terrorism then lets him conclude that the Resistance is at most political terrorists whose violence is really a just continuation of war against an occupier — the same act that gets relabeled justice once the revolution wins.
- ch. 23 "12. Are We Really Sure They’re Wrong" **·** Augustine of Hippo, Thomas Aquinas — Natural Law; Just War Theory; Moral Relativism — Timothy Hsiao argues we cannot condemn the burning of the disabled or Smith's son's mandated euthanasia by appealing to Nazi values, since cultural relativism would make the Resistance the real wrongdoers; the horror we feel presupposes an objective natural law known to reason, the violation of which grounded the Nuremberg trials. From Augustine's 'an unjust law is no law at all,' the chapter then tests the Resistance's armed rebellion against the five just-war conditions — proper authority (the Resistance as agent of the moral law), just cause, reasonable chance of success (the newsreels as grounds for hope), proportionality, and last resort — and finds it justified.
- ch. 24 "13. But Why Is Our World Better" **·** Utilitarianism; Moral Luck — Tim Jones asks why Juliana and Tagomi are right to call the Allies-win world 'better' when our world also holds the Red Army's mass rapes in Berlin, Hiroshima and Nagasaki, and the Czech expulsions — a German widow or hibakusha who found Axis-victory reels could call them 'a better world' without being evil. His answer is a numbers-of-victims argument: Juliana's world duplicates our atrocities (the Heisenberg Device on Washington mirrors Hiroshima) and adds unique ones like the Nazi genocide in Africa, so our world is better for the most people — though its victims deserve the empathy and remembrance Obama showed at Hiroshima.
- ch. 25 "14. Reel Lucky" **·** Thomas Nagel — Moral Luck — Elizabeth Rard uses Thomas Nagel's moral luck to show that Juliana's heroism and John Smith's Nazi career are largely products of circumstances beyond their control — Trudy's death in front of her, being born German twenty years before the war — while Frank's interrupted assassination of the Crown Prince shows luck intervening mid-action. The twist is that the Grasshopper newsreels would give High Castle characters what we lack: evidence of how the same people act under different luck, so their characters could be ranked across timelines in a way largely immune to luck, where we must judge from actual conduct alone.
- ch. 26 "V. A Maze of What-ifs" *(front)*
- ch. 27 "15. Farts, Butterflies, and Inner Truth" **·** Laozi — The Tao (the Way); Yin and Yang; Taoism (Daoism) — Franklin Perkins reads Frank's fear that his jewelry business might start World War III — 'you can't fart without changing the balance in the universe' — as the butterfly-effect problem of acting inside a complexity we cannot grasp, from the Roosevelt assassination down to Tagomi's Colt .44. Against the Germans' fatal flight into abstraction (Volk, Blut, Ehre), the chapter presents the I Ching, read through Chinese correlative cosmology of the Tao and yin-yang, as a manual for mole-like, concrete, adaptive interpretation in which each casting (hexagram 11's 'wall falls back into the moat,' the purple knee bands) means only through creative engagement, ending with hexagram 61: our world arose as contingently as a throw of the yarrow stalks.
- ch. 28 "16. How Close Is That World to Our World" **·** Robert Nozick, Edmund Gettier, Alvin Goldman — Possible Worlds; The Gettier Problem — Brett Coppenger treats the show as a nearby possible world identical to ours until the 1930s, then uses Gettier-style cases — Joe Blake forming justified true beliefs while driving through Alvin Goldman's Barn Façade County — to show such beliefs can still fail to be knowledge. Robert Nozick's truth-tracking conditions, defined via what happens in the closest possible worlds, handle the barns but generate 'abominable conjunctions' (knowing Jones killed the Crown Prince while not knowing the Crown Prince was killed), and the chapter closes on the thought that the large stock of shared knowledge is exactly what makes that dystopia feel only a few big events from our world.
- ch. 29 "17. When Worlds Diverge" **·** David Lewis — Possible Worlds — Ananya Chattoraj tests David Lewis's modal realism — possible worlds as real, spatiotemporally and causally isolated, each actual to its own inhabitants — against Tagomi's journeys: the TV Tagomi's silent bench appearance leaves his world a genuine Lewisian possible world where 'actual' just means 'where we live.' But the novel's Tagomi talks with the policeman, a passerby, and a coffee-shop patron, creating two-way spatial and causal relations that break Lewis's isolation criteria, so the book's two realities are better seen as 'island worlds' of one larger world with Tagomi as the bridge.
- ch. 30 "VI. A Video Darkly" *(front)*
- ch. 31 "18. How to Deal with Reality when We’re not Built To" **·** Bertrand Russell — Philosophical Skepticism — The Zawadzkis and Cisowski argue Dick's deliberate confusion enacts what psychology and radical skepticism say about cognition: our 'cognitive miser' brains build simplified models, and the films shatter them, producing Leon Festinger's cognitive dissonance — Frank's chain of external justifications for his rebellion, Joe and John facing excuses they cannot so easily make, Tagomi's overload after killing the two Nazi agents. Following Karl Jaspers, such limit situations can force a leap into a new mode of thinking (Tagomi's transport to a world where the killings never happened), with radical skepticism — Bertrand Russell's five-minute hypothesis — and the I Ching as vehicles for dealing with a reality we are not built to handle.
- ch. 32 "19. What if Evil Had Won" **·** Hannah Arendt — The Banality of Evil — Verena Ehrnberger reads the High Castle world through Hannah Arendt's Eichmann report: Smith's 'It was necessary work. We did it,' Kido's 'The law is the law,' and the surveillance room named Iwazaru ('speak no evil') show evil as thoughtless, self-protecting obedience rather than sadism, embodied in the disturbingly loving Smith family. The real conflict, on this reading, is not Resistance versus Reich but obedience versus the few — Tagomi, Wegener, Juliana, eventually even Smith — who dare to think and feel for themselves, and the chapter weighs the security obedience buys against the freedom and humanity it costs.
- ch. 33 "20. The Spirit of Abstraction" **·** Jean-Paul Sartre, W.E.B. Du Bois — Double Consciousness — Christopher Ketcham uses Gabriel Marcel's 'spirit of abstraction' — granting an abstract category arbitrary primacy over actual persons — to explain both Nazi anti-Semitism in the novel (Loetze's chatter, Baynes surgically remaking himself to pass as Swedish, Wyndam-Matson manufacturing stories that beat the merchandise) and America's own version from slavery through Plessy v. Ferguson's whiteness-as-property. Jean-Paul Sartre's Anti-Semite and Jew supplies the hater's passion-as-faith against the rational man's groping for truth, and W.E.B. Du Bois's double consciousness names the victims' divided self-image, while Marcel's claim that peace derived from war 'contains war in a latent condition' reads the show's cold peace as war continued by lies.
- ch. 34 "21. After Death It Can Get Worse" **·** Epicurus — Death Is Nothing to Us — Sam Director asks whether Amazon's unauthorized changes to Dick's novel posthumously harmed him, and against Epicurus's Letter to Menoeceus claim that the dead no longer exist to be harmed, he defends harm as desire-frustration that need not be experienced (Childan's unknown counterfeit antiques, Juliana's deception by Joe) and can involve desires reaching past death (Baynes's Operation Dandelion warning dying with Tedeki). Since Dick delighted in the Blade Runner footage and wanted science fiction to thrive, the chapter concludes the TV adaptation fulfilled rather than thwarted his desires — he is no worse off, maybe better off.
- ch. 35 "Bibliography" *(notes)*

- ch. 37 "Index" *(notes)*
