## The Onion and Philosophy - Fake News Story True, Alleges Indignant Area Professor

- ch. 1 "section-1" *(not yet read)*
- ch. 2 "The Onion and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 3 "Popular Culture and Philosophy Series Editor George A. Reisch" *(front)*
- ch. 4 "Title Page" *(front)*
- ch. 5 "For Crying Out Loud" *(no index entry yet)* — Sharon M. Kaye's introduction recounts the legend that The Onion got its name from an uncle's suggestion while the founders ate onion sandwiches, then argues the dictionary definition of 'onion' fits perfectly: 'bulbous' things are inherently funny and 'pungent' things penetrate and burn. The Onion's satire is therefore philosophical, she concludes, because philosophers have always been critics of the society in which they live—an uncomfortable wake-up call, like heartburn—and the chapters ahead will 'unpeel' the connections.
- ch. 6 "Science and Technology" *(front)*
- ch. 7 "Ridicule Is a Survival Strategy" **·** Thomas Aquinas — The Five Ways; Virtue Ethics — Kaye argues that the highest form of ridicule, which The Onion has mastered, targets not stupidity but cheating—specifically 'asscheating,' believing what you want to be true. She reads 'I Believe In Evolution, Except For The Whole Triassic Period' as skewering people who understand Darwin perfectly but exempt humans from evolution because admitting we are just another animal means admitting we die like everything else, the move behind Aquinas-style teleological add-ons that only invite a regress of gods supplying 'extras.' Articles on breast implants, the 'New Nietzschean Diet,' and Tour de France doping all show asscheats artificially inflating their evolutionary fitness; since natural selection stamps every organism with the purpose of survival and reproduction, asscheating is a vice, and ridiculing the asscheats who pollute the gene pool is itself a survival strategy.
- ch. 8 "Evangelical Scientists Refute Gravity With New ‘Intelligent Falling’ Theory" **·** David Hume, William Paley, William of Ockham — Ockham's Razor; Falsificationism; The Watchmaker Argument (design); The Problem of Evil — Ruiz uses Onion stories—'Jesus Answers Half The Prayers At Area Roulette Table,' 'God Answers Prayers Of Paralyzed Little Boy,' 'Christianity Celebrates One Billionth Unanswered Prayer'—to argue that belief in God lacks rational justification. Following Hume's empiricism (all ideas trace to sense perception), methodological naturalism, and Ockham's Razor, he shows that feelings of God's presence, scriptural circularity, and answered prayers (riddled with selection bias) all fail as evidence, and the prayer hypothesis is unfalsifiable—a theory that explains any outcome, like the brain tumor thanking God for its surviving surgery, is supported by no evidence. He closes by dismantling Paley's watchmaker design argument through the infinite regress ('who designed God?') and by treating 'Evangelical Scientists Refute Gravity With New Intelligent Falling Theory' as the reductio of design reasoning.
- ch. 9 "Area Philosopher (Me!) Is Sick Of The Stupid Debate About Stupid" **·** Philosophical Skepticism; Problem of Induction — Cuddy stages the generational-decline debate between Mark Bauerlein's The Dumbest Generation and Stephen Johnson's Everything Bad Is Good for You, showing that Onion stories can be marshaled for both sides: Aristophanes complaining that today's audiences 'just don't get me' vindicates the perennial declinist, while 'US Students Lead World in Detention' wryly supports Johnson's sleeper curve. Comparing Bauerlein to Aristophanes and the 'Our Dumb World' entry on Aristotle and Plato's schools closing, he concludes every era teems with idiots, so The Onion could have existed in any century. Because induction from earlier generations' failed predictions cannot settle whether Millennials really are dumber, he suspends judgment and toasts The Onion as the smartest perspective around.
- ch. 10 "Opinion" *(front)*
- ch. 11 "In All Fucking Seriousness" **·** Aristotle, Confucius — The Golden Mean; Virtue Ethics (arete); The Junzi (exemplary person) — Arp asks why well-placed 'fucks' in Onion headlines like 'Guy In Philosophy Class Needs To Shut The Fuck Up' are funny, and answers with the golden mean traced to Aristotle and Confucius, who both prized self-control and being a good role model to children: swearing is virtuous when it hits the mean between the vice of too much (gangsta rap's every-other-word profanity, the cemetery gardener's rant, Glengarry Glen Ross) and the vice of too little. He sorts Onion stories into vice tables—'Loved Ones Recall Local Man's Cowardly Battle With Cancer' illustrates the vice of cowardice, with courage as the hitting-the-mark mean—and concludes that a well-placed fuck is both funny and virtuous precisely because so many people treat the word as taboo.
- ch. 12 "More Asshole Morality" **·** Peter Singer — Hallquist observes that Dan Savage's 'Savage Love' column, syndicated in The Onion, treats 'asshole' not as a mere expletive but as a property that can be discovered, disagreed about, and used to explain behavior—raising exactly the puzzles philosophers raise about morality. He defends an expressivist account modeled on Ayer's: 'Dan Savage is an asshole' expresses an attitude while picking out a range of behavior defined by shared attitudes, and he answers the classic objections (logic requires truth-aptness, moral disagreement seems factual, we ask third parties who the asshole is) by adapting Singer's point that logic applies to commands and attitudes too, and by showing disagreement over assholery is a conflict of attitudes we still have urgent practical reason to resolve. Since the standard arguments against moral expressivism would equally refute this intuitively plausible asshole-expressivism, the objections must fail, giving moral expressivism a modest positive argument.
- ch. 13 "Jesus Delays Second Coming Because Of Offensive Article" **·** David Hume, John Stuart Mill — The Problem of Evil — Littmann runs Hume's Dialogues argument from evil through Onion headlines: 'God Answers Prayers Of Paralyzed Little Boy' (Timmy Yu's resounding no), 'God Outdoes Terrorists Yet Again,' 'God Wastes Miracle On Running Catch In Outfield,' and 'God Cites Moving In Mysterious Ways As Motive In Killing Of 3,000 Papua New Guineans.' He dispatches the theodicies one by one: limiting God's power or goodness (as Mill's lesser deity would) abandons the God of the tradition; the free will defense fails because police rightly restrain gunmen and no human freedom explains hurricanes; soul-building is pointless for a being who could implant compassion innately or stage fake evils; the 'no best possible world' dodge would excuse the reporter who watches a man burn; and the mystery defense makes reason useless. He concludes no all-knowing, all-good, all-powerful God exists, while insisting readers think it through for themselves.
- ch. 14 "Politics" *(front)*
- ch. 15 "Area Man And Woman Wonder What They Are Good For" **·** Feminism — Benatar uses The Onion's 'Area Man' to stage the Hart–Devlin debate: Lord Devlin made the man on the Clapham omnibus's 'real feeling of reprobation' the test for when law may enforce society's morality against private homosexual acts, and H.L.A. Hart replied that shared values can be wicked (Nazi Germany) and that societies transform rather than disintegrate when values change—confirmed by Onion Area Men who hate their gay coworker, have gay sex to dodge the office blood drive, or have spent eight years 'experimenting,' none of which dissolved Britain or America. The chapter adds the feminist complaint that treating the Area Man as the norm erases Area Woman, and closes with Joel Feinberg's crowded-bus thought experiment from Offense to Others: average disgust is relevant to regulating public offense but never decisive, so the ordinary person interests legal philosophy without being its arbiter.
- ch. 16 "A Newspaper Gone to the Dogs . . . Enlightened Dogs" **·** Diogenes the Cynic, Socrates — Cynicism (living according to nature) — Bayan traces 'cynic' to the Greek kynikos ('doglike') and the philosophy to Socrates' virtue, poverty, and integrity, through the proto-Cynic Antisthenes to Diogenes of Sinope—the man in the tub, the plucked chicken at Plato's Academy, 'you can step out of my sunlight'—arguing that Cynicism is fierce moral opposition to sham and hypocrisy, which is exactly why the powerful smear cynics. Distinguishing The Onion's cool, Swiftian irony from postmodern 'pseudocynicism,' he concludes the paper is genuinely Cynical: headlines like 'Black Guy Asks Nation for Change' (the Obama reveal), 'God Angrily Clarifies Don't Kill Rule,' and 'Dictator Slays Millions in Last-Minute Push to Be Time's Man of the Year' nuzzle the kind, bark at the greedy, and bite scoundrels, so Diogenes would have read The Onion with paternal fondness.
- ch. 17 "Banal Sex" **·** Heteronormativity; Objectification; Feminism — Marinucci reads The Onion's sex coverage as 'terminally ambiguous': 'Nation's Gays Demand Right To Library Cards,' 'Massachusetts Supreme Court Orders All Citizens To Gay Marry,' and 'New Bill Would Defend Marriage From Sharks' ridicule the idea that gay marriage threatens straight marriage, while 'Area Homosexual Saves Four From Fire: Heroic Neighbor Praised, Gay' exposes how heterosexism defines gay people sexually while heterosexual men pass as normal, natural, and neutral. Yet the paper also trades in gay stereotypes and mocks the very framework—characters like Amanda Oppel ('Lesbian Identity Ends Abruptly Mid-Junior Year'), the 'Bar Skanks Announce Plans To Kiss,' and Bruce Heffernan of 'Why Do All These Homosexuals Keep Sucking My Cock?' both defy and seem to reaffirm the stability of sexual identity categories. Weighing queer theory (via Jagose) and Golden's fluid 'elective lesbians' against the parody of 'Grad Student Deconstructs Take-Out Menu,' she concludes the paper's stance is deliberately undecidable, perhaps recommending we stop over-thinking sex and treat minority sexual identities as banal, as in 'Gay Couple Has Banal Sex.'
- ch. 18 "Scientists Find Anomalies In And Then Go Back and Kill Your Grandparents" **·** David Hume, René Descartes — The Metaphysics of Time Travel; The Cogito — This chapter is a satirical fake-news 'World' section rather than an essay: 'Vigilante Group Tracks and Kills Storm-Causing Chaos Butterflies' literalizes the butterfly effect of chaos theory, 'Great Thinkers Fired From Canon' mocks citation metrics by crowning 'Ibidem' history's most-cited philosopher, 'University Restructures To Remain Financial: Becomes Bank' parodies corporatized academia as sympathetic magic, and 'ADD Sufferers Fail Cogito: Don't Exist' has distracted patients pop out of existence for failing Descartes' 'I think, therefore I am.' The title story, about physicists whose experiment lets them go back and kill their grandparents, is printed with its sentences scrambled into a reporter's phone-call fragments, enacting its own point that, as Hume noted, causal order is never perceived only imposed—and with quantum time-symmetry and the grandfather paradox, time's arrow jumps wherever it likes, even into loops.
- ch. 19 "Funny Newspaper Makes Accurate Prophecy" **·** G.W.F. Hegel, Plato, Socrates — Geist (World Spirit); History Has a Direction — Auxier takes the September 28, 2001 Onion issue—'Hijackers Surprised To Find Selves In Hell,' published while late-night TV was still in mourning—to ask what makes some comedy permissible and durable, glancing back at Aristophanes' caricature of Socrates in The Clouds and the circle-men speech Plato puts in his mouth in the Symposium. Hegel supplies the machinery: comedy as a moment of Art within Absolute Spirit may only bring to naught what is already null, while irony that diminishes the noble degrades the ironist—which is why the Warren Commission is fair game for 'Smithsonian Janitor Finds Magic Bullet' but Martin Luther King, a World Historical Individual whose individuality embodies his movement, can never anchor the 'Mrs. Williams Finally Decides Dinner Guest Is Not Going to Show' story. Since 'the news' itself is the great nullity—partial time-slices pretending to explain complex events before their meanings are interpretable—The Onion's rapid 9/11 satire was legitimate and even prophetic, deflating the bullshit of those posing as arbiters of Objective Spirit.
- ch. 20 "Gene For Pedantry Same As Gene For Philosophy" **·** David Hume, G.W.F. Hegel, Jean-Paul Sartre, Friedrich Nietzsche, Martin Heidegger — Bundle Theory of Self; Master–Slave Dialectic; The Death of God; Existentialism — Loader's chapter is a spread of fake 'Existential Times' news parodies: an uncompliant student who answers every robot thought experiment with 'But you're not' drives his professor to swearing over the problem of other minds; Wisconsin scientists find the self as a half-centimeter of Silly Putty in the neck, joking that Hume 'may simply have been looking in the wrong place' when introspection found only a bundle of perceptions; a poll finds not one American philosopher has read Hegel's Phenomenology of Spirit beyond 'the bit about the master and the slave'; and a student essay on Sartre's Existentialism and Humanism wanders off into Planet of the Apes and calls Sartre 'Mr Horse.' The Philososcope prescribes page 232 of Heidegger's Being and Time, a movie loser quotes Nietzsche ('There is always some madness in love') before falling into a pond, an archaeology department's found remains of God back Nietzsche's 'God is dead,' and a Marxist begs capitalism to hold off collapsing until her book is published—philosophy satirized as pedantry, posing, and self-importance.
- ch. 21 "Business" *(not yet read)*
- ch. 22 "World Death Rate Holding Steady At 100 Percent" **·** John Stuart Mill, Immanuel Kant, Aristotle, Socrates, Diogenes the Cynic — Deontology; Consequentialism; Virtue Ethics (arete); Practical Wisdom (phronesis) — Dan Miori, a palliative-care physician assistant, uses Onion medical stories — 'Loved Ones Recall Local Man's Cowardly Battle With Cancer,' the 'Vehicular Manslaughter Doctor,' animal hoarder Tricia McCory's 'I'm A Diseased And Deformed Animal Lover,' and the Terry Schiavo American Voices — to survey how end-of-life decisions actually get made. He runs each dilemma through Mill's consequentialism, Kant's deontology, and Aristotle's virtue ethics (with Monty Python's Philosopher's Song as running gag), then through Beauchamp and Childress's four principles of medical ethics, showing that McCory's kindness to dying animals fails autonomy and nonmaleficence because kindness can be cruel. The chapter closes by restoring 'euthanasia' to its Greek meaning of 'good death,' arguing that accepting the 100 percent death rate means planning ahead with living wills and honest bad-news delivery rather than dying tube-filled in an ICU.
- ch. 23 "The Onion’s New Environmental Ethic" **·** Peter Singer, Immanuel Kant, Thomas Aquinas — Anthropocentrism; Animal Liberation; Speciesism; Ecofeminism — Matthew Altman reads Onion environmental satire as a staged escape from anthropocentrism: 'New, Delicious Species Discovered' and 'Beauty Of National Forest Enjoyed By Logger' expose that even appreciating nature is just valuing it for us, while the mascara-testing lab rabbit and 'Funny Monkey Tested On' make Singer's speciesism charge vivid against factory farming and animal experiments. He then widens the argument to a biocentric concern for species and ecosystems via 'Stuffed-Animal Biodiversity Rising' and 'Consumer-Product Diversity Now Exceeds Biodiversity,' and to ecofeminism via 'Raped Environment Led Polluters On,' where the forest as flirtatious woman links domination of nature to sexism and victim-blaming. Global stories like the Bush 'national air conditioner' piece show Western overconsumption displacing blame onto Third World overpopulation, and 'I'm Doing My Inconsequential Part' satirizes the self-satisfaction of green consumerism itself — leaving the measure of the Onion's philosophical importance as how dangerous it is.
- ch. 24 "Local" *(front)*
- ch. 25 "The Onion, Sarcasm, and the Good of Mankind" **·** Socrates — The Unexamined Life — Noah Levin recounts how the Onion article 'Area Man Committed To Being Spicy Food Guy' made him recognize his own obnoxious boasting about hot food and quietly change, presenting satire as continuing philosophy's self-help tradition back to Socrates' 'know thyself.' He distinguishes parody, satire, and sarcasm, then formalizes Onion jokes — the RIAA suing radio stations for giving away free music, and 'That Teen-Abstinence Rally Totally Rocked!' — as implicit reductio ad absurdum arguments whose absurd conclusions force rejection of a hidden premise. The sarcastic route beats a direct lecture, he argues, because the target must complete the three-step argument himself: identify the mocked trait, admit having it, and decide whether to change.
- ch. 26 "The Guy Is Not Alone. ." **·** Socrates, Bertrand Russell, Heraclitus — Everything Flows / Logos; The Absurd — Matt Laubenthal confesses he was the philosophy-class windbag and defends Darrin Floen of 'Guy In Philosophy Class Needs To Shut The Fuck Up' as heir to Socrates' gadfly role, noting the Athenians also wanted their relentless questioner dead. He teaches Russell's Paradox through a Burger King 's.h.i.t. load' — the set of all sets that are not members of themselves — and reconstructs Darrin's Heraclitean rejoinder that if everything flows and nothing stands still, the static either/or of set membership dissolves the contradiction. Along the way he reads the Onion's Kafka International Airport story ('Are you who you say you are?' repeated for eighty minutes) as existentialist absurdity, and the Star Trek Voyager holographic Doctor as a puzzle about whether a self could be nothing more than mathematical code.
- ch. 27 "News Archive" *(front)*
- ch. 28 "Get Serious" **·** Friedrich Nietzsche, David Hume, William of Ockham, Ludwig Wittgenstein, William James, René Descartes — Faith and Reason; Problem of Induction; Ockham's Razor; The Death of God — David Kyle Johnson takes Nietzsche's suggestion that God is slowly dying and reads The Onion's religion stories — the faith healer who loses a patient during a routine miracle, Kansas fundamentalists lobbying to overturn the Second Law of Thermodynamics, and 'Evangelical Scientists Refute Gravity With New Intelligent Falling Theory' — as evidence of secularization. He splits blind faith (irrational belief despite evidence, as with Bush's 'Blind-Faith-Based Initiatives') from genuine faith as belief without sufficient evidence, arguing religion does science badly whenever it shields creationism from falsification. But science rests on unproven assumptions too, he shows via Hume's problem of induction, Descartes' skeptical problem, and Wittgenstein's charge that naming a 'strong nuclear force' explains no more than God pushing things down. Ockham's razor then decides for atheism — an unexplained universe is simpler than God plus a universe — though William James's will to believe leaves a door open for faith where evidence is impossible, qualified by Clifford's warning that beliefs likely to harm others are never permissible.
- ch. 29 "Progress" **·** Jean-François Lyotard, Jean Baudrillard, Richard Rorty — Simulacra and Simulation (the hyperreal); The End of Grand Narratives; Language Games — Gregory Gilson argues that although The Onion deliberately reports falsehoods, it embodies postmodern theories of truth better than the newspapers it parodies. Lyotard's mercantilization of knowledge and the fragmentation of dominant narratives into separate language games explain headlines like 'Evangelical Scientists Refute Gravity' and 'Astronomer Discovers The Center Of The Universe Is His Beautiful 9-Year Old Son,' which smash incompatible discourses together, while Baudrillard's simulacrum and hyper-reality explain how The Onion — like Disneyworld — is branded fake precisely so mainstream media can seem real. Yet the chapter closes honestly: its own criticism of 'Bush Says He Still Believes Iraq War Was the Fun Thing To Do' assumed some targets were wrong, which assumes a Truth out there — the paradox of a satire whose truthfulness exceeds its own theory of truth.
- ch. 30 "Voltaire Lives" **·** Gottfried Leibniz — The Problem of Evil; The Best of All Possible Worlds — Douglas Ficek traces The Onion's God stories — 'Lord Under Investigation For Failure To Provide,' God refusing to heal paralyzed little Timmy Yu, and 'God Cites Moving In Mysterious Ways' — back through Voltaire's Candide to Leibniz's theodicy, the 'best of all possible worlds' doctrine that Dr. Pangloss keeps reciting through hanging, slavery, and syphilis until Candide concludes we must cultivate our garden. The chapter's sharpest claim is that theodicy works by victim-blaming: Jerry Falwell's 9/11 rant earned the headline-only 'Jerry Falwell: Is That Guy A Dick Or What?' because he excused God by blaming secular Americans, just as Pastor Ellsworth blames heathen tsunami victims. Following theologian Dorothee Soelle, Ficek names this 'theological sadism,' and offers the Bible-drop missionaries of 'Poverty-Stricken Africans Receive Desperately Needed Bibles' as a modern Pangloss who mistakes moral obliviousness for grace.
- ch. 31 "Entertainment" *(front)*
- ch. 32 "Point-Counterpoint Funny versus Really, Really Funny" **·** René Descartes, Bertrand Russell — Philosophical Skepticism — Andrew Terjesen starts from real cases of Onion satire taken literally — a Michigan sheriff warning about al-Qaida telemarketing, the Beijing Evening News plagiarizing 'Congress Threatens To Leave D.C.', Deborah Norville reporting that 58 percent of American exercise is televised — and explains them through the Problem of Other Minds. Descartes' hats-and-cloaks worry and Russell's analogy solution show how we infer that a straight-faced writer is 'just kidding,' but the analogy fails when cultural context, prejudice (Fred Phelps citing the gay-recruitment story, Christians forwarding the Harry Potter Satanism piece), or wishful thinking breaks the comparison with our own joking behavior. The upshot is that humor is engineered misunderstanding — the Onion works by analogy to real news, and could never warn readers off without destroying the joke.
- ch. 33 "Philosophy and Humor" **·** Ludwig Wittgenstein, William James — Language Games — Thomas Brommage builds on Wittgenstein's quip that a serious and good philosophical work could consist entirely of jokes: philosophy for Wittgenstein is a therapeutic activity that dissolves pseudo-problems and lays our linguistic practices open to view, never delivering theories. Onion humor works the same way — 'Bill Gates Grants Self 18 Dexterity, 20 Charisma' needs the shared form of life of D&D-style geek culture, the 'Ask a Salmon' advice column breaks the rule that answers answer, and 'Youthful Tendency Disorder' makes the familiar (children running, jumping, and playing pretend) strange enough to skewer ADHD over-diagnosis and pharmaceutical marketing. Since Wittgenstein asks why we feel a grammatical joke to be deep, jokes that expose our conceptual confusions can themselves be serious arguments — which is exactly what a philosophy joke book, and The Onion, deliver.
- ch. 34 "Kind of, Sort of a Lie, But neither Immoral nor Illegal" **·** Immanuel Kant, John Stuart Mill — The Categorical Imperative; Humanity as an End in Itself; Utilitarianism — Cynthia McWilliams imagines a reader beating her neighbor after taking 'Pedophile Nervous For First Day Of School' literally, and asks whether The Onion lied to her and whether that lie was wrong. She defines a lie as asserting what you believe false (intent to deceive is not required), then tests the satire on both classic accounts: on Kant's categorical imperative, a lie without intent to deceive does not use readers merely as means, while on Mill's utilitarianism the entertainment benefit outweighs harm unless serious harm is reasonably foreseeable — the limit case being the 1938 and Quito 1949 War of the Worlds broadcasts, the second of which killed six people in a riot. Her verdict: The Onion is 'kind of, sort of a lie' but neither immoral nor illegal, passing even the Brandenburg 'imminent lawless action' standard for free speech.
- ch. 35 "America’s Finest Philosophers" *(notes)*
- ch. 36 "Index of Onion Articles Referred to in This Book" *(notes)*
- ch. 37 "Inside The Onion and Philosophy" *(notes)*
- ch. 38 "The Onion and Philosophy" *(notes)*
- ch. 39 "Copyright Page" *(front)*

## The Princess Bride and Philosophy - Inconceivable!

- ch. 1 "Cover" *(not yet read)*
- ch. 2 "i" *(not yet read)*
- ch. 3 "ii" *(front)*
- ch. 4 "Title Page" *(front)*
- ch. 5 "Copyright" *(front)*
- ch. 6 "Dedication" *(front)*
- ch. 7 "Contents" *(front)*
- ch. 8 "Thanks" *(notes)*
- ch. 9 "Hello, My Name is Inigo Montoya. You Bought This Book. Prepare to Enjoy!" *(no index entry yet)* — The collection's introduction, written as a parody of the movie's bedtime-story frame: a man claiming to be both the sixteenth Dread Pirate Roberts and a descendant of S. Morgenstern tells the editors how the adventures of the Man in Black, Inigo, Fezzik, Buttercup, Prince Humperdinck, and Miracle Max teach lessons in logic, metaphysics, ethics, philosophy of language, and political philosophy. It repeats Vizzini's boast that Plato, Aristotle, and Socrates were morons (whom the philosophers 'weren't buying') and jokes that everything is 'inconceivable!' - 'I do not think it means what he thinks it means' - before closing with 'As you wish!'
- ch. 10 "Part I Deceptive Words" *(front)*
- ch. 11 "1. True Love and False Fronts" **·** David Hume, René Descartes, Immanuel Kant, Derek Parfit, Ralph Waldo Emerson — Methodological Doubt (evil demon); On Miracles; The Categorical Imperative — Fallis argues the movie is really about lying and duplicity, not true love: Humperdinck frames Guilder for Buttercup's abduction, the Dread Pirate Roberts is a serial persona, and even the heroes bluff their way into the castle. He treats this as 'adversarial epistemology,' showing that Descartes's malicious demon and Kant's argument that universalized lying is impossible only rule out extreme scenarios and cannot unmask mundane deceivers like Vizzini and Humperdinck (a point he sharpens with Parfit's objection that liars sometimes profitably tell the truth). What does real work are Hume's tools for evaluating testimony: Valerie catches Miracle Max lying about 'to blave' by asking what he has to gain, and Buttercup catches Humperdinck when his claim that every ship in the armada will accompany the honeymoon contradicts his promise of the four fastest ships.
- ch. 12 "2. Why Men of Action Don’t Lie" **·** Aristotle — Aristotle on Friendship (philia) — Mahon maps the film's non-romantic relationships onto Aristotle's philia: the loyal friendship of Inigo and Fezzik under Vizzini's abuse, expanded into a four-person 'family' once Westley, who defeated and spared them with respect, joins them to storm the castle. He argues deception in the film tracks the absence of philia - bad men incapable of friendship (Vizzini, Humperdinck, Rugen) lie to anyone, while friends never lie to each other and the early duels are sportsmanlike. Westley's line 'We are men of action. Lies do not become us' encodes an honor code that real men do not lie to real men even when enemies (hence Westley cheats Vizzini in the Battle of Wits with both goblets poisoned, and the chapter analyzes the resulting triple- and quadruple-bluff regress), while the one exception - Westley letting Buttercup stay deceived about Humperdinck - exposes the code's chauvinism, matching Aristotle's view that philia in its purest form exists only between male equals.
- ch. 13 "3. The Magic of a Promise" **·** John Searle, John Rawls, Thomas Hobbes — Justice as Fairness — Alloy reads the whole story as being about promises - Westley's 'I will always come for you,' Humperdinck's insincerely made and casually broken vows to Buttercup, and Inigo's vow of vengeance to his murdered father. He tests conventionalism, using Rawls's idea that breaking a promise cheats on a fair rule-bound practice (illustrated by Inigo's rule-governed duel and the Battle of Wits where both parties cheat), against expectationalism, on which obligation comes from the trust and expectations created, as when Humperdinck manipulates Buttercup through her trust. Both theories struggle with Inigo's promise to a dead man, so Alloy argues with Searle that it is responsibility for intent that makes a promise, and defends his own view - against the Hobbesian consensus - that every promise is also a self-promise whose binding force is the promisor's self-respect: promises 'make us people.'
- ch. 14 "4. That Moron Aristotle on the Means of Persuasion" **·** Aristotle, Socrates — Wolfe applies Aristotle's definition of rhetoric - seeing the available means of persuasion, where success depends on the speaker's character, the audience's emotional state, and the argument's content - to show that Westley is the story's master rhetorician while Humperdinck is a poor one. Humperdinck fails by coasting on his title ('I am your Prince and you will marry me'), whereas Westley plays on Inigo's mind during the cliff climb, demoralizes him with the left-handed switch, neutralizes Fezzik's strength with a chokehold, exploits Vizzini's overconfidence in the Battle of Wits, and reverses the 'to the pain' situation by targeting Humperdinck's cowardice and vanity. The chapter also sets Aristotle's neutral view of rhetoric against Socrates's suspicion of it as sophistry (relented from in the Phaedrus).
- ch. 15 "5. Does It Mean What Vizzini Thinks It Means" **·** Ludwig Wittgenstein — Language Games; The Private Language Argument — Fetterman takes Inigo's 'I do not think it means what you think it means' seriously and defends Vizzini: the line may merely record that two speakers define 'inconceivable' differently, or react to it differently. Using Wittgenstein, he argues communication requires agreement in judgments, so Vizzini and Inigo may simply be playing different language games, like users of two different dictionaries. He then runs Wittgenstein's private language argument against the possibility that Vizzini's usage is correct by his own idiosyncratic rules - a purely private definition could never be confirmed or be wrong, since only Vizzini checks it - and touches on the philosophical sense of 'conceivable' as logically possible (the square circle) and the group language game that lets everyone accept Miracle Max's 'to blave.'
- ch. 16 "Part II Courage and Reason" *(front)*
- ch. 17 "6. To the Pain" **·** Jeremy Bentham, John Stuart Mill, Peter Singer — The Hedonic Calculus; Higher and Lower Pleasures; Speciesism — Robison-Greene imagines the table of contents of Count Rugen's definitive book on pain, using the Zoo of Death and The Machine as her case studies. Bentham's dimensions of pain - intensity, duration, and propinquity - organize the analysis of Westley's torture, including Rugen's tactic of leaving The Machine beside the prisoner so anticipation itself hurts, and the metaphysical oddity of having years stolen from the far end of one's life. Mill's higher and lower pleasures explain why the orangutan's prolonged death disturbs us more than other kills (a chimpanzee can reflect on what is happening to her), and Singer's speciesism grounds the argument that animals' pain behavior shows they suffer, so inflicting it merely because they are animals is indefensible; by contrast Westley's empathy lets him out-sadist the sadists with the 'to the pain' speech.
- ch. 18 "7. Who Should Have Won the Battle of Wits" **·** David Lewis — Possible Worlds — Greene observes that the Battle of Wits never really happened - Westley implied the iocane was in one goblet but poisoned both - so he constructs a 'near possible world' (crediting David Lewis's realist take on possible worlds) in which a fair contest occurs between the same two men. Vizzini's verbal 'deduction' is specious, he is hopeless at reading people (every 'inconceivable!' marks an underestimate), and his hubris and compulsive trickery make him predictable, while Westley reads character expertly, from Inigo's oath on his father's soul to Fezzik's sportsmanship. Greene concludes that in the majority of possible worlds the Man in Black wins a fair battle, and cheated in ours only because his real goals were getting the knife from Buttercup's throat and seeing Vizzini dead.
- ch. 19 "8. I Will Never Doubt Again" **·** David Hume, René Descartes — The Will to Believe — Watson and Guidry-Grimes ask whether Buttercup was rational to accept the evidence that Westley was dead, and whether Westley's Fire Swamp optimism and Inigo's twenty-year hunt for the six-fingered man are wishful thinking or something better. After laying out the evidentialist tradition (Descartes's rule of evidence, Hume's 'a wise man proportions his belief to the evidence,' Clifford's condemnations), they use Adrienne Martin's account of hope as 'endorsed desire plus uncertainty' to argue that value-shaped belief can be rational when it sustains well-being and character. Their conclusion expands rationality beyond evidence-tracking: hope, unlike wishful thinking, takes evidence seriously while balancing it against what makes a life good, which is why Buttercup's despair on the wedding night, not her earlier resignation, is the real failure.
- ch. 20 "Part III Getting Even" *(front)*
- ch. 21 "9. Why Revenge Is So Sweet" **·** Punishment: Retribution vs Rehabilitation; Forgiveness — Haas asks why we cheer Inigo's vendetta against Rugen when moral philosophy condemns vengeance as emotionally driven injustice. Distinguishing impartial, proportional punishment from personal revenge, he then uses the honor-culture literature (Nisbett and Cohen, Tamler Sommers) to argue that in lawless Florin - where the 'justice system' is run by the villain who ordered the murder - Inigo's personal retribution is a justified duty of honor, since no institutional avenue exists. The deeper pull of revenge stories, he concludes, is that victims have a special moral standing to punish - which is why Rugen dying by anyone's hand but Inigo's would be unsatisfying - while a variant story (Westley as the killer) would call instead for atonement and forgiveness, showing that revenge is made admirable by how stories are told.
- ch. 22 "10. Making the Punishment Fit the Crime" **·** John Locke, Immanuel Kant — The State of Nature; Punishment: Retribution vs Rehabilitation — Heter defends Inigo's killing of Rugen as pure retribution - not deterrence or prevention, since Rugen 'deserves' to die for a murder long past. Locke's state of nature grounds the right to punish in victims, who merely transfer it to the state for prudential reasons, so in tyrannical Florin, where no reasonable expectation of justice exists, Montoya is within his rights to execute punishment himself. Kant's retributivism (the island whose inhabitants become accomplices if they spare convicted murderers, from the Metaphysics of Morals) supports the claim that murderers deserve death, and against the objection that proportionality need not mean identity of kind (following M.A. Moore, using Fezzik's hypothetical house-burning), the chapter argues death is the only punishment weighty enough to fit murder.
- ch. 23 "11. Have Fun Overthrowing This Evil Government!" **·** Thomas Hobbes, Augustine of Hippo, Thomas Aquinas — Just War Theory; The Social Contract — Gibson reads Humperdinck's fabricated-pretext war against Guilder (framing Guilder for Buttercup's murder, with Vizzini's crew as private military contractors) through Just War Theory: it fails the jus ad bellum just-cause requirement built on Augustine and Aquinas, since the war would be retribution sparked by staged wrongdoing, and contractor motives of profit compound the violation. Michael Davis's critique of the ticking-time-bomb defense then shows that Rugen's torture of Westley is exactly the 'institution of torture' whose moral cost forbids ever licensing torture, since the torturer must be trained on victims first. Finally, even Hobbes's Leviathan - the most absolutist of social contract views - requires the sovereign to protect the people's safety and administer equal justice, so Humperdinck's record makes his rule illegitimate and the storming of the castle a justified insurrection.
- ch. 24 "12. Should I Really Stop That Rhyming" **·** Aristotle, Immanuel Kant — Virtue Ethics (arete); The Golden Mean; Eudaimonia (flourishing) — Yuen uses the rhyming scene to analyze annoyance - rhyme hijacks Vizzini's attention, the Goldman/Morgenstern interruptions betray invested expectations, and fans' endless quotation is annoying through sheer repetition - concluding that annoyance is irreducibly relative and contextual. Aristotle's virtue theory supplies the moral frame: friendliness and wittiness, golden means between obsequiousness and surliness, buffoonery and boorishness, make being annoying a character flaw that damages the communal eudaimonia Aristotle takes the good life to require. Since these virtues may be supererogatory ideals rather than obligations, Yuen borrows Kant's imperfect duty to develop one's talents to explain why Inigo had to become a master swordsman - failing to hone the skill his one great goal demands would be a failure of self-respect.
- ch. 25 "Part IV Metaphysical Miracles" *(not yet read)*
- ch. 26 "13. The Shrieking Evils" **·** Hannah Arendt, Plato — The Banality of Evil; Moral Psychology (character, vice, weakness of will) — Piven argues that evil in the story runs from the overt villains to the beloved heroes: Humperdinck's rage at Westley is analyzed as malignant envy of true love, Rugen's torture as dispassionate sadistic curiosity, and Vizzini's pompous moralizing as narcissistic hypocrisy masking childhood shame and nightmares. He applies Hannah Arendt's banality of evil to Inigo and Fezzik, who soberly assault and abduct Buttercup, and then brands Westley - a pirate who admits he liked piracy and who slaps and strangles Buttercup to test her fidelity - as possibly the evilest character, likening his love-justified slaughter to the terrorist's logic. The point is that we adore these characters only by ignoring deeds we would condemn in real life.
- ch. 27 "14. The End of Inigo Montoya" **·** Aristotle — The Four Causes; Eudaimonia (flourishing); Potentiality and Actuality — Elsby and Luzecky read Inigo through Aristotle's doctrine of the four causes: his life is organized by a final cause, revenge on the Six-Fingered Man as the thing that is 'just good', which explains every action from fencing drills to eating breakfast. The famous mantra works because habit embodies the final cause - repeating 'My name is Inigo Montoya' lets trained movement carry him through wounds that should end the fight. When revenge is achieved the chain of ends terminates and 'Inigo Montoya' ends with it, which is why his rebirth as the Dread Pirate Roberts is needed; the chapter generalizes that our habits, not grand outside purposes, are how final causes give lives their ends.
- ch. 28 "15. It Would Take a Miracle!" **·** David Hume — On Miracles; Philosophical Skepticism; Faith and Reason — Barkman and Käärid ask whether Miracle Max's revival of 'mostly dead' Westley is a miracle, staging David Hume - for whom a violation of natural law is never more credible than mistaken or lying testimony, so Hume in Florin would call Max a deceiver and Inigo and Fezzik barbarous dupes - against C.S. Lewis, for whom a miracle is a new, unpredictable element introduced into nature rather than a violation of it. They add puzzles neither philosopher addresses: whether Westley was truly dead, whether a 'rotten miracle' would still count, and why Max's miracles seem to require 'unshattered' confidence, concluding that Florin's miracles are person-dependent in a way Hume's skepticism and Lewis's supernaturalism both miss.
- ch. 29 "16. My Name Is—Hang on, What Was It Meant to Be Again Are You Sure Well, Okay Then . ." **·** René Descartes, William James — Personal Identity; The Cogito; Pragmatism — Tim Jones argues the book is not really a love story but a warning about identity: against Descartes's transcendent self, the American pragmatists William James, Cooley, and Mead hold that the self is fluid and socially constituted, and Westley's serial reinvention (farm boy, Dread Pirate Roberts, Man in Black) - along with Mandy Patinkin's cathartic channeling of his father's death into Inigo - shows the freedom this mutability grants. The Goldman frame then exposes the danger: the fictionalized Goldman seizes Morgenstern's text, cuts its history, and rewrites it to his own ends, exactly as other people can seize our plastic identities and shape them for their interests rather than ours.
- ch. 30 "17. The Miracle of True Love" **·** Plato, Aristotle, Socrates — Philosophy of Love; Immortality and the Soul — Elsby and Luzecky use the Phaedo to make sense of Westley being 'mostly dead': Socrates's cyclical argument that opposites generate each other, Aristotle's distinctions between contradictories and contraries, and the observation that life and death here come in degrees together recast life and death as spectrum concepts rather than strict opposites. True love emerges as a second opposite of death: answering Simmias's objection that a soul, like a lyre's harmony, perishes with its instrument, they argue true love is a harmony binding persons and actions that persists beyond the particular lovers - which is how the film's miracle of true love conquers death.
- ch. 31 "Part V Life Lessons of a Fairytale" *(front)*
- ch. 32 "18. I Know Something You Don’t Know" **·** The Meaning of Life — Auxier argues the three contests turn on loyalty rather than morality: Josiah Royce's account of life's meaning as devoted service to a chosen cause explains the fair play of the duels on the Cliffs of Insanity, Inigo's and Fezzik's artistry, why Westley spares them 'for the sake of art, not goodness', and why Vizzini, Rugen, and Humperdinck, who serve only their own twisted egos, get no sporting chance. He reads Westley and Buttercup's love as a shared cause whose loss should have been morally educative (Royce on lost causes), faults passive Buttercup and pirate-playing Westley for failing to grow through it, and closes with Royce's 'loyalty to loyalty' as the meaning of the swordfight's mutual admiration and of Inigo's final act of mercy.
- ch. 33 "19. The Medical Ethics of Miracle Max" **·** Applied Ethics (bioethics, technology, AI); Utilitarianism — Shea treats Miracle Max as a one-man healthcare system to teach biomedical ethics: Max's lies about Valerie the 'witch', the fee, and the pill's duration violate the principle of autonomy and informed consent; the withholding-versus-withdrawing of the miracle pill and hypothetical Zoo of Death experiments engage nonmaleficence; his price and his duty to a mostly-dead stranger by the road raise beneficence; and who deserves resurrection under a monopoly raises justice, weighed across libertarian, utilitarian, and liberal-egalitarian (worst-off-maximizing) answers. The chapter concludes that principlism's four principles handle such dilemmas better than any single mechanical rule.
- ch. 34 "20. The Good and the Gracious" **·** Plato, Confucius, Niccolò Machiavelli, Thomas Hobbes — Li (ritual propriety); Justice in the Republic; The Ends Justify the Means — Olson and Taliaferro read manners as the story's moral barometer: the genuine, effusive compliments exchanged by Westley and Inigo atop the Cliffs of Insanity, and Fezzik's sporting wrestling, express care and connection, while Humperdinck's and Rugen's courtly politeness is camouflage for torture, murder, and war. Against Machiavelli's instrumental use of courtesy in The Prince, they recruit Plato's sophrosyne as 'good form' inseparable from justice, wisdom, and courage, and Confucian li as etiquette that instructs and steadies us toward virtue, with Hobbes's 'small morals' marking the hollow version; they add that manners also police class, from Buttercup's 'Farm Boy' to reality TV and the financiers of the Great Recession who used the correct fork.
- ch. 35 "21. Buttercup and the Eternal Feminine" **·** Simone de Beauvoir — Woman as the Other; One Is Not Born a Woman; Feminism — Archibald reads Buttercup wholly through Beauvoir's The Second Sex: as the farm's natural leader she is not yet 'woman as Other', but Humperdinck's ultra-feminized public presentation, the arranged marriage, the old woman's 'Queen of Filth' nightmare, and the men dismissing her advice all enact the myth of the eternal feminine that punishes a woman for asserting herself. Her leap toward the shrieking eels becomes an emblem of autonomy that society punishes, the 'privilege' of compliance a curse that leaves her suicidal, and the closing image of the couple riding together as one sketches Beauvoir's ideal of a relationship founded on mutual liberty.
- ch. 36 "22. The Dao of Master Westley" **·** Laozi, Zhuangzi, Confucius — The Tao (the Way); Wu Wei (effortless action); Taoism (Daoism); Socratic Ignorance (knowing that you don't know) — Doll tests each character against the Dao: Confucius's identification of the Dao with tradition and ritual fails because Florinese tradition is exactly what enables Humperdinck's murder and war plans, so she turns to Laozi's De and wu-wei, diagnosing Vizzini's fatal overconfidence (he lacked Socratic knowledge of his own ignorance), Humperdinck's paranoia, and Buttercup's passivity as failures to sense when to act and when to refrain. Westley emerges as Zhuangzi's ideal - like Cook Ting the butcher, mastering skills until instinct suffices: adapting through the Fire Swamp, treating Vizzini's death as 'an appropriate death', yet sparing Inigo and Fezzik because killing them would be against nature, which is why the giant and the Spaniard accept him as their sage.
- ch. 37 "23. Why No One Would Surrender to the Dread Pirate Westley" **·** Thomas Hobbes, Jacques Derrida — Clint Jones argues the Dread Pirate Roberts is Florinese cultural mythology rather than a man: like Billy the Kid or the Cowboy myth, the name carries a symbolic identity that lets Westley reinvent himself ('no one would surrender to the Dread Pirate Westley'), explains why only Buttercup, raised on Florin's stories, recognizes the Man in Black while the well-traveled Inigo, Fezzik, and Vizzini cannot, and why the palace guards panic at Fezzik in the Holocaust Cloak. Humperdinck figures as Hobbes's Leviathan, the embodiment of the state whose war with Guilder would shore up a Florinese identity destabilized by the New World, while Derrida's Politics of Friendship supplies the logic that a stable 'we' requires a clearly marked enemy.

## The Red Sox and Philosophy - Green Monster Meditations

- ch. 1 "section-1" *(not yet read)*
- ch. 2 "The Red Sox and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 3 "Popular Culture and Philosophy Series Editor George A. Reisch" *(front)*
- ch. 4 "Title Page" *(front)*
- ch. 5 "The Red Sox and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 6 "Foreword" *(no index entry yet)* — Red Sox official historian Dick Bresciani traces a New England inheritance of endurance from the 1620 Pilgrims through the 1903 first World Series, the fire sale of stars to the Yankees in the 1920s, and the heartbreaks of 1946, 1967, 1975, 1978 and 1986. He argues that the fan virtues of grit, loyalty and cautious optimism mirror the regional character forged by survival, and reads the 2004 ALCS comeback (Millar's walk, Roberts's steal, Ortiz's 12th-inning homer) as the moment fatalism gave way to faith, vindicated again by the 2007 title. The foreword frames the volume as a book by fans for fans that uses the Red Sox to examine faith, morality and community across the history of ideas.
- ch. 7 "Acknowledgements" *(notes)*
- ch. 8 "The Red Sox and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 9 "From Professor Thom’s to the “Little Professor”" *(no index entry yet)* — Editor Michael Macomber explains that the book grew from watching Red Sox games at Professor Thom's, a Sox bar in Manhattan's East Village, where exiled fans in Yankee territory fused deep game knowledge with intense passion. He argues that Sox fans display the melding of intellectual and emotional lives that philosophers find so tricky, and pays tribute to Dominic DiMaggio, the 'Little Professor,' whose intelligence (as Ted Williams notes in Halberstam's The Teammates) overcame physical shortcomings. DiMaggio's smart, underrated play is offered as the fitting emblem for a book that examines the Red Sox thoughtfully rather than merely emotionally.
- ch. 10 "I - Root Root Root fort Red Sox" *(front)*
- ch. 11 "A New Path" **·** The Meaning of Life; Fate and Foreknowledge — Marcus Giamatti narrates his upbringing as an 'Old School' fan under 'Dame Mutability,' his father's personification of the certainty that the Red Sox would always break New England's heart through Bucky Dent, Mookie Wilson, Calvin Schiraldi and Aaron Boone. He argues that being born a Sox fan is an inescapable duty and lifelong quest whose real payoff is not a pennant but the character, faith and perseverance built by enduring, so that when he lay curled up ready to quit during Game Four of the 2004 ALCS, Millar's walk, Roberts's steal and Mueller's single vindicated the whole method. He ends by vowing to pass this philosophy of survival to his unborn daughter Ophelia in Southern California, applying the one-pitch-at-a-time discipline to acting, music and life.
- ch. 12 "Red Sox Fandom and Internal Harmony" **·** Adam Smith, Epictetus, Aristotle — The Impartial Spectator; The Dichotomy of Control; Stoicism — John McHugh uses his urge to confront Grady Little, who left a spent Pedro Martinez in during Game Seven of the 2003 ALCS, to ask how fans should govern the primal emotions fandom generates. Stoic self-command fails because Epictetus's advice (avoid caring about games entirely, focus only on what you control) would extinguish fandom itself, so McHugh adapts Adam Smith's impartial spectator: the virtuous fan imagines being watched by a sympathetic but emotionally distant fellow fan, settling on a Chicago Cubs fan who gets Bartman the way a Sox fan gets Buckner and Grady. He concludes that we each harbor a team of role-specific inner consciences, and the practical task is harmonizing the Red Sox-fan identity with our other identities rather than suppressing it or being consumed by it.
- ch. 13 "The Authentic Red Sox Fan, or The Importance of Turning off NESN and Finding" **·** Immanuel Kant — Authenticity; Autonomy of the Will — Corey McCall asks what makes a 'true' Red Sox fan, contrasting the beer-soaked bleacher loudmouth with one-hundred-year-old honorary batboy Arthur Giddon, and treats the nostalgia and resentment around the 2004 bandwagon as the Enlightenment problem of authenticity in miniature. Through Kant's 'Sapere aude' and the ideal of autonomy, he argues the authentic fan is the critical fan who uses reason about the team (questioning Francona's moves, judging whole careers) instead of losing herself in uncritical devotion, reading Jason Varitek's 2004 muzzle of A-Rod as the moment the team's identity crystallized for fans who wanted to be like him. With the Curse gone, fans are freed from fated suffering but inherit Kant's burden of autonomously choosing how fandom will define them, since circumstances no longer decide.
- ch. 14 "April 6th, 2009" **·** Plato — Platonic Love (the ladder of love); The Meaning of Life — David Roochnik interlaces a loss diary (his daughter's soccer defeat and the Sox's 1998 playoff elimination on the same Saturday, the 2003 Grady Little collapse that gave him chest pain, the 2004 comeback) to argue that losing is an art that teaches what winning cannot. The loser's 'if only' imagination transforms the past, the 'it was only a game' rationalization forces honest reflection about what matters, and shared doom forges Red Sox Nation into a community in a way no Yankees nation could exist, since victors never need to think. He applies Plato's Symposium doctrine of Eros, that to love X is to lack X as Socrates lacks wisdom, to explain why eighty-six years of championship-lack made fandom meaningful, and closes fearing that winning in 2004 and 2007 made the Sox 'normal,' eroding the art of losing that glued the Nation together.
- ch. 15 "II - Curse Reversed" *(front)*
- ch. 16 "If Only . ." **·** Bernard Williams, Thomas Nagel, Immanuel Kant — Moral Luck — Bryan Pilkington applies Bernard Williams and Thomas Nagel's analysis of moral luck, where factors outside an agent's control change how we evaluate the very same act, to Bill Buckner's Game Six error of 1986, alongside constructed cases of Jon Lester skipping the last page of his scouting sheet and a Fenway Franks truck driver who skips his brake check. Drawing on Nagel on negligence, Williams's notion of agent-regret (only Buckner, not Bob Stanley or Rich Gedman, can feel it), and a standard of reasonable expectation for a veteran first baseman, he concludes Buckner can rightly be blamed for the error and even for losing Game Six, though not for the World Series or for the cruel vilification he received. The chapter thus disciplines the fan's 'if only' blame game: a curse is never a satisfying target of blame, but neither is the situation instead of the person.
- ch. 17 "The End of a Curse" *(no index entry yet)* — Erin Flynn argues that 'the Red Sox are cursed' was never an explanation: it commits the post hoc ergo propter hoc fallacy and an appeal to ignorance, and a curse, to explain anything, would have to be a force over and above the ordinary causes of losing, which no evidence ever identified. Borrowing Nelson Goodman's 'grue' paradox and the new riddle of induction, he coins 'blursed' (cursed-and-blessed relative to Dave Roberts's steal on October 17th, 2004) to show that the same 1920 to 2003 evidence supports the rival description just as well, so the evidence itself never favored 'cursed.' Since curses explain and predict nothing, the choice between descriptions is pragmatic and aesthetic: 'blursed' rightly renders the 1986 choke as the very thing that made the 2004 comeback the singular, bliss-producing event it was, so the Sox turn out never to have been cursed at all.
- ch. 18 "Only an Ortiz Can Save Us Heidegger’s Revelation of Being" **·** Martin Heidegger, Jean-Paul Sartre — The Question Concerning Technology; Thrownness (Geworfenheit); Authenticity — James Pontuso reads Red Sox history through Heidegger: technology that turns the world into 'standing reserve' breeds the anxious 'everydayness' of the mediocre Pinky Higgins teams, while the run of near-misses from 1967 through Bucky Dent in 1978 and Buckner in 1986 threw fans into an authentic encounter with tragedy, mortality and the mystery of Being that the Yankees' free-agent 'technology of power' could never teach. Fenway Park, quirky and continuous with the pre-Hitler past, serves as 'home' in Heidegger's homeless age, and the literati's tragic framing of the team (language as the house of Being) made its fate a matter of existential weight rather than mere sport. David Ortiz's 2004 ALCS heroics then function as Heidegger's longed-for revelation of Being, the answer to 'only a god can save us,' after which fans have lived through all three states: everydayness, tragic anxiety, and wonder.
- ch. 19 "Forgiveness and the Fenway Faithful" **·** Aristotle — Virtue Ethics (arete); Forgiveness; Eudaimonia (flourishing); The Golden Mean — Rory Kraft uses Aristotle's virtue ethics to explain why Red Sox Nation forgave Tim Wakefield (whose mistake Aaron Boone turned into the 2003 ALCS-ending homer) and Ted Williams, but scapegoated Bill Buckner for 'the error' and eventually cast off Manny Ramirez. Because Aristotle judges a complete life, one swallow does not make a summer, Buckner's .991 fielding percentage and 2,715 hits should contextualize his lapse, yet some acts are bad without degrees like Aristotle's adultery, theft and murder, and a World Series-deciding error crossed that line. Kraft distinguishes Aristotle's categories, Wakefield's mistake (ignorance of Boone's changed plan) versus Buckner's misadventure (contrary to reasonable expectation) versus Manny's voluntary injustices, concluding that learning when to forgive players who are family taught the Nation what forgiveness itself means, sealed by Buckner's 2008 standing ovation.
- ch. 20 "III - Fenway Faithful" *(front)*
- ch. 21 "Do You Believe It" **·** William James, David Hume — The Will to Believe; Faith and Reason — Patrick Tiernan argues that Fenway fandom is a genuine religion: William James's four marks of mystical experience (ineffability, noetic quality, transiency, passivity) map onto memories of Buckner's error and Dave Roberts's steal, and Peter Berger's 'signals of transcendence' structure the rituals of Truck Day, the hopes of spring training, the damnation of Bucky Dent and Johnny Damon, and the humor of 'Cowboy Up.' He roots the Curse of the Bambino in New England's Puritan-Calvinist legacy of persecution and original sin (Frazee's sale of Ruth), then deploys Hume's account of superstition from 'Of Superstition and Enthusiasm' to deflate the exorcism rituals fans actually performed, from a Sox hat on Everest to dredging for Ruth's piano, as post hoc fallacies. Even so, the rituals reveal the human need for religious practice, and Maslow's peak experience renders the 2004 comeback a brush with the sacred, so the Faithful believe because they have to.
- ch. 22 "The Lesson" **·** Søren Kierkegaard — The Leap of Faith; The Absurd — Karolina Lewestam and Orla Richardson argue that the Red Sox fan is a moral hero in Kierkegaard's sense: like Abraham in Fear and Trembling, the fan fully accepts that loss is inevitable (the Curse, Aaron Boone, Buckner's grounder) yet refuses the rational course of infinite resignation and keeps believing 'this could be the year.' Where liberal individualism licenses walking away from any loyalty and the modern reflex is to sue luck out of existence, the fan lives at the mercy of fate and thereby enacts Kierkegaard's movement beyond resignation into true faith, the absurd belief against all evidence that Isaac will be spared. Fenway thus becomes the best university for learning to live fully under uncertainty, with Ortiz's 'keep the faith' speech and Schilling's bloody sock as its lessons in perseverance and courage.
- ch. 23 "One with the Team" **·** Confucius, René Descartes — Ren (benevolence/humaneness); Xiao (filial piety); The Cogito — Chelsea Harry contends that Red Sox Nation embodies a Confucian rather than Cartesian self: against Descartes's cogito and the egocentric, endorsement-chasing player (A-Rod, the traded Manny), the Sox way treats selfhood as constituted by family, community and mutual sacrifice, from Mike Lowell's hometown discount to Yaz's hospital escape and Schilling's bloody sock. She maps Confucian benevolence (jen) onto Wakefield, Varitek and Ortiz describing how fan love powers their play, filial piety onto the retired numbers honoring franchise elders like Pesky, Doerr and Cronin, and Chung-ying Cheng's 'naturality' onto the fans' claim to be the team's twenty-sixth player, so Johnny Damon's defection reads as the treachery of an ungrateful villager. The 'je ne sais quoi' of team spirit is thus Confucian unity: becoming oneself by subordinating the I to the We.
- ch. 24 "Breaking the Eighth Commandment" **·** Augustine of Hippo, Thomas Aquinas — Faith and Reason — Joel Cade maps baseball's traditionalist-versus-sabermetric war onto the faith-and-reason debate: Joe Morgan's refusal to even read Moneyball repeats Tertullian's fideism, Ted Williams's Science of Hitting exemplifies Augustine's 'faith seeking understanding,' and Wade Boggs's game-day chicken ritual completes his rational hitting exactly as Aquinas says faith completes reason. Theo Epstein's 2004 front office fused the models under the motto 'understanding justifies faith,' using statistics to discover when supposedly obsolete traditional weapons like the stolen base actually work, which is why the seemingly anti-Moneyball Dave Roberts trade paid off in The Steal against Mariano Rivera in Game Four of the ALCS. The chapter closes with the joke that Augustine, who stole pears, would bless stealing second: reconciling faith and reason is how the Sox reversed the Curse, and maybe how faith- and reason-guided people might reconcile.
- ch. 25 "Red Sox Future" **·** G.W.F. Hegel, Parmenides, Immanuel Kant, Blaise Pascal — The Problem of Evil; Fate and Foreknowledge; History Has a Direction; The Meaning of Life — Peter Kreeft argues that the classic pre-2004 Red Sox fan's philosophy is 'Primitive Mysticism' across all eight divisions of philosophy: certainty comes by mystical intuition (fans 'just know' Murphy's Law will strike, as with Dent in 1978 and Buckner in 1986), Parmenides's identity puzzles frame the post-championship identity crisis, free will only chooses among fated tortures, and the greatest good is humiliating the Yankees in Manichean fashion. He opposes Hegel's rational philosophy of history ('the real is the rational') with a history written by 'an insane, drunken, demon-possessed leprechaun,' and inverts the problem of evil: eighty-six years of systematically improbable suffering is probable evidence for a God whose design is a test of faith rather than a refutation of Him. The 2004 title's new rational-optimist 'Enlightenment' identity now threatens to erase the lovable old mysticism, leaving the fan unsure whether to mourn or rejoice.
- ch. 26 "IV - The Rivalry" *(front)*
- ch. 27 "The Will to Nothingness, or What Happens when the Red Sox Win" **·** Friedrich Nietzsche — Master and Slave Morality; Ressentiment — Nolen Gertz runs the Sox-Yankees rivalry through Nietzsche's On the Genealogy of Morals: the Steinbrenner Yankees are masters whose 'good' simply means strong and winning, while Sox fans are slaves whose 'Yankees suck!' is ressentiment's imaginary revenge, intensified into the most spiritual and poisonous hatred by eighty-six years of impotence. The priestly slaves defeated the masters by cleverness rather than force, mirrored when Epstein's sabermetric 'religion' of prospects and projections terrified the Yankees into converting, building a farm system and embracing the slavish virtues they had scorned. The final twist is Nietzsche's will to nothingness: once the masters fell in 2004 the fans' suffering lost its meaning, breeding bandwagon guilt, pink-hat resentment and nihilistic exhaustion, so freedom requires a transvaluation that lets fans simply enjoy watching baseball.
- ch. 28 "A (Bronx) Cheer to the Red Sox “Without Whom, Not as Much”" **·** Friedrich Nietzsche, Arthur Schopenhauer — Will to Power; Philosophical Pessimism; The World as Will; The Meaning of Life — Writing as a Yankees fan, Konig asks what makes a championship worth wanting: after Schopenhauer's picture of endless striving as suffering, he brings in Richard Taylor (meaning lies in the activity itself, never its completion) and then Nietzsche's will to power, which seeks worthy resistance to overcome. Thought experiments—a childhood 'big game' that turns out to be tee-ball, and the 2004 Sox trouncing the minor-league Atlantic League—show a title without worthy opposition is hollow. Hence the Yankees' twentieth-century dominance would mean nothing without the Red Sox as the sustained, worthy enemy (Bucky Dent, the Pedro-Zimmer melee, the 2004 ALCS collapse included).
- ch. 29 "What Makes the Red Sox Great Is More than the Team" **·** Anselm of Canterbury — The Ontological Argument — Ulatowski argues the 'We Believe' motto is religious: Red Sox fandom is the a priori conviction that Boston is the sports city 'than which none greater can be conceived,' transplanting Anselm's ontological argument—necessary, contingent and impossible objects, existence as a perfection—to the Red Sox via Oliver Wendell Holmes's 'hub of the universe.' He rejects the parallel New York argument because it smuggles a posteriori championship counts into an a priori proof, and answers the fallacy-of-division worry by making Boston's greatness and the Red Sox's greatness an identity rather than a whole-to-part inference.
- ch. 30 "Are These World Series Champions Really Our Red Sox" **·** Søren Kierkegaard, Jean-Paul Sartre, Arthur Schopenhauer, Albert Camus, Bertrand Russell — Bad Faith; The Leap of Faith; The Myth of Sisyphus; The World as Will — Lee casts pre-2004 fandom as a Kierkegaardian leap of faith ('We Believe') and the Curse of the Bambino as Schopenhauer's irrational deterministic will, with the 1918–2004 cycle of spring hope and October collapse as Camus's Sisyphus. But he argues fans are really existentialists: blaming losses on fate or the Curse is Sartrean bad faith that evades responsibility, since fans did hold Bill Buckner (1986 error) and Grady Little (leaving a spent Pedro in during 2003 ALCS Game 7) individually accountable. The 2004 and 2007 champions remain 'our' Red Sox because we freely choose to care about the team's journey, not because it has a fixed essence; a coda borrows Russell's point about ambiguous reference for the word 'Yankee,' and Andy Wasif's appendix runs Yankees-as-evil through relativism, objectivism and subjectivism.
- ch. 31 "V - Views from the Grandstands" *(front)*
- ch. 32 "Missing Numbers" **·** Immanuel Kant, Theodor Adorno — Transcendental Idealism — Macomber explains fan judgment through Kant's determinate judgment—subsuming sensuous particulars (white sphere, red stitches) under the concept 'baseball'—then warns, with Horkheimer and Adorno's 'disenchantment of the world,' that reducing careers to single concepts (Buckner = 'error,' Dave Roberts = 'The Steal,' Damon = 'traitor') negates everything else a player did, which is why Damon is booed at Fenway while Nomar received a standing ovation. Retired numbers like Jim Rice's 14 invert the process: the harshest reduction becomes an invitation that demands the player's story be told, magnifying rather than erasing him.
- ch. 33 "Tie Goes to the Runner" **·** Aristotle — The Golden Mean; Virtue Ethics (arete); Practical Wisdom (phronesis) — St. Martin runs the Fenway 'pink hat' controversy through Aristotle's Nicomachean Ethics: the virtuous fan is the mean between the deficient bandwagoner who waves at NESN cameras mid-pitch and the excessive diehard who memorizes Dice-K's ERA to belittle others. Terry Francona's courage in sending Dave Roberts in the 2004 ALCS (versus a reckless Ortiz steal attempt) illustrates virtue as the mean between cowardice and foolhardiness, and Aristotle's contrast between the love of honor and the love of contemplation shows both pink hatters and stats-obsessed diehards err by making fandom about themselves. She concludes that accepting fans in any color hat is the truly virtuous mean.
- ch. 34 "Evidence Rewarded" **·** W.V.O. Quine — The Web of Belief — Goldwater reads Bill James's sabermetric revolution through Quine: science is a continuation of common sense, but mature science replaces the 'animal sense of similarity' (being a Yankee, batting average, RBI) with predictive, league- and park-adjusted categories, turning atomistic box-score tallies into holistic statistics that acquire meaning only from the whole web of data. Because science recognizes only repeatable types, it cannot countenance miracles—Carlton Fisk's 1975 homer greeted by the 'Hallelujah Chorus,' Dave Roberts's steal in 2004—whose meaning lies in happening exactly once. His resolution: GMs like John Henry and Theo Epstein need Quinean prediction, while fans keep the religious language of faith, hope, and redemption.
- ch. 35 "Tearing Down Fenway Now That Would Be Offensive" **·** John Rawls — Justice as Fairness — Mathis argues fans' contempt for ticket resellers is not about being overcharged, since resellers only charge what Fenway's scarcity market will bear. Following Rawls's A Theory of Justice, he treats access to Red Sox tickets as a public good deliberately sheltered from the free market by the random online waiting room, the five-year season-ticket list, baseball's antitrust exemption, and the 1924 Massachusetts anti-scalping law—all of which promote egalitarian access and a sense of community. Reselling offends because it exploits for private profit a good we collectively protect from market forces, as tearing down Fenway itself would.
- ch. 36 "November" **·** Pythagoras — Pythagorean Harmony of the Spheres — Auxier's rhapsodic essay channels Bart Giamatti ('the Philosopher') on baseball as sacred leisure and uses Pythagoras—everything is Number, each thing's essence an unnamed ratio—to argue the sacred geometry of Fenway cannot be reduced to the dimensions of mere space. He casts the scorecard-keeping quantifiers (Steinbrenner, the Mathematikoi) against the meaning-seeking true believers (Giamatti, the Akousmatikoi), and claims the Yankees' demolition of their own temple, not the 2004 comeback, is what truly lifted the Curse of the Bambino and guarantees Boston will never tear down Fenway. It ends with circular time: the season, like the Babe finishing his career in Boston, always comes home.
- ch. 37 "VI - So Good! So Good! So Good!" *(front)*
- ch. 38 "Teddy Baseball" **·** Zhuangzi — Taoism (Daoism); Wu Wei (effortless action); The Tao (the Way) — Lee reads Ted Williams's The Science of Hitting as a Daoist skill story from the Zhuangzi: 'get a good ball to hit' matches the cicada-catcher's total absorption in the cicada's wings, hitting 'by feel' like the carpenter's hammer matches Cook Ding carving without looking, and Williams's midnight batting practice after Korea matches the wheelwright grown old at his craft—effortless spontaneity produced by disciplined practice and unified attention, not mere mechanics. Williams's 'proper thinking' is Daoist sorting of objective conditions rather than abstract disputation. The closing tale of the old woman who cannot teach the Way to someone lacking the stuff explains why Williams failed as Senators manager: his players did not have the native talent to receive it.
- ch. 39 "Manny" **·** Aristotle, Jean-Paul Sartre, Immanuel Kant, Albert Camus — The Categorical Imperative; The Golden Mean; Authenticity; Existence Precedes Essence — Santaniello reads five nonconformist Red Sox through ethical theory. Ruth's sale to New York becomes a Kantian case: he tore up his signed three-year contract and demanded a trade while expecting others to keep promises, violating the categorical imperative on promise-keeping. Ted Williams's lifelong war with the 'Knights of the Keyboard' exemplifies existentialist authenticity ('It just wouldn't have been me' tipping his cap), Spaceman Bill Lee embodies Sartre's condemned freedom and existence precedes essence, Jim Rice's Hall of Fame delay is Aristotle's failure to hit the mean between silent deficiency on Boston's racism and excess toward reporters, and Manny's 'Gas is up and so am I' money grab shows Aristotle's point that wealth is an incomplete end pursued for the sake of something else.
- ch. 40 "Lester’s Duties and Capabilities" **·** Immanuel Kant — Deontology; Humanity as an End in Itself — Maguire asks whether the Royals did wrong by trying to hit in the late innings of Jon Lester's May 2008 no-hitter, working through Kant's distinction between perfect duties (always binding—playing hard, honoring the codified rules) and imperfect duties (praiseworthy obligations that admit exceptions—respecting the unwritten convention against bunting for a hit in a blowout). Sympathy for Lester's cancer recovery must not enter the calculation, since Jim Abbott's 1993 no-hitter shows the duty must universalize; bunting would be 'bush league' rather than wrong, a lapse in imperfect duty. He extends the analysis to Francona's pitch counts and warns that wanting Ellsbury to bunt against a Yankees no-hitter would treat the pitcher as a mere means to fan pride.
- ch. 41 "Where in the World(s) Are We" **·** David Lewis — Possible Worlds; Ship of Theseus — Ablondi asks whether fans' 'if only' claims—'If Bob Stanley had struck out Mookie Wilson, the Sox would have won in 1986,' 'if Pesky had hurried his relay in 1946'—can be true when they lack truthmakers in the actual world. He presents David Lewis's modal realism and counterpart theory as the standard solution, then objects: a Fisk counterpart with a different body, birthday and nickname, or a Red Sox made of 1986 Mets counterparts, is not recognizably Fisk or the Red Sox, and nobody cares about a Lonborg counterpart who won Game Seven. He concludes counterfactuals need some other explanation and that we should savor the actual Foulke-to-Mientkiewicz final out of 2004 instead.
- ch. 42 "The Boston Red Sox All-Philosophers Team" *(front)*
- ch. 43 "The Starting Lineup" *(notes)*
- ch. 44 "Stats" *(notes)*
- ch. 45 "The Red Sox and Philosophy" *(notes)*
- ch. 46 "Copyright Page" *(front)*

## The Rolling Stones and Philosophy - It's Just a Thought Away

- ch. 1 "Cover" *(not yet read)*
- ch. 2 "Title Page" *(not yet read)*
- ch. 3 "The Rolling Stones and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 4 "Popular Culture and Philosophy Series Editor George A. Reisch" *(front)*
- ch. 5 "It’s Only Philosophy but We Like It" **·** The Meaning of Life — George A. Reisch's introduction argues that Keith Richards' autobiography Life is more philosophical than Keith's own slogan that rock and roll 'starts from the neck down' allows: its optimistic framing of addiction, arrests, and band feuds displays a distinctive perspective and mental discipline rather than a tale of greed and jealousy. He reads the band's 1990 Prague concert, where Czech fans outlasted Soviet occupation, as evidence that the Stones' music carried a liberating outlook that helped defeat communism or at least outlasted it. The piece sets up the volume's premise that a band proud of its anti-intellectual image nonetheless repays philosophical reading.
- ch. 6 "The Rolling Stones and Philosophy" *(no index entry yet)* — Reisch continues the introduction by portraying Keith's devotion to music as a quasi-mystical, life-sustaining focus: his descriptions of guitars weaving with Ronnie Wood's read like a mystic struggling for words, and he would keep playing even without sex, cigarettes, or drink. The section argues the Stones' fifty-year endurance turned them from a youth band into an institution, a rock to lean on, noting that 'Lady Jane' had already punked the older generation and Henry VIII's wives long before the Sex Pistols. Their sheer longevity, surviving Brian Jones's death, Altamont, and heroin, is offered as a fact that should make the philosopher in all of us sit up and take notice.
- ch. 7 "The Rolling Stones and Philosophy" **·** Socrates — Reisch recalls Jagger's 1969 reply that he was 'financially dissatisfied, sexually satisfied, and philosophically trying' to argue that the search for satisfaction is a life-long quest rather than a hit single, pointing to the politics of 'Street Fighting Man' and 'Sweet Neo Con', the existentialism of 'Paint it, Black', and 'Sympathy for the Devil' as a dead-serious challenge to the Christian metaphysics of evil. He then compares the band's 1990 Prague concert to Socrates condemned to death for teaching unpopular ideas: the Stones and their fans, like Socrates, outlasted the regime that suppressed them. Eduard Freisler's sobbing father, told by no one after that night how to think, supplies the chapter's closing satisfaction at last.
- ch. 8 "I - One Thought (to the Body)" *(front)*
- ch. 9 "Chapter 1 - The Glimmer Twins" **·** Fate and Foreknowledge — Randall E. Auxier opens his chapter on the Jagger-Richards collaboration by musing that certain pairs seem 'ordained by the gods' to stay entangled for life, calling Mick and Keith 'destiny's children' who grew up a Stone's throw apart in Dartford. He admits he cannot quite believe in destiny yet claims to have seen cosmic entanglement first hand, and he poses the counterfactual of the two boys taking different buses on the day their collaboration began — a world with no Rolling Stones. The section frames the chapter's question of whether their life-long creative entanglement was inevitable or, as he punningly insists, evitable.
- ch. 10 "Pretty Pairs" **·** Philosophy of Love — Auxier ranks the Glimmer Twins against other songwriting pairs — Lennon-McCartney as a corporation rather than a cooperation, the Gershwins and John-Taupin as split lyricist-composer teams, Page-Plant and Waters-Gilmour as fellow full collaborations — and argues Mick and Keith matter most philosophically because they 'actually always loved each other', like twin sons of different mothers and a bickering married couple. Citing Christopher Andersen's love-triangle story of the Edith Grove flat, he claims the creative process is inseparable from eros in the ancient sense, with jealousy and envy as its close companions. Two huge personalities locked in one room without blowing up the house is what makes their collaboration so philosophically interesting.
- ch. 11 "Picking a Fight" *(no index entry yet)* — Auxier 'picks a fight' with work-centered philosophy of art, arguing there is no single thing that is 'the' work of art because artworks are living processes tied to their origins, creators, and shifting contexts. 'Street Fighting Man' meant one thing in 1968 and another now, Keith's X-Pensive Winos performances made 'Gimme Shelter' something it previously was not, and Aretha rather than Carole King completed 'A Natural Woman'. He opposes philosophers who study artworks-as-things to those, like himself, who study the creative process, contending that some people — Mick and Keith — can achieve genius only together.
- ch. 12 "Only Rock’n’Roll" *(no index entry yet)* — The section enlists Susanne Langer's demand that any philosopher of art first try to make art and take working artists as teachers, since artists speak a metaphorical language that resists translation into literal philosophical vocabulary. Auxier then reads Keith's tangled remarks on rhythm — the body's seventy-two beats per minute, the rhythm of trains, Elvis's drumless 'Mystery Train', why the music is about roll rather than rock — as exactly the plastic, powerful, metaphorical speech Langer predicted. The methodological moral is that any account of the Stones' creativity must begin from what Mick and Keith say and show about what they do.
- ch. 13 "Genius" **·** Immanuel Kant — Judgment of Taste and the Sublime — Auxier traces the modern habit of calling individuals 'a genius' to Kant's Critique of Judgment, quoting the definition of genius as the innate talent through which nature gives the rule to art and noting Kant's view that geniuses cannot explain their own powers. He applies the concept to the Glimmer Twins, suggesting that the songs, shows, and videos are better called works of genius than either man taken alone. His amendment to Kant is that genius sometimes operates only when two people work together, which returns the discussion to what Mick and Keith achieve jointly.
- ch. 14 "Let It Bleed" **·** G.W.F.
