Hegel — The Dialectic; Geist (World Spirit) — Reconstructing the twins' songwriting mechanics — Keith re-voicing Mick's 'Brown Sugar' in open-G tuning, the producer Don Was shuttling between them during Bridges to Babylon as Keith sang wordless fragments that Mick turned into lyrics — Auxier argues their genius lives outside either man alone, in sparks between two interdependent, mutually wounded subjectivities. He names their destructive-yet-creative bond a classic case of what philosophers call dialectic, citing Hegel's view that cultural history is made through strife and that a Zeitgeist moves history with or without the cooperation of individuals. The Stones surfed the 1960s Zeitgeist all the way into the twenty-first century, and their willingness to 'let it bleed' kept the collaboration generative after they stopped being good mates around 1968.
- ch. 15 "Fifteen Minutes of Fame" **·** G.W.F. Hegel — History Has a Direction; The Dialectic — Auxier argues the twins were serious artists with a sense of the history they were living — true students and initiates of the blues whose aesthetic and spiritual value they recognized before Americans did. He mobilizes Hegel's claim that art gives sensuous appearance to the movement of history: Keith's guitar sound and Mick's stage strut are the clothes history wears between its stages, with geniuses hitting targets no one else sees, ahead of the slower-moving innards of politics and religion. The Stones used their fame to hoist the blues, the bottom rung of the cultural ladder, toward the center just as pop art and the beat poets lowered the wall between high and low, leaving high culture itself begging at the banquet.
- ch. 16 "Art for Art’s Sake" *(no index entry yet)* — Auxier defines aestheticism — people whose moral and even religious values are drawn from the aesthetic realm — and casts the pure bluesman (Hank Williams, Townes Van Zandt) and the Stones as aesthetes arriving in a decade when high and low culture were in dialectical tango. Through Andy Warhol and Robert Fraser he argues a postmodern role of 'the artist' as celebrated performer emerged, pushing the idea of genius to its limit: no longer does nature give the rule to art, but the genius-subject tells both nature and culture what art is. Mick took the Warhol-esque role of serial persona reinvention while Keith remained the grounded serious musician, each brilliant in support of the other.
- ch. 17 "Sweet Home Chicago" *(no index entry yet)* — Auxier argues the twins' genius required schooling by masters — Muddy Waters, Howlin' Wolf, Willie Dixon, Little Walter at Chess — and that Romanticism's decline after Byron, Keats, and Shelley shows what happens when artists think being cool replaces classical training, which is also why Nickelback sucks. He describes the Stones as having professed America's own music back to it before ethnomusicology existed, citing Joe Walsh's debt to them, and explains why fans like himself long could not hear the connection between the Stones' English-sounding records and the Chicago and Delta blues. Their relation to Chicago blues, he suggests, is that of Impressionist painting to Post-Impressionism.
- ch. 18 "Doomed to Repeat It" **·** History Has a Direction — Auxier argues the Glimmer Twins' genius cannot be separated from its historical setting: not that history made them, but that their genius depended on getting history right and being just ahead of the spirit of the times. He predicts future historians of art will remember the Stones as serious artists who created something of lasting aesthetic value that had not existed before — mentioned second after the Beatles like Mozart after Beethoven, both of whom were rock stars who inspired riots by breaking rules. He closes by claiming the Jagger-Richards team is the most important collaboration of any kind in the period, and the one raising the most philosophical questions.
- ch. 19 "In The Shadows" **·** G.W.F. Hegel — Geist (World Spirit) — Auxier poses the puzzle that Mick and Keith give thoroughly contradictory accounts of their own collaboration, and asks how reliable anyone can be as an interpreter of their own creative experience. He sets Hegel against Susanne Langer as irreconcilable alternatives: for Hegel artists give sensuous expression to the objective spirit moving in history without understanding what they do, while Langer insists artists do understand but speak an untranslatable, discursively untrained language. The section ends with Mick's long shadow — the prancing frontman — having hidden Keith, the brooding semi-animal, from recognition as a creative force whose enigma now demands a theory.
- ch. 20 "Saviors of Rock’n’Roll" *(no index entry yet)* — Auxier describes how Keith emerged from Mick's shadow: Johnny Depp's modeling of Jack Sparrow on Keith revealed the pirate persona the public had always responded to, and Life surprised readers with how insightful, even intellectual, its author is. He argues the pair's public roles were interdependent — Mick the court jester smarter than the court, Keith with a 'talent for suffering' that kept the saviors of rock'n'roll real — so that together they could both get behind the gilded doors and rip the joint. His roommate's Steel Wheels crew report that the band is 'all business' leads him to conclude that the Stones' mystique, like the varnish on a Stradivarius, is itself part of the artwork: art is about creating illusions, and disguising the mundane business is as much a part of it as the finish on a violin.
- ch. 21 "The Songs" **·** The Dialectic — Auxier argues that amid the manufactured illusions the songs are what is real: they are the primary illusions symbolizing how it feels to live in our times, and the appearance of the Jagger-Richards credit from Aftermath onward made the Stones creative forces rather than mere personas. Using Keith's Life account of writing Exile on Main Street on the fly — make the bare bones of a riff, snap the drums in, see what happens — he locates the creative secret in the tension of the dialectic between Mick and Keith, which builds until Charlie Watts kicks in. The genius finally lies beyond the twins in the group: they are the left and right ventricles of the heart of rock'n'roll, but the body that dances is the band.
- ch. 22 "Chapter 2 - My Dinner with Mick" *(no index entry yet)* — Jere Surber recounts an after-concert dinner in 1980s Denver where Mick, contradicting the bad-boy image, was a reserved, witty, London School of Economics-educated conversationalist genuinely interested in philosophy, with a tired Keith polite at the end of the table. The episode leaves him puzzling over the different Jaggers — dinner companion, rooster-like frontman, revered rock businessman — and over how a 'nice bloke' could be at home in a rock'n'roll culture so often black, blue, and bloody. The anecdote sets the chapter's guiding problem: the violence that, through Altamont, attached itself to the Stones' name.
- ch. 23 "The Death of the Woodstock Nation" *(no index entry yet)* — Surber reconstructs the Altamont Speedway festival of December 6th, 1969: the twice-moved venue lacking basic amenities, Hell's Angels paid $500 of beer for stage security over Jerry Garcia's protest, the Grateful Dead refusing to play, and four deaths, culminating in eighteen-year-old Meredith Hunter being beaten and stabbed by Angels after pulling a gun during 'Under My Thumb' while the unaware band kept playing. He notes the Stones were not fairly to blame — the bad decisions were management's — yet the popular imagination, fed by Don McLean's 'American Pie' and Rolling Stone, cast them as devil-worshippers presiding over an orgy of violence. The section records how Altamont, four months after Woodstock, killed the credo of Peace and Love.
- ch. 24 "Before and After Altamont" *(no index entry yet)* — Surber argues rock enjoyed an innocence about physical violence before Altamont: even as Vietnam, the Civil Rights Movement, and assassinations escalated, Woodstock's 'back to the Garden' self-image cast rock as intrinsically peaceful and countercultural figures like the Hell's Angels as harmless 'barbarian brothers'. Altamont shattered that naive self-image by showing that the Counterculture harbored in its own midst the same potentials for violence it had projected onto the 'violent other' it rejected. After that revelation the door opened for rock to realize its own implication in violent institutions and to adopt increasingly violent themes, with punk, death metal, racist Southern rock, and gangsta rap among the offspring ultimately spawned at Altamont.
- ch. 25 "Violence and Philosophy" **·** Plato — Surber surveys three classic accounts of violence — Plato's view that violence results when reason fails to control passion, an older line through Homer, Goethe, Hegel, and Nietzsche treating violence as fundamental to the universe and history, and the Enlightenment hope (Locke's 'inconvenience') of legislating and educating it away — and finds all of them too naive for Altamont. He then introduces Žižek's book Violence and its Lacanian frame of three orders: the private Imaginary of images and memories (his dinner with Mick), the public Symbolic of codes that built 'Mick Jagger the Rock Star', and the unsayable Real encountered at traumatic ruptures like 9/11. For the Marxist Žižek the Real is chiefly economic — the global machinery of record companies that alone kept Jagger from retiring to a middle-class London suburb.
- ch. 26 "Violence, Rock, and the Rolling Stones" **·** Objectification — Applying the three orders, Surber sorts the Stones' violences: Imaginary violence flows from frustrated desire — 'you can't ever get what you want' — heard in 'Play with Fire' and the unnervingly threatening last line of 'Dead Flowers', and in the frenzied fan mobs that nearly killed Keith once Decca made him a spectacle. Symbolic violence is carried by words and images, and he charges the lyrics with two standing varieties: the objectification of women in 'Under My Thumb' and racial stereotyping in 'Brown Sugar' and the N-word quoted in 'Sweet Black Angel'. Behind both, the Real's economics quietly enables the whole scene, since concert violence is unintelligible apart from the transfer of millions of fans' small cash payments into record-corporation accounts.
- ch. 27 "So, Back to Altamont . ." *(no index entry yet)* — Surber reads Altamont as an overlay of all the violences at once: economic desires staged the fiasco (the Stones' free-concert countercultural prestige, speedway owner Dick Carter's publicity grab, the film-rights dispute that pushed the venue from Sears Point), three hundred thousand frustrated fans were compressed down a hill into the Angels, and symbolic sparks — the toppled motorcycle, the racial symbolism around the black concertgoer Meredith Hunter — inflamed the night. On the Žižek-Lacan framework there is no single culprit, which dissolves the puzzle of how two polite dinner companions could headline the concert that killed the Sixties. His conclusion turns the accusation around: the Stones were high priests of excessive desire 'playin' with fire', we liked them that way, and who really killed the Sixties may just be you and me.
- ch. 28 "Chapter 3 - The Beggar’s Genius" *(no index entry yet)* — Keegan Goodman opens with a close reading of 'Parachute Woman' from Beggars Banquet: a cassette-recorded, double-tracked, deliberately rough track whose indecipherable mumble and stock blues riff do nothing but 'lay a solid rhythm down'. He uses the song against the popular view that art works by adding something new to its tradition, arguing the track is original only in a superficial sense — nothing in its chords, harmonica, or parachute-woman imagery diverges from its blues lineage, and he likes it precisely because it does not even try to add anything new. The Beatles, with their harmonic surprises, submarines, and fields of fruit, serve as the anti-Stones foil.
- ch. 29 "As I Watch You Leaving Me, You Pack My Peace of Mind" *(no index entry yet)* — Goodman reads 'No Expectations' — Brian Jones's masterly slide-guitar farewell, its water-splashes-on-stone image eerily prescient of his drowning in a pool a year later — as a meditation on how repeating the past is never simple: 'our love is like our music: it's here and then it's gone'. He recounts how the Stones had to abandon their expectation of remaining a covers band, with Lennon and McCartney supplying the middle eight of 'I Wanna Be Your Man' and Andrew Oldham locking Mick and Keith in a kitchen until they wrote a song, concluding that history rewards those who do not expect to pass through again. Yet he argues the song also exposes the dominant view of art as meaningful variation on tradition, because it leaves us with no criterion for when a slight alteration — Jagger singing Otis Redding's lines — counts as genuinely new rather than imitation.
- ch. 30 "What’s Puzzling You Is the Nature of My Game" **·** Immanuel Kant — Judgment of Taste and the Sublime — Goodman turns to Kant's Critique of Judgment to solve the problem of how rock'n'roll can be genuinely new yet intelligible: a work must be made according to rules that give it context — tuning-noise with a song's notes is not a song — yet a work following the rules too well, like the cover bands in Keith's 'shitty little suits', is no good. Kant's genius resolves this: nature gives the rule to art through the artist's inborn productive faculty, which is why artists say 'the thing just wrote itself' and cannot describe scientifically how their products come to be, unlike engineers or businessmen whose creativity is fully determined by an articulable plan. The section sets this machinery up to explain what could make a Stones song new.
- ch. 31 "The Beggars at the Banquet" **·** Immanuel Kant — Judgment of Taste and the Sublime — Goodman presses Kant's theory against the obvious objection: the Stones were decidedly unoriginal, 'beggars at the banquet table of music history' who stole songs, singing styles, lyrics, and licks. His solution is that they altered the aesthetic object itself by making their bodies — Mick's maniacal dancing, Keith's slouch, Bill's statuesque motionlessness — a constitutive part of the music, so that 'Brown Sugar' and 'Jumpin' Jack Flash' summon the band's persons in a way 'Strawberry Fields Forever' never summons Lennon. Because the Stones know sincerity is the real enemy of rock'n'roll, Mick cannot get satisfaction while singing that he can't get no satisfaction: the work now includes the singer, a philosophical necessity rather than a psychological claim.
- ch. 32 "What Can a Poor Boy Do" *(no index entry yet)* — Goodman closes his chapter with the 1965 Ready, Steady, Go! footage: when Keith strikes the first note of '(I Can't Get No) Satisfaction', a visible change comes over both the swooning studio audience and the band, whose usual ironic distance suddenly has to be overcome. He argues the closing credits superimposed over Jagger's face and the transfixed crowd broke the viewer's link to the frenzy and returned each teenager to their own humdrum black-and-white living room, dramatizing that the closeness everyone wanted was unreachable. That unbridgeable gap, he suggests, is both why we love the Stones and what puts them out of our reach.
- ch. 33 "Chapter 4 - You Probably Think This Chapter’s about You" *(no index entry yet)* — Charles Taliaferro and Therese Cotter open by asking when a person is vain, offering Jagger's self-promoted persona — sexed-up, macho-chauvinistic, street-fighting, Satanic, wanting a girl under his sticky thumb with cameras rolling — as the test case. They assemble the evidence of his egomania reputation: Sonny Barger's account of the Stones sitting in trailers like prima donnas while the Altamont crowd grew angry, Don McLean's image of Satan 'laughing with delight' as the flames climbed, and Mike Doughty watching Jagger sprint across Shea Stadium for no reason but to say look at me. The section ends with Carly Simon's 'You're So Vain', whose chorus Jagger sings even though Simon keeps the real target, apparently Warren Beatty, secret.
- ch. 34 "Two Jaggers" **·** Friedrich Nietzsche — Reads the contrast between Jagger's shy private self and his stage persona as 'two Jaggers,' pointing to Gimme Shelter, where the strutting performer turns businesslike ('Whose war are we fighting, people?') and visibly stricken while reviewing the Meredith Hunter footage with Charlie Watts. The chapter recruits Nietzsche to explain the split: Jagger, the ex-LSE student who called the band 'my business,' has treated his own life as a work of art, becoming artist and artwork at once.
- ch. 35 "A Little Help from Nietzsche" **·** Friedrich Nietzsche — Deploys Nietzsche's aesthetics—the Twilight of the Idols claim that the meaning of art is life itself, and the Birth of Tragedy line that existence is justified 'only as an aesthetic phenomenon'—to argue Jagger's self-invention is philosophy in action. It answers the puzzle of how one steps back from oneself to see an artwork: by creating the second, stage Jagger, the ordinary suburban guy imposes form on himself with Nietzsche's 'artists' cruelty' and becomes something crowds travel to watch.
- ch. 36 "You’re So Not Vain" **·** Friedrich Nietzsche — Takes up the objection that living aesthetically is vanity (Carly Simon's 'one eye in the mirror') and answers with Jagger's 1969 Hyde Park eulogy for Brian Jones, where he read Shelley's Adonais—'Die, if thou wouldst be that which thou dost seek!' The chapter argues that self-ravishment creates glory energizing others, so a life made sublime in the Nietzsche-Shelley manner is wild exuberance rather than self-absorbed vanity.
- ch. 37 "II - A Rolling Stone Gathers No Illusions" *(front)*
- ch. 38 "Chapter 5 - The Head and the Groin of Rock" *(no index entry yet)* — Part 5 opener built on Keith Richards's quip that rock'n'roll 'starts from the neck down': Richards opposed playing Live Aid because 'nukes may obsess your brain, but they don't obsess your crotch.' The chapter stakes out rock as a felt, noncognitive, morality-free zone resisting co-optation by causes like No Nukes and Rock Against Racism, posing the head-versus-groin question the following sub-chapters test against Bentham and Mill.
- ch. 39 "To Riff or Not to Riff" **·** Jeremy Bentham, John Stuart Mill — Higher and Lower Pleasures; Utilitarianism — Stages Bentham's hedonism through the riff-versus-soliloquy question: 'push-pin is of equal value with the arts and sciences of music and poetry,' pleasures differing only in quantity, so a Keith Richards riff that out-pleases Hamlet's 'To be or not to be' is simply the greater pleasure, and ranking pleasures 'higher' or 'lower' is prejudice. The chapter closes by noting Bentham's pleasure-pain calculus even expands the moral community to animals, setting up Mill's dissent on behalf of higher pleasures.
- ch. 40 "Monkey Man" **·** John Stuart Mill — Higher and Lower Pleasures — Takes Mill's side against the 'doctrine of the swine' slur on hedonism: music belongs to the higher pleasures produced by distinctively human faculties. The chapter then asks whether Darwinian continuity between humans and other primates undermines Mill's divide—whether the pleasures of rock'n'roll, which evolved primates developed alongside language and cave painting, rely more on our animal or uniquely human faculties, with 'Monkey Man' as the emblematic case.
- ch. 41 "Only Rock’n’Roll" **·** John Stuart Mill — Higher and Lower Pleasures — Has the reader listen to 'It's Only Rock'n'Roll (But I Like It)' through headphones: the forty-four-second mark where a chorus and guitar part that sound like a horn section morph into a Chuck Berry riff shows that hearing is built from a lifetime of learned musical expectations. Quoting Mill's Autobiography on how music's pleasure 'fades with familiarity,' it argues the reverse happens too—a dynamic listener can be newly surprised by songs heard a zillion times.
- ch. 42 "Tell Me, Sister Dopamine" **·** Higher and Lower Pleasures — Explains the Stones' pleasures neurochemically: a two-phase dopamine rush of anticipation and reward behind the spine-tingling moment of a song, the same pathway as life's four F's—feeding ('The Spider and the Fly'), fighting ('Street Fighting Man'), fleeing ('Before They Make Me Run'), and sex—and the one heroin exploits. Because there is no time to process every stimulus, prediction-making brains let mental models pass for reality, which is why frustrated and fulfilled musical expectations carry such a charge.
- ch. 43 "Some Things Just Stick in Your Mind" **·** Higher and Lower Pleasures — Shows how the twelve-bar I-IV-V blues schema works by delaying, frustrating, and finally satisfying learned expectations, citing Huron's Sweet Anticipation. From the performer's side, Keith's 'Guitar Workshop' study of Jimmy Reed—leaving notes out and droning open strings at maximum unresolved tension—explains the jacked-up chords of 'Jumpin' Jack Flash' and 'Gimme Shelter,' plus the fake-horn trick heard in 'It's Only Rock'n'Roll.'
- ch. 44 "Satisfaction" **·** John Stuart Mill, Jeremy Bentham, Martha Nussbaum — Higher and Lower Pleasures; Utilitarianism — Adjudicates the higher/lower dispute by disambiguating 'higher': a moral ranking Bentham rejects as prejudice, or a neurobiological-evolutionary distinction about distinctively human capacities that Mill can accept. Adopting Martha Nussbaum's reading of Mill in 'Mill between Aristotle and Bentham,' it concludes that rock'n'roll is both a lower pleasure (the same four-F dopamine pathways Keith insists on) and a higher one, imagining Mill teleported onto the tour bus for the demonstration.
- ch. 45 "Chapter 6 - Paint the Flowers Black" **·** Jacques Derrida, Friedrich Nietzsche — Part 6 opener: against the Beatles' 'Good Day Sunshine,' Jagger screams to see 'the sun blotted out from the sky,' and the chapter reads that darkness as rock's version of the post-Enlightenment attack (Nietzsche through Derrida) on philosophy's sunny rationalism, which Derrida names 'white mythology.' The Stones, 'the Nietzsche and Derrida of rock,' repudiate pop's optimism by returning to the blues of the oppressed and painting the white mythology of pop black.
- ch. 46 "Flowers" **·** Jacques Derrida, Friedrich Nietzsche, Augustine of Hippo — Master and Slave Morality; Binary Oppositions — Reads the Flowers album's blooms as dark—'Ruby Tuesday' can't be chained to a life where nothing's gained, and the heliotrope becomes Derrida's figure for philosophy forever turning toward its conceptual sun of truth, knowledge, or the good. That heliotropic bias, the metaphysics of presence, is illustrated by Augustine's treatment of evil as mere privation of good, which Nietzsche's genealogy of slave morality overturns by deriving good and evil from the same source, 'beyond good and evil.'
- ch. 47 "Standing in the Shadow" **·** Jacques Derrida — Chronicles Keith's mission from Life: turning 'white America's brains and ears around' to a blues tradition so marginalized that Muddy Waters did menial labor at Chess Records to stay on the payroll. Even the Stones' prettiest ballads—'As Tears Go By,' 'Have You Seen Your Mother Baby (Standing in the Shadow?)'—drag top-forty pop into the shadow, turning away the metaphysics of presence from inside the hit parade.
- ch. 48 "Out of the Cave" **·** Plato, Jacques Derrida, Friedrich Nietzsche, Socrates — Allegory of the Cave; Deconstruction; Binary Oppositions; The Unexamined Life — Traces the light-versus-darkness metaphor tradition from Plato's cave and Socrates' unexamined life through medieval 'illumination' and the Enlightenment to St. Paul's glass darkly, then lets Nietzsche ('shadow is as needful as light') and Derrida's deconstruction of presence/absence oppositions overturn it. The Stones do the same work in song: 'Just as every cop is a criminal, and all the sinners saints' in 'Sympathy for the Devil,' with 'Here Comes the Sun' overturned by 'Paint It Black' and 'Let It Be' by 'Let It Bleed.'
- ch. 49 "Dandelions on My Cloud" **·** Søren Kierkegaard, Friedrich Nietzsche, Jacques Derrida — Subjectivity Is Truth; Will to Power; Perspectivism — Uses 'Dandelion'—truth as a bright yellow flower that blows away—to mark the withering of Enlightenment confidence in plain, visible truth under deconstruction. Kierkegaard's 'truth is subjectivity' explains why truth on 'Get Off of My Cloud' cannot be shared directly ('Don't hang around 'cause two's a crowd'), and Nietzsche goes further: there is no truth to find, only wills to power making truths—'you can't always get what you want... you just might find you get what you need.'
- ch. 50 "Back to the Roots (Again)" *(no index entry yet)* — Tells how the Beatles' Sgt. Pepper alter-egos provoked Their Satanic Majesties Request, a psychedelic misstep—'She's a Rainbow,' 'Dandelion'—that took the Stones away from their element after manager Andrew Oldham's departure. Beggars Banquet is the homecoming: 'Street Fighting Man' and Rev. Wilkins's 'Prodigal Son' return them, like prodigal sons, to their home in the blues 'with no expectation... to pass through here again.'
- ch. 51 "Dead Flowers and Concepts" **·** Plato, Jacques Derrida, Friedrich Nietzsche — Pits Plato's Republic—censor the poets because they feed emotion against reason and offer only appearances, as a painter offers approximate representations—against Derrida and Nietzsche, who side with poetry: abstracting metaphors into concepts strips them of color, leaving a 'conceptual graveyard' of dead flowers, an 'abstraction without extraction.' The chapter's claim is that Plato prefers dead conceptual flowers while the Stones' 'Dead Flowers' and Nietzsche's forgotten metaphorical roots keep concepts alive.
- ch. 52 "Philosophy’s Bad Boys" **·** Friedrich Nietzsche, Jacques Derrida — Closes the part by pairing philosophy's bad boys with the band: Nietzsche and Derrida, misfits who reminded philosophy it is nourished by the metaphorical soil it disowned, have their analogue in the Stones, who reintegrated Black American blues into white rock'n'roll stripped of its African and slave origins. White music, like white mythology, had become an 'abstraction without extraction'—it needed to be painted black and to 'let it bleed.'
- ch. 53 "Chapter 7 - Zen and the Art of Being a Stones Fan" *(no index entry yet)* — Part 7 opener, a fan's phenomenology: the author divides his life by Stones tours (the 1975 Tour of the Americas, the 1978 Some Girls tour), recalls his devout seventy-six-year-old father queueing six hours in Toronto for tickets 'because Keith is my favorite,' and describes the exponential pre-concert anticipation and the mystical magic of the opening notes. He closes by refusing the 'farewell tour' rumors and the media's age-ist clichés as celebrity trivialization.
- ch. 54 "Hey Paparazzi, Get Off of My Cloud" *(no index entry yet)* — Coins 'celebrity profiling' on the model of racial profiling and runs through the stereotypes—'Indestructible Keith,' 'womanizer Mick,' 'homebody Charlie'—arguing no label can capture people who overflow them, even though Oldham's maxim 'there is no such thing as bad press' and the ashes-snorting anecdote show the Stones manipulate their image. Profiling also hid from a dismissing philosopher colleague that the Stones are essentially a live band that never plays 'Satisfaction' or 'Brown Sugar' the same way twice.
- ch. 55 "My Philosophical Experiment" *(no index entry yet)* — Recruits the 1941 comedy Ball of Fire, where Barbara Stanwyck teaches encyclopedia professors the conga by making them dance it instead of following floor diagrams: the Stones, like the conga, must be felt 'from the inside,' and celebrity profiling blocks that feel for their live performance. Admitting we will never know the objective truth about the past becomes a virtue, freeing the author's experimental return to ancient Greece.
- ch. 56 "Live at Epidaurus" *(no index entry yet)* — Reconstructs the Greek world in which everything existing was aesthetikos—perception—with emotions like anger and erotic love moving as clouds that strike and possess us, and no transcendent God outside the kosmos. Its center is theoria: the collective ritual dancing of Dionysian festivals producing ekstasis, a god's-eye 'looking on' that later hardened into the theatron and finally Plato's abstract 'theory'—which is why a band inducing theoria at concerts counts as theorists in the original sense.
- ch. 57 "Zen and the Art of Being a Stones Fan" **·** Zen Buddhism — Draws the direct line from Dionysian festival to Stones concert: fans' intoxication, collective ritual dancing, and ecstatic 'looking on' at their own lives explain why the author organizes his biography around concerts. Songs like 'I can't get no satisfaction' and 'I'm shattered' shove systematically overlooked human realities in our faces, and a Prince Edward Island hostel's graffiti—'Zen is like the mind which understands The Rolling Stones'—completes the argument that the band shows the way to philosophy and a kind of religion.
- ch. 58 "Chapter 8 - Epicurean Satisfaction" *(no index entry yet)* — Part 8 opener: distinguishes morality, immorality, and amorality, then argues that because people adopt moral codes they believe lead to happiness, hedonism—happiness through the fulfillment of desire and indulgence in pleasures—is a genuine moral code rather than immorality. Hence the reassurance that your wild nights of indulgent partying are actually your way of behaving morally, under a different code than your parents' or boss's.
- ch. 59 "Out of Control" *(no index entry yet)* — Introduces Aristippus's Cyrenaics through Diogenes Laertius as the first systematic hedonists: all bodily pleasures good, better when more intense, frequent, and long-lasting, good 'even if it arises from the most unbecoming causes,' with happiness merely the sum of particular pleasures and pleasures of the flesh trumping pleasures of the mind. It maps this 'simple hedonism' onto 'Shake Your Hips,' 'Brown Sugar,' 'Rip This Joint,' and 'Out of Control,' with 'Monkey Man' as the movement's theme song.
- ch. 60 "A Rolling Stone Gathers . . . Happiness" **·** Epicurus, Cicero — Epicurean Pleasure (ataraxia); Practical Wisdom (phronesis) — Brings in Epicurus, speaking through Cicero, to correct the Cyrenaics: pursue pleasure rationally, declining pleasures that purchase greater later pains—Keith's drug-related legal bills, 'Sister Morphine's' desperation—and sometimes enduring pain for greater pleasure later, like kicking junk to enjoy raising children. Advocating simple living, a hierarchy of pleasures ranked by sober reasoning, and prudence (phronesis) as the chief virtue, it pictures modern Epicureans at a rock festival passing out condoms and running the needle exchange.
- ch. 61 "What Can a Poor Boy Do" *(no index entry yet)* — Poses the part's question: Cyrenaic seize-each-fleeting-pleasure indulgence or Epicurean choosiness—which moral code is more likely to deliver happiness? First glance says the songs preach Cyrenaic hedonism, but a closer look finds the most hedonistic lyrics full of agonizing desire and unfulfilled pleasure rather than achieved happiness, hinting the Stones may not be recommending the Cyrenaic road at all.
- ch. 62 "Now I Need You More than Ever" **·** Epicurus — Epicurean Pleasure (ataraxia) — Finds Epicurean confirmation in two songs: 'Let's Spend the Night Together,' where thwarted desire exacts bodily suffering ('tongue's getting tied... mouth's getting dry'), and 'Sister Morphine'—first written and recorded by Marianne Faithfull—where a dying addict is too weak to drag himself across the floor for the drug that is killing him. In both, pleasures bring pains so bad they can outweigh the goodness of the pleasure sought.
- ch. 63 "I’m Shattered" *(front)*
- ch. 64 "Can You Always Get What You Want" **·** Epicurean Pleasure (ataraxia) — Considers the escape route that would save the Cyrenaics: perhaps the pain in the songs is merely the frustration of not attaining pleasure, not a cost of pleasure itself, in which case the Epicurean advice to prefer simple, easy pleasures stands. Weighing song-as-fiction against the documented hedonism of Jagger (seven children by four women) and Richards (five drug trials), it concludes such men do not lack access to pleasure, so the nagging unhappiness in the songs genuinely tells against Cyrenaic hedonism.
- ch. 65 "Satisfaction" **·** Epicurus — Epicurean Pleasure (ataraxia) — Delivers the verdict: Cyrenaic hedonism has been well tried by the Rolling Stones and clearly is not an effective road to happiness, so the Epicurean modification deserves its shot—keep the dedication to bodily pleasure but let prudence filter choices by their consequences. 'Waiting on a Friend' (choosing a friend over the game of 'making love and breaking hearts') and Exile's 'Happy' suggest the band already leans toward tempered hedonism and real satisfaction.
- ch. 66 "III - Time Is on Rock’s Side" *(front)*
- ch. 67 "Chapter 9 - From Main Street to Guyville" *(no index entry yet)* — Part 9 opener, narrative: fleeing Allen Klein and the taxman after the 1967 drug busts, the Stones settle at the mansion Nellecôte on the Riviera, recording Exile on Main St. in the basement with the Rolling Stones Mobile while Gram Parsons teaches Keith country classics. The album's title and its cover collage from Robert Frank's The Americans declare that these English exiles belonged, if anywhere, in America's freakish cultural circus.
- ch. 68 "The View from Guyville" *(no index entry yet)* — Takes Liz Phair's claim that Exile in Guyville is a song-by-song response to Exile on Main Street seriously against the skeptical critical consensus—Jim DeRogatis, the anniversary liner notes, and her own doubting producer John Henderson, whom she silences with 'John! I did! Cross my heart and hope to die.' Since the claim never worked as marketing, the chapter proposes to figure out what it means, promising a philosophical explanation of the puzzlement rooted in assumptions going back to Plato's Academy.
- ch. 69 "Répondez S’il Vous Plaît" *(no index entry yet)* — Sorts the eighteen-to-eighteen song pairings into three relationship types: merely formal (tempo, key, structure), psychological or emotional (Phair treating Mick and Keith's songs like Rorschach inkblots and answering with her own associations), and critical or theoretical—a debate about exile, romance, and the human circus beyond songwriting craft. On a matched playlist you hear all three, from her clean Fender Duo-Sonic against Keith's crunchy Les Pauls to her vantage as a twenty-two-year-old woman launching a career in early-90s Chicago.
- ch. 70 "Bloody Hermeneutics" *(no index entry yet)* — Introduces hermeneutics as something every reader already does: meaning is jointly created by the writer's intentions and a reader's own history and expectations, so the famous ambiguous 'yeeaaahhh' opening 'Rocks Off'—like Mona Lisa's smile—has no single fixed meaning. Artworks become fluid and multivalent (Their Satanic Majesties Request is alternately failure and underappreciated gem), artists interpret while creating, and meaning flows into metaphysics: 'More let it bleed than let it be.'
- ch. 71 "Testing One Two Three" **·** Martin Heidegger — Asks how to know whether an interpretation of a lyric, a song, or a song-pair is trustworthy, and answers with coherence and Heidegger's hermeneutic circle: interpretation starts from a stockpile of assumptions and must circle back to revise them as new evidence enters, until, in Martin Eger's phrase, it converges rather than oscillating forever between opposing poles.
- ch. 72 "Really You Guys Are into Music" *(no index entry yet)* — Defines Guyville as Chicago's predominantly male indie-rock scene in Bucktown and Wicker Park (Urge Overkill, Smashing Pumpkins, Material Issue), which Phair confronted head-on: 'Really? Okay, so you guys are into music. Watch—I can make music.' But beyond being a woman's take on a male musical world, the chapter announces her real subject—what exile really means on Main Street, distinguishing romantic, geographical, and existential exile from one's self.
- ch. 73 "The Rolling Stones and Philosophy" *(no index entry yet)* — Pairs the tenth tracks in the romantic-exile register: Keith's 'Happy' ('I need a love to keep me happy,' with Anita and Marlon in tow) against Phair's 'Fuck and Run,' where she wakes alarmed among men who get out of bed citing 'a lot of work to do'—wishing for exactly the bond Keith has. In 'Loving Cup' versus 'Mesmerizing' the roles flip: where Mick begs for 'a little drink from our loving cup,' Phair wants to be the mesmerist casting the spell.
- ch. 74 "The Rolling Stones and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 75 "The Rolling Stones and Philosophy" *(no index entry yet)* — Pairs 'Sweet Black Angel'—Mick and Keith's freedom song for Angela Davis, the philosophy professor imprisoned in 1970 on murder charges and called 'a sweet black slave'—with Phair's eerie piano piece 'Canary,' which puts herself in a cage 'like a good canary' who learns her name, works up to her potential, earns her meat, and comes when called. Phair transforms Davis's prison chains into the chains of a proper Winnetka girl's education, ending with contemplated domestic terrorism: 'Send it up on fire, death before dawn.'
- ch. 76 "Mick and Liz Partners in Crime" *(no index entry yet)* — Argues Phair is not just responding song-by-song but cast herself as the women of Main Street: her 'Never Said' keeps a rendezvous with Mick's 'Tumbling Dice' philanderer secret, 'Flower' answers 'Let It Loose' with a lover's 'perfect suck-me size' lips, and 'Glory' answers 'Shake Your Hips' with a 'really big tongue'—hardly accidental given the Jagger lips-and-tongue logo Jim Pasche designed in 1971. Her opener '6’1”' diagnoses the man who can 'only get my rocks off while I'm sleeping' as one who has 'long since passed understanding what it takes to be satisfied,' a vine with no resting point.
- ch. 77 "With You It’s Always Take, Take, Take" *(no index entry yet)* — Confronts Phair's NPR account—that she wrote '6’1”' about a crush on Urge Overkill's Nash Kato, superimposing Mick's Exile character on him and writing as the girl 'Rocks Off' brushes past—yet treats it as another interpretive layer rather than a refutation, since she called Jagger's performance 'a perfect portrait' of Kato. Using Martin Eger's 'cascade of interpretations,' it shows Mick interpreting his own life, Phair interpreting Mick through Kato, and the author interpreting Phair: 'It's interpretations... all down the line.'
- ch. 78 "Exile from Ancient Greece—Yeah!" **·** Plato, Aristotle — Answers the anti-relativist who holds Main Street's greatness self-explanatory: if its meaning has nothing to do with what Liz Phair thinks, it has nothing to do with what the fundamentalist thinks either. The skeptics' error—expecting Guyville to sound like Main Street—inherits the Platonic-Aristotelian assumption that inspiration means imitation (knowing a statue's beauty means containing a copy of its shape), whereas the Stones themselves 'responded' to Muddy Waters and Chuck Berry without merely imitating; Phair's closer 'Strange Loop,' its riff morphing into '6’1”,' closes the hermeneutic circle and invites us to revisit, not finalize, Exile.
- ch. 79 "Phair’s Song-by-Song Responses to Exile on Main Street" *(not yet read)*
- ch. 80 "The Rolling Stones and Philosophy" **·** Philosophy of Love — Closing track-list of Chapter 9, pairing songs from Exile on Main St. with Liz Phair's song-for-song answer Exile in Guyville under the heading of romantic and sexual exile. 'Loving Cup' meets 'Mesmerizing' as unrequited lust from male and female sides, 'Happy' meets 'Fuck and Run' on whether lasting attachments are possible, and 'Ventilator Blues' meets 'Divorce Song' on the loaded-gun violence of failing relationships. The pairing argues Phair and Jagger/Richards map the same territory of oversexed dissatisfaction from opposite perspectives.
- ch. 81 "The Rolling Stones and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 82 "The Rolling Stones and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 83 "Chapter 10 - When the Whip Comes Down" *(no index entry yet)* — Randall Auxier opens Chapter 10 by defining rock'n'roll as pent-up energy stored against its tendency to diffuse, then released in little bursts between sixty and 160 times per minute in groups of four. Borrowing Keith Richards's word 'discipline,' he argues rock requires chaining the moment and whipping it 'until it feels bad enough to be good,' so that every explosion destroys as much as it creates. Even the gods, he concludes, must die a little death at the end of every bar.
- ch. 84 "Mad Men" **·** The Culture Industry — Auxier argues rock periodically cleanses itself of commercial packaging, citing Hall and Oates versus Johnny Rotten and Culture Club versus Kurt Cobain as each era's corrective upheaval. The Madison Avenue 'Mad Men' sell kids sanitized three-minute morsels like Herman's Hermits, but the kids' own bottled energy finds ground anyway, and once they 'get their ya-yas out' the packaged pop stops working. The Stones enter as the real rhythm that arrives when something has to be put 'under the whip.'
- ch. 85 "“It”" *(no index entry yet)* — Auxier distinguishes celebrity from 'It,' the untheorizable path to ground that lets a performer channel atmospheric energy, noting it lives just as well in obscure bar bands as in stars. He defines 'It' as the what-ness and that-ness of 'he has It,' a circuit completed when the right components are hooked up and tied down in the right order, and names this inquiry musical metaphysics. The chapter ends by recruiting Alfred North Whitehead, whose process philosophy of time and energy will supply the theory.
- ch. 86 "Musical Metaphysics" *(no index entry yet)* — Auxier presents Whitehead's claim that there are no things, only rhythmic patterns of vibration: molecules are 'performances,' and quoted passages from Process and Reality on the rolling stone dismantle materialist atomism. He maps the rhythm section onto Whitehead's phases of concrescence — Keith the datum, Charlie the synthesis, Bill the 'satisfaction' — with grooves and riffs as the protonic and electronic rhythms of intensity. The upshot, that what we do makes us what we are, explains why the Stones' rhythm section is the best ever.
- ch. 87 "The Sum of the Parts" **·** Ship of Theseus — Auxier asks why 'It' works only when the Stones are together, testing the band's fifty-year experiment against lineup changes: Brian Jones proved dispensable, the second guitar modular, yet Bill Wyman's 1992 departure genuinely weakened the circuit. He claims Keith-Charlie-Bill in series (data-synthesis-satisfaction) are the real power source, with Jagger merely the light bulb that draws the current — the Keith-Charlie-Bill band would have been famous without Mick, but Mick is nothing without the circuit. A lithium analogy renders the band loosely bound, unstable and flammable, and the chapter closes by insisting Charlie interprets Keith and Bill interprets Charlie, in that order.
- ch. 88 "A Date with Keith" *(no index entry yet)* — Auxier casts Keith Richards as Prometheus with a five-stringed lightning rod who pulls grooves and riffs down from the atmosphere, sloppily out of tune yet almost never off. Against critics who rank him below Clapton, Hendrix, Page and Beck, he argues that originating body-moving grooves and unforgettable riffs is harder than flashy lead work — the riff and groove are the batter and bake of the rock'n'roll cake. Keith's greatness lies in being the promethean point of origin every band needs.
- ch. 89 "Maestro Tempo and Mr. Groove" *(no index entry yet)* — Auxier distinguishes finding a groove again — pulling it up from the body's stored repetition, as the bar-band 'grooves guy' does before a song — from the conductor's interpretive choice of tempo. He personifies Maestro Tempo as a proper suitor of Miss Sound (Herman's Hermits territory) against Mr. Groove, who operates independently of how things sound so long as the feel dominates. In rock, he concludes, Sound must conform to Groove rather than the reverse.
- ch. 90 "Who Could Hang a Name on You" *(no index entry yet)* — Auxier separates discovering a groove — Whitehead's 'self-creative act,' explicable by neither what came before nor what comes after, hence unnameable — from merely recalling one from rehearsal. Quoting Keith's tribute to Dylan's pre-electric float against the 'relentless discipline of a rhythm section,' he lays out the standard skeleton of kick on beats one and three, snare slap on two and four. The tiniest rushing or relaxing of the four beats in mutual relation decides whether a groove is erotic and invigorating or limpid.
- ch. 91 "Alright Now" *(no index entry yet)* — Auxier inventories Keith's original grooves: the 'Start Me Up' riff, the one-one-one-one kick-in of 'Jumpin' Jack Flash,' the agonizingly slow 'Honky Tonk Women,' the slowest rock groove he knows in 'Wild Horses,' with 'Beast of Burden' credited to Charlie and 'Shattered' likely to Bill. He argues no single person has contributed more previously undiscovered grooves to rock, with Chuck Berry the only rival, whose songs collapse unless played in exactly the right groove. Musicians themselves name these grooves by their Stones songs.
- ch. 92 "Riffing" *(no index entry yet)* — Auxier analyzes the riff's first leg, tone: the endless knob-and-amplifier experimentation needed to make 'Nineteenth Nervous Breakdown' feel like what it is, with Keith as the creator of unique, deliberately unrepeatable tones like the 'Satisfaction' fuzz. He recounts Keith's revelation that 'Jumpin' Jack Flash' used no electric guitars at all — acoustics overloaded into a Phillips cassette recorder's built-in mic — proving clean sound isn't always the best sound. Rock guitarists, he says, must go to school on the tones Keith made.
- ch. 93 "You Make a Dead Man . ." *(no index entry yet)* — Auxier completes the riff with the 'sig lick,' the signature phrase — a violin arpeggio in 'Ruby Tuesday,' a melodic figure in 'Nineteenth Nervous Breakdown,' the six ascending notes before 'brown sugar' — that identifies a song within a couple of seconds. The sig lick is the culmination of pulling the lightning down, making the listener lose herself in anticipation and memory, dying a little death in the moment. He credits Keith and his rhythm section with perhaps six dozen one-of-a-kind winning riffs, presciently recognized as such.
- ch. 94 "The Singer, Not the Song" *(no index entry yet)* — Auxier separates Keith's guitar greatness from songwriting: riffs and grooves often occasioned the songs Jagger voiced, and the same song can carry different grooves, as the Folksmen's bluegrass 'Start Me Up' proves painfully. Keith's real feat was exploring the terra incognita of sounds the electric guitar first made hearable by human ears, demanding curiosity, tenacity and focus. To deny his greatness, Auxier says, is like saying Christopher Columbus wasn't a great sea captain.
- ch. 95 "A Steady Synthesis of the Data" *(no index entry yet)* — Auxier confesses he long judged Charlie Watts a mediocre drummer with hitches, jerks and an annoying snare-only habit on beats two and four, measuring him against conscious, conventional expectations of technique. Whitehead's dictum that consciousness is rare and unimportant reframes Charlie as the second phase of Stones concrescence, pulling the whole together beneath the radar of theory. The life lesson: when something is very good and your pet theory says it shouldn't be, dump the theory.
- ch. 96 "The River of Tight" *(no index entry yet)* — Auxier argues there is a kind of looseness on the far side of tight, distinguishing the Grateful Dead's chosen wildness beyond the 'River of Tight' from the Stones' well-chosen spots just past it. Tight playing is cerebral — music for Rush, Styx, Kansas and Yes — impressive to the ears and head but not to the ass and feet. The moral reaches past music: life cannot go to plan once you step on stage, so learn tightness, then forget it and live in the moment.
- ch. 97 "Charlie Chuckles" *(no index entry yet)* — Auxier reveals Charlie Watts's secret: he lags behind Keith's front-edge attack to the last possible micromoment, then lands a comic, jocular snort just beyond it, releasing Keith's manic intensity as levity — that is 'when the whip comes down.' 'Beast of Burden' exemplifies the design, its oodles of empty sonic space created by Charlie hopping to the front of the beat and falling way back every measure, as the near-random double snare slap exposes how far he has trailed. Keith's autobiography confirms the two test each other's rhythmic limits for fun.
- ch. 98 "Paying the Bill An Ode to Satisfaction" *(no index entry yet)* — Auxier reads Bill Wyman as the rhythm section's distributor of joy who was personally 'not destined for satisfaction,' the older man who never quite bought in. Bill's genius was refusing to lock with Charlie's kick drum, instead lagging behind even Charlie and bringing each bar back to Keith's root — a thick, deep four-string memory that realizes Whitehead's 'satisfaction,' the phase where what is made culminates and contributes to what comes next. 'Miss You' is his masterpiece of playing in the interstices, while 'Paint It Black' is the rare exception where the bass drives the backbeat.
- ch. 99 "What a Mess!" *(no index entry yet)* — Auxier reads 'Shattered' as a menacing tangle of bottom-end dissonance made for the gonads and solar plexus rather than the ear, with Bill's bass shutting the door behind the band — though never again after 1992. He then answers the chapter's opening promise about what rock destroys: ourselves, our youth, sacrificed on its altar. Naming Freud's thanatos, the death wish beneath the organized energies, he defines rocking as wanting to stop your own heart every four beats and find resurrection on the far side of the bar.
- ch. 100 "Chapter 11 - The Stones in Pepperland" *(no index entry yet)* — Bill Martin opens Chapter 11 by joking that Hegel's dialectical Reason and Marx's class conflict both missed the universe's most revealing tension: Beatles versus Stones. He dismantles the 'bad boys' and working-class image as confused — the Beatles were no posh outfit, and The Who could have taken both bands in a street brawl. He concedes, though, that musically the Beatles 'kicked all of their butts' with Sgt. Pepper.
- ch. 101 "Please Pass the Pepper" *(no index entry yet)* — Martin coins 'Pepperism' for the mid-sixties moment when rock, spurred by Miles Davis, Coltrane and Coleman in jazz, Cage and Stockhausen in classical music, and non-Western musics, broke its founding blues framework, with Sgt. Pepper making experimentation the main trend centered on 1968. Against it he sets the 'blues orthodoxy' reaction — 'that ain't rock'n'roll' — which turns the Beatles and Stones into genuine rivals over what rock should be. Yet in 1967 the bands briefly agreed on experimentation, and the result was Their Satanic Majesties Request.
- ch. 102 "It’s Only Their Satanic Majesties Request, But . ." *(no index entry yet)* — Martin weighs Keith's dismissal of Majesties as a 'put-on' and 'flimflam' against the album's value, arguing it reveals the Stones' genuine dark side — invoking Satan where the Beatles never would — and seals the case that the Stones were less innovative perfectionists, since an attempted Sgt. Pepper was bound to fail. Invoking Badiou's claim that every artist has at most one great idea, he grants the Stones' idea is blues-plus-rock, which Majesties is not. Still he prefers listening to it: its Ginsberg-like 'first thought, best thought' mess gives it an edginess the polished Pepper lacks, making 'really cool' a candidate aesthetic category.
- ch. 103 "In the Court of the Satanic Majesty" *(no index entry yet)* — Martin situates the Stones amid Pepperist experimentation — Jagger's film work with Roeg and Anger, and King Crimson's mind-blowing set opening the 1969 Hyde Park memorial for Brian Jones. The Stones' own ragged Hyde Park performance pointed to their turn away from Pepperism and back to the blues. He nonetheless argues Majesties should be seen as the Stones' only true concept album — indeed a 'concepts album' that prog fans should heed.
- ch. 104 "Beelzebub’s Bugged-Out Bequest" **·** Utopia and Dystopia — Martin identifies two threads in Majesties, hippie-utopianism and science fiction, reading the sorcerer Robes cover as what he calls 'sci-fi medievalism' and tracing four songs. 'Citadel' erupts from dissonance into a Bastille-like prison; 'In Another Land' is a utopian dream-within-a-dream whose 'Is this some kind of joke?' anticipates the paranoid machine-consciousness of The Matrix and Philip K. Dick's VALIS; Bill Wyman's '2000 Man' has an affair with a computer; and '2000 Light Years from Home' goes beyond Pepperism itself. The band stood, like rock between Woodstock and Altamont, poised between utopia and dystopia.
- ch. 105 "You’re So Very Lonely" **·** Utopia and Dystopia — Martin reads the album's science-fiction songs as studies in loneliness, isolation and alienation, the paired '2000' titles marking time and distance rather than literal measures. The closer 'On With the Show' turns Pepper's emcee routine into a cynical scam — 'we've got all the answers' — that deflates the utopian optimism of 'Sing This All Together' and anticipates the anti-utopian note of Lennon's 'Revolution' and Townshend's 'Won't Get Fooled Again.' He locates the album as ground tilled for Zappa's parody We're Only in It for the Money.
- ch. 106 "2000 Light Years from the Blues" *(no index entry yet)* — Martin shows the Stones disowned Majesties themselves — Mick's interpretive dance on the 1989 '2000 Light Years from Home' strips out the alienation and re-blueses the rhythm — concluding the Stones never belonged in Pepperland. The 3-D cover strands them without the carnival of artists and intellectuals that contextualizes Pepper's collage; their real idols were Muddy Waters and Screamin' Jay Hawkins, who would have looked as absurd there as 'Hey You, Get Off of My Nebula.' Their experimentalism was secondhand, taken at one remove from Sgt. Pepper, though it paid off for later bands like the Brian Jonestown Massacre.
- ch. 107 "Chapter 12 - Beatles versus Stones The Last Word" *(no index entry yet)* — Crispin Sartwell opens Chapter 12 by deflating the Beatles-versus-Stones debate and the modernist genius-cult that made it possible: Beethoven, Van Gogh, Nietzsche, Picasso and Joyce imagined as transcendent, 'God-touched' geniuses whose actual music is absurdly inadequate to the adulation. Great-figure narratives, he argues, simplify history into nodes and satisfy the impulse to worship, while promotion of any actual person to that status is nearly arbitrary. No one, he insists, is as far above everyone else as the Beatles are imagined to be.
- ch. 108 "Avant the Avant-Garde" *(no index entry yet)* — Sartwell distinguishes avant-garde art, which authenticates itself through radical negations of the past (impressionism through conceptualism), from traditional arts like country and blues, which authenticate through continuity, apprenticeship and settled forms such as the twelve-bar structure. Innovators from Robert Johnson to Hendrix change these traditions while preserving an underlying continuity, though Hendrix's shredded, burned blues licks poise him between tradition and avant-garde. Early rock'n'roll — Chuck Berry, Elvis — emerges as both eclectic and traditional, a syncretic blend of black and white southern styles.
- ch. 109 "Salt of the Earth" *(no index entry yet)* — Sartwell recalls Keith and Mick bonding as obsessive blues-record archivists and notes both early bands were essentially cover bands performing American black music for European audiences, the Beatles' 'Twist and Shout' the best such appropriation. He refuses to condemn white appropriations of black styles — no race owns art, and figures from Bix Beiderbecke to Elvis often revived, paid, and popularized the artists they venerated. African-American forms, he argues, have been the source of the world's popular music for a century, down to the participatory behavior of rock audiences.
- ch. 110 "The Riff Not Taken" *(no index entry yet)* — Sartwell contrasts the bands' moral personae: the wholesome Beatles of A Hard Day's Night and 'All You Need Is Love' versus the Stones flirting with Satan in Majesties and 'Sympathy for the Devil' and ending at Altamont, a good-boy/bad-boy split he likens to the Madonna-whore complex. The real divergence is musical: the Beatles developed a new vocabulary and became avant-garde artists, while the Stones, however sharper and more original, kept referring back to the rock, soul and blues vocabularies they started with, developing like a traditional art.
- ch. 111 "You’re 2000 Light Years from Home" *(no index entry yet)* — Sartwell dates the Beatles' avant-garde turn from Rubber Soul through Sgt. Pepper: a shift from blues tonality to British music-hall colors, orchestral and sitar instrumentation, and surreal lyrics like 'Glass Onion,' making 'Yesterday' a category mistake of a rock song. He judges much of the Revolver-to-Let It Be output overblown yet trivial — 'Mr. Kite' is no Guernica — and blames messianic audience worship ('bigger than Jesus') for the band's self-indulgence. The turn, he charges, produced the soulless anti-rock of Elton John and Billy Joel and the empty art-rock of Yes and ELP.
- ch. 112 "Let the Blues Be" **·** Plato — Theory of Forms — Sartwell declares the Stones 'a cosmic essence, a Platonic Form, of rock music': where the Beatles passed through mannerist, baroque and rococo decadence to an aesthetic dead end, Keith stripped the rock lick to its essence from 'Satisfaction' through 'Gunface.' Invoking Plato's view that Forms are remembered and discovered — essences that are both our origin and our destiny — he argues the Stones reverted continually to the origins of their music in blues, country and soul while exposing the center of the center. Beggar's Banquet through Exile on Main Street, he proposes, just is rock music, achieving one's own essence as a legitimate aesthetic project.
- ch. 113 "IV - Politics Sexual and the Other Kind" *(front)*
- ch. 114 "Chapter 13 - You Can’t Always Get What You Want" **·** Jean-Paul Sartre, Simone de Beauvoir — Philosophy of Love — Luke Dick opens Chapter 13 with Sartre's 1947 line about marriage taboos versus contraceptives in college back yards and sets Western monogamy — inherited through church and institutions, with divorce thanks to Henry VIII — against the Stones' womanizing image, 'Some Girls' included. A timeline frames the test: Sartre and Beauvoir's 1927 pact of open love, Jagger fashioning himself Don Juan incarnate ('Sexually, yes. Financially, no. Philosophically, trying'), Charlie Watts' steadfast 1964 marriage, and Keith stealing Anita from Brian Jones. The chapter asks whether humans are cut out for monogamy at all.
- ch. 115 "Saw Her Today at the Reception" **·** Philosophy of Love — Dick reads 'You Can't Always Get What You Want' as an anatomy of wanting to keep something and let it go at once, locating the revelation not in Mr. Jimmy or the demonstration but in the reception scene, where Mick confronts that love will never fulfill his expectations. He generalizes to ordinary life: domestic routine can blissfully stabilize a meaningful life or leave the heart 'looking bored behind the drum set,' and rock stardom's surfeit of mates only multiplies the strain. This raises the chapter's governing question — what is a tradition, and should it dictate anything?
- ch. 116 "A Footloose Man" **·** Jean-Paul Sartre — Existence Precedes Essence; Radical Freedom and Responsibility — Dick expounds Sartre's 'Existentialism Is a Humanism' through the essentialist picture — a God-given blueprint, like Keith's Bentley designed before manufacture — against Sartre's claim that we exist first and invent essences afterward, marriage and family included. Monogamy thus emerges as a human choice mandated by Western authority and imposed on conquered peoples, not an absolute ethic handed down from heaven, a freedom humans exercise even in dropping Deuteronomy's stoning rule. Keith, Mick and Simone discarded the inherited tradition of monogamy, while Charlie Watts chose to stick with it.
- ch. 117 "Practiced at the Art of Deception" **·** Philosophy of Love — Dick diagnoses Jagger's contradiction: he wants to be both knight and sorcerer, accepted by the aristocratic tradition while living out 'Some Girls,' but you cannot be establishment and counterculture at once. The pendulum swings from sleeping with Keith's girlfriend Anita — passion fulfilled, tradition left a vacuous hole — back home to Marianne while he craves the next mistress. This incongruous moral bearings problem guarantees Mick's unfulfillment, the very tension 'You Can't Always Get What You Want' sings of.
- ch. 118 "Glass of Wine in Her Hand" **·** Philosophy of Love — Dick portraits Keith as Bacchus incarnate, guided by camaraderie and a will for collective hedonistic comfort rather than any defined moral impulse, with romantic and platonic lines blurred — everyone nearby drinks the vapors from his cauldron. The chaos works inside the circle but turns deadly on contact with a world that needs yellow lines: Marlon running amok on the '76 tour, partners straying while Keith passes out. His verdict: Keith would be dead if he hadn't been a Rolling Stone.
- ch. 119 "If You Try Sometimes . ." **·** Philosophy of Love — Dick closes with Charlie Watts near seventy, married to Shirley Ann Shepherd since 1964, whose only indulgence at Hugh Hefner's Playboy mansion in 1972 was the billiard room. He weighs explanations — little impulse for sexual diversity, indifference to passion's initial stages, or resilience compelled by promises and tradition — while admitting marriages are tense, burning things where fatigue is common. Watts endures as he drums: he doesn't drop the beat when his arms get tired.
- ch. 120 "In Her Glass was a Bleeding Man" **·** Jean-Paul Sartre, Simone de Beauvoir — Existentialism — This section sets the Stones' marriages beside Jean-Paul Sartre and Simone de Beauvoir's fifty-year open 'pact', contrasting Charlie Watts's monogamy with Jagger's wandering. Sartre and Beauvoir refused religious or social templates and defined their own rules: 'contingent' love affairs permitted, provided each told the other everything. Even this pact of honesty had casualties — lies told to their other lovers, abortions, stolen partners — so the existentialist point is that self-authored relationships trade the guilt of broken monogamy for another kind of tension.
- ch. 121 "Get What You Need" **·** Jean-Paul Sartre, Simone de Beauvoir, Heraclitus — Existentialism; Radical Freedom and Responsibility; Everything Flows / Logos — The chapter closes by matching each man's relationship style to what he 'needs': Watts keeps the beat (literally knocking Jagger out for phoning 'Where's my drummer?'), Jagger gets serial seduction, Richards trades the Anita Pallenberg cauldron for Patti Hansen's steadying presence, while Sartre died in 1980 surrounded by quarreling lovers instead of family. Every arrangement, from an Okie widow's 'plate of shit you can deal with' to the Sartre-Beauvoir pact, involves trade-offs, and the existentialist lesson is that we remain responsible for whichever romantic path we choose. Heraclitus's dictum that life is tension, and Sartre's nausea at our own choices, frame both monogamy and the pact as equally legitimate gambits.
- ch. 122 "Chapter 14 - Riding the Devil’s Tank and Complicit in His Mayhem" *(no index entry yet)* — James Rocha opens Chapter 14 with Richards's 1971 remark that 'Everybody's Lucifer' and a mock-confession: his perfectly legal iTunes purchases of Stones records may, downstream, help finance cartel assassinations. He locates complicity in 'that mysterious gap between guilt and innocence' — you cannot be responsible for what other people do, yet you may still support, aid, or fail to stop their crimes. The chapter promises that 'Sympathy for the Devil' is the song that explains this strange concept.
- ch. 123 "Philosophy by Satanic Example" *(no index entry yet)* — Reading 'Sympathy for the Devil' itself, the section argues the song implies an ethical position on complicity rather than devil-worship. Through an invented adultery case (Anita, Keith, and 'Mick'), it shows accountability is not a zero-sum game — one wrongful act can total 220 percent responsibility — so Lucifer, one hundred percent responsible for the evils he confesses, can still turn around and implicate 'you and me' in them.
- ch. 124 "Please Allow Me to Introduce Complicity" *(no index entry yet)* — The section distinguishes three kinds of complicity: aiding and abetting (the groupie who took Keith Richards's driving test), membership in a collectively wrongdoing group (a state majority voting to ban the Stones), and the hybrid case — individual evil requiring mass support or inactivity. It applies the third to Altamont, where the Hell's Angels killed Meredith Hunter amid a feral atmosphere of drugs, paranoia, and three hundred thousand spectators no single one of whom is 'responsible', and to the drug wars, which persist only because users and ordinary spenders each play a small part.
- ch. 125 "Killing the Kennedys—What’s Your Alibi" *(no index entry yet)* — The section wrestles with the song's line 'I shouted out: Who killed the Kennedys? When after all It was you and me.' Since the author has an airtight alibi — he was not alive in 1963 or 1968 — the literal reading fails, so he proposes the Kennedys as the symbol of Camelot, the utopian promise of the 1960s. On that reading our failure to protect what they stood for, from Vietnam to the later wars, is our collective complicity in their metaphorical deaths.
- ch. 126 "Washing the Evil off Your Hands" *(no index entry yet)* — Pilate's hand-washing — 'Made damn sure that Pilate Washed his hands' — serves here as the emblem of the complicity skeptic: the thought that once responsibility fixes on someone else, everyone else's hands are clean. Noting that the ambiguous pronoun in 'washed his hands and sealed his fate' grammatically points at Pilate himself, the section argues Pilate stays complicit precisely because he permitted the execution, just as the author remains implicated in the drug wars by doing nothing to stop them.
- ch. 127 "The Stench of War" *(no index entry yet)* — Complicity is tested against the Iraq and Afghanistan wars: the author voted against Bush and protested as much of a 'street fighting man' as he could, yet may still be complicit through taxes, votes, bumper stickers, or mere failure to speak out. He then voices the skeptic's strongest reply — that one person's inaction can hardly 'allow' a war, since stopping a war takes as much collective action as waging one — conceding that complicity here begins to look 'rather harsh'.
- ch. 128 "The Puzzling Nature of the Complicity Game" *(no index entry yet)* — The section defends keeping complicity on two grounds: it names something real between innocence and responsibility (were Keef's drug runners responsible, complicit, or both?), and it is practically needed to shame people out of tiny roles in grave wrongs. From the song's 'kings and queens fought for ten decades for the gods they made' it draws the point that while leaders bear responsibility for war, no war effort succeeds without popular support — and zero multiplied a million times never adds up to a war, so the small contributions must be real to begin with.
- ch. 129 "Restraining Lucifer with Some Courtesy, Sympathy, and Taste" *(no index entry yet)* — The section reads 'Just as every cop is a criminal... I'm in need of some restraint' as Lucifer speaking for the collective evil our small complicities create. The song's own solution comes in the next stanza: 'have some courtesy, have some sympathy, and some taste' become the tools for discerning our everyday contributions to large wrongs and fighting them, since if we do not, 'Lucifer really will lay our souls to waste'. On this reading 'Sympathy for the Devil' is politically deeper than critics who dismiss the Stones as hedonists will allow.
- ch. 130 "Chapter 15 - The Most Dangerous Rock’n’Roll Band in the World" *(no index entry yet)* — Seth Vannatta opens Chapter 15 with Keith Richards's July 4th, 1975 stop at the 4-Dice restaurant in Fordyce, Arkansas, where forty minutes in the toilet getting high ended in a pull-over of a car stuffed with coke, grass, peyote, mescaline, and corn whiskey. Yet the police charged only a concealed hunting knife and 'reckless driving' from spun gravel, having tipped off the wire services in advance. The puzzle of these trumped-up charges sets up the chapter's real questions: what is law, and what exactly was rock's 'sin'?
- ch. 131 "What’s the Law against Rock and Roll" **·** Socrates — The section argues the arrest was never about the drugs: rock's 'original sin' is sex, from the term's origin as slang for sex through Elvis's censored hips and the Beatles' fainting crowds, so Arkansas's attempt to legislate rock out of existence was, the author quips, an attempt to outlaw horniness. Richards's own account of 'open season on the Stones' casts the band as suffering an extirpation 'befitting Socrates, another famous corruptor of the youth' — the state treating a mere band as a criminal corrupting force.
- ch. 132 "Muddy Water and Brown Sugar" *(no index entry yet)* — This section digs to the deeper sin: race. Rock was born of 'race music' and the collision of white country with black gospel and blues, and it desegregated southern dance floors separated by a rope, breaking Jim Crow in practice — as the White Citizens' Council quotes the section reproduces confirm. The Stones, white but foreign, worshipping Muddy Waters, at home in black juke joints yet harassed at white truck stops, embodied the South's fear of integration and miscegenation by selling 'Brown Sugar' to white girls, and capped that stance with 'Sweet Black Angel', their solidarity song for the imprisoned Angela Davis.
- ch. 133 "Keith Richards the Prophet" **·** John Dewey — Pragmatism — The arrest becomes legal philosophy: since statutes like 'reckless driving' are general while conduct is specific, law requires the art of judgment. Against John Austin's positivism (law as the sovereign's command backed by sanction), it adopts Oliver Wendell Holmes Jr.'s 'bad man' view — law is just the prediction of what courts will in fact do — making Richards a 'prophet' of consequences, as when the band declined to raise a giant inflatable phallus in San Antonio. It closes with pragmatist accounts, Holmes's law as the 'external deposit' of our moral life and Dewey's law rooted in custom and judicial behavior, explaining why the South's history of racism and sexual anxiety seeped into Arkansas law.
- ch. 134 "Judge Richards Presides" **·** Pragmatism — The farcical Fordyce proceeding — a golf-drunk judge sipping bourbon hidden in his sock, a BBC interview mid-trial, and Richards himself pounding the gavel at the press conference ('Case closed') — confirms for the author that Holmes was right: law is prediction all the way down, at every level from the bad man to the judge. It concludes that the 'devil' of rock was a projection of white southern fear and repressed libido, externally deposited in law yet too indeterminate to predict, as the Stones' motorcade escape at 2 a.m. showed.
- ch. 135 "Chapter 16 - How Come You’re So Wrong, My Sweet Neo Con" **·** The Public Sphere — Joseph Foy introduces 'Sweet Neo Con' from A Bigger Bang (2005) as the Stones' most direct political statement — Jagger's blunt, non-metaphorical attack on Bush-era Christian hypocrisy, phony patriotism, and corporatist cronyism. Noting that the left (Michael Moore, Bill Moyers) cheered while the right (Matt Drudge) called it geriatric pandering, he argues both sides misread the song, which actually crystallizes the band's lifelong role in defining the public sphere.
- ch. 136 "No Place for a Street Fighting Man" **·** The Public Sphere — This section explains why the Stones seem apolitical beside Lennon's bed-ins and Dylan's protest songs: they rarely joined overt activism, and 'Street Fighting Man' remained only a hint. It then argues the judgment rests on too narrow a conception of the public sphere — the space connecting state and private life — because by legitimizing an anti-establishment counter-cultural identity and pushing rebellion into the mainstream (capped by the SS-uniform protest after Ed Sullivan censored 'Let's Spend the Night Together'), the Stones did political work without electoral politics.
- ch. 137 "Like a Rollin’ Stone" **·** The Public Sphere — Building the strong/weak publics distinction — rally-goers pursue decisions, concert-goers form identity — the section argues the Stones' influence runs through 'political socialization': prolonged emotional attachment to a rebellious worldview, from the tongue logo on dorm walls to 'Satisfaction's' indictment of mainstream media, gradually makes fans more distrustful of centralized power and state enforcement of cultural agendas. That slow identity shift, not candidate endorsement, is their politics.
- ch. 138 "My Sweet Neo Con" *(no index entry yet)* — This section reconstructs neo-conservatism from Daniel Bell and Irving Kristol — ex-radicals disillusioned by Stalinism who, unlike traditional conservatives, would wield state power to restore traditional values — as the precise opposite of the Stones' licentious image, with Altamont's killing of Meredith Hunter cited as the movement's stock exhibit. It quotes Allan Bloom's portrait of Jagger as a 'shrewd, middle-class boy' playing the possessed demon while winking at the money-handlers, and Robert Bork's Slouching Towards Gomorrah: the neo-con fear that if Jagger is happily 'beyond the law', so is every one of his fans.
- ch. 139 "Oh No, Not You Again" *(no index entry yet)* — The section details 'Sweet Neo Con's' specific targets: Pentagon money over social programs, profiteering by Halliburton and KBR, unilateral regime-change warfare, and Eisenhower's revived 'military-industrial complex' — so scathing that Richards, living in the United States, worried about audience blowback. Its thesis is that the song breaks nothing new: it merely makes overt the anti-authority battle lines the band had always drawn covertly.
- ch. 140 "If You Turn Out Right, I’ll Eat My Hat Tonight" **·** The Public Sphere — The short closing section argues that in the mass-media age the line between political message and private life has dissolved: 'nearly all activities are now political', so sorting public-sphere discourse from private identity formation no longer tracks anything real. The Stones' covert 1960s rebellion and the overt 'Sweet Neo Con' are therefore one continuous project of resisting the single social mold neo-conservatives tried to impose — and Foy bets it will not be the last confrontation.
- ch. 141 "Chapter 17 - Keef for President" *(front)*
- ch. 142 "My Name Is Called Disturbance" *(no index entry yet)* — This section auditions Keith for the presidency by separating the actor's art — Reagan, Ventura, and Franken winning office by expertly provoking audiences' emotions — from the musician's. Keith's creativity runs the other way: a private 'aesthetic conversation' with his own soul and the muses rather than empathy for a character, which is why his music is believable where actors' albums fail. Whether he should be president thus turns on what values that refined instinct about 'the good' would supply in a leader.
- ch. 143 "A Palace Revolution" **·** Plato — Ring of Gyges (why be moral?); Tripartite Soul; The Philosopher King; Justice in the Republic — The fantasy election brings in Plato's Republic: Richards's defense attorney Bill Carter functions as his Ring of Gyges, letting him vanish from legal consequences and raising Plato's question of whether we act justly only from fear of punishment. The tripartite soul is mapped onto Keef — appetites (drugs, sex, Jagger's 'Bitch'), spirit (the Glimmer Twins' rivalry), rationality (busting his head reaching for a library book) — and justice becomes the harmony under rationality's rule. In the ideal city of philosopher kings, auxiliaries, and workers, the sign at the gate reads 'NO POETS, NO ROCK'N'ROLLERS': Richards is exactly the corrupting poet Plato bars.
- ch. 144 "Shout and Scream and Kill the King" **·** Plato — Tripartite Soul; The Philosopher King — The election plays out: Richards blows away the auxiliaries with 'Play with Fire' and whips the working-class appetites into a frenzy (put on Exile on Main Street after a few rounds to test Plato's fear of the poets). Young Keef's soul is appetite-led — the 'Blue Lena' Bentley with its hidden drug compartment embodies a spirit in service of drugs and artistic hunger, with rationality demoted to scoring the fix. The resulting Republic of Keef, driven fast on the edge of a cliff, vindicates Plato's warning that wherever the appetites lead, tyranny soon follows — as the author concedes through his own pipe-smoking.
- ch. 145 "Compromise Solution" **·** Plato — Justice in the Republic — This section pushes back on Plato: the author has never met anyone as harmonious as the just man and calls the philosopher king 'a fairytale'. Quoting Republic Book VIII's portrait of the democratic soul that 'lives from day to day indulging the appetite of the hour', he concedes the description fits his own life and polity — yet insists that a life without good sex, good food, and good conversation would be 'bereft of serious value', which sets up the book's compromise.
- ch. 146 "What Can a Poor Boy Do, but Sing in a Rock’n’Roll Band" **·** Plato — Justice in the Republic — The finale concedes Plato's point that justice involves self-control — enough to make a voter pause at the sexual appetites of JFK and Bill Clinton — while refusing the ascetic cure and the President-Keith ticket. The author decides he could not live under Keef's rule but wants him permanently at the edge of town, playing a juke joint: a spirit worth tuning into as a reminder that control is 'ultimately an illusion', visited on midnight rides and returned from before morning comes.
- ch. 147 "V - Undercover of the Knight" *(front)*
- ch. 148 "Chapter 18 - Sympathy for The Stones" *(front)*
- ch. 149 "Killing One “Traditional” Bird with Five Stones" *(front)*
- ch. 150 "The Devil Went Down to Altamont" *(no index entry yet)* — This chapter-opening section recounts how 'Sympathy for the Devil' tauntingly catalogs historical horrors — Christ's passion, the Romanovs, the Holocaust, the Kennedys, the Hundred Years War — over a samba rhythm, and how violence broke out at Altamont as it was performed, leaving four dead including Meredith Hunter (stabbed during 'Under My Thumb'). It argues the satanic label misses the point: the lyrics are really 'a rough sketch of virtue and a call for societal compassion', the kind of reorientation of the soul — a periagoge — that befitted even St. Augustine.
