- ch. 151 "What Happens in Carthage Stays in Carthage" **·** Augustine of Hippo — The Problem of Evil — The section likens Augustine's adolescence in Carthage — the Las Vegas of his day — to a rolling-stone youth of sex and sin, noting that Jagger was actually the more responsible teen, enrolling at the London School of Economics. Its key move: in the song's catalog of atrocities the Devil only watches ('I watched with glee') and never causes, so if Lucifer is not the guilty party, the evil must be human — exactly the question Augustine's Confessions was written to answer.
- ch. 152 "(I Can’t Get No) Satisfaction . . . Or the Satisfaction that I Ought To" **·** Augustine of Hippo — The Problem of Evil — Augustine's theory of evil is explained through a concert-photography analogy: a dark photo of a brightly lit stage contains no real darkness, just a camera lacking proper exposure, fixable with editing. Likewise, evil is not an entity or polar opposite of good but a privation — something missing the 'good-making' features it ought to have — so no Devil is needed to explain the darkness in the world or in us.
- ch. 153 "Who’s to Blame" **·** Augustine of Hippo — The Problem of Evil; The Divided Will — The section turns to Augustine's pear theft, where 'it was the sin that sweetened' the act: evil is chosen for the thrill of the forbidden, not forced by any demonic power. It names this 'moral impotence' — knowing the right yet willingly choosing the wrong, as with a missed curfew — an instinct no era has cured. The background vocals' 'Who? Who?' and Jagger's 'I'll tell you one time, you're to blame' thus land the blame for history's catastrophes squarely on 'you and me'.
- ch. 154 "Sympathy for The Stones" **·** Socrates — This section stages the Stones' defense as a rerun of Socrates' trial in the Apology, charge by charge: atheism (deflected by the song's own invocation of Lucifer and of right and wrong), sophistry (the Stones were not in it for money in the 1960s, as Socrates took no fees and lived poor), and corrupting the youth (Socrates never sought his crowds — they found him, just as fans camped out for Stones tickets). Like Socrates, who accepted his death sentence while Athens committed 'the great sin against philosophy', the band was exiled to the south of France rather than back down, and music was the better for its refusal.
- ch. 155 "Chapter 19 - How Mick Learned to Love the Devil" **·** Augustine of Hippo — Richard Berger opens Chapter 19 with the biographical key to 'Sympathy for the Devil': Marianne Faithfull gave Jagger Mikhail Bulgakov's The Master and Margarita during their relationship, and the song — along with 'Sister Morphine' and 'Wild Horses' — grew from that literary influence. The novel is itself a critique of Saint Augustine's philosophy of evil, which makes 'Sympathy' a very moral song: a response to and updating of Augustine rather than a hymn to black magic.
- ch. 156 "Just as Every Cop Is a Criminal, and All the Sinners Saints" **·** Augustine of Hippo, Plato — The Problem of Evil; The Divided Will; Fate and Foreknowledge — This section lays out Augustine's journey from the Manichean two-gods heresy, through reading Plato, to the view of evil as an 'absence' rather than a force. Its core is the dilemma of Confessions 7.3: if God is wholly good, who sowed the 'seed of bitterness' — the wicked will — into us, and who made the devil? Augustine's answer runs through original sin (the pride of Adam and Eve, demons as fallen angels) and an awkward compromise on which humans are free yet constrained by predestination, so that evil becomes necessary — and confession itself a good.
- ch. 157 "Please Allow Me to Introduce Myself" **·** Augustine of Hippo — Fate and Foreknowledge — The section tells how Bulgakov's novel, begun in 1928, stifled by Stalin, and published in English only in 1967, reached Jagger through Faithfull in 1968. It summarizes the opening: Satan disguised as Professor Woland interrupts two Moscow atheists, savages the literary elite's vanity and secularism, and argues in explicitly Augustinian, predestinarian terms that man cannot even vouch for his own tomorrow — in a Russia so Godless it takes the Devil himself to restore belief, since everyone, Devil included, is part of the divine plan.
- ch. 158 "And I Was ’Round when Jesus Christ Had His Moment of Doubt and Pain" **·** Augustine of Hippo — Fate and Foreknowledge — This section follows the novel-within-the-novel — the Master's gospel of Yeshua Ha-Nozri before a reluctant Pilate, with Matthew the Levite embellishing the miracles — and its frame: Margarita's masked ball of the risen dead, Berlioz's talking severed head, and God's final order to Woland granting the lovers peace and forgiving even Pilate. Bulgakov thus agrees with Augustine that evil exists yet all of us carry good that can unite with the Divine by turning from the material world; his Satan, operating 'like an employee of a large organization' inside God's constraints, is why we naturally have sympathy for this Devil.
- ch. 159 "But What’s Puzzling You Is the Nature of My Game" **·** Augustine of Hippo — Fate and Foreknowledge; The Problem of Evil — The section situates the song after the 1967 Redlands Bust and amid 1968's collapses — Paris riots, Vietnam, Bobby Kennedy's assassination, which forced the lyric change from 'Who killed Kennedy?' to 'Who killed the Kennedys?' the night before a recording session. Against both Augustine and Bulgakov, it argues Jagger's Devil is far more human and unpredestined: by demanding we 'guess my name', the song fingers us as Satan, tracing the world's malice to our own impulses rather than any divinely scripted plan.
- ch. 160 "I Tell You One Time, You’re to Blame" **·** Augustine of Hippo — Fate and Foreknowledge; The Problem of Evil — The closing section follows the song's aftermath through Godard's session film — Brian Jones inaudible in his booth as a Dylanesque draft turns into a 'rocking samba' — to Jones's death, the Altamont killing (shown by Gimme Shelter to have happened during 'Under My Thumb', not 'Sympathy'), and the end of Mick and Marianne. Its verdict: Augustine's narrow, predestined free will leaves humanity out of the picture, while Jagger's human Devil puts responsibility back on us — a world without the Stones would be less enlightened about evil, and their song 'demands more of us, perhaps, than Augustine's Confessions'.
- ch. 161 "Chapter 20 - A Devil’s Trick of Opposites" *(not yet read)*
- ch. 162 "It’s Just that Demon Life Has Got Me in Its Sway" **·** Augustine of Hippo — The Problem of Evil — Reads "Sympathy for the Devil" through Saint Augustine's Confessions rather than only through Bulgakov's Master and Margarita, which supposedly inspired the lyrics. Augustine's stolen pears and his confession that he loved evil "solely because it was evil" show him needing "the companionship of others sinning," which the chapter pairs with the song's "Who killed the Kennedys?... It was you and me" and with Lee Harvey Oswald's refusal to have acted alone. But the paths split: Jagger's Devil makes evil real and ours, while for Augustine evil has no positive existence at all.
- ch. 163 "It’s Only Rock’n’Roll, But I Complicate It" **·** Augustine of Hippo, Plato — The Problem of Evil; The Divided Will — Casts Augustine as the first Christian rock artist — a rebellious, sex-obsessed convert who, like Plato, ranked the body below the soul — and explains his privation doctrine: "evil has no positive nature; but the loss of good has received the name 'evil.'" Evil is "a swerving of the will which is turned toward lower things," so free will is crucial: God permits evil because virtues like courage could not exist in a world without it. The chapter closes with the constellation of opposites (soul/body, pride/humility, temporal/eternal) Augustine says we must live through to approach philosophical truth.
- ch. 164 "I Shouted Out, Who Killed Meredith Hunter" **·** Augustine of Hippo — The Problem of Evil — Takes the refrain "what's puzzling you is the nature of my game" to mean the song presents the Devil as a real, personal force present at the crucifixion, the Russian Revolution, World War II and the Kennedy assassinations — not the mere lack of goodness Augustine allows. It cites Richards's 2002 remark, on the heels of 9/11, that confronting evil puts him "out of a job," and reads "Sweet Neo Con" as Jagger still indicting ideology. Against Augustine's weak-willed sinner, "every cop is a criminal" locates evil in human strength and resolve — with Godard's Eve Democracy answering "Yes" to "Is the Devil God in exile?"
- ch. 165 "Under Our Thumb" **·** Augustine of Hippo — The Problem of Evil — Corrects the popular error that Meredith Hunter was killed while "Sympathy for the Devil" played: eyewitnesses and the Gimme Shelter footage show the murder happened during "Under My Thumb" at the Altamont free concert of December 6, 1969. It surveys the assigning of blame — Rolling Stone captioning Jagger's picture "Is Mick responsible for the killing?", Norma Coates blaming the era's burden on the counterculture — and concludes Jagger sides against Augustine's privation view: Hunter did not die "at the hands of a bunch of privations," since the evil the band saw was an in-your-face reality.
- ch. 166 "A Wild Murder Couldn’t Drag Me Away" **·** Augustine of Hippo — The Divided Will; The Problem of Evil — Draws Augustine and Jagger together as brothers who fell far before finding restraint: Augustine's many conflicting wills must collapse into one good will by actually choosing, and his Manichean past taught him some truths must be performed or willed, like his "grant me chastity — just not yet." The chapter reads Gimme Shelter as likewise double — harrowing documentary or joyous concert footage, forced to choose — and argues the post-Altamont Stones had to make "Sympathy for the Devil" a performative force for understanding evil rather than a scapegoat, then stayed in the flesh, confronting good and evil through the blues.
- ch. 167 "Chapter 21 - Lucifer Rising and Falling" **·** Augustine of Hippo — The Problem of Evil — Opens Dan Dinello's chapter with Richards's 1971 "Everybody's Lucifer" and sets up its two rival accounts of evil: the Christian Satan enshrined by Augustine — Lucifer expelled for prideful disobedience, seducing God's weak-willed children into sin — and the moral authorities of the 1960s (press, police, clergy) who cast the Stones as "Satan's Jesters" luring youth astray. The Stones answered with a nineteenth-century Romantic Lucifer, the rebellious angel fighting moral repression, blaming the mob of hypocritical authorities for the world's horrors.
- ch. 168 "Diabolical Beginnings" *(no index entry yet)* — Traces the "devil's music" pedigree from the blues (Elmore James, Howlin' Wolf, Robert Johnson's crossroads legend) into the Stones' cultivated notoriety — urine arrests, drug busts, "Satisfaction" as an anthem against authority, "Let's Spend the Night Together" scandalizing the London Palladium. It catalogs the establishment's hysteria (Daily Mirror, Chicago Daily News) and closes with Allan Bloom's The Closing of the American Mind, which calls Jagger a nihilist "demon" and "satyr" who "legitimated drugs" and was "beyond the law, moral and political."
- ch. 169 "From Satyrs to Martyrs" **·** Augustine of Hippo — The Problem of Evil; The Two Cities — Shows how Augustine forged the machinery of Christian evil — sex as sin, the invention of Original Sin, The City of God's mandate to compel heretics, and the goat-god Pan as Satan's visual model — then maps it onto the 1960s persecution of the Stones: press mockery, police drug busts, Bill Wyman hauled into court as a "shaggy monster." The persecution backfired, making the Stones martyrs and pushing Jagger toward the self-embraced "Lucifers of Rock" image of "Paint It, Black" and the Their Satanic Majesties Request cover.
- ch. 170 "Invocation of My Demon Brother" **·** Anarchism — Connects the Stones' demonic turn to the counterculture's occult fashions — the Levitate the Pentagon rally, Rosemary's Baby — and to filmmaker Kenneth Anger, disciple of Aleister Crowley, whose "Do What Thou Wilt" law espoused a form of anarchism and whose Lucifer was the light-bearing god, not Christianity's dark devil. Anger cast Jagger as a latter-day Lucifer for Lucifer Rising (with Richards as Beelzebub), and the chapter quotes Jagger's 1967 claim that the teenage "telepathy" at his shows was "a protest against the system," while reading "Jumpin' Jack Flash" through the Spring Heeled Jack folklore.
- ch. 171 "Flowers of Evil" *(no index entry yet)* — Expounds the Romantic conception of evil the Stones projected: reacting to the French, American and Industrial revolutions, William Blake's Marriage of Heaven and Hell made Satan a symbol of energy, creativity and liberation from oppressive reason — "the road of excess leads to the palace of wisdom" — while Charles Baudelaire's Flowers of Evil found in Satan a figure of political revolution and dark, seductive beauty. Like "Sympathy for the Devil," Flowers of Evil was misread as satanic: Baudelaire's real claim is that humans, not Satan, created hell on earth, and externalizing evil as a mythological creature stops us confronting and defeating it within ourselves.
- ch. 172 "The Devil Is My Name" **·** The Problem of Evil — Reports Jagger's own genealogy of the song — "an old idea of Baudelaire's," Bulgakov's Master and Margarita (given to him by Marianne Faithfull), and his Lucifer Rising conversations with Kenneth Anger — and follows its studio metamorphosis from country dirge to voodoo samba under Godard's camera. The finished recording, whose working title was "The Devil is My Name," builds into a celebration of humanity's hatred and hypocrisy from the crucifixion through the Blitzkrieg to the Kennedys (pluralized when RFK was shot on June 5, 1968, during the sessions), putting humanity on trial by the devil rather than the other way around.
- ch. 173 "Lucifer’s Dream Ends" **·** The Ethics of Political Violence — Narrates the collapse of the Lucifer project through 1968-69: "Street Fighting Man" banned as incitement to violence after Mayor Daley's police beat protestors, Jagger reading Shelley's "Adonais" at Hyde Park after Brian Jones's death, and Anger salvaging Invocation of My Demon Brother from the stalled film. At Altamont the Hell's Angels stabbed Meredith Hunter during "Under My Thumb" while Jagger pleaded "just cool out"; Lester Bangs blamed egoism, hype and greed rather than the devil, and the Stones survived by trading rage and chaos for controlled spectacle — from Rock Satans to a knighted Elder Statesman of Rock.
- ch. 174 "Chapter 22 - Frenzy" **·** The Apollonian and the Dionysian — Opens Randall Auxier's chapter with the 2011 Vancouver Stanley Cup riot and its viral kissing-couple photograph to pose the driving question: why do built-up group emotion, sex and violence fuse so explosively, and why does that fusion in public still "get yourself in trouble with the Man"? The contrast is pointed: the laid-back West Coast crowd "right across the Bay from hippie central" couldn't get through a 1969 Stones concert without violence and death — "It's just a shot away, you know?"
- ch. 175 "Please Allow Me to Introduce Myself" **·** The Apollonian and the Dionysian — Confesses that Brian Jones — violent and cruel even in Keith Richards's autobiography, with no kind words from Mick and Charlie on the fortieth anniversary of his death — is for Auxier the face of the Sixties' negative energy and the figure "laughing with delight" in Don McLean's "American Pie" verse on Altamont. The section sets up the whole chapter as a meditation on "what gets sacrificed on the altar of rock'n'roll, when the Dionysian frenzy turns ugly."
- ch. 176 "The Nature of My Game" **·** The Apollonian and the Dionysian — Distinguishes the individual craziness of a Brian Jones (and perhaps Meredith Hunter waving his pistol at Altamont) from the crowd frenzy that takes over when the Stones play, quoting Richards's Life memories of girls who "lost themselves" — "beyond what they wanted to be... You stood as much chance in a fucking river full of piranhas." Richards says "the music might be the trigger, but the bullet, nobody knows what that is"; Auxier argues the Stones deliberately crafted personae and performances that tap a deep human susceptibility to frenzy, and goes hunting for the bullet.
- ch. 177 "Reading the Riot Act" **·** The Apollonian and the Dionysian — Reads Stones-concert riots — the Wisbech Corn Exchange brawl Richards recounts in Life — against the British Riot Act of 1714 and the purely aesthetic riot at the pagan ballet The Rite of Spring in 1913, noting conventional crowds forgive a sports brawl more readily than a Stones tragedy. In every such release of mass emotion there is a moment when "all order has disappeared and we come face to face with... whatever it is 'we' are without that imposed order," leaving nothing for memory to hang on — and the chapter insists the scary tone of violence formed around the Stones in a way it never did around Beatlemania.
- ch. 178 "A Little Help from Their Friends" *(no index entry yet)* — Argues the frenzy needed "a little help from their friends": on the first US tour nobody knew the band and nothing happened — even in Texas — until manager Andrew Oldham planted screaming teens in a New York audience, giving crowds "a context and a clue" to abandon decorum. The Led Zeppelin comparison — same dark blues, sex and drugs, but no riots — suggests the times made the frenzy possible, yet the chapter still holds the Stones pushed collective emotion past "the line between light and dark."
- ch. 179 "Gimme that Old Time Religion" **·** Plato, Socrates — The Apollonian and the Dionysian; Philosophy of Love — Carries the frenzy back to the Eleusinian Mysteries and the Dionysian festivals that were falling into ill repute by Plato's day: Socrates himself was an initiate, and Plato wrote on how eros makes humans "mad with passion," whitewashed his teacher in the Symposium, and claimed Socrates never got drunk. Plato also held that certain kinds of music ignite desire and lead to weakness and "effeminacy," associating manliness with rational self-restraint and advocating banning such modes — in short, "no Stones concert allowed in Plato's city."
- ch. 180 "I’ve Been Around for a Long, Long Year" *(no index entry yet)* — Argues ritual — music, dance and sex at its core — long predates writing, citing the 10,000 B.C.E. site of Gobekli Tepe, and that fertility cults slid into human and animal sacrifice, fusing sex and death in ways "we don't like to look at." It introduces Georges Bataille's "accursed share" — the surplus energy of big-brained, bored humans — and his uncomfortably clinical account of the proximity of death and sensual desire as the frame for the Stones' debauchery, "more of an upgraded resurgence" of the cults than a degraded echo.
- ch. 181 "I’m in Need of Some Restraint" **·** Moral Relativism; Food Ethics — Tells how the Abraham and Isaac story introduced "redemption," a substitution rite that curbed the "pride of place" of first-borns while replacing human sacrifice — a transition the Egyptians and Greeks also made independently. Using Shirley Jackson's "The Lottery," the volunteering Druid victims and the martyr Perpetua, it argues any horror can be normalized by piety and tradition — Sarah Palin or Michele Bachmann would have defended first-born sacrifice in ancient Ur — and needles the reader with ritual chicken sacrifice versus KFC: "which of those two, in a just world, would really be more inhumane?"
- ch. 182 "Stole Many a Man’s Soul and Faith" *(no index entry yet)* — Extends the normalization point to the present: Mel Gibson's Apocalypto versus his own gory Passion of the Christ, and the Eucharist as "eating the god," a pagan survival in Christianity that conventional believers consume on Sundays. It identifies the underlying need as "enthusiasm" — the transcendent build-up of group emotion aimed at gods, the Dallas Cowboys or the Rolling Stones — which the mainline churches killed and declined over, Pentecostal revivals and soccer matches feed, and "everybody everywhere" gets at a Stones concert.
- ch. 183 "Use All Your Well-learned Politesse" **·** Friedrich Nietzsche — The Apollonian and the Dionysian — Sets Johann Jakob Bachofen's ruined-reputation matriarchy hypothesis (and Marija Gimbutas's "civilization of the goddess") against patriarchal control, finding the division alive in the male insecurity displayed whenever men prance for girls — Elvis, the Beatles and the Stones alike. Bachofen's young friend Friedrich Nietzsche named the two energies: Apollonian regimentation and order versus Dionysian overflow, "the accursed share," and the chapter reads the 1960s as one more Dionysian break-out that the "heavy-handed, patriarchal Cold War" could not bottle up.
- ch. 184 "Mother’s Little Helpers" **·** Friedrich Nietzsche — The Apollonian and the Dionysian; Master and Slave Morality — Introduces the Cambridge scholar Jane Ellen Harrison, whose books trace Greek religion from a swampy matriarchy to the Olympian age, with Dionysus as the holy child of the Mother — flayed and divided among the faithful — the very pattern Christianity retold in the Jesus story. Auxier credits Jagger's "keen intellect" and wide sense of history, then endorses Nietzsche's complaint that Christianity is "a puny, slave morality" that renders humanity effeminate: "That's exactly what I like about Christianity," since over-weaning masculinity needs opposing.
- ch. 185 "Won’t You Guess My Name" **·** The Apollonian and the Dionysian; Gender Performativity — Riffs on Harrison's Themis to cast Mick as Kouros, the golden boy served by the Kouretes — "the original name of the Rolling Stones" — until he surfaces as Dionysus among the Olympians, "sort of like the irony of Mick Jagger becoming a Knight of the Realm." It splits the males into orgy-tolerant girly-men and woman-hating "manly men" of the Sour Grapes Clubs, warns not to underestimate the girly-men, and contrasts Elvis, who went into the army and became acceptable, with Mick, who kept the Kouretes under his thumb — deliberately feminine, dangerous, and cooler than the Beatles.
- ch. 186 "“They’re So Ugly It’s Appealing”" **·** The Apollonian and the Dionysian; Gender Performativity — Takes the screaming fan's verdict ("They're so ugly it's appealing") and Ian Stewart's sidelining for looking "like a man" as evidence the Stones deliberately cultivated an androgynous ugliness to violate gendered expectations — "they weren't just a bunch of guys who bent the rules, they were off-sides." The blatant misogyny of the lyrics is read as compensation for that effeminacy among the Dionysian libation bearers in a patriarchal age, keeping the manly men at arm's length — while noting only Brian Jones actually abused women.
- ch. 187 "Pleased to Meet You" **·** The Apollonian and the Dionysian; Feminism — Blames "the Father of All Pissing Contests" — the Cold War — for forcing English boys who love women into songs hostile to them ("Under My Thumb," "Stupid Girl," "Yesterday's Papers"), and argues two theses: the Stones don't hate women, this culture hates women; and the men who call the goddess's servants "fags" are the ones who built and maintain that misogynistic culture. The Dionysian frenzy of the 1960s took a dark turn as 1968's death toll (Vietnam, MLK, RFK) exposed the world the manly men had built: the Beatles quit, "but the Stones looked the demon square in the eye and guessed its name."
- ch. 188 "From Woodstock to Altamont" **·** The Apollonian and the Dionysian — Crowns the Stones' prophetic 1969 tour, ending at Altamont, as "the genuine Dionysian frenzy" that ended the Sixties — Woodstock being merely "a glorious and well-deserved vacation from the bullshit" — and reads Brian Jones's death as his own choosing, empires falling "for about the same reasons." Auxier recalls his father taking him to Gimme Shelter in Memphis, the movie that permanently formed his refusal of the patriarchy, and advises parents to make a friend of Mick Jagger, since "Dionysus will have his day" and will take his accursed share of your kids sooner or later.

## The Sopranos and Philosophy - I Kill Therefore I Am

- ch. 1 "The Sopranos and Philosophy" *(not yet read)*
- ch. 2 "The Sopranos and Philosophy" *(not yet read)*
- ch. 3 "The Sopranos and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 4 "The Sopranos and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 5 "The Sopranos and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 6 "The Sopranos and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 7 "The Sopranos and Philosophy" *(front)*

- ch. 10 "The Sopranos and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 11 "The Sopranos and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 12 "The Sopranos and Philosophy" **·** Jean-Paul Sartre, Viktor Frankl — Existentialism; The Absurd; The Meaning of Life — Argues The Sopranos is a morality play and existential drama, an X-rated Waiting for Godot about despair and man's search for meaning. Tony's crew are Gabbard's 'band of lost boys' fleeing Christopher's 'regularness of life' through violence: on Sartre's account they assert Being over Nothingness by acting, and on Ernest Becker's they deny death and insignificance through heroic crime. The chapter contrasts Tony's professed code of honor and family with his actual motive (the thrill of the hunt, unlike Vito Corleone's necessity) and frames the series' tension as his Janus-faced attempt to be a Machiavellian boss by day and a loving family man by night, a dichotomy he cannot sustain.
- ch. 13 "The Sopranos and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 14 "The Sopranos and Philosophy" **·** Sun Tzu, Plato — Strategy Without Conflict; Yin and Yang; The Tao (the Way) — Argues Tony misappropriates Sun Tzu's Art of War: he applies tactical precepts like deception, formlessness and 'Balk the enemy's power' (citing the book in 'He Is Risen' after Melfi's jab in 'Big Girls Don't Cry'), but ignores the philosophical core. The chapter contrasts Sun Tzu's monistic one-world view of yin-yang interdependence, harmony and winning without fighting with the Greek two-world dualism of Plato and with the Christianity that lets Tony tell Melfi 'soldiers don't go to hell' in 'From Where to Eternity'. Tony's sausage panic attacks in 'Fortunate Son' show his failure to see how his crimes ripple through his family, and because he lacks the commander's exemplary character and harmony, Tony is 'just a soldier', not a ruler.
- ch. 15 "The Sopranos and Philosophy" **·** Plato, Aristotle, Epicurus — Tripartite Soul; Eudaimonia (flourishing); Epicurean Pleasure (ataraxia) — Argues ancient ethics explains why Tony, who has 'the world by the balls', still feels like a loser. Plato's Republic diagnoses him as a 'civil war' of unintegrated motivations (loving father and murderer at once, as in 'College'), a condition power over others only worsens; Aristotle teaches that happiness is activity with self-sufficient, integrated desires rather than a list of possessions, and Epicurus's 'sober reasoning' is the therapy Tony never gets. The Hawthorne quotation Tony reads in 'College' and his Gary Cooper ideal from 'The Happy Wanderer' frame the point: Cooper in High Noon is unmesswithable because he does not need vengeance, whereas Tony's compulsive revenge shows the world has the grip on him.
- ch. 16 "The Sopranos and Philosophy" **·** Friedrich Nietzsche, Albert Camus — Ressentiment; Existentialism; The Absurd — Argues the show's underlying moral vision is Nietzschean passive nihilism filtered through film noir. Tony, unlike the active nihilist who converts value-collapse into self-overcoming, sinks into resentment and life-negation: he wants to rip the throat of the 'happy wanderer', therapy never redeems him, and he quits it with 'I'm a fat fucking crook from New Jersey' in 'Calling All Cars'. The chapter traces the noir lineage (Tony watching White Heat, the Godfather references, Bogart imagery at Satriale's) and reads A.J.'s 'D-Girl' existentialism (Camus's The Stranger, 'There is no God') and Livia's 'It's all a big nothing' as the family inheritance of meaninglessness and death-dread.
- ch. 17 "The Sopranos and Philosophy" **·** Niccolò Machiavelli — Virtù and Fortuna; The Ends Justify the Means — Argues Tony is at times a practitioner of The Prince and ignores Machiavelli at his peril. He plays the fox with Junior (the figurehead-boss arrangement of 'Meadowlands', the Augustus 'never ate alone' lesson of 'Pax Soprana') and his virtù makes Richie's coup unsellable, but with Richie he seeks love instead of fear, and with Ralph he violates the advice to abstain from subjects' property and women (taking over Pie-O-My and sleeping with his goomah), which ends in Ralph's murder. Fortune, Machiavelli's river, runs through Chris's double-parked car, Janice shooting Richie, Gigi's death on the pishtoon, and the Season Four economic downturn Tony failed to build dykes against.
- ch. 18 "The Sopranos and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 19 "The Sopranos and Philosophy" **·** Augustine of Hippo — Ethical Egoism — Argues that bracketing his crimes, Tony is an 'ethical manager': against Junior's vanity-driven blunders and Christopher's Scarface-style impulsiveness in 'Meadowlands', Tony shows stakeholder judgment - holding the line on business through Annalisa's sexual provocation in the Naples cave ('Commendatori'), defusing war with Carmine over the Esplanade ('Whitecaps'), and following Melfi's Eldercare advice to give Junior 'the illusion of being in control'. The chapter concedes Tony acts within an egoistic horizon of self, family and crew rather than universal principle, but finds real moral self-restraint in him, invoking Augustine's dictum that 'even robbers take care to maintain peace with their comrades' and Tony's own 'I dunno about morals, but I do got rules'.
- ch. 20 "The Sopranos and Philosophy" **·** David Hume, George Berkeley — Reason Is the Slave of the Passions; Moral Relativism — Argues Simple Subjectivism ('right for Tony') fails as a reading of the show because it cannot make sense of genuine moral disagreement - Carmela and Tony's adultery battle in 'Whitecaps', Agent Ciccerone versus Tony on extortion - nor ground outrage at the killings of Pussy and the sobbing Matt Bevilaqua. Humean ethical subjectivism, grounded in a common human nature and the sentiment of sympathy (with Berkeley's pin example to explain vice as perception-dependent), fares better: it lets us condemn Tony while explaining his warped sympathy - grieving Pie-O-My more than his human victims - as the product of a mob upbringing that Melfi's alexithymia diagnosis in 'House Arrest' targets.
- ch. 21 "The Sopranos and Philosophy" **·** Henry Sidgwick — Consequentialism — Argues Tony is a good man and a bad man for the same reason: a moral code of partiality toward family, crew and Italian-Americans ('This family comes before everything else' in 'Fortunate Son', avenging Janice, caring for Livia 'because she's my mother'), whereas morality must ultimately be justified from the impartial point of view (Sidgwick's 'point of view of the universe'). Using the drowning case - save Meadow or two strangers - the chapter shows partiality can receive an impartial justification, then applies this to Dr. Melfi's decision to treat Tony, defensible only while harms to others (her patient's suicide when she is driven into hiding) do not outweigh the good of his treatment.
- ch. 22 "The Sopranos and Philosophy" **·** Carol Gilligan, Nel Noddings, Immanuel Kant — The Ethics of Care; Caring as the Ground of Ethics; Feminism — Answers the title with 'fugheddaboutit' - Carmela is no feminist - but argues she reasons as a Gilligan-style care ethicist. Her moral voice evolves from Church-based justice arguments against Angie's divorce ('Commendatori'), through the justice-tradition confrontation with Dr. Krakower's refusal of 'blood money' ('Second Opinion'), to mature care ethics that balances self with others when she throws Tony out after the Svetlana revelation ('Whitecaps'). Using Noddings on reciprocity and 'total engrossment', the chapter reads the marriage's failure as failed caring, and the Furio romance and the Georgetown ricotta-pie intimidation as care reasoning that defies the submissive female stereotype feminists fear care ethics valorizes.
- ch. 23 "The Sopranos and Philosophy" **·** René Descartes, Thomas Hobbes, John Locke — Communitarianism; The Cogito; The Social Contract — Argues Tony's self is communitarian - constituted by his roles as father, husband, don and executioner - against the Liberal metaphysics of individualism descending from Descartes's cogito (Livia's 'in the end you die in your own arms') and Hobbes's state of nature and social contract, with the mob boss miscast as a Hobbesian sovereign ('a supreme commander at the top, not the fuckin' Dave Clark Five'). The Naples trip in 'Commendatori' (Paulie discovering he is not really 'Napoletan''), the Columbus Day violence of 'Christopher', and the endless Godfather and Goodfellas mimicry (Christopher's bakery foot-shooting, Tony's Se7en-like murder of Ralphie) show identity as narrative, performed with and through others, not possessed by an isolated self.
- ch. 24 "The Sopranos and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 25 "The Sopranos and Philosophy" *(no index entry yet)* — Argues the 'sympathy for the Devil' puzzle - why law-abiding viewers bear a pro-attitude toward Tony while abhorring his real-world counterpart - is genuine, and dismantles three rival explanations: fascination (Richie Aprile is just as oxymoronic yet earns no sympathy), wish fulfillment (Ralph is as incontinent and is hated), and identification (imagining one shares Tony's desires yields absurdities like being jealous of his affair with Valentina). The solution is alliance: within the show's fallen moral world of corrupt cops, blackmailing FBI agents, hypocritical priests and monstrous Livia, Tony is relatively the most moral major character ('I knew his brother; he was worse'), so sympathy for the fictional Tony is consistent with loathing an actual one - and Chase uses our alliance to dramatize the danger of rationalization.
- ch. 26 "The Sopranos and Philosophy" **·** Plato — Argues against Plato's and Tolstoy's worry that art corrupts by infecting audiences with a character's emotions: The Sopranos does get us to identify with a killer (feeling relief when Tony strangles Febby Petrulio in 'College'), and its serial length, psychological intimacy via Melfi's office, and verisimilitude make Tony unusually attractive. But the show supplies alternative moral centers - Melfi's own therapy, the 'House Arrest' shark-and-alexithymia scene, her restraint after her rape in 'Employee of the Month', the 'Funhouse' graduation-versus-crime montage - that let viewers see Tony from outside, so the series invites a complex, reflective moral response rather than imitation and may even be good for us.
- ch. 27 "The Sopranos and Philosophy" **·** Aristotle — Argues Season One is an Aristotelian tragedy: Tony is the 'man in-between' who earns pity and fear, and his tragic flaw is not his sins but his inability to bear his conflicting family roles, manifest in the panic attacks that drive him into therapy. Like Oedipus fleeing Corinth, the cure brings the catastrophe - Livia exposes the therapy to Junior and the hit follows ('Cunnilingus and psychiatry brought us to this') - while the plot maps onto Greek family tragedy, Livia as Medea trying to kill her son and Tony as a failed Orestes. The Green Grove FBI tapes are the recognition scene, the loss of mother and uncle is the reversal, and catharsis is the healthy outward purging of the viewer's own bottled emotion.
- ch. 28 "The Sopranos and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 29 "The Sopranos and Philosophy" *(no index entry yet)* — Applies Austin's speech-act theory (locution, illocution, perlocution; with Strawson's condition that a listener must recognize the speaker's intention) to the show's language. Analyses include Junior's 'high colonic' insult and 'come heavy' threat in 'Meadowlands', Tony's indirect hit order 'you get him under control?' answered by Gigi's 'with extreme fuckin' prejudice', Tony's conferral of the boss title on Junior, the made-man oath and Paulie's verbal transfer of the sports book in 'Fortunate Son', and Livia's deniable illocutions - advising Junior to tax Hesh, and on the FBI tape permitting (perhaps desiring) Tony's murder - which show how a woman with no power to order exercises power through warning, advising and persuading.
- ch. 30 "The Sopranos and Philosophy" **·** René Descartes, Socrates — Argues Tony is 'self-blind': against Descartes's claim of infallible self-access and pursuing Socrates's 'know thyself', and using Shoemaker's term, Tony rationally attends to his own mind and still fails to know it. He denies knowing Pussy is a rat despite all the evidence until the 'Funhouse' dead-fish dream tells him 'You knew'; he cannot say whether he foresaw that his 'friendly' loan to Artie would ruin him ('Everybody Hurts'); and he perceives only love for Livia while rage leaks out ('You fucking bitch, that's my mother' in 'I Dream of Jeannie Cusamano'). These cases - with Christopher's unrecognized desire to get caught and Melfi's unnoticed fear of Tony - support the perceptual inner-sense model of self-knowledge over the constitutive model, since brute error about one's own states is possible.
- ch. 31 "The Sopranos and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 32 "The Sopranos and Philosophy" *(no index entry yet)* — Takes Chase's claim that the show is 'all about people who have made a deal with the Devil' and tours Dante's Inferno to assign Sopranos characters their circles. The goomah arrangement convicts no one of lust because nobody thinks it sinful, but Carmela's knowing complicity ('I have forsaken what is right for what is easy' in 'College') makes her incontinent; the killers fill Circle Seven's boiling blood, with the suicides Vin Makazian and Gloria Trillo among the trees. Fraud is punished below violence, so Silvio the pimp, Janice the lead-cloaked hypocrite, and Hesh the song-thief sink into the Eighth Circle, Melfi faces the false-counselor question via Guido da Montefeltro (absolution does not absolve), Livia and Junior belong in the ninth circle for betraying the heads of the two families, and Tony's 'soldiers don't go to hell' is left for the reader to judge.
- ch. 33 "The Sopranos and Philosophy" **·** The Problem of Evil; Free Will vs Determinism — Uses the show to argue the problem of evil is evidential rather than logical, and tests each theodicy against Sopranos cases: evil as natural-law by-product (Junior's and Jackie's cancers), punishment for sin (Carmela's cancer scare, Ralph telling Father Phil his hospitalized son is God's payback in 'Whoever Did This'), God's higher morality (rejected as meaningless as 'a triangle with four sides'), long-run goods (Artie's restaurant fire), and character-building (rejected with Melfi's 'How many people have to die for your personal growth?'). The free-will defense also fails - God could have given us Meadow-like dispositions - and Tony's 'You're born to this shit' versus Melfi's insistence on genuine choice dramatizes the debate. The chapter ends with Tony as the Old Testament Yahweh writ small: a jealous, vengeful, tribal ruler whose appeal is no more mysterious than His.
- ch. 34 "The Sopranos and Philosophy" *(notes)*
- ch. 35 "The Sopranos and Philosophy" *(notes)*
- ch. 36 "The Sopranos and Philosophy" *(notes)*

## The Ultimate Game of Thrones and Philosophy - You Think or Die

- ch. 1 "Cover" *(not yet read)*
- ch. 2 "The Ultimate Game of Thrones and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 3 "i" *(front)*
- ch. 4 "ii" *(front)*
- ch. 5 "Title Page" *(front)*
- ch. 6 "Copyright" *(front)*
- ch. 7 "Contents" *(front)*
- ch. 8 "Invitation to Initiation" *(no index entry yet)* — A short introduction that opens with the 'Hardhome' scene where Stannis tells Sam Tarly to 'keep reading' after learning from an old manuscript that dragonglass kills White Walkers, arguing that knowledge is itself a weapon in Westeros. Chaillan promises that the volume's philosophers ('Maester Kant on the King's Road,' 'Lord Machiavelli' in the Red Keep, Kierkegaard on Stannis) will initiate fans into the show's philosophical depth, from whether it is immoral to watch its violence to whether the Red Wedding can be justified.
- ch. 9 "xi" *(front)*
- ch. 10 "I. Life Is Not a Song" *(front)*
- ch. 11 "1. Arya Stark as a Rough Hero" **·** David Hume — Dadlez asks whether Game of Thrones is morally defective for making audiences root for Arya Stark, a child murderer whose victims run from the insurance seller in Braavos to Walder Frey and his sons baked into a pie. Working through Hume's 'rough heroes' worry from 'Of the Standard of Taste' against Anne Eaton's 'robust immoralism' and the halo effect, she argues Martin's text never endorses Arya's killings but shows their cost to her - her suppression of doubt before the soup-shop assassination, her nightmare repetition of the names on her kill list, her reclaiming of 'Arya Stark of Winterfell' - so our investment is empathetic concern rather than corrupting approval, and the story is not ethically flawed.
- ch. 12 "2. Ned Stark One Man in Ten Thousand" **·** Aristotle, Martha Nussbaum — The Golden Mean — Kirby reads Ned Stark's arc through Aristotle's Poetics: his excessive honor is a hamartia ('too much of a good thing'), his acceptance of Robert's summons and refusal of Renly's help and Littlefinger's plan are the peripeteia, and the fan mourning after 'Baelor' is catharsis binding viewers into a community. With Martha Nussbaum's work on Greek tragedy he then argues the series' deepest lesson is tragic conflict - Agamemnon sacrificing Iphigenia mirrored by Stannis and Shireen, and Maester Aemon's 'love is the death of duty' warning to Jon - where no hierarchy of values dissolves the moral residue, so we must suffer into learning instead of trusting philosophical prioritization.
- ch. 13 "3. There’s Only One God and His Name Is Death" **·** Plato, Aristotle — Theory of Forms — McKeever observes that central characters rarely die suddenly and unexpectedly in art even though such deaths define our emotional lives, and argues this is a 'representational limit of art,' like Hollywood ugliness and costume-drama archaic speech. Using Plato's theory of art as a copy of a copy (a picture of Tyrion's chipped goblet imitating the Form of the goblet) and Aristotle's catharsis, he then argues Martin overcomes the limit through epic scale: with so many viewpoint characters, Ned can die at the end of season one and Joffrey mid-season-four because attention simply shifts, so making a work epic is the general cure for what art cannot represent.
- ch. 14 "4. Tyrion’s Humor" **·** Immanuel Kant, Thomas Hobbes, Sigmund Freud — Hietalahti reads Tyrion's wit - slapping Joffrey, his mock confession at the Eyrie, the dwarf-jester show at the Purple Wedding - through the classic theories of humor: Kant and Kierkegaard's incongruity, the Platonic-Hobbesian superiority laugh, and Freud's hidden hostility. Against those who see only a jester, he argues Tyrion's humor is social criticism grounded in what Erich Fromm calls affirmation of life: unlike the Joker, who laughs because nothing matters, Tyrion mocks hollow ritual, sham trials, and gods who want enemies burned because everyone matters, and having lost everything in A Dance with Dragons he still refuses the poisonous 'sweet death,' holding that life itself is worth living.
- ch. 15 "II. There Are No Men Like Me, Only Me" *(front)*
- ch. 16 "5. Guilty of Being a Dwarf" **·** Jean-Paul Sartre — Existence Precedes Essence; Bad Faith; Authenticity — Engels runs Tyrion's arc through Sartre's existentialism: his facticity as a dwarf makes everyone from Catelyn to Tywin presume his guilt, while 'Never forget what you are, wear it like armor' states the Sartrean authenticity of owning facticity while freely choosing one's essence. Catelyn's dagger accusation, the comic confession at the Eyrie, the wildfire ship at Blackwater, and the trial outburst ('I am guilty of a far more monstrous crime: the crime of being a dwarf') show him choosing rather than being defined; the murders of Shae and Tywin dramatize the slide into bad faith - embracing the 'monster' essence others assigned - before his choice to join Daenerys resumes the burden of self-creation.
- ch. 17 "6. Jaime the Savior" **·** Aristotle, John Locke — Natural Rights — The Coxes defend Jaime's kingslaying with Aristotle's Nicomachean Ethics Book III on voluntary, involuntary, and 'mixed' actions: like a storm-besieged crew jettisoning cargo, or the Blackfish's 'a knife to his throat is not a valid order,' Jaime faced a duress - kill Tywin on Aerys's order or let King's Landing burn - so his regicide was a mixed action that is praiseworthy as the least harmful option rather than blameworthy. They add Locke's right of revolution, where a ruler who makes his will rather than law the rule puts himself in a state of war with his people, to argue the Mad King's 'Burn them all' tyranny justified the killing, making the reviled Kingslayer the savior of the city.
- ch. 18 "7. Jon Snow, a Misshapen Christ Figure" **·** Aristotle — Virtue Ethics (arete); Eudaimonia (flourishing) — Rosa argues Westeros is a moral wasteland stripped of exemplars - heroes die, and vice spreads by imitation, as with Joffrey, Ramsay, and the Mountain - so Jon Snow functions as the series' Christ figure: the humble hidden king revealed in Bran's vision, the reformer uniting wildlings and Watch against death, killed by his brothers in 'Mother's Mercy' and risen in 'Home.' Using Aristotle's virtue ethics - Bran's archery lesson as the image of eudaimonia, hamartia as missing the mark, character formed by watching and imitating exemplars like Ned at the deserter's execution - the chapter claims the world is redeemable only through 'minority moral reform' embodied in a complete life, as with Jesus, Gandhi, and Martin Luther King Jr.
- ch. 19 "8. Brienne and the Struggle for Recognition" **·** G.W.F. Hegel, Miranda Fricker — Struggle for Recognition; Epistemic Injustice — Giladi reads Brienne as a challenge to the Westerosi recognition order: winning the melee at Renly's camp and requesting the Kingsguard, she embodies knightly virtue in a society that deems it naturally male, and the crowd's bewildered, muted applause is a recognition injustice that withholds the social conditions of her self-confidence and authenticity. He analyzes Jaime's mockery in 'The Prince of Winterfell' - sexualizing her as 'boring as you are ugly' where he would never treat Barristan Selmy so - as stripping Brienne of her status as a conversational peer and equal rational enquirer, while the attempted gang-rape by Locke's men in 'Walk of Punishment' shows the violent backlash her struggle for recognition provokes.
- ch. 20 "III. Power Is Power" *(front)*
- ch. 21 "9. Varys’s Riddle—Where Does Power Come From" **·** Plato, Augustine of Hippo — Allegory of the Cave — Iuliano uses Varys's riddle - king, priest, and rich man each bidding the same sellsword to kill the others - and 'power resides where men believe it resides' to stage a contest between John Austin's command theory of law (sovereign orders backed by force) and H.L.A. Hart's The Concept of Law, where authority rests on social acceptance. Cersei's 'power is power' scene with Littlefinger shows obedience that is mere coercion - Hart's gunman, being obliged rather than obligated - making the Lannisters gunmen writ large; the Hound's walkout on Joffrey exposes the illusion collapsing, and Plato's cave explains why Westerosi smallfolk prefer the comforting shadow to the truth that would free them.
- ch. 22 "10. How to Be Free" **·** Seneca — Stoicism; Premeditatio Malorum — O'Connell and Sirvent argue Seneca's Stoic freedom - release from fear of death and from every attachment - illuminates the two most enslaved groups in the story. The Unsullied's training strips them of possessions, names, lust, and fear, with Grey Worm's shame at fearing death for Missandei's sake showing exactly what attachment does to this freedom, while the Faceless Men's 'valar morghulis' mantra works like Seneca's recommended contemplation of death, letting Arya drink blindly from the cup at the House of Black and White. The chapter concludes that a fearlessness built by slaves and assassins really is a kind of freedom beyond their servitude, though Seneca would recoil at the brutal methods that produce it.
- ch. 23 "11. The Iron Bank Will Have Its Due" **·** Karl Marx — Class Struggle; Historical Materialism — Ewing argues money is the real determinant of the Iron Throne: Lannister gold made Cersei queen, Tyrell grain and taxation bought Margaery's crown, Baelish's financing decided the Battle of the Bastards, and the Iron Bank disciplines kings. Reading Westeros as a late-feudal economy through Marx - smallfolk whose work and taxes support lords who 'reap where they never sowed,' a hierarchy held by coercion and fear as the High Sparrow tells Olenna ('You are the few, we are the many'), and money dissolving feudal bonds into a 'money aristocracy' - he predicts the throne will go not to dragons or White Walkers but to the capitalist-financier figure of Petyr Baelish.
- ch. 24 "12. Margaery for Queen!" **·** Niccolò Machiavelli — Consequentialism; Virtù and Fortuna; Moral Relativism — Jones asks whether Margaery - who grooms Tommen, admits she was 'good at seeming good,' and may count as an abuser despite Westeros's norms - could still have been the best queen in the Seven Kingdoms. Against the prosecution's portrait of a cold egoist, he argues her charity to Flea Bottom's children shows a queen is good by what she does rather than why she did it, so selfish ambition welded to shrewdness can genuinely serve the common good. Comparing her with Ramsay (cruelty that provokes revolt) and Ned (benevolence without calculation that gets him killed), the chapter draws an implicitly Machiavellian conclusion - the people's best bet is hunger for power joined to the cunning to keep them onside - while doubting that cultural relativism can fully excuse her treatment of Tommen.
- ch. 25 "13. Bran, Hodor, and Disability in Westeros" **·** Aristotle, Lucretius — Philosophy of Disability; The Four Causes — Pierce uses Westeros's many disabled characters - Bran's paralysis, Jaime's lost hand, Hodor, Aemon's blindness, Varys and Theon's castration - to test the medical and social models of disability and the 'mere difference' view, asking whether disability is merely unaccommodated impairment or intrinsically worse for the one who has it. With the left-handedness analogy and the distinction between local and global harm, he then pits Aristotle's natural teleology (legs are for walking and eyes for seeing, so their loss is a real local bad) against Lucretius's Epicurean anti-teleology (no purposes in nature, so no intrinsic lack), concluding that even if disabilities are locally bad, Bran, Hodor, and Varys can be globally flourishing and heroic.
- ch. 26 "IV. The Things I Do for Love" *(front)*
- ch. 27 "14. The Gods Hate Incest" **·** Sigmund Freud — Natural Law — Barkman defends the near-universal Westerosi condemnation of incest. Against Freud's Oedipal account - which Jaime and Cersei's childhood romance seems to fit - he deploys Edward Westermarck's aversion hypothesis, primate evidence, and the Taiwanese simpua-marriage studies to argue that co-raised siblings should feel natural sexual aversion, so the twins are either instinctually malformed or unconvincingly written, while cases like Black Walder and Littlefinger with Sansa fit the effect fine. He then grounds the taboo in Natural Law - deduced from 'do no harm,' care of the weak, and natural family roles, plus the harms of inbreeding and endogamy - and concludes that children of incest like Joffrey are abominations by choice, not by birth.
- ch. 28 "15. Joffrey on the Couch" **·** Sigmund Freud — The Unconscious — Wills asks why audiences so relish hating Joffrey and answers with Freud's Totem and Taboo: the incest taboo was instituted precisely because the desire persists in the unconscious, and Joffrey - the product of 'accumulative incest' with cousin-grandparents and sibling parents - embodies the broken taboo. Reading the furious fan reactions (his death 'wasn't horrible enough,' the meticulous lists of his worst acts) as projection and successful repression, the chapter argues that vilifying Joffrey channels our own repressed taboo-breaking urges, while Westeros's distanced past setting lets us dissociate from the savagery exactly as Freud's staging of primitive peoples did.
- ch. 29 "16. Hope for Bastards and Sand Snakes" *(no index entry yet)* — Desveaux argues the hidden organizing concept of Game of Thrones is filiation - the legal system that transmits family belonging, name, status, and destiny. Sigils and house colors (the blond Lannister children as visible evidence of incest), Ned's law-enforcing beheading of the deserter, and the nature-names of bastards - Snow in the North, Sand in Dorne - all display its force: Jon's authenticity and open-mindedness come from standing outside it, Ramsay's legitimization after the Red Wedding and the Sand Snakes' Dornish independence mark its exceptions, while whores and smallfolk beyond all filiation have no protection at all, a contrast the chapter uses to measure how far modern law has removed these penalties.
- ch. 30 "17. Sex, Consent, and Rape in Westeros" **·** Objectification — Underwood distinguishes the legal consent of fifteenth-century-style Westeros - under the conjugal-debt doctrine neither Drogo nor Ramsay legally raped their wives, while every Cersei-Jaime union was treasonous regardless of desire - from the moral standard of consent developed from Alan Wertheimer's work. Applying his fraud and disregard cases, she argues Sansa's marriage obtained through Littlefinger's deception and Daenerys's coerced early nights with Drogo are immoral though legal, and proposes 'reciprocal subjectivity' as the criterion: Ramsay treats Sansa purely as an object, but the scene at Joffrey's body shows Jaime raping Cersei while still loving her as a subject, proving mutual recognition is necessary yet not sufficient for morally permissible sex.
- ch. 31 "V. I Swear before the Old Gods and the New" *(front)*
- ch. 32 "18. Gods and Beetles" **·** Augustine of Hippo, Alvin Plantinga, William of Ockham, Epicurus, J.L. Mackie — The Problem of Evil; Faith and Reason — Cooper takes Jaime's challenge to Catelyn - 'if your gods are real and just, why is the world so full of injustice?' - as the problem of evil and tests three Westerosi theologies. Melisandre's Manichean divine patronage fails by Augustine's argument that evil is no rival substance but a corruption in the creature; Ockham's voluntarism, symbolized by cousin Orson smashing beetles while Tyrion watches in dread, is rebuffed by Suarez and by Tyrion's own 'why are all the gods such vicious c--ts?'; and the humanist free-will defense in Catelyn's reply ('evil exists because of men like you'), rooted in Augustine and developed by Plantinga, answers Jaime but leaves Ivan Karamazov's problem of suffering children - met only by existential responses like Dorne's 'we don't hurt little girls.'
- ch. 33 "19. Prophetic Foreknowledge in Game of Thrones" **·** Fate and Foreknowledge; Free Will vs Determinism — Rennick argues that true prophecy threatens no one's freedom: a true prophecy merely reports the future the way a true history book reports the past - Jojen sees Jon among the wildlings because Jon chose as he chose, not the reverse - while fallible, misread prophecies (Melisandre mistaking Alys Karstark for Arya, the Stallion prophecy dying with Rhaego) constrain even less. Self-fulfilling prophecies like Azor Ahai actually demonstrate agency, since Stannis and Melisandre's belief in the prophecy brings the events about, and even infallible foreknowledge of a choice - 'you will watch season seven' - leaves open the alternate Westeros where you did otherwise, the standard reply to the God's-foreknowledge problem applied to green dreams and flames.
- ch. 34 "20. Cersei, Sansa, and the Problem of Evil" **·** Immanuel Kant — The Problem of Evil — McCrossin reads Cersei and Sansa's post-imprisonment rises in season six as opposite answers to the problem of evil, framed by Susan Neiman's claim that Enlightenment thought split between making evil intelligible and refusing to. Cersei, taught by Tywin that 'the gods have no mercy - that's why they're gods,' becomes Sade's cruel god of Juliette, doing whatever feels good as the gods do; Sansa, hardened by Ramsay and Littlefinger's betrayal into 'no one can protect anyone,' embodies the Kantian response that virtue and happiness come apart, so one governs only what can be controlled - character, family, justice - as she engineers the Battle of the Bastards and has Ramsay eaten by his dogs without mercy or vengeance.
- ch. 35 "21. Stannis—Knight of Faith or Tragic Hero" **·** Søren Kierkegaard, Immanuel Kant — Teleological Suspension of the Ethical; The Leap of Faith; Deontology — Devlin asks whether Stannis, who burns Shireen to save his army, is Kierkegaard's knight of faith or merely a tragic hero. Stannis's duty-obsessed deontology - knighting Davos while cutting off his fingertips, striking 'beloved brother' from a letter as a lie - is Kantian, and the Shireen dilemma looks like Abraham's teleological suspension of the ethical; yet because Stannis always calculates and justifies ('Faith? In a real war, the side with the greater number wins nine times out of ten'), he never makes the leap on the strength of the absurd, and Davos ends up following Kant's own verdict on Abraham: 'If he commands you to burn children, your lord is evil.' He is therefore a tragic hero in the line of Agamemnon and Jephthah, rationally comprehensible and fittingly undone by Brienne's 'do your duty.'
- ch. 36 "22. The Many-Faced God and Indian Philosophy" **·** Shankara, The Buddha, Nagarjuna — Atman and Brahman; Non-Self (anatman); Emptiness (sunyata); Dharma — Van Eyghen maps the Faceless Men onto Indian philosophy: their one God of many faces parallels Vedanta's Brahman in personal form as Ishvara, and Arya's training to become 'no one' parallels shedding the ego toward identity with Brahman - with Jaqen as guru and the stick-beating ritual as an example of identity fusion in Harvey Whitehouse's cognitive science of religion. But the Faceless Men may go further than Vedanta toward Buddhism, since they worship a god with no face of his own and aim at no-self rather than union, while Arya's pragmatic adoption of the shellfish-merchant disguise echoes the Bhagavad Gita's Krishna: perform your duty without attachment to any self.
- ch. 37 "VI. Knowledge Is Power" *(front)*
- ch. 38 "23. How Can We Know Anything in a World of Magic and Miracles" **·** David Hume, Thomas Reid, Plato — Problem of Induction; Common Sense Philosophy; Philosophical Skepticism — Baldwin asks whether Westerosi characters can know anything inductive in a world without lawlike nature: seasons of unpredictable length, wargs in animal minds, Faceless Men changing faces, and resurrections by Thoros, Melisandre, and Qyburn. After laying out the Platonic justified-true-belief analysis of knowledge (those who saw the pyre are justified in believing Dany is the mother of dragons; those relying on rumor are not), he runs Hume's problem of induction - we never perceive causal connection, so no jar of wildfire must explode - against Reid's common-sense reply that reason and induction stand or fall together, concluding that Westeros gives its people special reasons for skepticism while we, lacking evidence of magical irregularity, are rational to trust everyday inductive belief.
- ch. 39 "24. Samwell Tarly’s Renaissance" *(no index entry yet)* — Alves reads Westeros as a technologically stagnant society where knowledge is hoarded by monopolies - the Maesters' Citadel chain links and the Alchemists' wildfire secret - and asks what Samwell Tarly's library discoveries at Castle Black plus his battlefield experience with dragonglass against the White Walkers add up to. Arguing that empiricism - observation and experiment against the dubious authority of old texts, the mix that birthed modern science out of alchemy and astrology (with Mircea Eliade and even Newton as border cases) - is exactly what Sam practices, the chapter suggests he is igniting a Westerosi renaissance of lost Valyrian-age knowledge, while the Doom of Valyria warns, like Prometheus and Babel, against knowing too much.
- ch. 40 "25. Should Catelyn Have Trusted Brienne" **·** Alvin Plantinga — The Web of Belief — McNabb and Andrews ask whether Catelyn was warranted in trusting Brienne as her sworn sword after Brienne failed to save Renly, and test two theories of warrant. On accessibilist coherentism, Catelyn's accessible evidence - Brienne's prowess, her devastation at Renly's death, 'Renly's death was no fault of yours' - fits her web of beliefs like a new strand in a spider's web; on Plantinga-style proper functionalism, illustrated by the accident-created Labman replica and the Lord of Light randomizing a design plan, Catelyn's naturally formed belief that Brienne is trustworthy is warranted if her faculties are properly functioning, truth-aimed, and well designed - which Brienne's record through rescuing Sansa vindicates.
- ch. 41 "VII. You Think I Would Trade My Honor" *(front)*
- ch. 42 "26. Horrifying Violence, Gratuitous Sex, and the Truth" **·** Plato, Augustine of Hippo, Aristotle, Sigmund Freud — Just War Theory — Silverman concedes Game of Thrones can be watched viciously - sadistically relishing Theon's flaying or using it as soft pornography - but denies the show is inherently immoral. From Plato's Republic he takes the test of whether a narrative serves or obscures reality (Socrates was executed partly on the slander of Aristophanes' Clouds) and argues the show is honest where epics lie: war is ugly and kills innocents, measured against the Augustinian just-war criteria that even the Brotherhood Without Banners decays past; humans really are as sexually preoccupied as Plato's appetitive soul, Freud's id, or Augustine's original sin; and honor without practical wisdom - Ned versus Tyrion on Aristotle's ideal - is a recipe for ruin.
- ch. 43 "27. Was the Red Wedding Massacre a Good Thing" **·** Niccolò Machiavelli — Consequentialism; The Ends Justify the Means; Utilitarianism — Van der Deijl takes Tywin's line to Tyrion - 'explain to me why it is more noble to kill ten thousand men in battle than a dozen at dinner' - as a serious consequentialist challenge about the Red Wedding. Working through utilitarianism, prioritarianism, and dirty-hands cases like Hiroshima and shooting down a hijacked plane, he concedes the massacre could have been justified had breaking Guest Right and killing 3,500 brought peace at minimal cost; but the consequences fail - Joffrey and Roose Bolton keep power, the institution of guest right erodes, and Tywin's real motive was family power rather than the greater good - so the massacre fails even the consequentialist test, and Tywin is a power-hungry conspirator, not Machiavelli's prince.
- ch. 44 "28. Severed Heads, Dirty Hands" **·** Thomas Hobbes, Michael Walzer, Immanuel Kant — The State of Nature; The Ethics of Political Violence — Ivanković and Božac argue that ruling Westeros necessarily means dirtying one's hands, staging the paradox through Varys's counsel to Ned and Jaime's 'finest act.' Hobbes supplies the baseline that anything preserving peace beats the war of all against all, Walzer permits dirty hands only to avert communal death (Daenerys swindling the Astapor slavers), and Kant's absolute refusal to lie - mirrored in Ned's honor - costs Ned his head and the realm a war. The chapter also indicts the reverse: Aemon's refusal of the throne and other too-clean refusals of responsibility are moral failures, Jon's killing of Qhorin Halfhand shows dirty hands are not just for kings, and rulers must resist the downward spiral while keeping, as Dany does, a gentle heart for the true goal.
- ch. 45 "29. The Best of Friends" **·** Aristotle — Aristotle on Friendship (philia); Eudaimonia (flourishing) — Geyer sorts Game of Thrones friendships by Aristotle's three kinds in the Nicomachean Ethics. Robert and Ned's bond, formed in Jon Arryn's fosterage and shared war, is friendship of pleasure, which frays over Rhaegar's 'dragonspawn'; Tyrion and Bronn is friendship of utility, dissolving exactly as Aristotle predicts when Lollys's marriage pays better than a trial by combat in 'Mockingbird'; and Jon and Sam, each valuing the other's character for its own sake at the Wall, approach the only true friendship - the virtue friendship - supporting the claim that eudaimonia needs such relationships alongside external goods.
- ch. 46 "30. Trying to Do Good with Arya, Tyrion, and the Hound" **·** Aristotle, Jeremy Bentham, Immanuel Kant — Practical Wisdom (phronesis); Utilitarianism; The Categorical Imperative — Thomas sets Aristotle's practical wisdom against Bentham's utility calculus and Kant's consequence-proof rules, then watches all three struggle in season six. Arya's merciful refusal to poison Lady Crane backfires when the Waif kills her more painfully anyway; Tyrion's compromise with the Masters - phasing out slavery gradually, 'I trust their self-interest' - nearly loses Meereen when they attack by sea; and the resurrected Hound, after Brother Ray's massacre, concludes that 'lots of horrible shit in this world gets done for something larger than ourselves,' so being good in Westeros, as everywhere, means choosing without being able to know the consequences in advance.
- ch. 47 "31. Paying the Iron Price" **·** Aristotle — The Golden Mean; Virtue Ethics (arete) — Hansberger reads Theon's return to the Iron Islands as an Aristotelian failure of virtue: excess lust toward Yara on the road, the 'small soul' of gold-bought jewelry where Balon demands iron-price honor, and the rashness rather than courage of trying to hold Winterfell with twenty men. But Balon's virtues are the vices of a piratical society that rewards reaving and rape, and through Charles Taylor's ethics of authenticity the chapter argues Theon's deeper failure is not knowing who he is - Stark or Greyjoy - so Ramsay's breaking of him into Reek completes a collapse of self that began at home; authenticity, it concludes, depends on virtue and social relations, which is why Theon can only begin to recover by choosing to serve Yara.
