## The Ultimate Walking Dead and Philosophy - Hungry for More

- ch. 1 "Cover" *(not yet read)*
- ch. 2 "i" *(not yet read)*
- ch. 3 "ii" *(front)*
- ch. 4 "Title Page" *(front)*
- ch. 5 "Copyright" *(front)*
- ch. 6 "Dedication" *(front)*
- ch. 7 "Contents" *(front)*
- ch. 8 "Thanks" *(notes)*
- ch. 9 "To the Reader" *(front)*
- ch. 10 "Hungry for More" **·** Ring of Gyges (why be moral?); The State of Nature; The Meaning of Life — Wayne Yuen's introduction argues that philosophy needs extreme scenarios to stress-test moral, political, and religious theories, and that The Walking Dead supplies two: the reanimation of the dead and the collapse of social, political, and economic order. He frames the book around the show's most overt question - why be moral at all when survival pushes against it, as with Rick's compromises and the choice between searching for a missing girl or writing her off as having 'looked at the flowers' - plus how religion fares when the dead rise, and the string of attempted societies (the Prison, Woodbury, Terminus, Grady Memorial, Alexandria) that mirror our own world of failing nations and unjust rule. The essays, he says, hold a mirror to our values: we can become the walking dead without any zombies.
- ch. 11 "Part I Afraid of Being Alive" *(front)*
- ch. 12 "1. Reading the Signs" **·** Friedrich Nietzsche, Epicurus — The Death of God; Faith and Reason; Death Is Nothing to Us; Language Games — Di Bernardo reads the religious language of The Walking Dead as sustained irony - a 'doubleness of sense' - arguing that the walkers, churches, and signs comment critically on faith rather than endorse it. He tracks the church scenes (the Southern Baptist church of 2.1 'What Lies Ahead' with its timed bells and sitting-dead congregation, Gabriel's Saint Sarah's where Maggie answers that it is 'just four walls and a roof'), the ambiguous 'sanctuary' and Terminus signs, and the comic-book Jesus (Paul Monroe) as a machine-gun savior, concluding that the scriptural promise of eternal life through consuming flesh and blood yields only undead life. Against Epicurus's consolation that death is nothing to us, and in the shadow of Nietzsche's madman announcing the death of God, the chapter warns against investing absolute trust in any signs - religious or secular - a warning capped by Rick biting out Joe's throat as his own 'day of reckoning.'
- ch. 13 "2. Maybe Jenner Was Right" **·** Albert Camus, Alvin Plantinga, Jean-Paul Sartre — The Absurd; The Myth of Sisyphus; Rebellion and Revolt; Properly Basic Belief — Cowan uses Jenner's suicide in the exploding CDC (1.6 'TS-19') to pose Camus's question 'Why should I not commit suicide?', and rejects Jenner's despair the Camusian way: Michonne in 4.9 'After' - slaying her own walker look-alike and then her two pet walkers - is his Sisyphus in revolt, unlike Gareth, whose revolt lacks the respect for human dignity that makes Sisyphus happy. He then objects that Camus never explains why human life has intrinsic dignity in a godless universe, noting that Sartrean authenticity could equally license the Governor's Woodbury experiments or Gareth's cannibalism, and turns to Hershel's providential hope as the religious alternative. Following Plantinga, he argues belief in God can be properly basic through a sensus divinitatis - Hershel seeing God's hand in 2.4 'Cherokee Rose' and the tornado-spared barn of 5.10 'Them' - so if God exists, Jenner was not right.
- ch. 14 "3. I Want You to Survive" **·** Tom Regan, Jean-Paul Sartre — Animal Rights (subjects-of-a-life); Existence Precedes Essence; The Meaning of Life — Seth Walker asks what makes a life worth saving, testing the show's survival choices against instrumental value (usefulness to the group) and Tom Regan's subject-of-a-life criterion of inherent value. Shane's sacrifice of Otis for Carl, Lori's fatal pregnancy and Judith's birth, the Governor's obsession with Meghan, and the hunts for Sophia all show that neither usefulness nor Regan's equal, non gradated inherent value explains why children are selectively elevated - and perceived 'innocence' fails with the colder Carl and with Lizzie. He concludes with Bob Stookey's line in 4.10 'Inmates' ('Not everything has to mean something... but it can'): following Sartre's claim that man is nothing but what he makes of himself, the survivors are condemned to be free and must make their survival mean something, as Daryl and Carol's search for Sophia did.
- ch. 15 "Walker Bite. Let’s Just Do What’s Right" **·** Immanuel Kant — The Categorical Imperative; Humanity as an End in Itself; Deontology; Consequentialism — Devlin and Cooper present Dale as the show's Kantian deontologist: he acts from duty regardless of consequences, applying the humanity formula - treat people always as ends, never merely as means - when he blocks the execution of Randall in 'Judge, Jury, Executioner' and refuses to let the group do consequentialist math about abandoning the search for Sophia. Shane, the Governor, and the Termites serve as the consequentialist foils who treat Otis, the prison residents, and their victims as mere means to survival. The chapter argues Dale dies with his humanity and dignity intact, and that the series' recurring drama is a three-sided battle between his deontology, the survivors' consequentialism, and Rick's eventual virtue ethics.
- ch. 16 "Part II Not the Good Guy Anymore" *(front)*
- ch. 17 "4. Lie or Die" **·** Immanuel Kant — Deontology; Moral Relativism; Utilitarianism — Hawkes uses Rick's bar confrontation with Dave and Tony in 2.8 'Nebraska' to ask when lying is right, after defining lying through the Governor's white flag at the National Guard camp and Eugene's false cure claim. He rejects moral relativism - the Claimers' rules, Terminus's 'butcher or cattle', the Governor's 'we will do the only thing' - because it forbids criticizing rapists and cannibals and erases moral progress, then finds utilitarianism shaky given Eugene's unforeseen deadly consequences and Rick's refusal to hand Michonne to the Governor at 'Arrow on the Doorpost.' He defends a duty-based deontology in which the duty to protect the farm overrides the duty not to lie, and closes with Robert Kirkman's own lie about an alien invasion that got The Walking Dead published.
- ch. 18 "5. Back from the Dead" **·** Aristotle, John Stuart Mill — Virtue Ethics (arete); Consequentialism; The Greatest Happiness Principle; Eudaimonia (flourishing) — Devlin and Cooper trace Rick's moral arc as a struggle between Mill-style consequentialism - twisted by the apocalypse into a 'Greatest Survival Principle' of mere survival (Dawn Lerner's Grady, Enid's 'JSS') - and Aristotelian virtue ethics. Shane's murder of Otis, the Governor's rule over Woodbury and killing of Martinez and Pete, and Terminus's cannibalism all show survival-only reasoning dissolving humanity, a decline the show underlines with literal mirror reflections of Shane as a walker. Rick falls after killing Shane and nearly trades Michonne to the Governor, is brought back by Lori's judging vision and the sight of Carl going cold, and reclaims virtues as habits grown like Hershel's seeds - 'We get to come back' (4.8 'Too Far Gone') - though Seasons 4 and 5 leave him balancing a harder character, down to 'We didn't want to waste the bullets,' with the drive to survive.
- ch. 19 "Walker Bite. Differentiating the Dead" **·** Language Games; Binary Oppositions — Di Bernardo argues that the swarm of names for the undead - walkers, roamers, lurkers, biters, empties, geeks - shows the world does not carve itself into pre-existing essences: communities construct their own classifications, as when Alice mocks Rick's group's roamers/lurkers taxonomy ('They all bite. Biters makes a ton more sense') and titles like 'Governor,' 'The Kingdom,' and 'Jesus' are disavowed by their bearers. Naming is a conferral of power that reveals the namers, since without absolute authority every name is a self-authorized exercise of power. Finally the living dead destabilize the strict living/dead opposition, and Rick's dogmatic 'us against them' slips into his own admission 'We are the Walking Dead!', with painful ethical consequences for how we police inclusion and exclusion in our world.
- ch. 20 "6. Punishing Negan" **·** Punishment: Retribution vs Rehabilitation; Forgiveness — Yuen asks why Rick imprisons Negan after the Saviors' war instead of executing the man who beats people to death with 'Lucille' and runs a mafia-like protection racket. He surveys retributivism (fair play and eye-for-an-eye equality) and deterrence, including Glenn's lawless Challenger driving, then defends Rick's refusal to kill via 'we're better than him,' the weakness of execution as a deterrent for Negan's followers, and the brutalization effect that would poison the character of the fledgling civilization. But he argues Negan's basement isolation - no exercise, no decency, his victim as judge and warden - fails Rick's own civilizing vision, and proposes restorative justice with apology, forgiveness, and mediated reparations as the punishment a civilization that claims human life is precious ought to build.
- ch. 21 "Part III Lose a Lot of Soldiers and Still Win the Game" *(front)*
- ch. 22 "7. Machiavelli Advises the Governor" **·** Niccolò Machiavelli — Virtù and Fortuna; The Ends Justify the Means — Littmann reads The Prince as advice the Governor already follows: kidnapping Andrea and Michonne, butchering the National Guard survivors for supplies, murdering his way to leadership of Martinez's camp, and maintaining a benevolent image through Woodbury picnics and gladiatorial walker-fights while ruling through fear at the gates. Machiavelli's maxims - crush rather than lightly injure, be feared but not hated, be lion and fox, adapt to the times - explain both the Governor's successes and his failures, such as massacring his own army outside the prison or letting rage over Penny's death cost him his eye. The chapter ends by grading Machiavelli largely right about dirty hands and military power but blind to resources like medicine and fuel, and draws the real lesson: shape the political environment so Machiavellian strategies stop paying.
- ch. 23 "8. The Warring Dead" **·** Michael Walzer — Just War Theory — Morris applies just war theory's distinction between justice of war and justice in war to Season 3's Woodbury-prison conflict, staging Walzer's moral symmetry of combatants - soldiers on both sides may permissibly kill each other regardless of their cause - against McMahan's critique, illustrated by the bank robber who kills a cop in 'self-defense.' McMahan's distinction between objective justification and excuses like coercion and ignorance is worked through Maggie giving up the prison's location under threat to Glenn in 'When the Dead Come Knocking' and the Woodbury militia's ignorance of the Governor's raids. He concludes the prison group fights a justified defensive war, the ignorant Woodbury fighters are excused but not justified, and real culpability lies with the Governor and his henchmen.
- ch. 24 "9. Anarchy, State, and Apocalypse" **·** Thomas Hobbes, John Locke, Jean-Jacques Rousseau — The State of Nature; The Social Contract; Anarchism; Democracy — Da Silva and McKendry test the political legitimacy of four post-apocalyptic governance types: the Claimers' anarchy that collapses into Joe adjudicating 'claims', the Governor's and Rick's dictatorships ('This is not a democracy anymore!'), the prison Council's representative democracy with the three questions as a quasi-constitution, and Terminus's quasi-theocratic cannibal creed ('You're the butcher or you're the cattle'). Using Hobbes's state of nature and war of all against all, hypothetical social contract, and the twin tests of power and morality, they judge even the benevolent Rick-tatorship unjust because concentrated power is fallible and unaccountable. They conclude, with Churchill, that the Council's imperfect democracy is the worst form of government except for all the others.
- ch. 25 "Walker Bite. If I Don’t Save the World, I Have No Value" **·** Humanity as an End in Itself; Aristotle on Friendship (philia) — This Walker Bite uses Eugene Porter's exposed lie about being the scientist who can cure the outbreak (5.5 "Self Help") to ask what makes a person valuable once their usefulness collapses. Eugene himself sums up an instrumental view of his own worth - "If I don't cure the disease, if I don't save the world, I have no value" - and admits he sabotaged the bus to stall the DC mission. Against this, Tara's "We're friends. We have each other's backs... You're stuck with us. No matter what" argues that persons are valued for themselves within a community rather than as means, and the chapter lands on Abraham's "people need people" as the real ground of Eugene's place in the group.
- ch. 26 "10. We’re Not Free to Save Lee" **·** Jean-Paul Sartre, Nick Bostrom — Free Will vs Determinism; Bad Faith; Compatibilism; The Simulation Hypothesis — Bowman uses Telltale's Walking Dead videogame, where Lee always dies no matter what the player does, to stage the free will versus determinism debate. Laplace's demon grounds the determinist pole (the game's outcome is fixed like set particles), while Sartre's radical freedom and bad faith explain the player who says "I thought I could save both" at Hershel's farm rather than owning her choice. The chapter resolves the tension with fatalism or soft determinism - a compatibilist itinerary where Lee's death is a fixed benchmark but the player's choices in between define the character - and extends Bostrom's simulation hypothesis to ask whether we are Lee-like players inside someone else's game.
- ch. 27 "Part IV Look at the Flowers" *(front)*
- ch. 28 "11. I Ain’t No Hollaback Girl!" **·** Carol Gilligan — The Ethics of Care; Moral Psychology (character, vice, weakness of will) — Callens explains Carol's killing of Lizzie in "The Grove" through Kohlberg's stages of moral development, arguing Carol reasons at the post-conventional level (pre-apocalypse law and punishment no longer bind) while Rick, Hershel, and Daryl stay conventional, and then layers Maslow's hierarchy of needs on top: because survival needs are perpetually threatened, characters regress between stages, and only self-actualized Carol accepts reality and makes group-preserving choices like executing Lizzie and mercy-killing Karen and David. Gilligan's justice-versus-care critique supplies the gendered frame - Carol moves to the middle of the justice-care continuum, her diminished care (unlike Season Two's grief over Sophia) is what makes her post-conventional, and her non-lethal Terminus rescue contrasts with Rick's summary execution of Gareth.
- ch. 29 "12. They Just Want Me to Be Like Them" **·** Personal Identity; The Ethics of Care; Moral Psychology (character, vice, weakness of will) — Castro and Beisecker diagnose Lizzie's walker fixation as prolonged grief disorder: she displaces grief for her father onto the walker "Nick," denies final death ("They're just different"), and treats reanimation as a viable form of life she might join ("Maybe I should change"). They argue Carol's suppressive guardianship - the "look at the flowers" distraction and calling Lizzie weak and confused - aggravated the avoidance at the root of the disorder, so Lizzie's "transformation" of Mika was a misguided moral experiment rather than murder by a monster. The chapter contrasts Tyreese's affirming care ("You each do things your own way") as the guardianship that might have saved the girls, and notes Carol's refusal to let Lizzie reanimate denies her the dying-wish courtesy the group once granted Jim.
- ch. 30 "13. Carol’s Transformation" **·** Aristotle, John Stuart Mill, Thomas Aquinas — Virtue Ethics (arete); Utilitarianism; Natural Law — Delfino and Lesinski trace Carol's arc from abused wife (Ed's beatings, Sophia's death, "I didn't think I could be strong") to the judge-jury-executioner she once refused to be, reading the transformation through Aristotle's claim in the Nicomachean Ethics that repeated actions forge moral character. They then evaluate her killings: the murder of Karen and David fails on both act- and rule-utilitarian grounds (it did not stop the flu, and Glenn and Sasha survived while the group was shattered), whereas killing the delusional Lizzie is defensible both utilitarianly and by Aquinas's natural-law exception that an evildoer may be killed for the welfare of the whole community when no public authority remains. Her confession to Tyreese and Tyreese's forgiveness complete the phoenix rebirth symbolized by the burning walkers in "Consumed."
- ch. 31 "Walker Bite. Innocence in Shambles" **·** Moral Psychology (character, vice, weakness of will); Applied Ethics (bioethics, technology, AI) — Yuen argues that childhood innocence is both an excusing condition - children like Lizzie lack the rational capacity to judge their own acts morally, which is why her killing of Mika shocks and why the audience half-excuses her - and a social arrangement that shields children from adult demands, one Carol deliberately ends by secretly teaching the kids knife skills. He then extends Lydia's comic line that "Childhood was always a myth brought about by the illusion of safety" to real-world child labor in impoverished countries, where innocence is a luxury families cannot afford, and closes by asking readers to ask how we could make innocence affordable for working children rather than simply denouncing sweatshops.
- ch. 32 "14. Carol Didn’t Care" **·** Jeremy Bentham — Utilitarianism; Personal Identity; Moral Psychology (character, vice, weakness of will) — Yuen grants that executing Lizzie was not wrong but argues Carol fell short of a moral ideal by ignoring Lizzie's preferences, deploying R.M. Hare's move from Bentham's happiness-maximizing classic utilitarianism to preference utilitarianism - illustrated by the group refusing to let Eugene test Aaron's water in "Them." Since Lizzie prefers a reanimating death ("She didn't hurt her brain"), a heart shot like the group's courtesy to Jim would have maximized preference satisfaction without endangering anyone. He answers the objections that Lizzie is a child and that walkers are mindless: her attempts to reason and her coherent post-apocalyptic values (like generational value shifts on homosexuality via Aaron and Eric) deserve respect, and whether a walker would still be Lizzie matters less than that the preference is hers now.
- ch. 33 "Part V Every Direction Is a Question" *(front)*
- ch. 34 "15. My Zombie, My Self" **·** Plato, Aristotle, Epicurus — Personal Identity; Aristotle on the Soul; Death Is Nothing to Us — Greene asks what Walking Dead-type walkers are and answers with the Platonic method of definition learned from Socrates: place them in the genus undead (died but not at rest, neither alive nor dead) and differentiate them by differentia - corporeal, mindless, lacking superhuman or magical powers, still decomposing. Aristotle's hierarchy of life functions (nutrition, locomotion, reason) explains why walkers are not alive despite ambulating, and the standards of death (putrefaction, heart-lung, whole-brain, higher-brain) all certify that walkers are dead yet active. The chapter closes on personal identity - whether you survive becoming a zombie, as Carol's face at the barn and Carl's "she'll turn" about Lori presuppose - and admits the author chooses the self-interested reading that he could become his own zombie, for the sake of the scare.
- ch. 35 "16. The Truth about the Walkers" **·** Gilles Deleuze, Sigmund Freud — The Rhizome; The Unconscious — Kempner reads the show's serial bait-and-switch over the walkers' origin - Jenner's failed CDC, Eugene's exposed lie on the road to Washington - as an enactment of Deleuze and Guattari's multiplicity against "the reduction to the ONE." Their anti-Oedipal attack on Freud, who fixed the Wolfman's dream to a single castrating father, mirrors how characters who hunt one knowable cause (Rick's hope of CDC rescue, Hershel's barn of curable walkers, Abraham's mission) are punished with death, while Michonne survives precisely by accepting walkers as an irreducible pack rather than something to be understood. The chapter concludes the series should never explain the walkers: multiple causes and small-t truths replace any single Truth, which is why relief never arrives and survival means living with unknowability.
- ch. 36 "17. What if Our Reality Is One Big Hallucination" **·** George Berkeley — Philosophical Skepticism; Subjective Idealism (esse est percipi); Scientific Realism vs Anti-Realism — Arp starts from Rick's hallucinations of Lori - the disconnected phone in "Hounded," the touch and kiss in "Home" - and Michonne's confessed visions, showing how Rick distinguishes them through coherence with causal law and through third-person verification (Michonne watching him stroke empty air; Morgan's isolation in "Clear" showing why social checks matter). He then widens this into the theory of knowledge: the perceiver-perception-perceived triangle, epistemological and metaphysical realism versus idealism and antirealism, Berkeley's picture of the mind as a movie theater we cannot exit, and the Walking Dead twist on the falling tree in the forest, concluding that even if reality were one big hallucination we would still live - and refuse to walk off cliffs - as realists.
- ch. 37 "Walker Bite. Are You Being Saved" **·** Applied Ethics (bioethics, technology, AI); Utilitarianism — Callens reads Beth's captivity at Grady Memorial in "Slabtown" as an allegory of the American healthcare system: patients like Noah are rescued, then enslaved by insurmountable medical debt, forced to work off charges that never end. Lt. Lerner's "No, but you needed it" and her non-medical control over Dr. Edwards's triage pose the chapter's question - whether a system that saves people "for the greater good" is care or exploitation, echoing the "death panels" debate over the Affordable Care Act and asking "Are we being saved in order to be enslaved?"
- ch. 38 "18. Gruesome Walkers" **·** David Hume, Karl Popper, Bertrand Russell — Problem of Induction; Falsificationism; Philosophical Skepticism — Yuen turns the walkers into a lesson on Hume's problem of induction: the pre-apocalyptic belief that the dead do not rise was justified by all past experience until Rick wakes to a world where they do, and Rick's distrust of Alexandria repeats the same inductive move from Woodbury, Terminus, and Grady - useful, Hume says, since custom compels us, but never knowledge. Russell's chicken illustrates the fatal surprise, and Goodman's grue (the pun of the title) shows the outbreak could be a future-swinging regularity like the seventeen-year cicadas, undermining even Jenner's experimental science. Popper's falsificationism is offered as the rescue - Shane's reanimation falsifies the "walkers make walkers" theory, leaving the "everyone is infected" theory corroborated but never known.
- ch. 39 "References" *(notes)*
- ch. 40 "We’re Friends with the Chick with the Sword and the Kid in the Hat" *(notes)*
- ch. 41 "Index" *(notes)*
- ch. 42 "272" *(front)*
- ch. 43 "273" *(notes)*

## The Undead and Philosophy - Chicken Soup for the Soulless

- ch. 1 "section-1" *(not yet read)*
- ch. 2 "Zombies, Vampires, and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 3 "Popular Culture and Philosophy Series Editor George A. Reisch" *(front)*
- ch. 4 "Title Page" *(front)*
- ch. 5 "A New Lease of Life for the Undead" **·** Friedrich Nietzsche, Baruch Spinoza — The Übermensch — The editors' introduction surveys the twenty-first-century vampire and zombie boom (Twilight, True Blood, Zombieland, Fido, Pride and Prejudice and Zombies, Humans Versus Zombies, zombie walks) and asks why zombies specifically fascinate us. It proposes that the vampire embodies a Nietzschean super-humanity beyond good and evil, still charged with unheeded guilt, while the zombie is sub-Nietzschean: a Spinozan force of decomposition, a completely non-moral interaction of matter with other matter. Zombies work as hyperdead memento mori through which we imagine our own annihilation and our transformation into something posthuman, even as they are repackaged as one more consumer product distracting us from the very Real they reflect.
- ch. 6 "PART I - It’s Alive (Sort Of)" *(front)*
- ch. 7 "Not to Put Too Fine a Point on It" **·** Thomas Nagel, Bernard Williams, Epicurus, John Stuart Mill — Death Is Nothing to Us; Higher and Lower Pleasures — Richard Greene asks why becoming Undead is bad for the one who becomes Undead and why we rank it worse than death, restricting himself to unfriendly vampires and zombies (setting aside soulful exceptions like Angel, Spike, Louis, and Ed from Shaun of the Dead). He tests Nagel's deprivation view (vampires gain infinite experiences, so nothing is deprived) and Williams's desire-frustration view (undeath replaces rather than frustrates desires: scotch for blood, pork roast for raw flesh), then Mill's better-Socrates-dissatisfied comparison and the thought that the Undead's evil nature makes undeath objectively bad. Each fails, so he concludes undeath is not objectively bad, only comparatively bad, and one might as well make the best of it.
- ch. 8 "Dead People" **·** René Descartes, John Locke — Personal Identity; Personal Identity (memory theory); Mind–Body Dualism — William Larkin uses Romero's films - Karen Cooper devouring her father in Night of the Living Dead, Roger begging Peter to shoot him in Dawn, Riley's 'Those people are all dead' in Land - to argue that persons are res corporealis, bodily continuers, rather than Descartes's res cogitans. Our terror of becoming a zombie ourselves and our grief at recognized neighbors in the horde reveal that deep down we think persons survive as corpses, so bodily continuity suffices for identity while psychological continuity is unnecessary. The Locke-style body-switching intuition (prince and cobbler) that supports the psychological approach is dismissed as theoretically biased data compared to what good zombie fiction draws out of us.
- ch. 9 "Zombies and Us" **·** David Hume, Derek Parfit, John Locke — Personal Identity; Bundle Theory of Self; Personal Identity (memory theory) — Hamish Thompson asks why we kill zombies with so few qualms and whether zombification releases us from obligations to the person who was, testing brain-death criteria, altered-identity and self-defense justifications against Shaun of the Dead (Shaun's mother and Ed), Day of the Dead's Bub, White Zombie, and Invasion of the Body Snatchers. He borrows Hume's bundle theory - the self as a fiction, a bundle of perceptions rather than an essence - and Parfit's degrees of psychological continuity to dissolve the sharp alive/dead and same-person/other-person dichotomies that make zombie-killing easy. The payoff is that we are already partly zombie (Shaun's checkout line, Ed on the sofa, the author's all-night gaming), differing only in our capacity for brief autonomous, goal-directed choices like Shaun's three-plan montage.
- ch. 10 "Resting in Peace" **·** Philosophical Zombies — Manuel Vargas argues the Undead are not a natural kind but a nominal kind - vampires, zombies, and composite creatures like Frankenstein's monster share nothing except our classificatory practice - and that most actual Undead would probably be of natural origin (viruses, viroids, seeds, cryonics cases) rather than supernatural. He distinguishes philosophical zombies (Chalmers's conscious-less duplicates) from genuinely Undead beings, then defines malevolent evil as acting on a non-instrumental desire to harm others. On that definition zombies (mere appetite, like a deadly virus) and even vampires (whose damnation, contrary to early Buffy, need not add malicious motives) probably lack it, so if we ever meet the Undead they would be less likely to be evil than any random living person.
- ch. 11 "Do Androids Dream of Electric Sheep" **·** David Chalmers, John Searle, René Descartes, Saul Kripke, Alan Turing — Philosophical Zombies; Mind–Body Dualism — Larry Hauser stages the philosophy-of-mind zombie debate through Blade Runner's Replicants: Descartes's animal automata and his hats-and-coats worry about other minds, Turing's universal machines grounding functionalism, Kripke's necessity of scientific identifications (water is necessarily H2O), and Searle's silicon-chip replacement scenario show how zombies 'eat' behaviorist, functionalist, and identity-theoretic accounts of mind. He then deploys his own 'good zombies' - the beloved who might be a zombie, super-smart NEXUS 9s, venerable zombie thinkers - to block the dualist retreat on the cognitive side of the mind. The essay closes by doubting, with Chalmers's phenomenal zombies in view, that such zombies are coherently conceivable after all, and asks whether Deckard has been 'retiring' persons like Rachel all along.
- ch. 12 "PART II - Undead White Males" *(front)*
- ch. 13 "Heil Heidegger Dracula’s Willing Executioners" **·** Martin Heidegger — Being-Toward-Death; Authenticity; The They-Self (das Man) — Adam Barrows reads Stoker's Dracula through Being and Time: the staking of Lucy is the liberation of a true humanity trapped in a deathless shell, and Dracula's endless, mirror-less, selfless existence is Heideggerian inauthenticity - the 'they' that tranquilizes the dying, Renfield's seduction repeating the neighbors' comforting, the refusal of being-toward-death - so vampire-hunting becomes a philosophical task of enforcing authentic, death-claiming being. The chapter then turns the critique on Heidegger himself: his Nazi membership and fundamental ontology's contempt for the everyday leave a gap for racial purity thinking, making Van Helsing's 'butcher work' look like the ritual expulsion of the inauthentic from ontological purity, and asking whether, in adaptation after adaptation, we are Dracula's willing executioners.
- ch. 14 "“Hey, Let’s Get the Stuff We Need!” Putting the Mall in Its Place" **·** Aristotle — Eudaimonia (flourishing); Virtue Ethics (arete); Practical Wisdom (phronesis) — Matthew Walker reads Dawn of the Dead through Aristotle's Nicomachean Ethics and Politics: the survivors' mall orgy curdles into boredom ('What have we done to ourselves?'), illustrating pleonexia, the graspingness for more than one's share of the goods of fortune that Aristotle roots in an unlimited desire for mere living - which may even explain why Romero's dead return, grasping from beyond the grave for more un-death. Aristotle's dismissal of the life of enjoyment as fit for cattle, his account of eudaimonia as the life in which our rational capacities flourish, and his defense of acquisition bounded by the good life explain why unlimited consumption zombifies the living, and why Peter and Fran must finally leave the mall empty-handed.
- ch. 15 "Fast Zombies" **·** Baruch Spinoza, Gilles Deleuze, Sigmund Freud — Substance Monism (Deus sive Natura); The Unconscious — K. Silem Mohammad explains the horror of zombie slowness through Freud's uncanny - the return of the repressed, from White Zombie's hypnotized slaves (a figure for alienated industrial labor) to Romero's shuffling radioactive ghouls - then rebuilds undeath on Spinoza's Ethics: death as a body's parts acquiring a different proportion of motion and rest, the mind as a 'spiritual automaton,' and (via Deleuze's ethology of composition, decomposition, and bad encounters) zombies as bodies that decompose living bodies and recompose them as zombie bodies, replacing moral good and evil with good and bad. The fast zombies of 28 Days Later and the Dawn remake abandon hunger and the uncanny for pure rage and 'the Real,' signaling a late-capitalist permanent crisis in which the living have had their chance and blown it.
- ch. 16 "PART III - Dirty Rotting Scoundrels" *(front)*
- ch. 17 "Postmodern Postmortem" **·** David Chalmers, Seneca — Philosophical Zombies — Dale Jacquette distinguishes Hollywood zombies from Chalmers-style philosophical zombies, imagines a smiley-face birthmark that makes the latter visible, and argues that since zombies lack consciousness, pain, and intention, they are amoral - so zombie gladiatorial games would wrong no one, an improvement on the Roman munera Seneca condemned as pure murder. The thought experiment then turns on itself: even with zombie-only upbringing, screened audiences, and everything short of actual suffering on the zombies' side, the games would still be wrong for what they symbolically enact - the social condoning of slaughter of genetically human beings for entertainment. The conclusion is that consciousness and personhood do not exhaust morality: zombies get a moral pass, while the entire moral burden falls on the conscios who organize and watch.
- ch. 18 "Universal Rights Vampires, Space Aliens, Robots, and Humans" **·** Moral Psychology (character, vice, weakness of will); Punishment: Retribution vs Rehabilitation — John Draeger asks which Undead can be held accountable for bloodthirsty behavior: Romero's zombies are amoral, like a poisonous plant or Cleckley's empathy-free psychopath Milt, while Dracula meticulously plans his move to London and so passes the cognitive test. Stoker's narcissistic Count stays amoral, but Coppola's Dracula (who warns Mina of her curse), Rice's guilt-torn Louis, and the self-deceiving, cruelty-enjoying Lestat show empathy, self-control, and moral reflection, making them immoral and responsible - which entitles vampires to due process rather than Van Helsing's vigilantism, to punishment aimed at rehabilitation, and to equal rights including civic participation and marriage, since the same standard would govern space aliens, robots, and humans.
- ch. 19 "Cold Clammy Consistency" **·** Aristotle, Peter Singer — Animal Liberation; Food Ethics; Practical Wisdom (phronesis); The Golden Rule — Wayne Yuen argues vampires are moral agents - rational by Aristotle's standard of practical wisdom (Lestat's investments and his judgments about evildoers tasting better) and free despite their blood-craving, since craving is a strong preference, not the compulsion of the heroin addict or the castaway drinking seawater. Since animal blood is a viable alternative (Louis's rats, Angel and Spike's butcher's blood), the justifications for drinking human blood - taste, reaching full potential, spiritual peace - all fail against the principle that a world with less unnecessary suffering is better. Then the consistency argument turns on us: factory-farmed meat inflicts unnecessary suffering for the sake of taste while vegetarianism is fully viable, so condemning vampires while eating chicken is inconsistent - we as human beings are vampiric.
- ch. 20 "Good Guy Vampires" **·** John Stuart Mill, Aristotle — Higher and Lower Pleasures; Mind–Body Dualism — Robert Arp models the vampire's ontology with substance versus property dualism - Rice's vampires whose souls outlast the stake versus Blade's vampires who perish with their bodies - and uses Mill's distinction between lower bodily and higher mental pleasures from Utilitarianism to explain how vampires can both feel nothing bodily and suffer mentally (the opening quotation's regret at missing the wind in one's hair). Their real plight is the hedonistic paradox: pleasure pursued for its own sake yields pain, mistaken pleasures (Lestat on the dead twins, Blade's rave), or the boredom of an immortality that has 'seen it all.' The good guy vampires from Barnabas Collins to St. Germain escape by pursuing higher pleasures and activities for their own sake, vindicating Mill's closing advice that only those whose minds are fixed on something other than their own happiness find it.
- ch. 21 "Deserving to Be a Vampire" **·** Epicurus, Martha Nussbaum, Aristotle — Death Is Nothing to Us; Immortality and the Soul; Applied Ethics (bioethics, technology, AI) — Ted Preston asks whether becoming a vampire would be morally good or desirable, working through Forever Knight's Nick draining his lover, Blade feeding on stored blood, and The Lost Boys' promise that Michael will 'never grow old'. He argues vampires are not evil by nature: if they cannot control their hunger they are pitied rather than blamed (though we remain Aristotle-responsible for choosing to become addicted), and consensual, non-lethal feeding is no worse than mortals eating animals, since the bite only becomes murder by choice. Against Epicurus's claim that death is nothing to us, he uses Furley and Nussbaum's deprivation view - death vindictively empties our long-term projects like marriage or a career - so vampiric immortality genuinely preserves value, while the standard boredom objection fails because there are repeatable pleasures in a changing world. The thought experiment ends as moral psychology for mortals: since neither of us will become a vampire, live a life that would be worthy of eternity.
- ch. 22 "PART IV - Digging Up the Body Politic" *(front)*
- ch. 23 "Possibilities for Human-Vampire Co-operation" **·** Robert Nozick, Peter Singer, John Rawls, Adam Smith — The Minimal State; The Drowning Child (obligation to aid); Speciesism — Douglas Glen Whitman contrasts two pop-culture templates: Buffy's vampire brothel, where willing humans pay vampires for consensual bites (Giles calls the customers 'willing victims'), and Blade's implied government blood bank, where humans are forced to donate. Running Robert Nozick's personhood criteria (sentience, rationality, free will, moral agency, soul) against vampires, he argues no standard admits humans while excluding them, so staking vampires violates rights and, on Singer's suffering-equals-suffering logic, refusing consideration is a kind of speciesism. The welfarist side - Singer and Unger's principle that we must prevent bad at little cost, as in the drowning child - would mandate Blade-style forced blood transfers, but Whitman contends such coercion breeds the very xenophobia (Adam Smith's Poor Law settlement rules, anti-immigrant welfare politics) that blocks recognizing vampire personhood. Only a libertarian market of voluntary exchange turns human-vampire relations from a zero-sum war into a positive-sum commerce.
- ch. 24 "A Political Philosophy of Vampires" **·** Jean-Jacques Rousseau, Sigmund Freud — Binary Oppositions; The Absurd — Phillip Cole takes Rousseau's remark that the eighteenth-century vampire epidemics had complete legal proof - the Arnold Paole exhumations - and argues Rousseau's real point was political: authorities manufacture imaginary monsters, as in the witch craze, to exploit fear and hold power over their own populations. Freud's account of the uncanny explains why the horror is not death itself but the returning dead (the watching eyes of Scratchy in Itchy and Scratchy, versus the serene corpses of Six Feet Under, whose absurdity the show meets with wry amusement), and Julia Kristeva's abjection explains the corpse as the ultimate boundary-breaker. The vampire then resurfaces as the demonized outsider - Stoker's Dracula coded as the unassimilated Jewish immigrant, today's terrorist - exposing political borders as arbitrary fabrications. Buffy completes the collapse of the us/them binary: Angel and Spike join the slayer, and the most shocking killings (Faith's accidental human kill, Warren's stray bullet hitting Tara) are all too human, so the real subject of border policing is the community's own members.
- ch. 25 "The Possibility of a Non-Repressive Civilization" **·** Sigmund Freud, Herbert Marcuse — Civilization and Its Discontents; The Death Drive (Thanatos); Utopia and Dystopia — Simon Clark reads Romero's four Dead films through Freud's Civilization and Its Discontents: the zombie is the return of the repressed, the ripped-out instinctual life of a civilization that demands work, and it multiplies exactly as humans (Stephen defending the mall, Ben fighting Mr. Cooper) lapse into unlawful self-gratification. The bite unites Eros and Thanatos - an erotic, engorged cannibalism that also regresses victims to rotting matter, which Clark glosses with Creed's vagina dentata. He then argues Day of the Dead's beach ending (John fishing, Sarah at peace) stages Marcuse's non-repressive civilization where work becomes play and liberated sexuality rejoins Eros, while Land of the Dead goes further: the evolved, tool-using zombies led by Big Daddy are the martyrs who actually found Marcuse's impossible pleasurable community, leaving living humans still fenced inside repression.
- ch. 26 "No Way Out" **·** Thomas Hobbes, Jean-Jacques Rousseau — The Social Contract; The State of Nature; The General Will; Communitarianism — Leah Murray reads Romero's Dead series as an argument about the American social contract, individualism versus communitarianism. Night of the Living Dead's farmhouse is Hobbes: fear throws strangers Ben and Mr. Cooper into a makeshift commonwealth whose individualist half ('every man for himself' in the basement) fails, while Dawn shows the Hobbesian next step - martial law as Leviathan - after the state of nature returns. Land of the Dead's Fiddler's Green is a fear-born contract corrupting into Kaufman's hierarchy, and Big Daddy's zombies form the counter-model, a Rousseauian community of the general will based on mutual bonds rather than terror, as the priest in Dawn already urged ('we must stop the killing... or lose the war'). Following Tocqueville on civic association, she concludes Romero's survivors - Ben, Peter, Sarah, Riley - vindicate communitarian cooperation, even though Night's bureaucratic ending suggests there may be no way out.
- ch. 27 "PART V - Leaving a Good-Looking Corpse" *(front)*
- ch. 28 "The Meta-Fear of Fear" **·** Martin Heidegger, Sigmund Freud, Henri Bergson — Being-Toward-Death; The Unconscious; Ockham's Razor; Laughter (the comic as mechanical encrusted on the living) — Noel Carroll traces Halloween's Undead imagery to the Celtic Samhain and its Catholic absorption into All Hallows' Eve, then asks the paradox: why do we take pleasure in images of the death that, as Heidegger says, is the deep source of Angst we spend our lives denying? He examines Ernest Jones's Freudian analysis - the vampire as wish-fulfillment disguising an incestuous desire, horror as the price the super-ego exacts - and rejects it as theoretically expensive, invoking the demand for simpler, more economical explanation. His own hypothesis is the 'meta-fear of fear': adolescent viewers (and aging ones) use horror films and the holiday's zombie costumes to test and master fear itself, with parody and Bergson's laughter as companion devices of emotional control - a folk remedy that needs no unconscious to work.
- ch. 29 "The Male Vampire as Metrosexual . . . Sort Of" **·** Michel Foucault — Discipline and Docile Bodies; Feminism; Objectification — Joan Grassbaugh Forry asks how conventional standards of beauty, femininity, and masculinity operate in the vampire aesthetic of Interview with the Vampire, Buffy, and Queen of the Damned. Using Foucault's figure of the soldier whose body is 'made' by discipline, and Bartky and Bordo's feminist extension of it to dieting, deportment, and ornamenting the female body, she argues vampires expose the ideology of natural beauty: they are youthful, elegant, and effortlessly beautiful - no gym, no reflection - so their beauty is pure naturalness with the discipline erased. Gender decides the payoff: ultra-feminine female vampires (Drusilla's dresses and tea parties, Akasha, childlike Claudia) are femmes fatales whose threat must be punished by death, ridicule, or infantilization, while feminized male protagonists (Louis, Angel, Spike painting his nails and watching Passions - the 'metrosexual') win audience identification and tragic dignity. Beauty, in short, is a shield for the male vampire and a death sentence for the female.
- ch. 30 "Oh Yes, There Will Be Blood" **·** Aristotle — Personal Identity; Immortality and the Soul; Free Will vs Determinism — Rachel Robison asks why blood, of all substances, carries metaphysical powers in vampire lore - Elizabeth Bathory's youth-giving baths, Dracula's control over Mina once she drinks his blood, Sookie's bond with Bill in True Blood. Her explanation is historical: the ancient cardiocentric view (Aristotle, Empedocles, Chrysippus placing the rational center in the heart and making blood its product) plus Galenic humorism and bloodletting made blood the carrier of the self, so drinking someone's blood plausibly transmits bits of their personal identity, knowledge, and even domination over one's free will. She then tests the show's synthetic 'True Blood': either some essential property of blood (or of the act of drinking it) carries the power, or the legend is parasitic on our lingering habit of locating emotion and character in the heart ('hot blooded') - either way the myth stays credible because our metaphors of blood never died.
- ch. 31 "A Meditation on the Word" **·** Michel Foucault, Thomas Aquinas, Friedrich Nietzsche, G.W.F. Hegel, Socrates — Power/Knowledge; Will to Power; Philosophy of Love — Randall Auxier and Eileen Townsend fictionalize the history of philosophy as vampire predation in letters, emails, and IMs (1983-2009) between the 800-year-old Etienne Lavec - a student of Siger de Brabant who drained Thomas Aquinas over the Summa's Treatise on Angels, which wrote vampires out of existence - and Paulie, a freshman turned at Memphis State. Lavec recounts tasting Pico, Spinoza, Hegel, and the willing Kierkegaard, sparing Nietzsche who 'stole some of my ideas about free death', and contrasts vampire self-knowledge (embracing desire) with the Socratic beginning in affirmed ignorance. The centerpiece is Foucault: Lavec summarizes his genealogies of how institutions create the sick, the mad, the prisoner, and the pervert, fails to drain him, and is then haunted for decades by posthumous publications, taunting 'nietzsche26' emails, and finally a catacombs trap - power/knowledge that refuses to die. Lavec's embedded 'Meditation on the Word' recasts the Logos of John's Gospel so that vampires are the uncreated darkness at the boundary of the divine Word, pure self-grounding desire 'beyond good and evil', while the closing Twilight-era chats satirize Stephenie Meyer's sparkling, abstinent Edward as Foucauldian 'sin by words'.
- ch. 32 "Philosophers by Day . ." *(notes)*
- ch. 33 "Hemolytic Index" *(notes)*
- ch. 34 "Zombies, Vampires, and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 35 "Zombies, Vampires, and Philosophy" *(notes)*
- ch. 36 "Copyright Page" *(front)*

## The Walking Dead and Philosophy - Zombie Apocalypse Now

- ch. 1 "The Walking Dead and Philosophy" *(not yet read)*
- ch. 2 "The Walking Dead and Philosophy" *(not yet read)*
- ch. 3 "The Walking Dead and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 4 "The Walking Dead and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 5 "The Walking Dead and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 6 "The Walking Dead and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 7 "The Walking Dead and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 8 "The Walking Dead and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 9 "The Walking Dead and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 10 "The Walking Dead and Philosophy" **·** René Descartes, David Chalmers, Thomas Hobbes — Philosophical Zombies; The State of Nature; Mind–Body Dualism — Editor Wayne Yuen's introduction argues that zombies are worth doing philosophy over: an apocalyptic world without government, law enforcement, or private property raises live questions about how survivors should treat one another, whether life becomes 'nasty, brutish, and short', and whether it is wrong to kill a walker. He frames walkers philosophically through Descartes's mindless human-looking automatons and David Chalmers's P-zombies, creatures functionally like us with no inner life. Morgan keeping photo albums instead of supplies in 'Days Gone Bye' shows that what we leave behind after the apocalypse is how we lived - our philosophy.
- ch. 11 "The Walking Dead and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 12 "The Walking Dead and Philosophy" **·** David Chalmers, René Descartes, Daniel Dennett, Gilbert Ryle — Philosophical Zombies; Mind–Body Dualism; The Hard Problem of Consciousness; What Is It Like to Be a Bat? — Gordon Hawkes uses the P-zombie debate to ask whether there is something in a person no walker can eat. Rick telling Morgan over zombified Duane that 'that's just his body' voices Cartesian dualism, while Dr. Jenner's TS-19 scan - the neocortex 'you part' that never comes back - voices materialism, as does Dr. Stevens's dying claim that he is 'just evolving into a different, worse life form'. The chapter stages Chalmers's zombie argument (a molecule-identical twin with no inner experience) against Dennett's zimbo objection, with Ryle's 'ghost in the machine' marking the interaction problem for dualism, and closes on the intuition that no brain scan of Test Subject 19 reveals what the zombie fever feels like.
- ch. 13 "The Walking Dead and Philosophy" **·** Daniel Dennett — Personal Identity; Philosophical Zombies; The Hard Problem of Consciousness — Greg Littmann asks whether 'you' survive a walker bite, testing bodily identity, behaviorism, and functionalism as theories of personal survival against Jenner's verdict that after death 'everything you ever were... gone'. His centerpiece adapts Daniel Dennett: survivors yoke walkers to plows and tanks, dangle meat to move them between positions labeled 1 and 0, and gradually build a rope-and-corpse computer functionally identical to a human brain - which behaviorism and functionalism must call conscious, yet which we can conceive as utterly dark inside. The lesson is that we cannot know whether any system, walker, animal, or AI, is conscious, so treatment of non-human systems may need to err on the side of caution.
- ch. 14 "The Walking Dead and Philosophy" **·** The Gettier Problem; Reliabilism — Richard Greene asks what, if anything, walkers know. Rick's optimistic feeling that Lori and Carl survived turns out true, yet is not knowledge - true belief is necessary but insufficient, the gap Gettier cases exploit - and since walkers can never weigh evidence or think about their thoughts, only externalist, reliability-based accounts of knowledge can count them as knowers. Walker behavior defeats Jenner's 'mindless instinct' line: Jenny turning the handle of her old house, the little girl walker retrieving her teddy bear, a walker smashing the department store window with a rock, and the horde seeing through Rick and Glenn's gut disguise once rain washes the scent away. Zombie knowledge is real but limited to simple things recalled from the person's past, the more complex the task the less likely they know it.
- ch. 15 "The Walking Dead and Philosophy" **·** Immanuel Kant, Thomas Aquinas, Boethius, Peter Singer — Respect for Persons; Speciesism; Personal Identity — Delfino and Taylor ask whether Morgan's zombified wife is still a person, which decides whether shooting her is murder. On the dominant Western view - Boethius's 'individual substance of a rational nature', Aquinas's image-of-God intellect, Kantian dignity against Singer's speciesism challenge - walkers lack intelligence and so are not persons, so Morgan should shoot. They then argue walkers are metaphysically impossible beings, square-circles: dead tissue that somehow sees, smells, and runs down prey without functioning organs, energy intake, or the reanimated neocortex that movement and sensation require. Zombies fascinate because they are projections of our fears - dead yet moving, an afterlife of horror - a Hamlet-like caught-between-being-and-nonbeing.
- ch. 16 "The Walking Dead and Philosophy" **·** Immanuel Kant — Humanity as an End in Itself; Autonomy of the Will — McKendry and Da Silva build a Kantian case that walkers deserve respect as 'residual humans': Rick dwelling on organ-donor Wayne Dunlap before harvesting his corpse for camouflage shows that bodies carry the dead's chosen ends, so using Wayne is honoring, not using, him. Because walkers are corpses bound by the presumptive wishes of the deceased, and retain residual subjectivity (Jenny returning again and again to her family's door), survivors wrong them by treating them as mere objects - Merle's sport killing of walkers on the rooftop is the moral low, Rick's swift, risk-accepting headshots of Hannah and Deputy Leon the respectful execution. Jim's informed choice to be left by the road to turn, over Shane's and Rick's objections, shows the autonomy of the infected that no one else may override.
- ch. 17 "The Walking Dead and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 18 "The Walking Dead and Philosophy" **·** Thomas Nagel — What Is It Like to Be a Bat?; Personal Identity — Dave Beisecker amplifies Noel Carroll's paradox of horror: why would anyone enjoy watching, let alone compete for, AMC's 'stagger-on role' as a repulsive walker? Curiosity fails as an answer, since by Nagel's bat argument no amount of staggering around a set tells us what it is like to be a zombie - and since zombification destroys memory, desire, and self-concern, we would not survive it as persons in any interesting sense anyway. His solution is that zombies are the endearing losers and clowns of the undead world: playing one is a way to celebrate our inner awkward nerd and laugh at universal human ineptitude, which is exactly why Kirkman's shambling walkers, unlike fast zombies or glamorous vampires, invite impersonation.
- ch. 19 "The Walking Dead and Philosophy" **·** John Locke, Thomas Hobbes — The State of Nature; Labor Theory of Property; The Social Contract; Natural Rights — Jason Walker argues the zombie apocalypse is a Lockean rather than Hobbesian state of nature: rights to life, liberty, and property persist without government. Rick's 'I don't think those rules apply anymore' to Andrea over the mermaid necklace is justified by Locke's spoilage and 'enough and as good' provisos - the owner is almost certainly dead and the necklace would otherwise lie forever wasted - while Morgan's house-squatting, which protects the Thompsons' home, and Rick voluntarily sharing guns with Guillermo's nursing-home defenders show self-ownership and norms at work. The counter-cases, Chris's cannibal band ('a man's gotta eat') and the Governor, who treat persons as mere means, live out Hobbes's war of all against all and are destroyed by the Lockean right to punish that victims retain.
- ch. 20 "The Walking Dead and Philosophy" **·** Feminism; Gender Performativity; Stoicism — Ashley Barkman argues that feminism is unrealistic and inefficient in the apocalypse: citing sex-difference research such as Bandura's Bobo doll experiments, she claims masculine aggression and strength are what leadership requires when authority must be asserted physically, as when Rick pipes Merle and shoves Daryl, or Shane tackles Ed and Jim. The quarry laundry scenes stage the debate - Jacqui's protest at 'all the Hattie McDaniel work' versus Lori's 'this isn't about women's rights... doing what needs to be done' - and Barkman sides with Lori, treating the division of labor as survival logistics under a natural-law premise that it is moral to act according to nature. Andrea the sharpshooter and Michonne the swordswoman show women can excel as heroic exceptions without leading, and the chapter concludes leadership should stay with good men like Rick.
- ch. 21 "The Walking Dead and Philosophy" **·** Judith Butler, Richard Rorty — Gender Performativity; Feminism; Heteronormativity — Pye and O'Sullivan use Judith Butler's claim that gender is performative to read the walkers as genderless: they keep the trappings of biological sex but perform no gender, and like drag queens they 'trouble gender' when the little girl walker turns to face Rick. The survivors, by contrast, drag patriarchy along as cultural baggage - Lori telling Carl 'I'm a girl, you talk to him' while Shane teaches frogs, Ed bullying the laundry line, the men confiscating Andrea's gun while Daryl keeps his crossbow - though counter-examples (Andrea and Amy fishing for the camp, Guillermo's camp of caretaking 'vatos' led by a former custodian and a nurse, Rick teaching Andrea the safety) show gender can be learned differently. Invoking Rorty's thought that assumptions become visible only when their contradictories sound plausible, they argue survival itself demands shedding these roles, since the genderless dead threaten everyone equally.
- ch. 22 "The Walking Dead and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 23 "The Walking Dead and Philosophy" **·** Albert Camus, Thomas Nagel — The Absurd; The Myth of Sisyphus; Rebellion and Revolt; The Meaning of Life — Rachel Robison-Greene reads the survivors through Camus: walkers are mere digger wasps, living tedious, meaningless loops without the consciousness that would make it agonizing, but the living - Morgan longing for the real Jenny back, Andrea losing Amy - face the absurd confrontation between human longing and an indifferent universe, just like Oran in Camus's The Plague. Of Camus's options, suicide (Jacqui and Dr. Jenner staying in the exploding CDC) and religion ('GOD FORGIVE US' on the farmhouse wall) are escapist, so she offers the absurd hero: Sisyphus with his rock, Rieux with his plague, matched by Rick hunting Atlanta for his family, Merle's self-preservation, and Daryl fighting uphill with an arrow in his side. Against Nagel's ironic alternative, she argues we cheer Rick when he alone will shoot Sophia in Hershel's barn because his subjective commitments stay noble even with no objective meaning behind them.
- ch. 24 "The Walking Dead and Philosophy" **·** Niccolò Machiavelli — The Ends Justify the Means; The State of Nature — Si Sheppard grades The Walking Dead's three settlements by political Realism: the Governor's Woodbury - cohesive, feared rather than loved, feeding his people arena spectacle to keep them from turning on him - beats Rick's West Central Prison, which never becomes a state at all. Rick lets Tyreese's vigilante murder pass, ruling as a first-among-equals with no institutions, no constitution, and no monopoly of legitimate force in Weber's sense, then covertly guns down Thomas and Dexter while hiding behind his cop credibility - 'it's me or you' reasoning indistinguishable from Shane's. Realism cares about power, not morality, so Rick's flaw is not cruelty but the absence of institution-building: he was never a bad father, just a bad founding father, who only becomes a Realist after Alexandria falls.
- ch. 25 "The Walking Dead and Philosophy" **·** Jean-Paul Sartre — Existence Precedes Essence; Radical Freedom and Responsibility; Authenticity; Existentialism — Kempner reads The Walking Dead through Sartre's 'Existentialism Is a Humanism': with God and the old value-system gone, survivors who cling to pre-made essence die — Hershel keeps his zombified son locked in the barn awaiting restoration, and Shane tries to steal Rick's old cop-and-husband identity rather than live his own existence. Rick's waking in the hospital is a rebirth: he first exists in the new world, then creates a way of being human, and his relapses into the sheriff uniform nearly get him killed. Carl shooting Shane to save his father completes the family's existential freedom (children are no longer 'innocent'), walkers — without will or choice — are the ultimate inauthentic beings, and the volume's tagline, 'forced to finally start living,' makes the zombie world a sternly optimistic humanist story.
- ch. 26 "The Walking Dead and Philosophy" **·** Jean-Paul Sartre, Søren Kierkegaard, Albert Camus, Theodor Adorno — Bad Faith; Authenticity; The Absurd; The Look (hell is other people) — Round argues the real horror of The Walking Dead is humanity itself, read through existentialism: walkers cannot choose, so the humans who attack, torture, and steal from each other are the true 'zombie Other.' She traces Rick's self-made, revisable moral code — from law-enforcer to 'You kill? You die' to killing Dexter — as authentic situation-based morality, paralleled with Kierkegaard's Abraham and with Abraham Ford's confession 'I did what I wanted to do' set against Eugene's bad-faith 'I did what I had to do.' Suicides like Jim, Jacqui, and Carol embody existential angst before an absurd world, the Governor's Woodbury is an Adorno-style inauthentic hierarchy that manipulates its people, and Rick's 'WE ARE THE WALKING DEAD!' speech uses facticity to insist the group admit what they have already become.
- ch. 27 "The Walking Dead and Philosophy" **·** Jean-Jacques Rousseau, Aristotle, Thomas Aquinas — Natural Goodness and Corruption by Society; Faith and Reason; Virtue Ethics (arete); Free Will vs Determinism — Greeley argues the zombie crystallizes specifically modern fears: with Aristotle and Aquinas he reads walkers as humans stripped of their essential nature and potential, which Rick's uncustomary eulogies ('I'm sorry this happened to you') acknowledge. Following Philippe Aries, he ties modern death anxiety to urbanization, hidden cemeteries, and the loss of religious consolation, citing the CDC arc where Jenner's science offers only a self-destruct and Jacqui falls back on 'the wrath of God.' Rousseau's thesis that society corrupts explains why the survivors in the woods seem more alive — warlord Guillermo turns out to be protecting nursing-home patients and pizza-deliverer Glenn becomes a hero — while the 'Bradbury's Storewide Sale' sign, the Walmart trampling, and Fromm's shift from utopias to dystopias suggest the shuffling horde is an image of our own consumerist, distracted, dehumanized modernity.
- ch. 28 "The Walking Dead and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 29 "The Walking Dead and Philosophy" **·** Peter Singer, Immanuel Kant, Aldo Leopold, Arne Naess — Anthropocentrism; Animal Liberation; The Land Ethic; Deep Ecology — Hinzmann and Arp ask whether zombies can be morally harmed: Jenner's fMRI in 'TS-19' shows only the brain stem reactivates, leaving walkers with a merely shark-like 'perceptual mind.' Against anthropocentric theories, under which Michonne may keep her chained pet walkers as guard-dog tools, they set Singer's utilitarian expanding circle, Leopold's land ethic, and Naess's deep ecology, each of which could support a 'PETZ' zombie preserve. Hershel's barn of 'sick people' and Shane's massacre of it in 'Pretty Much Dead Already' show how fragile such preservation is, while the Governor's death-match arena ringed with chained zombies and his kept zombie daughter illustrate Kant's point that cruelty toward mere animals corrodes the humanity of the cruel person himself.
- ch. 30 "The Walking Dead and Philosophy" **·** Immanuel Kant, Jeremy Bentham, Tom Regan — The Hedonic Calculus; The Golden Rule — Allaire argues walkers have no moral value and can be killed without remorse: a Bentham-style act-utilitarian tally favors killing any walker, and, following Kant, genetic humanity is neither necessary nor sufficient for membership in the moral community. Applying Mary Anne Warren's five personhood criteria, he shows walkers lack pain response (Hannah's severed half still dragging itself at Rick), language, planning (Glenn's gun-bag mission versus aimless shuffling), and self-reflection, so as beings incapable of duty they can have no rights. Morgan's inability to shoot his walker-wife and Rick's 'I'm sorry this happened to you' illustrate Kant's 'duality of the human situation' — we irrationally project personhood onto former people — and our hesitation is precisely the walkers' greatest advantage.
- ch. 31 "The Walking Dead and Philosophy" **·** Aristotle, Confucius, Laozi — Natural Law; Practical Wisdom (phronesis); Virtue Ethics — Barkman takes Andrea's mermaid-necklace question ('I don't think those rules apply any more') as a test of natural law: self-evident principles — general beneficence (Glenn's 'shit creek' rescue, Morgan's warning that it 'takes away a piece of your soul'), special beneficence toward family (Lori's 'Don't be so trusting,' Morales's 'best for my family'), and piety (Guillermo staying with the abandoned elders) — still bind after the state's collapse, while 'do not steal' is merely derived, so the unowned necklace is fair game and Rick could take some but not most of Andrea's food. Aristotle's practical wisdom resolves the cannibal hunters' collision between preserving their lives and not killing innocents — starving is the morally preferable choice — since virtue weighs principles 'all things considered.' Against Carol's 'we can make new rules' relativism the natural law outlives governments, and Father Gabriel errs by calling walkers 'abominations,' conflating bad (dysfunctional) with evil (willful) — whereas the Governor, who knowingly ignores the moral law, reduces himself to walker level.
- ch. 32 "The Walking Dead and Philosophy" **·** Friedrich Nietzsche, Plato, Aristotle — Master and Slave Morality; Theory of Forms; Will to Power; Amor Fati (love of fate) — Rard tests whether objective morality survives the apocalypse by running four theories against Season Two: Plato's Forms explain universal truths about walkers ('walkers survive so long as their brains are relatively undamaged') but cannot handle white lies or changed circumstances, and Aristotle's virtue ethics reads Rick's rush from the shot Carl as rashness and Shane's supply run as courage, yet virtues prove relative and luck-dependent in a collapsed world. Natural law founders on clashing intuitions — Merle's racism and Hershel's barn of walkers each claim self-evidence. The chapter's center is Nietzschean: Hershel's barn is slave morality that pities the 'sick,' Maggie breaks from it, and Shane's sacrifice of Otis and opening of the barn enact a will-to-power master morality without joy, while Rick alone moves toward amor fati — embracing the world as it is, refusing guilt over Lori's affair, and insisting the baby be born into the Walk Age.
- ch. 33 "The Walking Dead and Philosophy" **·** Thomas Hobbes, Albert Camus — The State of Nature; The Drowning Child (obligation to aid); The Absurd; The Meaning of Life — Moosa uses the apocalypse as a moral laboratory: since 'murder' is by definition wrongful killing, Carl's shooting of Shane must be judged by his reasoning (saving his father), and Hobbes's state of nature — 'solitary, poor, nasty, brutish, and short' — fits Kirkman's world, yet the survivors import morality into it, as when parents mercy-kill their children just as one would turn up a dying child's morphine. Taking up Camus's claim that suicide is the one truly serious philosophical problem, he argues our world is nearly as bad as the zombie landscape, so consistency commits comfortable readers to the same duty of aid (helping sufferers rather than opting out) that justifies the survivors' fighting on. Finally he argues procreation is the worst thing survivors can do — nonexistent children cannot suffer, so Lori's pregnancy and Glenn and Maggie's crib plans are immoral, and adopting existing children as Andrea and Dale did is the consistent course, an obligation that transfers to our world right now.
- ch. 34 "The Walking Dead and Philosophy" **·** Immanuel Kant — Utilitarianism; The Categorical Imperative; Punishment: Retribution vs Rehabilitation — Yuen distinguishes heroic survival cannibalism, as in the Andes crash of Alive, from the 'Fear the Hunters' cannibals: both utilitarianism and Kantianism permit eating those who already died out of necessity, since the dead lose no utility and corpses lack the rationality that grounds Kantian dignity. But Chris's band killed children to eat them and kept the living Dale as a food store, which fails both theories and cannot be excused by 'we don't do this because we want to,' since alternatives existed — joining other survivors, eating walkers ('zombitarianism'), or starving, because survival is never a moral obligation. Rick's execution of the hunters is then defended as Kantian retributive punishment: if Chris universalized his maxim that people may be hunted for food, then, as Rick suggests while biting Chris's neck, 'if Dale was good as a steak, so is Chris.'
- ch. 35 "The Walking Dead and Philosophy" *(notes)*
- ch. 36 "The Walking Dead and Philosophy" *(notes)*
