## The Wire and Philosophy - This America, Man

- ch. 1 "The Wire and Philosophy" *(not yet read)*
- ch. 2 "The Wire and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 3 "The Wire and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 4 "The Wire and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 5 "The Wire and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 6 "The Wire and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 7 "The Wire and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 8 "The Wire and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 9 "The Wire and Philosophy" **·** Socrates, Aristotle — The Unexamined Life; Eudaimonia (flourishing) — Prof Joe's introduction casts The Wire, like Socrates, as a gadfly that refuses to let any viewer rest comfortably: tough-on-crime conservatives, Hamsterdam-friendly libertarians, hopeful liberals watching Royce and Carcetti, techno-optimists watching Freamon's wire, and capitalists watching the docks and the Baltimore Sun all get their beliefs challenged with no easy answers. It retells Socrates's refusal to escape Athens as valuing the quality of life over its quantity, and sets that against the Puritan work-ethic myth that blames the corner boys and fiends for their own fate while Aristotle's flourishing is rebranded as the American pursuit of happiness. The essay closes by previewing the book's six 'Cases' (the Game, tactics, Omar, boundaries, tragedy, sentencing) around Socrates's own question: how ought we to live?
- ch. 10 "The Wire and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 11 "The Wire and Philosophy" **·** Michel Foucault, Albert Camus — Discipline and Docile Bodies; The Myth of Sisyphus; The Absurd — Crosby takes Bunk's 'misdemeanor homicide' exchange about Marlo as the show's thesis that post-industrial capitalism makes human lives worth less, season by season devaluing beat cops, longshoremen, would-be reformers, the schoolkids of Season Four, and finally the journalists meant to sound the alarm. She deploys Foucault's Discipline and Punish to explain how institutions train docile participants and then require their regular sacrifice to keep operating, so that Simon's 'indifferent gods' of Greek tragedy are now postmodern institutions whose lightning bolts hit everyone in the 'hood. She ends with Camus's Sisyphus: Simon's call to commit to a righteous cause you probably will not win is the only response that offers human dignity, because 'this ain't Aruba... this America, man.'
- ch. 12 "The Wire and Philosophy" **·** Heraclitus, Albert Camus — Everything Flows / Logos; Rebellion and Revolt — Fagan traces the show's ubiquitous 'Game' to Heraclitus's fragment of Aion as a child moving game-pieces: the drug trade, police, docks, schools, and newsroom all have rules, fixed roles (D'Angelo's chess lecture where pawns get capped quick), winning reduced to stats, strife as justice, and cyclical recurrence with 'new dancers, new faces around the throne.' Bodie's dying rant that the game is rigged, the failed escapes of Wallace, D'Angelo, and Stringer's 'games beyond the game,' and even Omar's fatal loyalty to Butchie show that neither rule-following nor Omar-style code-breaking gets a piece off the board. Against critics who call the show nihilistic, he argues it rejects the Heraclitean picture through Camus's rebellion: Colvin, Bubbles, and Cutty fashion dignity by resisting the Game even though rebellion almost certainly fails.
- ch. 13 "The Wire and Philosophy" **·** John Locke, Thomas Hobbes — The State of Nature; Natural Rights — Allen asks how a virtual war zone can exist inside the United States, starting from Hobbes: Marlo's cool killing of the security guard who just wants to be recognized as a man looks like the rights-free state of nature where 'nothing can be unjust.' But he argues Locke's sharper distinction fits better - the underclass are nominal citizens whose rights to life, policing, and education exist on paper while their substance is denied, which for Locke places an oppressed minority in a state of war within a state, and makes the drug game a rational choice for Michael, Dukie, and Randy. Marlo's 'you want it to be one way, but it's the other way' names that collapsed protection, and the chapter ends with Locke's warning that majority apathy lets oppression persist, reading The Wire as protest art meant to move viewers to political action.
- ch. 14 "The Wire and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 15 "The Wire and Philosophy" **·** Plato, John Stuart Mill, Jeremy Bentham, Peter Singer — Utilitarianism; Justice in the Republic; Virtue Ethics — Bzdak uses Wee-Bey's 'you askin' too much' - and his ultimate yes to letting Bunny take Namond out of the Game - to pose the Puzzle of Impartiality: morality seems to require equal consideration (Plato's Socrates rejecting justice as helping friends and harming enemies; the impartialism built into Bentham's, Mill's, and Singer's utilitarianism), yet the show's most admirable moment is a father's partiality, and its worst parents, De'Londa and Raylene, fail exactly by not being partial to their own children. He tests a Greater Good Principle against cops protecting cops, Kima choosing honesty over loyalty to McNulty and Freamon, and Bunk's disgusted speech to Omar about the code that splits the community into victims who matter and victims who don't. The principle handles Carver reporting Colicchio but not the burning-building cases, and the show's verdict is that both too much and too little partiality destroy lives, with no single criterion deciding.
- ch. 16 "The Wire and Philosophy" **·** Aristotle, John Stuart Mill, Immanuel Kant — Practical Wisdom (phronesis); Virtue Ethics — McAleer ranks three Season-long deceptions - Sobotka's smuggling least wrong, McNulty's fake serial killer worse, Templeton's fabricated Sun stories worst - and argues that only the agents' motives and character explain the degrees of wrongness, against Mill's claim that motive has nothing to do with an action's morality and Kant's distinction between acting in accordance with and from duty. Aristotle's virtue ethics and his difference between practical wisdom and mere cleverness vindicate these verdicts: Sobotka lies to save a dying union, McNulty's cleverness is corrupted by his need to be the smartest boy in the room while Lester's additions lack all wisdom, and Templeton lies from naked careerism with no redemption arc. The chapter closes with the 'Haynes Principle' - 'you need a lot of context to seriously examine anything' - as the case for a messier, character-based moral philosophy.
- ch. 17 "The Wire and Philosophy" **·** Aristotle, Plato — Virtue Ethics (arete); Practical Wisdom (phronesis); Justice in the Republic — Grinnell reads Rawls's dotted case lists, Marimow's rip-and-run, Burrell's 'dope on the table,' and Prez being told he teaches the test rather than math as Aristotle's function argument gone wrong: professionals who reduce their purpose to stats and pleasing bosses are 'doing well' instead of 'doing good,' which is just what Plato says badly governed cities reward with honor. The characters who grasp their real function - Daniels refusing cheap felony stats, Bunk refusing to turn cases black without the true murderer, Freamon hunting bodies in vacants despite Landsman's fury - defy the Game at career cost, while Colvin's Hamsterdam and McNulty's fake killer show the opposite failure of zealous judgment without humility or rule of law. The chapter ends on the paradox that trusting professional judgment risks messes, but defining professions by numbers eliminates excellence altogether.
- ch. 18 "The Wire and Philosophy" *(no index entry yet)* — Thompson reads the desk-pushing gag as the show's theory of bureaucracy (rule by bureau), arguing along Weberian lines that formal rules, hierarchy, and quantification are the hallmark of all modern institutions - the Barksdale crew's 'don't talk in the car' and Robert's Rules of Order included - and that credit flows up while shit rolls downhill, making people interchangeable and disposable 'human resources.' Cases like Kima's shooting triggering politically motivated 'dope on the table' raids, the fourteen dead women fought over as clearance-rate liabilities, and D'Angelo's Chicken McNugget lecture show institutions betraying their missions, yet the chapter insists the response is not cynicism but 'giving a fuck when it ain't your turn': Daniels refusing to juke stats, Carver learning 'it all matters,' Gus spiking Templeton's story, and McNulty finally drawing a line he won't cross. (Its explicit framework is Max Weber's bureaucracy thesis, missing from the taxonomy - see holes.)
- ch. 19 "The Wire and Philosophy" **·** Immanuel Kant, Derek Parfit, David Lewis — The Categorical Imperative; Utilitarianism — Fallis tests Kant's claim that a universal 'lie whenever it is to your advantage' would necessarily destroy itself, and David Lewis's stricter thesis that communication requires mostly truth-tellers, against a West Baltimore where Avon, Omar, the cops, the lawyers, and Templeton all lie constantly. Parfit's rejoinder - that as long as some people sometimes tell the truth, deception stays possible - explains how Bubbles's lies keep working, while the heroin market shows Kant's mechanism at full scale: Stringer cutting product and rebranding 'pandemic' until 'every dope fiend in the city knows that Avon been putting out piss, calling it shit' and Westside dope collapses. Since Gant's and Greggs's costly truths still anchor the practice, the chapter concludes The Wire cannot quite refute Kant: it is not possible for nobody to walk the straight and narrow track.
- ch. 20 "The Wire and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 21 "The Wire and Philosophy" **·** Friedrich Nietzsche — The Übermensch; Master and Slave Morality; Will to Power; Amor Fati (love of fate) — Clifton measures Omar against four Nietzschean criteria for the overman: as a free spirit he is 'in the wind, so to speak,' detached from home, money, and eventually even lovers like Brandon and Butchie; as a non-dogmatist about truth he uses it as a tool, lying under oath at Bird's trial yet sparing Brother Mouzone when the lie no longer holds; and as a noble of the Genealogy he is an active hunter who embraces the will to power, telling Wee-Bey 'you come at the king, you best not miss.' But Omar fails the fourth test, value-creation: his code keeps the good/evil scale intact rather than overturning it, so in the Three Metamorphoses he remains the lion who slays 'Thou Shalt' but never becomes the value-creating child. The verdict, in Nietzsche's own phrase, is that Omar is noble but 'human, all too human.'
- ch. 22 "The Wire and Philosophy" **·** Plato, Judith Butler, Socrates, Aristotle — Gender Performativity; Aristotle on Friendship (philia); Socratic Method (elenchus) — Fisher argues The Wire makes homosexuality a source of excellence: Omar and Kima blend masculine and feminine traits in something like Butler's gender-performative terms, build Aristotelian friendships of virtue (Kima and Cheryl pushing each other's careers), and can make the sacrifices - solitude, giving up family - that excellence in the drug war demands, while the homophobia of the streets and the precinct forces them to keep proving themselves. Omar's crossword comfort with 'them myths' frames him as a contemporary Socrates: a mentor-lover of boys, accused of amorality by Levy much as Socrates was charged with corrupting Athens's youth, answering with a Socratic reversal ('I got the shotgun, you got the briefcase'), and dying for his code in the city he would not leave. The chapter reads the show's refusal of coming-out storylines - Rawls's silent gay-bar cameo, Snoop's never-confirmed sexuality - as its most radical statement about sexuality in America.
- ch. 23 "The Wire and Philosophy" **·** Aristotle — Virtue Ethics (arete); The Golden Mean; Eudaimonia (flourishing) — Trerise argues Omar is virtuous in Aristotle's full sense: honest on the stand ('I robs drug dealers... I got the shotgun, you got the briefcase'), courageous, consistent, and generous, with even his vengefulness a mean between extremes given a world where no one else will check Avon or Marlo - unlike Marlo he never guns on citizens and even pays Prop Joe for clock repairs. Virtue theory's attention to context separates his killings of Stinkum, Savino, and Stringer from Marlo's power-consolidating murders and shows him achieving whatever eudaimonia his world allows, though his obsession after Stringer's setup, which got Tosha killed, marks his one real excess. The catch is exemplification - Omar is disturbed that children play at being Omar, and Michael becomes the new him - yet the chapter concludes that some vigilantism is a social necessity on the streets and that Omar's swapped body tag names the injustice done to the street's truly virtuous.
- ch. 24 "The Wire and Philosophy" *(not yet read)*
- ch. 25 "The Wire and Philosophy" **·** Carol Gilligan, Nel Noddings — The Ethics of Care; Feminism — The Rochas argue that the only two women in the Game, Snoop on the street side and Kima on the police side, embody a 'militant feminism' in the lineage of the Black Panthers' Kathleen Cleaver and the Weather Underground's Bernardine Dohrn: women can excel at anything men can, including violence (Snoop as Marlo's natural soldier, repurposing a Hardware Barn nail gun, wearing womanhood only as a costume), and violence is justified when used for just ends (Kima overcoming fear to police). They then use Gilligan and Noddings's care ethics to explain the finale: Kima's dedication to relationships and care for the overlooked homeless victims lets her discern that McNulty and Freamon's fake-serial-killer scheme is an immoral masculinized rationalization, so she turns in her friends and becomes the show's moral conscience.
- ch. 26 "The Wire and Philosophy" **·** W.E.B. Du Bois, Kimberlé Crenshaw — Class Struggle; Intersectionality — Curry refuses to celebrate The Wire, arguing that Simon's class-based realism ('more about class than race') misrepresents Black urban life, and he contrasts it with Black film from New Jack City to Menace II Society where these issues were already explored. Reading scenes like Rawls's 'project nigger' murders no one cares about, Bunk shrugging at Stringer's unsolved killing, and McNulty telling Brianna that nobody will look into D'Angelo's death, he argues the show registers an indifference specific to Black life, not to the poor as such. Drawing on W.E.B. Du Bois's account of colonialism and the slave trade as the genesis of modern capital ('capital noir') and rejecting the intersectional race-class-gender frame as a 'discursive distraction', he demands a racial realism in Derrick Bell's sense: racism is permanent, the underclass is really an racialized undercaste, and the War on Drugs continues Jim Crow by other means.
- ch. 27 "The Wire and Philosophy" **·** John Locke — Consequentialism; The Social Contract; Punishment: Retribution vs Rehabilitation — Hoskins and Wikoff use Cutty's release after fourteen years in Jessup to ask what it means to have 'paid your debt to society' when ex-offenders still face bans on housing, jobs, welfare, student loans, and voting. They test the standard defenses: the consequentialist safety argument fails because these bans push men like Cutty back into the Game ('This game is rigged, man'); the competence argument collapses since McNulty, who did not even know the Democratic candidate, is no more competent a voter; and Judge Friendly's Locke-inspired social-contract claim that criminals forfeit their voice in lawmaking cannot explain why Prez's pistol-whipping or Clay Davis's corruption would not equally forfeit theirs. They conclude that continuing to punish after the sentence ends is unjust, and that Cutty's boxing gym shows former prisoners can reform.
- ch. 28 "The Wire and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 29 "The Wire and Philosophy" **·** G.W.F. Hegel — The Dialectic — Gurland-Blaker reads The Wire as Simon intended it, a Greek tragedy with institutions (police, docks, schools, politics, press) in place of the gods. Using Hegel's Aesthetics, he argues art unveils truth in sensuous form and that we identify with Sobotka, Bubbles, and Marlo because their magnified struggles are recognizably our own; dramatic tragedy works by staging collisions between incompatible but equally justified codes, exactly as Antigone's loyalty to divine law against Creon's law of the polis maps onto Avon's street rep, Stringer's business rationality, Marlo's name, and McNulty's vanity, all of which go down together. Stringer is the paradigm: the American Dream destroyed by the very virtues that constituted it ('deserve got nuthin' to do with it'), while the show out-Hegels Hegel by locating the deepest tragedy not in the heroes' deaths but in the restoration of the status quo, where lieutenants become colonels and mayors become governors and nothing changes.
- ch. 30 "The Wire and Philosophy" **·** Friedrich Nietzsche, Aristotle — The Apollonian and the Dionysian; Amor Fati (love of fate) — Brittingham argues The Wire is a Nietzschean rather than merely Aristotelian tragedy, a wrestling between the Apollonian orientation of order, statistics, and illusion (Rawls's clearance rates, Burrell's bureaucracy, Stringer's business plans) and the Dionysian reveling in the Game's chaos and suffering (Avon accepting that 'you only gotta fuck up once', McNulty's intoxicated self-destruction). Stringer is Nietzsche's 'theoretical man' whose faith that thought can correct existence, embodied in the New Day Co-Op, cannot save him from Omar's 'It ain't about money', while McNulty is the Dionysiac man inside the Apollonian institution until Freamon's 'the job will not save you' forces his resignation. The chapter closes by reading the characters' resigned embrace of their fates as Nietzschean affirmation: since neither the job, intelligence, violence, nor the gods will save you, the only response is to say yes to life anyway.
- ch. 31 "The Wire and Philosophy" **·** Karl Marx, G.W.F. Hegel — Class Struggle; Commodity Fetishism; Utopia and Dystopia — Zizek reads The Wire as subject-realism, a city's collective self-staging (with Simon as its DJ-curator) of the class struggle between the two Americas, where postmodern institutions play the Olympian gods and the self-propelling abstraction of capital is the Lacanian Real running the show, a point he anchors in Marx's prosopopoeia of commodities and 'real abstraction'. Following Jameson he catalogues the show's utopian gestures woven into reality (Frank's dream of revitalizing the port, Colvin's Hamsterdam, Prezbo's dice-based math, Avon and Stringer's last drink, D'Angelo's family utopia), then argues the Major Crimes unit of McNulty, Freamon, Kima, and Omar is a proto-Communist conspiratorial collective whose honest individual resistance (from Snot Boogie to McNulty's firing) merely feeds the system's self-reproduction. Hence his provocation: the only radical step beyond Simon's social-democratic reformism is the strike of the 'inherent transgressors', withdrawing the resistance that keeps the system alive so that Fate itself, not just its victims, can be changed.
- ch. 32 "The Wire and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 33 "The Wire and Philosophy" **·** G.W.F. Hegel, Karl Marx, John Dewey — Master–Slave Dialectic; Alienation; Experience and Education; Struggle for Recognition — Vannatta reads Season Four through Hegel's master-slave dialectic of recognition: Marlo's ultimatum to Bodie, Lex killing Fruit for Patrice, and D'Angelo's chess lesson (the pawn as slave producing for the king) all dramatize the struggle for recognition, while Marx's inversion of Hegel explains the alienation of the dockworkers, McNulty exiled to harbor patrol, and Freamon buried in pawn-shop detail by the chain of master-slave directives from Rawls through Marimow. The heart of the chapter is education: Prezbo's dice-and-card math projects embody Dewey's synthesis of child and curriculum against the 'banking concept' Freire attacked, but 'You don't teach math. You teach the test' shows the institutional logic winning, and Parenti's grant-funded project class with Colvin illustrates Freire's warning that the oppressor's humanitarian false generosity treats the corner kids as objects of study rather than partners in dialogue, leaving even its successes unable to show the kids why corner life is problematic.
- ch. 34 "The Wire and Philosophy" **·** Thomas Hobbes, Aristotle, Thomas Aquinas — Virtue Ethics (arete); Cardinal and Theological Virtues — Cooper argues The Wire stages two rival pictures of power: Hobbes's New School, where power is 'present means to some future apparent good', the good is only the apparently good relative to each desire, and honor is reputation indifferent to whether it is earned through love or fear (Clay Davis's graft, Ziggy's leather jacket, Avon displaying Brandon's mutilated body to 'send a message', Marlo spending exorbitant sums and torturing Butchie so his name keeps ringing out), and the Old School of Aristotle and Aquinas, where power is oriented to perfection and objective goods like family, friendship, and truth. Against the fear-based honor of the kingpins he sets McNulty's failed redemption and the successful reforms of Cutty's boxing gym, Walon's sobriety, and Bubs, whose prudence in taking the Sun job, temperance, justice at the Viva House, and fortitude in facing Sherrod's death embody the cardinal virtues, showing that even in the 'dark corner of the American experiment' virtue has the power to move.
- ch. 35 "The Wire and Philosophy" **·** Michel Foucault — Power/Knowledge; Discipline and Docile Bodies — Eckstrand uses Foucault to explain the show's central pattern that 'our thing is part of the big thing': power is exercised rather than possessed, flowing through networks in every direction, so even pawn Wallace can have kingpins, captains, and prosecutors running around after him, and McNulty's fake serial killer shows how the police-Sun-politics network can be worked from below. The stat game and standardized tests are Foucauldian power/knowledge, producing a truth ('shining up shit and calling it gold') that hides systemic crime and poverty while punishing truth-tellers like Daniels and reformers like Colvin and Freamon, and its three tools of discipline, hierarchical observation, normalizing judgment, and examination, are visible from Comstat and the project-class window to the grand jury that tames the dockworkers' union. Even reform becomes part of the machine it targets (Templeton's Pulitzer chase), so the best hope is the constant accumulation of small victories by those who keep fighting.
- ch. 36 "The Wire and Philosophy" **·** Cornel West, Mahatma Gandhi, Plato — Civil Disobedience; Ahimsa (nonviolence) — Cherry asks why even law-abiding citizens in The Wire obey the no-snitching code, answering through Cornel West's diagnoses: the police's 'evangelical nihilism' is Thrasymachus's might-makes-right acted out by Herc, Carver, and Prez at the Towers and by Walker breaking Donut's fingers, while the bosses' 'paternalistic nihilism' (Burrell juiking stats for Royce) leaves citizens not merely frightened but healthily hopeless, as Randy's 'You gonna look out for me? You promise?' shows after Herc burns him and the detail vanishes. Drawing on Angela Davis's argument that the criminal justice system inherited slavery's master-slave dynamic through the Black Codes, vagrancy laws, and the drug war, she reframes silence as active rebellion: a Gandhian philosophy of non-co-operation ('Fuck the Police') that withdraws the state's claim to citizens' dependence because witnesses like William Gant and Nakeesha Lyles are left unprotected.
- ch. 37 "The Wire and Philosophy" *(notes)*
- ch. 38 "The Wire and Philosophy" *(notes)*
- ch. 39 "The Wire and Philosophy" *(notes)*

## The Wizard of Oz and Philosophy - Wicked Wisdom of the West

- ch. 1 "section-1" *(not yet read)*
- ch. 2 "Title Page" *(not yet read)*
- ch. 3 "The Wizard of Oz and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 4 "Popular Culture and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 5 "Ideas and Images of Oz" *(no index entry yet)* — The editors' introduction argues that Oz endures because it is not one story but a shifting plurality of tales (storyteller Chris Chandler's point, likening it to the Iliad), which each reader can enter through any character, from the Lion to, after Gregory Maguire, the Wicked Witch herself. It models the volume's method with a small logical puzzle about 'there's no place like home'—the phrase is negative because Dorothy herself is part of what home means, so Kansas has a new Dorothy—and previews the book's recurring questions: the Yellow Brick Road as a many-meaninged journey metaphor, the virtues of brain, heart, and courage, evil (why must a little girl kill the Witch?), and the reality-status of Oz versus Kansas.
- ch. 6 "The Wizard of Oz and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 7 "I - Are You a Good Witch or a Bad Witch" *(front)*
- ch. 8 "There’s No Place Like Home" **·** Immanuel Kant — The Categorical Imperative; Humanity as an End in Itself; Utilitarianism — Luke Dick stages Immanuel Kant as the 'Supreme Critical Chancellor of Munchkinland' and runs the film through Kantian ethics: against hedonism, utilitarianism, and command-based religion, the formula of universalizability condemns the Wizard's lies and his use of the companions as mere means, while the ends-in-themselves principle raises the awkward case of Glinda and Dorothy keeping the dead Witch of the East's ruby slippers. Judging the characters, the Lion's rescue of Dorothy is morally praiseworthy (duty against strong inclination), the brainless Scarecrow falls outside Kant's jurisdiction, and the sentimental Tin Man is pre-emptively condemned. The chapter finally rebels against its Chancellor: cases like returning the slippers, or Toto versus Almira Gulch, show that a world of absolute morality lies only 'somewhere over the rainbow,' and real moral life in Kansas needs Baum's Tin Man heart reconciled with Kantian duty.
- ch. 9 "Dorothy Growing Up in the Land of Oz" **·** Aristotle, Plato, Alasdair MacIntyre — Virtue Ethics (arete); Aristotle on Friendship (philia); Practical Wisdom (phronesis); The Golden Mean — McCall and Auxier use Aristotle's virtue ethics to unpack the Wizard's claim to be 'a good man but a bad wizard' and to argue that no external token—the diploma, heart watch, or medal—can confer virtue, which is cultivated only through practice and experience. Each companion lacks something specific: the Scarecrow the intellectual virtues and phronesis, the Tin Man the feelings and stable states of character that regulate emotion, the Lion the sophrosyne that balances thinking and feeling into courage as the mean between recklessness and cowardice. Aristotle's three grades of friendship (utility, pleasure, virtue) frame the companions' bond with Dorothy, and MacIntyre's account of virtues internal to practices explains why the Wizard's public recognition still matters. Dorothy herself begins as the child Aristotle says cannot yet be virtuous—fleeing Miss Gulch in Kansas—but by defending Toto against the Lion and leading the quest she grows into a responsible moral agent, which is what her delayed homecoming requires.
- ch. 10 "And Your Little Dog Too!" **·** G.W.F. Hegel, Immanuel Kant — The Dialectic; History Has a Direction; Struggle for Recognition — Altamura and Fritzman argue the film has the tripartite structure of Hegel's Phenomenology of Spirit: gray Kansas is a partial truth (thesis), 'somewhere over the rainbow' the antithesis, and the synthesis is Dorothy's discovery that the home she yearned for was Kansas all along—a truth that emerges only through misrecognition, which is why Glinda cannot simply tell her the slippers' power. They read 'Over the Rainbow' through Hegel's 'unhappy consciousness' and side with Hegel's one world against the Kantian two-worlds reading (Kansas as the knowable island, Oz as the unknowable beyond) that they put in the mouths of heckling 'bullies' and detect in Rushdie. Against Rushdie's line that the companions 'already possess what they seek,' they insist the trio really were hollow men who became brainy, tender, and brave through the journey, the Wizard's trinkets supplying Hegelian public recognition. From the Philosophy of History they cast Dorothy as a world-historical individual whose unintentional destruction of both Witches and exposure of the Wizard advances Oz's freedom—Toto's deliberate pulling of the curtain included.
- ch. 11 "Dorothy and the Art of War" **·** Socrates, Plato — Justice in the Republic — The Bells read Baum's book against Bleeding Kansas and John Brown: 1900 readers, they argue, heard 'Kansas' as the slave-or-free question, making Dorothy the Great Emancipator who frees the Munchkins, the Scarecrow, the Tin Woodman, the Winkies, and finally herself from the Witch's servitude. They pair her with Socrates, recasting the Republic's opening scene at Cephalus's slave-owning mansion (Thrasymachus's 'injustice on a sufficiently large scale' pays) as an abolitionist confrontation, and quoting the Phaedo's claim that philosophy is practicing for death to explain why vastly outnumbered reformers—Socrates, Brown at Harpers Ferry and Osawatomie, Dorothy facing the Witch's forty wolves, forty crows, bee swarms, and Winkie army—do not fear the odds. The shared moral: the love of wisdom, pursued in a community of inquirers, charms away the fear of death, which is why tyrants and slave-owners, whose own best part is enslaved, live in terror and cannot sleep at night.
- ch. 12 "Lessons Learned" **·** W.E.B. Du Bois — Virtue Ethics — Curry argues that The Wiz, the all-Black 1978 remake, dramatizes the acquisition of a distinctively Black 'racial virtue'—a habitual excellence defined in quasi-Aristotelian terms and grounded in W.E.B. Du Bois's picture of races as 'vast families' bound by common history and a divinely rooted race feeling (with Alexander Crummell and William H. Ferris as supporting race theorists). Dorothy, a Harlem kindergarten teacher who has never been south of 125th Street, lacks knowledge of self, and each companion personifies a virtue she must claim: the newspaper-stuffed Scarecrow, whose crows' 'crow commandments' and song 'You Can't Win' embody internalized racist miseducation; the Tin Man's capacity for feeling; and Fleetwood Coupe de Ville's courage outside the New York Public Library, glossed through Ferris's call for 'lion-like men.' Evillene's sweatshop creed that 'suffering is food for the soul' personifies the redemptive-suffering myth and failed-integrationism the chapter rejects, while Glinda's 'believe in yourself' and 'A Brand New Day' make honest admittance of racial reality the precondition of 'co-authoring' the world.
- ch. 13 "II - If I Only Had a Mind" *(front)*
- ch. 14 "Epilog-Rolling" **·** Derek Parfit, Plato — Teleporter Paradox (personal fission); Personal Identity — Patton turns the Tin Woodman's backstory—the enchanted axe that costs Nick Chopper his legs, arms, torso and heart, then his head, each replaced by the tinsmith—into a cyborg thought experiment about how much of a person can be swapped out before the person is gone. After a framing nod to Plato's Republic on censoring frightening tales (his father's ban on 'mental violence' fantasy), he brings in Derek Parfit: the R-relation, the 'My Division' brain-split, and the Branch-Line teletransportation case are mapped onto the Woodman (including a hypothetical straw-bodied second Nick) to argue that survival gets different answers from first-person and third-person perspectives. The closing wildcat episode, where the tinned Woodman neatly decapitates a wildcat to save a field mouse, is offered as textual evidence that the tin head failed to preserve moral consistency—so the original Nick Chopper is gone for good, and the Scarecrow's shared brain explains why the two friends later refuse to part.
- ch. 15 "“Here, Here, What’s All This Jabber-wapping When There’s Work to Be Done ”" **·** The Pragmatic Method; Truth as What Works; Stoicism — Tschaepe reads Professor Marvel and the Wizard as skilled givers of 'folk explanations'—stories about desires, beliefs, and feelings that work where logical, scientific, or mechanistic explanations fail—because each explanation is fitted to the pragmatic considerations of its audience and problem. With the companions the Wizard works through a failed dualist argument (a brain is not a mind) for the Scarecrow, an abbreviated Stoic ataraxy pitch for the Tin Man, and Aristotle's discretion-is-valor point for the Lion, settling in each case on folk psychology plus a token (Th.D. diploma, heart-shaped clock, Legion of Courage medal) that supplies the recognition actually needed. Dorothy, whose desire for home is real rather than mistaken, gets no folk explanation from anybody: she must combine the heel-tapping instruction with her own articulated desire—'there's no place like home'—which the chapter treats as pragmatically, not logically, effective.
- ch. 16 "III - I’ve a Feeling We’re Not in Kansas Anymore" *(front)*
- ch. 17 "The Loneliest Witch" **·** Hilary Putnam, Gottlob Frege, Henri Bergson — Twin Earth (semantic externalism); Sense and Reference; Possible Worlds — Auxier gives three theories of why water melts the Wicked Witch, one for each register of Oz: as dream (Bergson's account of dreams as memory explains the fire/water effect-swap), as Baum's fairytale (Susanne Langer's presentational symbols make dryness-wickedness versus water-as-life the tale's moral physics), and as Gregory Maguire's possible world, where the philosophy of language takes over. He narrates Frege's sense/reference distinction—mayor of the Munchkins—to explain how fiction can support truth and falsity ('Dorothy melts the Witch' true, 'Dorothy tried to melt her' false), then deploys Putnam's Twin Earth thought experiment: 'water' in Oz is XYZ while Kansas water is H2O, so Elphaba's word 'water' does not mean what Dorothy's does, and meanings just aren't in the head. Elphaba—the unique being straddling two worlds, killed by H2O she cannot name and terrified by dreams of oceans—is offered as an even stronger argument for Putnam's semantic externalism than Twin Earth's molecule-for-molecule doppelgangers.
- ch. 18 "Conscious Unity “There’s No Place Like Home”" **·** Plato — Theory of Forms — McLachlan maps the whole story onto Schelling's romantic eschatology: gray Kansas is the Ungrund, the boring unconscious unity; the tornado (and Dorothy Gale as 'the gale') is the alienating rupture; and 'there's no place like home' is conscious unity regained through love of concrete, limiting others—richer than the unity she left because it now contains real relationships. Each companion's quest begins in egoism (the Scarecrow as an almost perfect Platonist, all disembodied contemplation like the spider-watching Spinoza; the Tin Man's self-loving imperviousness to pain), while evil in Oz—Miss Gulch's totalizing order, the Witches' slave states, the hidden Wizard—is Schelling's second evil: the attempt to control everything and abolish the freedom of others. Against escape-eschatologies (Plato's shadow-world, Bunyan's Pilgrim who flees his family crying 'eternal life,' Dante turning from Beatrice to the divine light), the Schellingian lesson is that even God becomes personal through relation—which is why the Dainty China Princess's refusal to be a mantel ornament marks the exact point of Dorothy's education in love.
- ch. 19 "Roll Credits" **·** Jean-Jacques Rousseau, Aristotle, Immanuel Kant, David Hume — The Problem of Evil; Natural Goodness and Corruption by Society; Virtue Ethics (arete) — Dunn contrasts Baum's book, where evil is wonderfully 'small'—Dorothy melts the Witch in a fit of righteous anger at a stolen Silver Shoe, and personal plus social virtues (kindness repaid by the field-mice Queen) reliably conquer it—with the 1939 film, which smuggles back the 'heartaches and nightmares' Baum banished and resolves the Witch only by an accidental stroke of luck. He sorts Oz's harms into moral evils (the Witch's enslavements, the Wizard's frauds) and natural evils (the cyclone, the Deadly Poppy Field), cites Hume's catalogue of the world's ills, and observes that Baum needs neither Kant's postulate of God and a future life to join virtue with happiness nor John Hick's 'vale of soul-making' theodicy—Oz simply delivers the reward. Dorothy's abundant virtue at age five rebukes Aristotle (children, he says, cannot be virtuous or happy) and instead embodies Rousseau's Emile, natural goodness uncorrupted by civilization, while Chesterton's Doctrine of Conditional Joy and Tolkien's insistence on serious, non-technological magic frame the Old-World Christian outlook that Baum's demystified American fairy tale—and the movie's ending—quietly betray.
- ch. 20 "Getting to Oz" **·** David Lewis, Charles Sanders Peirce — Possible Worlds; Personal Identity — Auxier uses the Wizard's demand that Dorothy kill the Witch to split 'ought' from 'can'—ethics from the metaphysics and logic of possibility—and then asks whether Oz is a genuinely possible world. He explains David Lewis's modal realism (every genuine possibility actual in its own causally isolated world) through counterfactuals about which network aired the film, dissolves the silver-versus-ruby slippers conflict by assigning book-Oz and film-Oz to different possible worlds, and argues that since no flat contradiction can be eked out of the Oz stories, Lewis is committed to Oz's being real—though Maguire's inter-world travel and Elphaba's hybrid, soul-less trans-world identity (her counterpart in our world being, playfully, Dorothy) stretch Lewis's 'counterparts' doctrine to its limit. Peirce supplies the closing caution, with Boole, that logic is a set of norms for how we can and ought to think rather than a proven mirror of other worlds, so believing in Oz's full reality remains permissible fun rather than an obligation.
- ch. 21 "IV - (Completely) Over the Rainbow" *(not yet read)*
- ch. 22 "Wait, Did I Hit Pause Where’d the Remote Get to . ." **·** John Dewey — Pragmatism; Experience and Education — Seng takes up the fan phenomenon of syncing The Wizard of Oz with Pink Floyd's Dark Side of the Moon and tests the three explanations fans offer: authorial intent (rejected on Pink Floyd's own repeated denials), Jungian synchronicity (rejected as needlessly complex, with the L. Frank Baum coat worn by Frank Morgan as the test case), and pure fluke. He argues the symmetries (the cash-register intro of 'Money' as Dorothy enters Technicolor, 'The Great Gig in the Sky' over the cyclone) are neither objective nor mind-created reality, but meanings made by a third factor in the experience: the viewer whose perceptual habits, in Dewey's and Louise Rosenblatt's sense, supply interpretations like reading the color-splash as prosperity or as the cost of Dorothy's lost innocence. Dorothy's own longing for home becomes the emblem of how habit, not reasoning, governs what we perceive and like.
- ch. 23 "Nightmares" **·** Søren Kierkegaard, Maurice Merleau-Ponty — Anxiety (the dizziness of freedom); Embodiment (the lived body); Phenomenology — Auxier asks why the flying monkeys and the shriveling feet of the Wicked Witch of the East give children lifelong nightmares, and answers with a phenomenology of three frightening sequences in escalating duration: the instant shriveling shot, the five-minute cyclone (paired with 'The Great Gig in the Sky' in the Dark Side of the Rainbow sync), and the monkey air-raid. Kierkegaard's Concept of Anxiety supplies the depth psychology: innocence is ignorance, and the child's dreaming spirit projects its own nothing outward, fastening fear onto figures like the monkeys (who are, he notes, played by the Munchkin actors themselves). Merleau-Ponty's lived body versus habit body explains the mechanics: cinema seizes the body's usual 'tour of inspection,' Fleming manipulates active and passive synthesis (the scoreless build-up to the window blow, the barely noticed arsenal of weapons in the Haunted Forest, the dimming lights), and children construct a rigid, inescapable habit body within a single viewing, so the nightmare is the monkey inside our own projected world.
- ch. 24 "V - No Place Like Home" *(front)*
- ch. 25 "Home Again" **·** Existentialism — Flamm and Rea read the Oz story as an existential fable of the appearance-reality distinction in human development: children delight in admitted unrealities while adults live by ideals, and Dorothy's growth is learning to trade the one for the other, dramatized in the China Princess scene where Dorothy must abandon her plan to keep the animated figure frozen on Aunt Em's mantel. The Wizard embodies the American humbug in the P.T. Barnum mold, forcing green spectacles on the Emerald City's citizens so the city only appears green, science as technical illusion without deeper ideals, while the silver shoes reveal that the imaginative escape Dorothy sought was hers all along. Her homecoming is therefore bittersweet and existential: she can never return to the Kansas she left, 'but she can return to herself,' carrying the crayon box of self-chosen ideals with which to color her own reality.
- ch. 26 "Must I Repeat Myself" **·** Plato, Søren Kierkegaard, Socrates — Allegory of the Cave; The Unexamined Life — Seng maps Dorothy's journey onto Plato's cave allegory: Kansas is the cave of shadows, Oz the painful ascent into light, and the crucial shared point is Socrates' demand that the freed prisoner go back down into the cave, which explains why Dorothy, once enlightened, must return to gray Kansas bearing self-knowledge. He surveys the film adaptations (1910, Semon's 1925, Eshbaugh's 1933, Fleming's 1939, Return to Oz, and The Wiz) to show that each rewrites 'home' as self-knowledge rather than a place, culminating in Glinda's The Wiz speech, 'If we know ourselves, we're always home, anywhere.' Kierkegaard's contrast between recollection and repetition then sorts the homecomings: Baum's Dorothy wills recollection of what was lost, while Diana Ross's Dorothy wills repetition, holding to her self amid change, which Seng says Kierkegaard regards as the equivalent of faith.
- ch. 27 "A Happy Ending" **·** Aristotle, Plato, Martin Heidegger, Jeremy Bentham — Eudaimonia (flourishing); Aristotle on Friendship (philia); Being-Toward-Death — Bickham asks why Dorothy is so eager to leave heroic, Technicolor Oz for the gray, childless, Gulch-dominated farm, and whether, like Hamlet's delay, her rash choice can be evaluated as hers rather than her author's. Aristotle's account of the good life as the exercise of our highest capacities, requiring friends and material goods, makes Oz look like the better home for the competent twelve- or thirteen-year-old he takes Dorothy to be, against the hedonisms of Aristippus, Epicurus, and Bentham and Plato's body-denying dualism. But caring about Dorothy first needs defending from the paradox of fiction, which he works through Currie's simulation theory and Walton's make-believe emotions before dissolving the puzzle: storytelling suspends the reign of logic, so wishing Dorothy well is natural. Finally Heidegger's being-toward-death shows 'happily ever after' is impossible anywhere, and he throws the problem to the reader: write a happy ending for Dorothy in Kansas.
- ch. 28 "You Can Go Home Again" **·** Personal Identity; Communitarianism — Fortune stages the movie as a morality play between two conceptions of personhood: the Wizard is an atomist, a self-sufficient ball from the Chuck E. Cheese ball pit who is the same humbug in Omaha or Oz and who reduces heart, brains, and courage to a testimonial, a diploma, and a medal, while Dorothy is a relationalist, a pineapple chunk that belongs on one particular pizza, a person constituted by her web of relations to home. 'There's no place like home' is therefore the relationalist's slogan, an acceptance of home for everything it is, good and bad, with Toto's fate still unresolved under Miss Gulch's law. He closes by suggesting the film's delayed television-era triumph is existential nostalgia: modern, rootless, ear-budded Americans keep watching because the relationalist country it mourns is 'what's in our blood.'
- ch. 29 "VI - Something Wicked This Way Comes" *(front)*
- ch. 30 "Mirror, Mirror" **·** Simone de Beauvoir, John Stuart Mill, Marilyn Frye, Peter Singer, Mary Wollstonecraft — One Is Not Born a Woman; Animal Liberation; Ecofeminism; Feminism — Sailors convicts Dorothy of being the stereotypical female of Baum's and Fleming's versions, disowning responsibility for killing both witches and living to please others, then contrasts her with Elphaba of Wicked as a feminist hero who leaves home, protects Animals, and mixes 'hot' male and 'cold' female anger into a single fury. She reads Elphaba's indeterminate gender through Marilyn Frye's point that easily identifiable groups are the easiest to oppress, through de Beauvoir's 'One is not born, but becomes a woman' and Mill's line that the 'nature of women' is an eminently artificial thing. Singer's liberation logic, together with the Wollstonecraft-Taylor 'Vindication of the Rights of Brutes' episode and the ecofeminist link between the oppression of women and animals, is applied to Elphaba's Animal-rights activism and her position as 'neither man nor beast,' before the Witch's mirror recognition of Dorothy forces the author to soften her verdict.
- ch. 31 "As Nasty as I Wanna Be" **·** Plato, David Hume — Ring of Gyges (why be moral?); Reason Is the Slave of the Passions; Consequentialism; Deontology — Greene asks whether Maguire's Elphaba is really wicked, first clearing away her reputation (she adopts the 'Wicked Witch' name because 'it liberates you from convention,' echoing Glaucon's challenge in the Republic about the just person with an unjust reputation and vice versa) and then her nature (the midwife-biting birth, green skin, and witchcraft establish no vampire-like evil essence, since Glinda and Nor are good witches). Following Hume's view that morality is not found in the world but in our assessments, he treats wickedness as a matter of performing actions we judge evil, and weighs Elphaba's life under both headings: good intentions with horrific outcomes (the bees that kill the chef, the icicle that kills Manek, Sarima's family executions) and bad intentions with none (her two failed attempts on Madame Morrible). The verdict is deliberately suspended: if she is wicked at all, it can only be through the consequences of her actions.
- ch. 32 "Ineptitude or Wickedness" **·** David Hume, Karl Marx — Reason Is the Slave of the Passions; Ideology and False Consciousness — Durand begins from Hume's moral sentiments, the way first impressions (Miss Gulch, the farmer-actors, Professor Marvel) fix our judgments, and shows how Wicked delivers the new information that muddies them, relocating villainy from the green Witch to the green Wizard: an alien despot who scorns Oz's communal moral sense ('I am beyond that lisping childish recital of wrongs and rights'), stations an army of observation at the Witch's borders, and schemes to annex Munchkinland after Nessarose's fall. Marx's 'opium of the masses' frames the religion of Frex and Nessarose as the tool that first pacified, then repressed, the Munchkinlanders, while the Wizard's 'reunification' that is really annexation shows imperial ideology at work. Elphaba's own 'wickedness' comes out as habituated vengeance and an embraced reputation, so the title question is whether she is inept rather than evil, 'a bad witch, but not a wicked one,' and no worse than the Wizard who is merely 'a very bad wizard.'
- ch. 33 "Who’s Afraid of Virginia Woolf" **·** Friedrich Nietzsche, Plato, Socrates — The Apollonian and the Dionysian — Auxier tells the history of matriarchy theory from Bachofen onward, whose stages (Tellurian earth-motherhood, lunar water cults, then Dionysian and Apollonian fire clearing the way for patriarchy) were adapted by Nietzsche in The Birth of Tragedy, quoted by Engels, and developed by Jane Ellen Harrison, Jung, and Gimbutas. He then reads Maguire's Oz myths as survivals of that declining matriarchy: the Kumbric Witch as the Tellurian earth stage, Mother Yackle's Philosophy Club as degraded Dionysian rites, the silver slippers as the totality of feminine power, and the Silver-slippers portrait Boq finds as the earth-to-water transition, noting that Plato's victory of reason over myth is what dragged women's traditional authority down with it. The chapter's payoff is its title claim: Elphaba is Virginia Woolf, her Charmed Circle the Bloomsbury Group, her terror of water and fiery, watery death a repetition of Woolf's drowning 'baptism' under patriarchy.
- ch. 34 "Doctors of Thinkology" *(notes)*
- ch. 35 "Index" *(notes)*
- ch. 36 "The Wizard of Oz and Philosophy" *(notes)*
- ch. 37 "Copyright Page" *(front)*

## Transformers and Philosophy - More than Meets the Mind

- ch. 1 "Transformers and Philosophy" *(not yet read)*
- ch. 2 "Transformers and Philosophy" *(not yet read)*
- ch. 3 "Transformers and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 4 "Transformers and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 5 "Transformers and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 6 "Transformers and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 7 "Transformers and Philosophy" **·** Alan Turing — The Turing Test; Personal Identity; Applied Ethics (bioethics, technology, AI) — The editors' introduction frames the volume around First Contact with a robotic civilization: citing SETI's Seth Shostak, they argue aliens are more likely to be AI machines than little green men, with the Autobot-Decepticon civil war catching humans in between on a moral as well as physical plane. They pose the book's questions about Optimus Prime and Megatron: whether Transformers have moral status and minds despite passing the Turing Test, whether they have souls and are capable of Platonic or erotic love, and what makes Optimus the same being whether robot or vehicle. Probing Transformer nature, they conclude, is really probing human nature, since Transformers are our own creations.
- ch. 8 "Transformers and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 9 "Transformers and Philosophy" **·** Applied Ethics (bioethics, technology, AI) — Hall predicts what real alien Transformers would be like: any starfaring civilization is at least Kardashev Type 1 and would send nanotechnological, shape-shifting robots rather than biological explorers, since minds upload into machine bodies and colonies go all-robot. He proposes a logarithmic 'Hall scale' of ethics, arguing that interstellar cooperation requires Level 10 morality whose expanding inclusiveness would give visitors enough concern for indigenous species to leave Earth as a nature preserve, since robots need only the matter and energy of Mercury and the outer planets, not our biosphere.
- ch. 10 "Transformers and Philosophy" **·** Alan Turing — The Turing Test — Shook dismantles the four assumptions behind the argument that advanced aliens would already be here (that they want to find us, that travel is easy, that visits would leave traces, and that disguised visitors must be benign), showing the Transformers scenario - a warring robotic civilization that crashes into Earth unnoticed and disguises itself as Chevys and boomboxes like Bumblebee and Frenzy - is more probable than SETI's radio-based angel-alien mythology. War, not peace, drives the technology of real civilizations, as Trailbreaker's 'Simple. Disguise!' explanation of transformation shows, so First Contact will likely be with militaristic strangers absorbed in their own conflicts, not missionaries.
- ch. 11 "Transformers and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 12 "Transformers and Philosophy" **·** Maurice Merleau-Ponty, René Descartes, George Berkeley, Edmund Husserl — Embodiment (the lived body); Phenomenology; Mind–Body Dualism — Using the hidden sides of Transformers - a robot inside a Camaro, a tank inside a robot - the authors stage the philosophy of perception: Descartes's innate geometrical rules and Berkeley's bodily cross-referencing both fail to explain how Sam Witwicky can know the steelyard robot is his Camaro, while Merleau-Ponty's embodied account of focus, background, and 'indeterminate vision' explains it through the perceiver's lived body. They extend this to the 'Autobot Spike' mind-transfer episode and to CGI: virtual Transformers on screen feel hollow because light-created images have no hidden surfaces for our bodies to investigate and grasp.
- ch. 13 "Transformers and Philosophy" **·** Aristotle, John Locke, Judith Jarvis Thomson, Thomas Aquinas — Personal Identity; Personal Identity (memory theory); Aristotle on the Soul; Potentiality and Actuality — Spicher argues that when Bumblebee trades his beat-up 1976 Camaro for a shiny 2007 model only his appearance changes (an accidental change), never his identity, because transforming is part of a Transformer's essence like a human wearing a mask. Locke's memory continuity and Thomson's bodily criterion each fail - the embryo, coma, and corpse problems, plus the thought experiment of Megatron's memories transplanted into another body - so he defends an Aristotelian composite of form and matter in which the Spark is the soul-like form organizing the machine body, preserving identity through transformation as form guides matter toward actuality.
- ch. 14 "Transformers and Philosophy" **·** Philosophical Skepticism — Steiff reads the 2007 movie as a sustained lesson in perceptual error: Sam's Porsche disappointment at the dealership, the parents' 'earthquake' misperception, and the Camaro that no one at Bobby B's lot suspects show that we see what context, expectation, and conditioning (the kitten experiments) lead us to see. She distinguishes illusion - misperceiving an object really present, as Sam misperceives Bumblebee as just a car - from hallucination, and weighs direct realism against representative realism's 'veil of perception' using film tricks from Melies's jammed camera to the Lumiere brothers' panic-inducing train, leaving Sam's knowledge resting on fallible but indispensable sense experience.
- ch. 15 "Transformers and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 16 "Transformers and Philosophy" **·** John Searle, Alan Turing, René Descartes — The Chinese Room; The Turing Test; Mind–Body Dualism — Pike asks what the All Spark's zap would have to do to give brought-to-life vending machines and steering wheels genuine minds, testing logical behaviorism's dispositions and Turing's conversation test against Searle's Chinese Room ('syntax is insufficient for semantics'), and weighing Descartes's God-given soul against physicalist alternatives: functionalism, Searle's biological naturalism, and reductive physicalism. Since we can never access another mind from the inside, he concludes that by abductive inference to the best explanation - Optimus Prime's mourning, planning, and moral decision-making being too complex for mere programming - we have as much reason to credit a Transformer with a mind as any human around us.
- ch. 17 "Transformers and Philosophy" **·** Aristotle — Aristotle on the Soul; Virtue Ethics (arete); Immortality and the Soul — Eyestone argues Transformers are genuinely alive with souls by applying Aristotle's De Anima: self-motion, perception, and intellect fit Ratchet (who tells Buster 'I'm a machine, and I hear you!'), and even the troublesome nutritive faculty is secured, since Energon ingestion, agelessness, and building each other suit naturally immortal, semimortal beings whose reproduction resembles Primus's creation. He extends arete to machines - Megatron has the excellence of a military leader but not of a person, while Optimus's chosen, trained goodness (Grimlock's 'Autobot training,' the Aerialbots' decision in 'War Dawn') makes him virtuous - and answers his title question 'why not?', since Aristotle's mortal soul gives Transformers no worse a shot at heaven than we have.
- ch. 18 "Transformers and Philosophy" **·** Moral Psychology (character, vice, weakness of will) — A fictional five-session counseling narrative in which a burned-out Optimus Prime - running out of gas, embarrassed by 'WASH ME' written in his own dirt - secretly sees therapist Dr. Sara Sawyer. Through the empty-chair technique he speaks to the dead Jazz, admitting the survivor guilt of a leader who was fighting Megatron when Jazz fell, and confesses the night he nearly smashed a human teenager's car mistaken for a Decepticon; the Miracle Question helps him imagine peace. The dialogue dramatizes an internal transformation: the machine learns to name grief, anger, and sadness, admit mistakes to his team, and take R and R - in the authors' phrase, becoming more human.
- ch. 19 "Transformers and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 20 "Transformers and Philosophy" **·** Applied Ethics (bioethics, technology, AI) — Arp defines personhood through five capacities (rationality, robust mental states, genuine language rather than information transmission, social relationships, and free autonomous action) and argues Transformers meet them all: Bumblebee's homing-signal syllogism, Frenzy's Air Force One hack, Megatron's curses when foiled, and Optimus's fatherly bond with Sam. A thought experiment of gradually replacing a man's brain, then body, with silicon shows that cognitive capacities, not carbon, make a person, so deceiving Decepticons are morally responsible persons who deserve blame and 'robot jail' rather than the scrapping reserved for mindless machines.
- ch. 21 "Transformers and Philosophy" **·** Plato, Aristotle, Immanuel Kant, Zeno of Citium — Platonic Love (the ladder of love); Aristotle on Friendship (philia); Stoic Cosmopolitanism (citizens of the world); Respect for Persons — Watson and Arp ask whether Ironhide and Chromia's embrace means Autobots can love, rejecting programmed loving behavior, implanted souls, and wired-in human brains as insufficient for genuine personhood, then sort Transformer relationships into Western conceptions of love. Empedoclean cosmic Love and Strife map onto Primus versus Unicron; Platonic philia ascends to the Form of Beauty, with Transformer souls originating in and returning to the All Spark; Aristotle's three friendships separate Optimus and Sparkplug's virtue-friendship from Megatron and Starscream's mere utility; and Stoic-Kantian equal love explains Autobots saving Decepticons who immediately try to kill them, alongside Christian agape in Autobot family bonds.
- ch. 22 "Transformers and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 23 "Transformers and Philosophy" **·** Immanuel Kant, Thomas Hobbes — The Categorical Imperative; Humanity as an End in Itself; Ethical Egoism — Michaud reads the Autobot-Decepticon war as a contest of ethical systems: Optimus's fair fighting, his refusal to ignore human casualties, and Bumblebee's friendship with the inferior Spike instantiate Kant's categorical imperative and the treatment of rational beings as ends in themselves. The Decepticons run through the egoist tradition - Hobbes's psychological egoism from Leviathan, Starscream's self-defeating normative egoism, and Megatron as Ayn Rand's rational egoist taunting 'You still fight for the weak; that is why you lose' - leaving the reader to choose between Kant's world of peaceful obligation and Rand's world of power where, like Starscream, you die alone.
- ch. 24 "Transformers and Philosophy" **·** Friedrich Nietzsche, Plato — Master and Slave Morality; The Übermensch; Moral Relativism — Barkman takes the 1986 movie poster's 'Beyond Good. Beyond Evil.' as a Nietzsche allusion and argues the cartoon refutes it: Skyfire's 'It is wrong,' Orion Pax's repudiation of admired power in 'War Dawn,' and Optimus's humble 'Thrones are for Decepticons' make Megatron a would-be superman and villain while Optimus is a Platonic guardian of a universal moral law binding even Unicron and the Quintessons. A reductio against cultural relativism (the Decepticon invasion of Earth, First Aid's failed pacifism in 'The Ultimate Weapon') plus C.S. Lewis-style archetypes explain why Optimus - a Christ-type who died saving the world - endures while GoBots do not.
- ch. 25 "Transformers and Philosophy" **·** Virtue Ethics — Neil maps Optimus Prime onto Joseph Campbell's hero's journey: the naive dock worker Orion Pax is deceived and nearly killed by Megatron, rebuilt by the ancient Primus as the first Autobot, and raised to leadership through the four stages of separation, trials, supernatural aid, and return. Evolutionary 'group-fitness' psychology explains why children wept at his 1986 death, and his cooperative coaching-style leadership is cataloged episode by episode - honoring the duel he lost to Megatron's cheating in 'Heavy Metal War,' refusing to condemn Mirage without evidence, exposing the evil clone by insisting on rescuing Spike, and turning Omega Supreme from revenge to save San Francisco before his sacrificial passing of the Matrix.
- ch. 26 "Transformers and Philosophy" *(front)*
- ch. 27 "Transformers and Philosophy" **·** William James — Shook diagnoses Megatron through William James's expansive, possessive self turned pathological: a megalomaniac whose self encompasses everything and who must control it all ('My hunger for power!', 'Compassion is for fools!'). Reconstructing Megatron's pronouncements into a 'philosophy of Strength' - might makes right, perpetual war against the weak, 'Peace through tyranny' echoing Orwell - he identifies it as fascism on the model of Hitler and Mussolini's perpetual-war state, against which Optimus's cooperative 'till all are one' Community is not a rival regime but an ethical ideal that only we can put into practice.
- ch. 28 "Transformers and Philosophy" **·** John Searle, Alan Turing, Daniel Dennett — The Chinese Room; The Turing Test; Applied Ethics (bioethics, technology, AI); Philosophical Zombies — Koepsell accepts that Autobots are virtuous and Decepticons vicious, then asks whether ethical war-bots are even possible, using the military's autonomous-weapons program, Asimov's Three Laws, and a kitten-sensing washing machine to argue that designed-in 'ethics' is only the designer's morality, like A Clockwork Orange's conditioned Alex, because it involves no real choice. Searle's Chinese Room and Turing's test then cast doubt on whether any mind, even our own, makes genuinely free choices, so he concludes that the appearance of virtue - behavior that reliably works - is all we ever have for humans or Autobots alike, and we should build machines as well as Optimus Prime and Bumblebee.
- ch. 29 "Transformers and Philosophy" **·** Aristotle, Philippa Foot — Eudaimonia (flourishing); Virtue Ethics (arete); Practical Wisdom (phronesis); Natural Rights — Plauche grounds Optimus Prime's line 'Freedom is the right of all sentient beings' in 'Aristotelian liberalism': since Energon, like our food, is scarce and requires labor and creativity, any volitional artificial life needs a moral code whose standard is flourishing (following Foot's natural goodness), cultivating intellectual and moral virtues from prudence, independence, and honesty to benevolence and courage. Ricardo and Mises's law of association - illustrated by Ratchet and a human mechanic both profiting from specialization despite Ratchet's absolute superiority - plus the ruinous cost of war give even superior aliens reason to respect our rights to life, liberty, and property rather than enslave or exterminate us, as Megatron would.
- ch. 30 "Transformers and Philosophy" *(notes)*
- ch. 31 "Transformers and Philosophy" *(notes)*
